Acknowledgments




Writing Indigenous Methodologies, both the first and second editions,
was a challenge, and it would not have been published without a
force of individuals encouraging and compelling me forward. There
are many to acknowledge. Deep gratitude to Indigenous graduate
students, Indigenous faculty colleagues, Indigenous Elders, and the
Indigenous community for the grace, generosity, clarity, and vision as
you uphold Indigenous culture and research. This book is an ode to
you. To the many non-Indigenous friends of this book, thank you for
being there: the world expands where support exists.
   To my family – Monty Montgomery, Rachel and Dave – for your love
and support throughout this writing. Monty, thank you for helping
me to clarify my thinking and to word-craft ideas, and for reminding
me to hear the morning. To both my mothers, you are both a source of
love and inspiration, and I thank you. To both my fathers, though you
are in the spirit world, I felt your presence as I wrote. To my siblings,
nieces, nephews, aunties, and uncles in both my families, you have
given me a belonging in this world, and it is only through belonging
that I could embark on this work. To good friends, too many to name,
who have been a source of camaraderie, support, laughter, and above
of all encouragement in writing this book, you have my gratitude.
   I would like to acknowledge the supervisory committee for my PhD,
Budd Hall, Leslie Brown, Leroy Little Bear, and Barbara Whittington;
xii   Acknowledgments


      you guided me through my doctoral work and gave me confidence to
      first write this book. To the six individuals who shared their research
      stories with me, Kathy Absolon, Jeannine Carriere, Laara Fitznor,
      ­Michael Hart, Graham Smith, and Cam Willett, I give thanks.
          To the manuscript reviewers of this book: thank you. I have come
       to appreciate the critical work of peer reviews and know this book
       has benefited from your care and feedback. Thank you to the artists
       Ernie Scoles and Leah Dorion whose work appears in the first and
       second edition, respectively. I would like to thank the past and pres-
       ent University of Toronto Press editors, the late Virgil Duff, Douglas
       Hildebrand, and Meg Patterson, for believing in the first and second
       editions of this book. Thank you for prompting, at times cajoling, and
       always believing in Indigenous Methodologies: Characteristics, Conversa-
       tions, and Contexts. To all who have made this book possible: Hai, Hai,
       miigwetch, and thank you.
PROLOGUE

 Opening the Circle




 Tânisi. Margaret, nitisiyihkâson, Kovach, nitaspiyikasôn. My Nêhiyaw
 name is sākohtēw pīsimw iskwēw. I am Nêhiyaw and Saulteaux,
 and my relations are of the Pasqua and Okanese First Nations in
 southern Saskatchewan. I am a member of Pasqua First Nations #79
 ­located in the Qu’Appelle Valley. Pasqua is a Saulteaux and Nêhiyaw
  First Nation, a member of File Hills Qu’Appelle Tribal Council, and
  a part of Treaty Four territory. Pasqua was settled in 1876 and is an
  ­Algonquian-language-speaking community. I was born to my birth
   mother in a rural Saskatchewan hospital and registered to Pasqua
   First Nations at birth. On my mother’s side, my bloodline is Plains
   Cree and Saulteaux. On my father’s side, it is Plains Cree. I was
   adopted at the age of three months and raised within a loving fam-
   ily in a small, rural Saskatchewan farm near Qu’Appelle Valley. My
   adoptive parents are of Eastern European, of Hungarian descent. My
   mother speaks the language, and my father spoke Hungarian as well.
   Both of my fathers have now made the transition to the spirit world.
   I was raised knowing that culture counts. I was raised knowing com-
   passion counts. I have good relations with both my families. I am a
   daughter and a s­ ister. I am a partner of over twenty years (my part-
   ner’s name is Monty); I am a stepmom, an auntie, a friend, a professor,
   a curriculum developer, a researcher, a student, a seeker – all form my
   identity. I honour all whose love has sustained me.
2   Indigenous Methodologies


        For this prologue I am introducing myself purposefully for it is re-
    lational work. In community, I would share this through talk – I would
    give enough information about my lineage and those who raised me
    for people to “suss me out.” People would nod. I would know if they
    understand. It is different in writing. For the protocol of introduc-
    tion, Māori scholar Graham Hingangaroa Smith suggests a prologue
    in Indigenous research. A prologue is a function of narrative writing
    that signifies a prelude. It encompasses essential information for the
    reader to make sense of the story to follow. While not every written
    narrative needs a prologue, it can be a useful device. Within Indige-
    nous research writing, a prologue structures space for introductions.
    It is a precursory signal to the careful reader that woven throughout
    the varied forms of our writing – analytical, reflective, expository –
    there will be story, our story, for story is who we are.
        I understand my responsibility, so why am I having such difficulty?
    All that this prologue asks of me is to share enough about myself to
    prepare the reader for my work. I go to write and get tangled in years’
    worth of reflections, unable to make choices about what to put on pa-
    per. Both a lot and not much is different since I started this venture,
    and I do not know what to say about it exactly, how to start writ-
    ing. I think of Anne Lamott’s (1994) Bird by Bird: Some Instructions on
    Writing and Life. The title, Bird by Bird, comes from a childhood mem-
    ory she shares with the reader midway through the book. Through
    a memory flash, Lamott tells a story of her father and brother. The
    father is a writer. The brother is a school-aged student at the time. The
    brother is working on a homework assignment, an essay on birds that
    is due the next morning. The deadline is coming down hard, and the
    brother is panicking. Too many birds. Too much to say. Can’t write.
    The father-writer comes into the kitchen and asks what’s going on.
    According to my memory, the dialogue roughly unfolds with the boy
    unloading. “Can’t do it!” “I am toast.” The father-writer says to his
    son “slow down,” “breathe,” – “It’s bird by bird, kiddo.” Lamott’s
    point: start small, be specific, breathe, it will come. I take a note from
    Lamott’s songbook. I think about the title of Lee Maracle’s (2015) book
    Memory Serves: Oratories. I begin to craft a prelude for this research
    methodology book, for my research story – it is of no use trying to sep-
    arate the two. This is a story of coming to know with narrative appear-
    ing in bits and pieces throughout. This research story will reveal itself.
                                               Prologue: Opening the Circle   3


As I struggle to start, flashes of memory surface. Bird by bird through
each recollection, the past is prologue, memory the prelude …

Memory serves, circa 1969. Early memories. I am five or six. I grew up
on a farm, and when I was young, I played by the slough, in the trees
around our house, and sometimes on the unbroken prairie between
my house and my Auntie’s place not too far way. I remember run-
ning around on the prickly grass, picking dandelions, and collecting
odd-shaped rocks. A neighbour said that arrowheads were commonly
found on the prairie. I was old enough to understand that arrowheads
were from Native people, and that I, too, was of Native ancestry. My
parents told me this. They told me to be proud of who I am. In this
memory, I am searching for arrowheads. I am connecting arrowheads
with the land and circling it back to me. I am connecting my worlds.
The memory fades and another surfaces. I am running around the
kitchen while the adults (Mom, Dad, aunties, uncles, neighbours) sit
at the kitchen table talking politics. This was my first critical social
theory classroom. They are talking about Tommy Douglas. I hear my
Dad saying that Tommy Douglas was a good man because he was
not a big shot and he fought for the little guy. Since then, I have sat in
many classrooms, racking up a few degrees, voraciously taking in the
words of brilliant critical theorists such as Habermas and Freire. Dis-
tilling the message of these theorists accounting for the haves and the
have nots, I hear Dad’s words: big shot, little guy. By these memories,
I know this research, Indigenous methodologies, is about miskâsowin.
Miskâsowin is Nêhiyaw. Miskâsowin says, go to the centre of yourself to
find your own belonging (Cardinal & Hildebrandt, 2000).

Memory serves, circa 1980. Late at night in Regina (reflecting on my
youth), I write in my journal, “If I have any romantic notions about
my life it is that I was born under a prairie sky with a Bob Seger tune
playing in the distance.” I know this is romanticism because I was
born indoors, and Seger did not record until after my birth. Yet, my
beginnings allow for a self-constructed story. I imagine that is what
happens when you are an adoptee: you fill in blanks. Although a cer-
tain mystery follows adoptees, my life has not been that exotic. I was a
Native kid who grew up ‘round and about a small rural Saskatchewan
town. I was loved but conflicted, questioning where I belonged, trying
4   Indigenous Methodologies


    to stay at distances yet needing connection. I could go on – there are
    many possibilities for proceeding with the prologue, but I do not want
    to write a life narrative. This work is about research nested within
    universities, so I will focus there.

    Memory serves, circa 1984. I am at university – my first year at the
    ­University of Regina and I’m meandering around: majoring in psy-
     chology, drawn to sociology, and then registering in the bachelor
     of social work program. Like many young Indigenous students of
     my generation, post-secondary education has become a portal for
     self-discovery. Exploring identity unleashes anger that I thought
     ­
     I had worked through. It also evokes a lot of sadness, though that was
     ­always there. In my mind, identity and education always intersect.
      In the early 1980s, I read my first poem by an American Indian poet:
      Chrystos (1988), Not Vanishing. Later, much later, in a PhD course at the
      University of Victoria, an Indigenous professor will ask us to explore
      our narratives and write these musings on found paper. I photocopy
      Chrystos’s poem, “I Am Not Your Princess,” and I write my mus-
      ing on the back of it. If our soul can hold print markings, Chrystos’s
      words are somewhere on mine: “Don’t assume I know every other
      Native Activist / in the world personally / That I even know names of
      all the tribes / or can pronounce names I’ve never heard” (Chrystos,
      1988, p. 66). This poem pushes back against outsider definitions and
      assumptions and it sticks in my psyche. This poem sticks because my
      own attempts to conform to an outsider’s view of the “Indigenous
      Standard” (i.e., “all Natives are this, all Natives are that”) have failed
      miserably. While I have admittedly been pulled into this unpalatable
      proposition of my own volition, I become resentful if others label me/
      us. I respond to the poem because it does not pull any punches, and
      I appreciate the edge. I get angry about the racism that Indigenous
      people experience. I am writing this here because it drives my work.

    Memory serves, circa 1990–2005. As a social worker, I have worked
    with First Nations organizations serving on-reserve communities in
    counselling and social development, but mostly within adult educa-
    tion. I have taught in social work community-based programs and
    higher education, but at heart I am a curriculum developer. Curriculum
    makes space like nothing else I know in education. In serving equity
                                                Prologue: Opening the Circle   5


and inclusion, curriculum is a mighty tool of social justice. In 2003, I
enrol at the University of Victoria to complete an interdisciplinary PhD
(in education and social work); my research topic focuses on Indige-
nous curriculum and instruction. I start taking research methodology
courses and am not able to understand why research textbooks do not
offer Indigenous frameworks as a methodological option for research.
I cannot leave this alone. It is a curiosity that I ­cannot abandon. I want
to know about the intersectionality between Indigenous knowledges
and research. I switch my research topic. This will take me beyond a
cerebral quest. Indigenous methodologies are steeped in body, story,
place, land, community, and spirit. I return home to Saskatchewan to
start my research. Monty (my spouse) and I are driving down Victo-
ria Avenue East in Regina, Saskatchewan, just before Thanksgiving
weekend. We are heading to my mother’s house. Leaving Regina, we
drive past the A&W, then a bingo hall, and I see a big Safeway sign in
the distance. As we get closer, we can read the words in block letters:
“Utility Turkey on Sale here.” Who knows why, but it strikes a chord.
We get to my mother’s place, and I ask her, “What exactly is a utility
turkey?” As best as I can remember, she says, “A utility turkey is not a
perfect bird, but you can roast it.” As I think about writing a prologue,
it occurs to me that this is pretty much the direction I am seeking with
this prologue, to share just enough to predicate possible conjectures. It
is not a definitive memoir; it is a “utility prologue.”

Memory serves, circa 2006–2007. I conclude my research in Saskatch-
ewan and return to Victoria, BC. I retroflect on an excerpt from my
research journal. On an early Wednesday morning, I go to the library
with Keith Basso’s (1996) book Wisdom Sits in Places in hand. I have
not looked at the book since my flight to Saskatchewan in September.
Flying over the prairies near Regina, on a crisp autumn day, I can see
the golden fields quilted together and feel that customary sense of
familiarity with this place. On an intuitive level, I understand Apache
Elder Charles Henry’s point (from Basso’s book) that, in the grand
scheme, the meaning places have in our lives transcends our own
­momentary existence. I think of the name-place stories of Pasqua, my
 First Nation community – mostly that I do not know them. Yet even
 with this deficit of the cultural stories, I maintain a powerful connec-
 tion to this part of the country that has shaped who I am … The notion
6   Indigenous Methodologies


    of name-places keeps simmering in my mind, and I consider how the
    sky, water, and earth, among other things, contextualize our life. To
    me, it is intriguing the way the Western Apache stories intermingle
    knowing with communicating. Basso’s analysis of the association be-
    tween language and ethnography in understanding a culture makes
    sense, though I question if other variables need consideration. And
    I wonder how Indigenous theorizing translates to the contemporary
    urban Indigenous life of Foucault readers, Starbucks, and sport utility
    vehicles in contrast to oral storytelling, black tea, and trucks. With
    the love, support, mentorship, and guidance of many, in 2007, I walk
    across the stage at the University of Victoria Convocation Hall to re-
    ceive my PhD. I am wearing the University of Victoria black doctoral
    robe with red panels down the front and a turquoise beaded choker.

    Memory serves, circa 2007–2015. I return to Saskatchewan. In 2007,
    I accept a faculty position at the University of Saskatchewan. I am a
    newly minted PhD, pre-tenure assistant professor. I am writing about
    Indigenous methodologies from that place, perspective, and vulnera-
    bility. I move through the ranks to associate professor. In 2015, I present
    at the Think Indigenous Conference hosted by the Indian Teacher Edu-
    cation Program (ITEP), University of Saskatchewan. The talk finishes
    and I leave the podium. I see an Indigenous woman walk towards
    me holding my book, Indigenous Methodologies, so I can see it. There
    are yellow Post-it notes all over the place sticking to marked pages.
    I smile. I like the vibe of a working book. We reach each other, and
    she tells me she is a graduate student. Without apprehensiveness or
    skipping a beat, she says that she is using Indigenous methodologies
    in her research. I am caught momentarily breathless by this matter-of-
    fact assertion of Indigenous methodologies in research. Tears well to
    my eyes. This is what it’s about. This is what is has always been about.
    The Indigenous graduate student leaves but before she goes, she says,
    “I love your book, but it’s time to update it.” I hesitantly nod. She is
    right, it’s time … still I am reluctant.

    Memory serves, circa 2019. Monty and I visit Arizona the summer
    before the pandemic of 2020. We visit the Grand Canyon. I visited
    the Grand Canyon once before in 1989. I was twenty-five years old, a
    master’s of social work student at Carleton University and interning
                                                Prologue: Opening the Circle   7


at the Assembly of First Nations in Ottawa. In August of ’89, a few of
us from the Assembly visited the Navajo Nation on their invitation.
While there, three of us took an impromptu road trip to the canyon.
Pre iPhone, GPS, or Google Maps, this road trip is not preplanned. We
ask around for directions, tap our collective noggin to choose a route,
and throw a folded, torn Arizona/New Mexico road map into the car.
Filling up with gas and day-old gas station coffee, we head off trying
not to miss the highway signs directing us. The world and I are on the
cusp of shape-shifting, metamorphosizing, becoming … Thirty years
later, I am returning to the Grand Canyon.

August 2019 – Sedona, Yavapai County. It is late summer 2019. Monty
and I have booked a one-day tour of the Grand Canyon. Our guide
collects us at our Sedona lodgings. She seems like someone I can trust.
She moved to Sedona about ten years ago to leave the high pressure,
rat race of another life. She tells us, “It was gonna kill me if I didn’t.”
Since arriving in Arizona, our guide has been a photographer, bar-
tender, and park ranger, and she’s now living locally in Sedona. Our
tour group is small. There are eight of us including a couple from
Washington State, a mom from Texas travelling alone with her two
young boys, Monty and me from Canada, and the guide. We’ve all
come to Sedona for our reasons. Today we are heading to the Grand
Canyon. It is a three-hour drive on Highway 89A to Flagstaff. We head
out through Navaho territory, stopping for Indian tacos, and then take
Highway 160 to the Grand Canyon. We reach our destination after
lunch. Arriving at the South Rim, the guide parks the van in the park-
ing lot, and we jump out and walk towards the canyon. We find a
safe edge for our first view of the day. There it is, the Grand Canyon,
before us: expansive and startling. We pause. Standing on the South
Rim, I can see the north side from here. The canyon is a mile deep and
eighteen miles across. It’s one big split. It’s the physical manifestation
of a liminal space in-between.
   Looking out over the Grand Canyon, I see the deep wide split of
this landscape. The chasm that connects the South to the North Rim
seems impossible to traverse. Who can reach across? Our guide sees
Monty and me standing on a ridge lost in thought. I see her and ask
something I’ve been wondering about. “Do people trek across the can-
yon?” I ask. To me, it doesn’t seem possible. There are so many edges
8   Indigenous Methodologies


    and sharp angles, full of rattlers with so many ways to fall. “Yeah,
    people do it all the time,” the guide says. “I did it,” she mentions.
    “It took me about twelve hours.” I am floored and say, “Really, from
    where we are standing it doesn’t seem like you could?” “Yeah,” she
    says again, “folks do it, but it’s not easy. I will give it that. You have to
    be intentional, plan, prepare, and be in shape because once you start
    the only way through it is through it.” She finishes up, “And it’s not
    good to go it alone.” It sounds hard, I think, but good to know that
    with preparation and a hiking companion it is doable. All three of us
    look over the beauty of the canyon, humbled by nature’s power, and
    are lost again in thought.
       The day is winding down, and everyone is tired from a good day
    together. It’s time to head back to Sedona. Driving home, the Wash-
    ington State couple are asking our guide about different routes to the
    canyon’s North Rim. They travelled to Sedona by car and are heading
    back north tomorrow. The guide asks, “What route did you take to get
    here?” The man says, “We went through Utah,” and I hear him say,
    “We drove through Winslow.” Our guide smiles “Winslow, Arizona?
    Did you stand on the corner?” We all smile at the Eagles’ song refer-
    ence. “Yeah, we did,” he says, “there’s a park there now and a souve-
    nir store across the street.” The memory vanishes.

    Memory serves, circa 2019–2020. I am no longer a graduate student. I
    am a full professor at the University of Saskatchewan and starting to
    write this second edition in the fall of 2019. In the white space between
    these two sentences is a world where I no longer live. Yet that I am
    pushing back a lump in my throat as I write tells me that emotion has
    not yet divorced itself from memory. For me, an Indigenous graduate
    student and then an Indigenous scholar, the academic life has been a
    bewildering tangled space of diminishment and praise each with its
    own sharp edges waiting to carve my identity at every turn. Losing
    myself, finding myself. And the soothing voices, the sustaining forces,
    those offering me space and pouring me a cup of tea.

    August 2020. I am returning to BC as I have accepted a faculty position
    at the University of British Columbia. I am a professor in the Faculty
    of Education. I arrive at a new morning where new beginnings sum-
    mon me. Of it all, I know this to be true: For Indigenous graduate
                                               Prologue: Opening the Circle   9


students to thrive, Indigenous scholars to progress through the ranks
of tenure and promotion, and for the academy to decolonize, we will
require an academic culture with fewer sharp edges and rattlers and
more supportive guides to assist in negotiating the divide. We will
need family, community, and ceremony on this journey.

This book emerges from my journey – the people I have met, the places
I have been, the questions I have asked, and the meaning I have made
from the abundant and powerful knowledge shared with me. Our life
story shapes our research interpretations. Past is prologue. Memory is
preface. We know what we know from where we stand. We need to
be honest about that. I situate myself not as a knowledge-keeper – this
has not been my path – rather my role is facilitator. I have a responsi-
bility to help create entry points for Indigenous knowledges to come
through. True to form, during this writing I periodically bemoaned to
Monty that my voice is not Indigenous enough (whatever that means)
for this task. He kept saying, “Keep going, Maggie. What you’re doing
is important.” And so, I have kept going. This writing remains from
the heart: it comes from who I am and all that I am – nothing more, or
less for that matter. It comes from my own need and longing to engage
with my Nêhiyaw and Saulteaux ancestry and to say to my academic
world that my culture counts. It is written from my voice, in my style,
and it reflects who I am. The Elders say that if our work comes from
the heart and if it is done in a good way, it will count. My hope is that
this work will count for Indigenous people in a useful way – that is all.
Introduction




Indigenous Methodologies: Characteristics, Conversations, and Contexts was
first published in 2009. At that time, Indigenous-informed research
methodology was new to many. Since 2009, a momentum in Indigenous
research has inspired local, national, and international discourse and
practice. Indigenous methodologies are being talked up in classrooms,
community halls, conference venues, and public gatherings. Publica-
tions on Indigenous methodologies are many, and Indigenous research
is burgeoning. In light of this growth, the second edition updates and
expands upon the first edition in s­ everal ways with one ­pivotal differ-
ence. In 2009, the first edition arose from an ache and call for a research
culture, a moral ethos, in which ­      Indigenous ­methodologies could
thrive. This new edition finds Indigenous methodologies strong and
offers strategy for this evolving, unfolding research approach. The aim
of the second edition of Indigenous ­Methodologies is to honour the past,
heed the now, and envision the future.
   Indigenous Methodologies (2009) arose from a Nêhiyaw value of giv-
ing back. From my doctoral research, I sought to offer the knowledges
gifted to me so they might assist others. As a graduate student, I was
aware that the public dissemination of my research was an expecta-
tion. Once I wrote and defended my dissertation, I could no longer
evade disseminating the findings. I was wishing to share but hesitant. I
worried that publishing in academic journals would make my research
vulnerable to a colonial-infused degradation. I questioned the risk of
                                                             Introduction   11


subjecting a relational, oral-based knowledge system to academic pub-
lishing with a history of being hostile to it. Conducting, defending, and
publishing Indigenous-informed research in academic venues exposed
Indigenous knowledges to risk. Once they were in the public domain,
could I protect the knowledges? This was not a fear I alone carried. In
general, Indigenous scholars articulated the fear of misinterpretations,
appropriations, manipulations, and dismissals accompanying Indige-
nous knowledges in the academy. The transformative potential for ac-
ademia in welcoming diverse knowledges is significant, but what does
this “welcoming” mean and at what cost to Indigenous peoples? In
accepting the responsibility of publishing a book on Indigenous meth-
odologies in research, I needed to be clear on why I chose to persist.
   At its core, my purpose for writing this book is to serve Indigenous
culture. Upholding Indigenous methodologies is about Indigenous
cultural sustainability. Cultural longevity depends on the ability to
sustain cultural knowledges. At the heart of a cultural renaissance,
Indigenous or otherwise, is a restoration and respectful application of
that culture’s knowledge systems. Colonial history and contemporary
racism persist in disrupting the ability of Indigenous peoples to up-
hold knowledges by cultural methodologies. While colonialism and
racism, both past and present, have interrupted this organic trans-
mission, many Indigenous peoples recognize that for our cultural
knowledge to thrive, it must live in many sites, and so we cannot, will
not, disengage. These sites include education and research. At pres-
ent, there is more space, more Indigenous methodological literature
to call upon, and more familiarity with Indigenous methodologies.
Still, there is risk in bringing Indigenous knowledges into the acad-
emy while individual and systemic racism persist; however, proceed
we must because much is at stake.
   I proceed with a book on Indigenous methodologies because it
has a social justice imperative that impacts Indigenous people, one
that influences policy and practice. Policy and programming grow
out of research. While the influence of research methodologies is
not always visible in the research-policy-practice cycle, the influence
of research methodology on how research questions are asked, to
whom, the findings that are found, and whether knowledge will be
shared with communities cannot be understated. Methodology in-
fluences research outcomes. Research outcomes create policy. Policy
12   Indigenous Methodologies


     generates programs. Programs guide practice. It turtles all the way
     down. There has been a crisis in Indigenous educational, child wel-
     fare, and criminal justice policy (among other sites) in this country.
     Why? ­Because the research that influences policy and shapes prac-
     tices impacting Indigenous communities most often emerges from
     knowledges not of Indigenous culture and context. When we begin
     to unpack the r­esearch-policy-practice phenomenon, we find meth-
     odological choices deeply implicated in the interpretation of research
     and the recommendations made. Indigenous methodologies are well
     positioned to unpack and unsettle the research-policy-practice cycle
     influencing Indigenous life. On a positive note, change is happening
     as Indigenous researchers make inroads in transforming policy and
     practice – although it’s an uphill climb.
           I persist with this book because the vitality of Indigenous methodol-
     ogies in academia signals an academy transforming. There is an increas-
     ing Indigenous presence in post-secondary sites. There is a growing
     number of graduate students and academics, including those not In-
     digenous, who wish to move beyond polarized silos. This wish is indic-
     ative of a desire to construct new, mutual, relational forms of dialogue,
     research, theory, and action – a sphere where contempt withers and a
     moral ethos can flourish. As long as the academy mirrors a homogene-
     ous reflection of bodies, minds, and methods, our move in this direc-
     tion is stalled. The influence of Indigenous knowledge ­systems, which
     are relational at their core, within research methodology discourses has
     the potential to transform a normative h      ­ omogeneity and the n    ­ ature
     of the academy itself. For allies of Indigenous m       ­ ethodologies, to be
     ­effective is to respect Indigenous methodologies on their own terms. To
      do this, one must care to know what on their own terms means. Above
      all, one must simply care. As Indigenous researchers, our responsi-
      bility is to assist others to learn our ways; however, the burden is not
      fully ours. ­Allies have a role in pushing back against an all-consuming
      ­Eurocentrism. In reaching towards healing and unity and turning away
       from polarization and divisiveness, society has the capacity to form
       ­relationships that are respectful, equitable, responsible, and healing.
           This book, then, moves with purpose. It is evoked by a relational
        ethos. It is emergent and exploratory, analytical and conceptual, practical
        and applied, and personal and political. Through presenting and hearing
        Indigenous voices, this work offers conceptual possibilities for a research
                                                              Introduction   13


methodology grounded in Indigenous philosophy. It is anchored in story.
And so, my purpose for writing this book is best found in story.

Circa 2009. I am presenting before a diverse group of people in a col-
lege setting. Many in attendance are students and faculty. I am sharing
the findings from my research into Indigenous methodologies. A sig-
nificant part of the presentation is devoted to the methodology of the
study, which is epistemologically grounded and informed by N    ­ êhiyaw
knowledge. Respecting protocol before starting the ­         presentation,
I ­acknowledge the territory and introduce myself. I share aspects of my
background with the audience – Indigenous community affiliation, per-
sonal background, professional experience – to offer enough identity
markers to situate me. I then introduce my research. After unpacking
terms like methodology and epistemology and why I choose to use these
terms when referring to Indigenous research, I spend considerable time
focusing on the Indigenous methodology used for my study. I outline
why I chose this emergent approach based on ancient knowledges and
why, I feel, it was the only approach that could respond to my specific
research question. I then speak about the characteristics of the method-
ology, share the research question of the study, and offer an overview of
the research conversations and what the participants in it said.
    Throughout the presentation, I observe the usual audience re-
sponses – some taking notes, others sitting forward with thoughtful
expressions, and still others possessing those unnerving blank stares.
After I finish, I thank the audience for attending and ask for questions.
Having presented extensively on my research, the comments I hear
are similar to ones I have heard in other venues. No audience member
readily contests the central finding of the study – that there is a dis-
tinctive Indigenous approach to research – rather, people are eager to
talk about how Indigenous methodologies can be applied. People in
the audience wonder how an Indigenous approach can work for an
urban Aboriginal population. Does the methodological approach of
my study work only for Nêhiyaw researchers? Can researchers not of
Nêhiyaw or Indigenous identity use this approach? These are critical
questions, but that is not why I share this story nor is it why this talk
has stayed with me through the years.
    As I am packing up and gathering my effects to accompany my
host for dinner and debriefing, a young Indigenous student cautiously
14   Indigenous Methodologies


     comes up to me. She says to me that she enjoyed the presentation, but
     she was wondering about something. She tells me that she is Indige-
     nous but grew up in the city and did not have any connections with a
     community. She says that she is drawn to using an Indigenous meth-
     odology, but she does not think that she can because she does not have
     the necessary cultural connections. We talk about her aspirations and
     hesitations, and as she is speaking my stomach is churning, for she is
     not seeking guidance on a relatively straightforward methodological
     question. Rather, her query is more complex. It gets to the heart of why
     Indigenous methodologies matter in the first place. I choose my words
     carefully, for standing before me is the future. This young Indigenous
     student is questioning whether she can embrace her ­Indigenous cul-
     ture. She is hesitant. It does not seem that her reasons are stemming
     from a lack of desire; they are more about belonging. I do not ask for
     specific reasons, but I suspect that some of them are ours collectively
     born of a colonial history that shadows our being. I listen, share some
     of my experience, the ins and outs, ups and downs. I say that our cul-
     ture, family, kin, kith, and community wait for us. I explain that we
     have the right to know who we are and that this right involves respon-
     sibilities. But there are people to help us out. We are not alone.
         I remember offering her the best guidance I could should she choose
     Indigenous methodologies – start where you are; it will take you
     where you need to go. I do not know what choice this student made
     and will likely never know. I wish I could have been less obscure, but
     then again it is not my place to direct another’s path so steeped in
     personal identity. I share this story to acknowledge this Indigenous
     student and how our talk has kept me going through many a hesitant
     day. Since this time, I have heard variations of this narrative. Not all
     but many from young Indigenous people who share a story that holds
     an undertone of deep desire to come back to one’s own culture. Many
     young people not of Indigenous identity are attracted to Indigenous
     approaches because, I believe, they are part of a generation seeking
     ways to understand the world without harming it.
         Leaving this memory, I come back to the present. As I write I feel the
     kókums and the mósums, the uncles and the aunties, sitting with me, and
     they ask, “Why are you writing this book?” For many reasons … but it
     is this young Indigenous student that comes to mind most vividly when
     I answer the question, “Why a book on Indigenous methodologies?”
                                                               Introduction   15


WHO WILL FIND THIS BOOK OF INTEREST?

This book provides a conceptual and methodological framework for im-
plementing Indigenous methodologies and serves as an entry point for
those wishing to learn about Indigenous research. It is an offering to com-
munity researchers, faculty, graduate students, reviewers of Indigenous
research grants, ethics board members, and university administrators
seeking ways to recruit more Indigenous students and support Indige-
nous faculty. This book is intended for the novice graduate student, the
seasoned scholar, and the community researcher. It will have relevance
for academic research and for those active in applied research outside
academia who have conducted (or seek to conduct) research within In-
digenous contexts. It is for the Indigenous community whose members
have a stake in ensuring that research is conducted in a respectful way.
   This book reaches out to Indigenous graduate students in a special
way. It is for those who have bounded into a research methodologies
class and felt the confusion of knowing that something does not fit, but
not knowing what that is. As increasing numbers of ­Indigenous students
are entering graduate studies and choosing ­Indigenous ­research, we
must respond to this group’s methodological needs. As an I­ndigenous
research professor for over a decade, I know the n   ­ ecessity of relevant
course materials to uphold Indigenous r­ esearch and the students of it.
I hope that this book will assist research i­nstructors to include Indige-
nous methodologies in their courses. My wish is that Indigenous gradu-
ate students, their professors, their communities, and all interested folk
can see this book as upholding ­Indigenous-informed research design. I
hope it will encourage dialogue and action among Indigenous research-
ers and allies in the field of Indigenous methodologies and that it will
contribute towards a continued and sustained conversation.



CHANGES TO THE SECOND EDITION

In contemplating the second edition of Indigenous Methodologies, I was
confronted with several questions: What do I leave in original form?
What do I revise? What do I add? This project was not unlike the
kitchen renovation my spouse and I undertook several years ago. It
started with a plan for a refresh, to change the kitchen cabinet knobs
16   Indigenous Methodologies


     and add a new backsplash. Next, we were knocking down walls, add-
     ing an island, refreshing the whole space. By the time we were done,
     each nook and cranny had a new look, but it was still our kitchen. It
     took time, energy, and some relational tension. We got through with
     our relationship surviving the renovation, and when the dust settled,
     it all turned out – if different from the starting plan. Such is the case for
     this second edition. It has expanded from an original “­refresh plan”
     and evolved into a significant overhaul of updating cited l­iterature,
     polishing sentences, clarifying concepts, reorganizing paragraphs,
     moving chapters around, and adding new chapters, but the consist-
     ent message remains: Indigenous methodologies are founded upon
     ­Indigenous knowledges and are guided by Indigenous people.
            There remains an ongoing need, and demand, for published
      ­Indigenous research. With that caveat, publications are flourishing,
       and this is encouraging. This new edition captures a selection of
       that new literature. Alongside newer literature, the reader will find
       “first wave” voices articulating Indigenous scholarship in academic
       ­contexts (Deloria, 1969; Hampton, 1995; Ortiz, 1990). The second edi-
        tion includes definitive texts and updated literature on decolonizing
        and Indigenous methodologies (Absolon, 2010; Chilisa, 2012; Debas-
        sige, 2010; Kovach, 2009; Restoule et al., 2018; L.T. Smith, 1999, 2012;
        ­Wilson, 2008; Windchief & San Pedro, 2019). This scholarship reveals
         how we might use research methodology to represent Indigenous
         knowledges, voice, and community.
            Both the first and second editions of this book include conversations
         with six researchers from my doctoral research.1 The six Indigenous
         researchers, in alphabetical order, are Kathy Absolon (Anishnaabe),
         Jeannine Carriere (Métis), Laara Fitznor (Nêhiyaw), Michael Hart
         (Nêhiyaw), Graham Hingangaroa Smith (Māori), and Cam Willett
         (Nêhiyaw). Their stories, and my own reflections, explore how Indig-
         enous researchers have woven Indigenous cultural knowledges into
         their research frameworks and the challenges of doing so. Arguably,


     1 All of the conversations in the second edition of Indigenous Methodologies (i.e., those
       with Absolon, Carriere, Fitznor, Hart, Smith, and Willett) were originally excerpted
       for my doctoral dissertation (Kovach, 2006) and then published in the first edition
       of this text (Kovach, 2009). Some interviews have been very lightly edited to follow
       current conventions of punctuation and spelling, for example, and to correct typo-
       graphical errors. Otherwise, these interviews are as they appeared in the first edition.
                                                                Introduction   17


the research voices from the six conversations, including my own
story, represent a previous generation of Indigenous research. (We are
now firmly into a new generation.) However, these conversations re-
main in this book because the aspirations, struggles, and resoluteness
that these researchers reflect upon are consistent with the current as-
pirations and struggles of Indigenous researchers now. These stories
remain because they offer valuable survival tips.
   In this new edition, the reader will find three new chapters. One
focuses on Indigenous community engagement, another on Indige-
nous theory, and a third highlights the oral dissemination of knowl-
edge and capacity building in Indigenous methodologies. The chapter
order has shifted to accommodate the new additions. Many of the
original chapters have been expanded upon and reorganized. Given
the extent to which this book is used in research classrooms, this new
edition includes discussion questions at the end of each chapter to
help readers engage with Indigenous methodologies. Below is a brief
summary of each chapter.



CHAPTER OVERVIEW

A prologue starts this work. A prologue is a personal introduction,
narrative, and method for illustrating a study arising from “self-
in-­relation” (Graveline, 2000, p. 361). The prologue is a dedicated space
for story, and while narrative animates this text, it is with intention that
Indigenous Methodologies begins with story. The book then opens the
circle with this introduction that outlines the book’s purpose, struc-
ture, and audience.
    Part 1 includes two chapters and outlines the backdrop for the
text. It focuses on the force and function of an Indigenous concep-
tual framework in Indigenous methodologies. Chapter 1 positions
Indigenous methodologies within the qualitative research landscape.
Its purpose is to place Indigenous methodologies as a legitimate ap-
proach among other valid forms of inquiry and to respond to common
questions asked about Indigenous methodologies. Chapter 2 defines
the terms Indigenous methodologies and Indigenous research, how they
intersect and diverge, and why it’s necessary to know the difference
between them. A focal thrust of Chapter 2 responds to the question
18   Indigenous Methodologies


     of why conceptual frameworks matter in Indigenous methodologies.
     The chapter provides an example of a Nêhiyaw conceptual framing.
     While the nuances, intricacies, and complexities of Indigenous knowl-
     edges exist beyond the capability of conceptual framing processes,
     the use of an Indigenous conceptual framework positions Indigenous
     thought as having a rightful place in academic knowledge production.
         Part 2 includes Chapters 3 to 6 and outlines four core foundations
     of an Indigenous conceptual framework within an Indigenous meth-
     odological study: epistemology, ethics, community, and self. Chapter 3
     introduces Indigenous epistemologies by highlighting Nêhiyaw knowl-
     edges. This chapter illustrates how Indigenous cultural epistemology
     is pivotal in Indigenous knowledge construction. Nêhiyaw scholar
     Michael Hart shares his thoughts on the interrelationship between In-
     digenous epistemology and methodology. Chapter 4 follows and out-
     lines the axiological and moral ethos of Indigenous methodologies.
     Kathy Absolon, an Anishinaabe scholar, reflects upon the big “E” (eth-
     ics) and the big “R” (reciprocity) in an Indigenous ethical framework.
     Chapter 5 is a new chapter and highlights community engagement
     in Indigenous research. This chapter responds to the question of who
     the Indigenous community is, considers the experience of engagement
     stress, and articulates the challenges of applying, as processes, the First
     ­Nation principles of ownership, control, access, and possession (OCAP)
      in university-driven research. Chapter 6 concludes Part 2 and reflects
      upon the experiential as a core foundation of the Indigenous conceptual
      framework. Cam Willet, a Nêhiyaw thinker, reflects on self and purpose.
         Part 3 highlights story, theory, interpretation, presentation of find-
      ings, and knowledge mobilization. It begins with Chapter 7, a homage
      to story as gift in Indigenous methodologies. This chapter empha-
      sizes the responsibilities and strategies associated with hearing story
      as a research method. In this chapter, Métis scholar Jeannine Carriere
      shares a research story from her experience. Chapter 8 is the second of
      three new chapters and highlights the role of Indigenous theory and
      theorizing in Indigenous methodologies. My conversation with Māori
      scholar Graham Hingangaroa Smith, as he reflects on Indigenous the-
      ory, concludes this chapter. Chapter 9 and Chapter 10 focus on mean-
      ing making. Chapter 9 considers analysing research findings using an
      Indigenous theoretical lens and highlights how a thematic analysis
      strategy might be used in Indigenous methodologies. Chapter 9 closes
                                                               Introduction   19


with a reminder from Nêhiyaw scholar Laara Fitznor about truth and
trust in Indigenous research. Chapter 10 concentrates on the presenta-
tion of findings in Indigenous methodologies and the use of metaphor.
­
Chapter 11, the third of three new chapters, concludes Part 3 of this
book. This chapter emphasizes the significance of the oral dissemina-
tion of knowledge within Indigenous methodologies and highlights the
community capacity-building objective of Indigenous research design.
   Part 4 offers concluding thoughts and is a continued call to decolo-
nize the academy, to see Indigenous peoples, to learn from Indigenous
knowledges. The conclusion asserts that Indigenous methodologies
can only survive and thrive with the support of Indigenous students
and scholars. Through the unified forces of hope and strategy, the call
is to uphold Indigenous research and methodologies and, in doing
so, broaden what counts as scholarship. An epilogue closes the circle
because, of all, this book starts and ends with story.



NOTES ABOUT TERMINOLOGY

Throughout this text, I use the word Indigenous to refer to Indigenous
peoples and culture. However, I periodically use the term Aboriginal
when referring to a specific Canadian context, such as in reference to
Aboriginal rights. I use the term Indian in conjunction with the Indian
Act (for example, when referring to the Canadian status Indian pop-
ulation that act delimits). Periodically, I use the term tribal peoples and
tribal knowledge as consistent with literature published by Indigenous
scholars. In the current political context, the term tribal has come to be
associated with isolationist, polarizing viewpoints. However, the term
tribe in Indigenous scholarship, particularly within a United States
context, references members of federally recognized Indian tribes,
such as those set out in federal law within the United States. The term
tribal is an extension of the term tribe used in that context.
   In referencing Indigenous philosophy, I rely upon the terms Indig-
enous epistemology and Indigenous knowledge, singular and plural, in
specific instances. The term Indigenous knowledges acknowledges both
the shared commonalities and the diversity of many Indigenous ways
of knowing. Arguably, Indigenous methodology, like Indigenous theory,
is a contested term. Referring to the term theory, Cree scholar Neal
20   Indigenous Methodologies


     McLeod (2007) writes that thinking beyond the colonial box is “a the-
     oretical activity,” but acknowledges there are those who hold the posi-
     tion that “theory is an inherently Western idea and cannot be rendered
     within Indigenous philosophies” (p. 98). This same argument applies
     to the term methodology. Working at the intersection of Indigenous and
     Western thought, I use the terms theory and methodology in service of
     Indigenous knowledges and Indigenous cultural sustainability. I have
     chosen to use the term Indigenous methodologies, in the plural, to de-
     scribe the theory and method of conducting research that flows from
     an Indigenous conceptual framework.
          The terms Western and Indigenous are used to distinguish one tra-
     dition from the other. It is acknowledged that Western thought is not
     homogenous and that Western culture encompasses a range of dif-
     fering philosophical positions within it. Western thought, however,
     does come from a Western intellectual tradition arising from ­Western
     culture. Differentiating Western and Indigenous traditions is not
     ­
     meant to create a “this or that” polarizing dichotomy. It is simply to
     recognize the identity of each – and to offset the assimilating tendency
     of Eurocentrism. Eurocentrism, to be clear, is not interchangeable with
     the term Western thought in this book. Rather Eurocentrism is the term
     for Western dominance. Eurocentrism is a danger zone for Indigenous
     peoples. Integrated throughout the chapters in this book are defini-
     tions of terms relevant to the chapter’s topic.
          This book is born of a research story. At times, the reader will feel
     a shift between the voice of my present and past self. The journey
     started in a landscape of ocean air and cedar trees and travelled to a
     place where strong winds push sagebrush across the arid plain. Like
     sweet grass of the Great Plains, the writing style has three braids:
     ­expository, analytical, and narrative. I have intentionally paused the
      expository writing at moments throughout this text to allow for the
      experiential and first-person voice to come through. Nêhiyaw scholar
      Winona Stevenson (2000) writes, “Seldom do you hear a Cree Elder
      profess ‘I know.’ What is heard is ‘I believe’ or ‘I believe it to be true’”
      (p. 19). Using the first person honours the experiential while engaging
      the abstract and theoretical. I honour all three forms in my writing be-
      cause of all the teachings of Nêhiyaw and Saulteaux culture that have
      guided me through this journey, the teaching that keeps coming back
      is this: “Keep it whole, girl.” This remains my way of trying to do that.
         PART ONE

Situating Indigenous
      Methodologies
This page intentionally left blank
CHAPTER ONE

 Indigenous Methodologies
 and Qualitative Inquiry




      Strangely, there has been very little attention paid to Indian method-
      ologies for gathering data and, consequently, the movement is pri-
      marily an ad hoc, personal preference way of gathering new ideas and
      attempting to weld them to existing bodies of knowledge.
                                                              Deloria (1999)



 As I write this chapter, I seek out a definition of qualitative research in
 The Sage Dictionary of Qualitative Inquiry (Schwandt, 2007) to compare
 it against my own. As I reach for this book, I see Keith Basso’s Wisdom
 Sits in Places (1996) nearby in the mishmash of books lining my shelf.
 I open The Sage Dictionary, flip to “Q” and spot “Qualitative Inquiry.”
 It reads: “Qualitative is a not-so-descriptive adjective attached to the
 varieties of social inquiry that have their intellectual roots in herme-
 neutics, phenomenological sociology, and the Verstehen tradition”
 (p. 247). Verstehen is a term from the German language meaning to
 understand using empathy.
     I think about the term Verstehen or to understand. My thoughts
 ­return to Keith Basso’s Wisdom Sits in Places. I think of Apache Elder
  Uncle Charlie whom I have met only through Basso’s work. I wonder
  what Elder Uncle Charlie would have to say about finding common
  understandings through differing knowledge-seeking systems and
  the value of an interpretative approach as a bridge for empathetic
24   Situating Indigenous Methodologies


     learning about each other. In traversing cultural knowledge para-
     digms, the first level of complexity arises with language. I consider
     Indigenous philosopher Anne Waters (2004), focusing especially on
     her analysis of the “dualist binary ontology” of the English language
     compared with the “nonbinary complementary dualist construct”
     that serves the thought and language of many Indigenous cultures
     (pp. 97–98). I am left contemplating how difficult it must have been
     for Indigenous people and the first visitors to understand one another
     given the distinctive language structure and culture of each.
        With colonization, Indigenous people were forced to forfeit their
     languages, and so a majority of Indigenous people in Canada now
     have English as their first and common language. Having a common
     language, however, has not served to increase cultural understand-
     ings. Rather, the full-on attempt at linguistic genocide has led to the
     absence of flourishing Indigenous languages and has compromised
     a full understanding of Indigenous beliefs and practices. A common
     colonial language is not the panacea for a mutual understanding be-
     tween Indigenous and settler peoples. Instead, understanding is a
     layered endeavour. Given the limitations of language alone and the
     complexities instilled within the word understand, at what point can
     we say that we do indeed understand something? Is it possible to
     have understandings across cultures? Yet, for a compassionate world
     to prevail, seek to understand we must.
        I reflect on qualitative research. Qualitative inquiry is an approach
     to research that tells a story through words not numbers. It is story in-
     terpreted from experience with an aim of offering further insight into
     human experience from human experience. I think of Joan Didion’s
     (1979) famous quotation, “We tell ourselves stories in order to live.”
     I think of Thomas King’s (2003) familiar words, “The truth about sto-
     ries is that that’s all we are.” Relationships, stories, and words mark
     qualitative inquiry. Many qualitative approaches, such as feminist
     methodologies and participatory action research, value the relational.
     This includes Indigenous methodologies. Indigenous methodolo-
     gies, which adopt a relationist and non-isolationist approach, as-
     sert an Indigenous epistemology (Archibald et al., 2019; Oliveria &
     Wright, 2015; L.T. Smith, 2012; Windchief & San Pedro, 2019). Indig-
     enous ­methodologies, based as they are on Indigenous epistemolo-
     gies, distinguish themselves from qualitative approaches embedded
                             Indigenous Methodologies and Qualitative Inquiry   25


in Western history and intellectual tradition. However, Indigenous
methodologies coexist with qualitative research.
   While there has been an increasing willingness within Western
academic institutions to acknowledge Indigenous methodologies,
­
there remains a hesitancy. Does this hesitancy exist because aca-
demic institutional culture is simply antithetical to Indigenous meth-
odologies, or does this signal a deeper lack of recognition and trust
of I­ndigenous peoples? Are we polarized or lost in translation? It is
useful to e­ xplore the commonalities between Indigenous methodolo-
gies and the interpretative tradition of qualitative research to help us
answer these questions. This chapter, then, considers the landscape
where qualitative research finds itself, contemplates how Indigenous
methodologies coexist within that landscape, and reflects upon the
convergence and divergence between the two.



A BACKDROP OF QUALITATIVE RESEARCH

Early influential grounded theorists Strauss and Corbin (1998) define
qualitative research as “any type of research that produces findings
not arrived at by statistical procedures or other means of quantifica-
tion” (p. 11). They argue that qualitative research, as a label, is confus-
ing because different people can interpret it differently. Nevertheless,
they formulate a working definition of qualitative research as a “non-
mathematical process of interpretation” for purposes of spotting pat-
terns within the data and from which a theory can emerge (Strauss &
Corbin, 1998, p. 11).
     Denzin and Lincoln (2003) highlight the socially constructed nature
of qualitative research by saying, “Qualitative researchers stress the
­socially constructed nature of reality, the intimate relationship between
 the researcher and what is studied, and the situational constraints
 that shape inquiry” (p. 13). In the fifth edition of The Sage Handbook of
 ­Qualitative Research, Denzin and Lincoln (2018) state that for a sustain-
  ing vibrancy in qualitative inquiry each generation must look to the
  past and the future: “Each generation must offer its response to current
  and past criticisms” (p. x). They go on to say that in this spirit, quali-
  tative inquiry must invite voices that have been silenced. Qualitative
  inquiry is about encompassing diverse voices and interrelationships.
26   Situating Indigenous Methodologies


        There is a range of qualitative methodologies, and each is distinc-
     tive. Participatory action research (PAR) is an approach integrating
     a power analysis through critical theory. PAR offers theory, method,
     and action for giving back to a community through research as praxis
     (McTaggart, 1997; Stringer, 2014). Narrative inquiry (Clandinin, 2013;
     Clandinin et al., 2018) and autoethnography (Diversi & Moriera, 2018;
     Spry, 2016) are Western methodologies highlighting meaning making
     from story and personal narrative.
        To build upon the interactive, storying nature of qualitative re-
     search methodologies, Rossman and Rallis (2003) accentuate the re-
     flexivity found in qualitative research. Palaganas et al. (2017) write
     about qualitative research as a reflective journey of learning. It is an
     approach, they argue, that demands that researchers be continually
     aware of their own biases as a means of consistently locating them-
     selves in the research. A self-reflective narrative research process that
     honours multiple truths is congruent with an ethos of nisitohtamowin
     (a Cree word for understanding) or “self-in-relation” (Graveline, 1998,
     p. 57) and is found in Indigenous scholarship (Styres, 2017).
        Because qualitative research is interpretive, the stories of both the
     researcher and the research participants are reflected in the ­research
     analysis. It is here that contemporary reflexive qualitative research
     diverges most clearly from traditional positivist approaches. One
     approach is a seeker of a singular, static objective truth. The other
     seeks contextualized experience and acknowledges that the truth
     heard depends upon the voice heard. However, be it reflexive qual-
     itative research or traditional positivist methodologies, both are his-
     torically embedded within and arise from Western thought and thus
     cannot help but formulate interpretations through a Western gaze
     or construct.
        Ladson-Billings (2012) points out that Western concepts of truth
     have assumed epistemological privilege. Perhaps early ­researchers did
     not see a culturally biased imposition or a Western gaze in o  ­ peration,
     for they certainly did not and/or could not admit to it u  ­ nder the pre-
     vailing paradigm of scientific research. I am not convinced that this
     is past. While critical theory and postmodern analysis have created
     space within Western science for representation, voice, and a mul-
     tiplicity of truths, Eurocentrism within research has yet to be fully
     ­unpacked in the academy or in the systems that support it.
                             Indigenous Methodologies and Qualitative Inquiry    27


A TROUBLING HISTORY

In reflecting on the history of qualitative research, Denzin and Lincoln
(2003) suggest that there are “seven moments of qualitative research”
(p. 19). I believe we have entered a new moment when inclusivity of
voices in research practice is possible. I am instinctively drawn to the
idea of a seventh moment – for I think of seven generations, seven
fires with all the hope those terms imply. However, pause I must to
reflect critically on moments one to six as they relate to Indigenous
research, starting with what Denzin and Lincoln (2003) refer to as the
“traditional period” (p. 19).
      In the traditional period of the twentieth century, qualitative research
was largely influenced by positivism. Most prominently, ethnographi-
cal research design was employed as qualitative “objective” studies of
the “other.” Ethnographies of the “other” in the Americas frequently
meant depictions of “exotic” Indigenous cultures (­Ladson-Billings,
2003). Positivist studies of Indigenous peoples reveal an epidemic
of researching Indigenous cultures from the outside by the outside
(L.T. Smith, 2012). These early qualitative studies were responsible for
­extractive research approaches that left those they studied disenfran-
 chised from the knowledge that was taken from them.
      Qualitative research, as ethnography, with the powerful imagery
 of words unavailable to quantitative approaches dependent upon
 ­numerical symbolism, allowed researchers into the storied world of
  the “other.” In early educational ethnographies concerned with the
  plight of the marginalized “other,” Native American children were
  a primary research sample (Yon, 2003). Educational ethnographies
  became a powerful tool to assist in the enculturation of Indigenous
  peoples through education. The ethnographers themselves cited
  the problematic nature of short-term “smash and grab” ethnogra-
  phies that gathered qualitative data from quick in-and-out interview
  ­sessions (Martin & Frost, 1996, p. 606).
      Gaining access to the world of the “other,” qualitative researchers
   of the traditional moment of qualitative research interpreted their ob-
   servations from their own cultural stance. When they interpreted a
   culture through their own cultural lens, an outside, skewed gaze was
   bound to occur. In the case of Indigenous communities, qualitative
   researchers were often implicated in deficit theorizing and colonial
28   Situating Indigenous Methodologies


     extraction through their research. The oft-quoted statement by Linda
     Tuhiwai Smith (2012) says it all: “the word itself, ‘research,’ is probably
     one of the dirtiest words in the Indigenous world’s vocabulary” (p. 1).
     Ojibway scholar Roxanne Struthers (2001) succinctly summarizes the
     history of settler research in Indigenous communities by saying it was
     not “managed in a germane manner” (p. 127). Within a Māori context,
     Bishop (1997) states that research benefits often went to the researcher,
     “not the people being researched” (p. 36).
          The marginalization of Indigenous thought and experience in qual-
     itative research causes philosophical, ideological, methodological, and
     emotional conflicts for many Indigenous researchers. Confronting this
     challenge is critical for an inclusionary spirit to prevail. In early influ-
     ential writing on Indigenous research, Eber Hampton (1995) describes
     a violence directed at graduate students holding alternative views
     on knowledge production. He explains the felt experience: “I like the
     ­analogy of Cinderella’s slipper because we are not Cinderellas; the slip-
      per doesn’t fit” (p. 8). We are not yet free from Cinderella’s slipper. For
      those wishing to use a methodology consistent with their own cultural
      epistemology, one that differs from the norm, it feels as though one is
      personally and/or structurally shut out, and the space is not inviting.
          There is an epistemological difference between Western and Indig-
      enous thought, and subsequently a need for both Western and Indig-
      enous methodologies. Indigenous methodologies offer a systematic
      but different approach to knowledge construction and are prompting
      Western institutions to expand the notion of what counts as knowl-
      edge. A response has been the articulation and assertion of Indige-
      nous methodologies in the past decade (Chilisa, 2012; Debassige,
      2010; Oliveria & Wright, 2015; Windchief & San Pedro, 2019). How-
      ever, Indigenous communities are still being researched, albeit with
      more political finesse.
          Researching on not with Indigenous people persists, and it is still
      deeply political. While we may be in a more inclusive moment of
      qualitative research, Indigenous communities remain subject to settler
      ­researchers who pursue research on Eurocentric terms. ­Ladson-Billings
       (2003) asserts that a significant site of struggle for Indigenous re-
       searchers will be at the level of epistemology and ideology, as these
       play out in methodology. I am reminded of Vine Deloria’s words from
       Custer Died for Your Sins: “Ideological leverage is always superior to
                           Indigenous Methodologies and Qualitative Inquiry   29


violence … The problems of Indians have always been ideological
rather than social, political or economic … [I]t is vitally important
that the Indian people pick the intellectual arena as the one in which
to wage war” (Deloria, 1969, pp. 251–252).
   Indigenous methodologies are a call to the non-Indigenous scholars
to adjourn disbelief and, in the pause, consider alternative methodo-
logical possibilities. While this is not a matter of one worldview over
another, how we make room to honour both and bridge the epistemic
differences is not going to be easy. This will be a challenge so long as
the colonial relationships surrounding the reproduction of knowledge
persist inside institutional centres. Excluding Indigenous methodolo-
gies sustains a colonial mind-set – fortunately, we can choose not to
sustain a colonial mind-set.



INTERPRETATIVE TRADITIONS: INDIGENOUS
METHODOLOGIES AND QUALITATIVE RESEARCH

Assuming Indigenous methodologies are simply a “type” of meth-
odology falling under a Western qualitative research paradigm can
assist Western researchers in situating Indigenous methodologies.
And a place to start is a place to start. Yet I believe that presuming
that Western research paradigms (qualitative or otherwise) can claim
Indigenous methodologies is to discount the epistemological founda-
tion of Indigenous methodologies. From their start, Indigenous and
Western cultures are distinct – and so too are their philosophies. One
emphasizes qualities such as process orientation, holism, and collec-
tivism and the other emphasizes output orientation, atomism, and
individualism. Certainly, each has borrowed from the other, but each
has retained an identity that differentiates it from the other. There is,
inarguably, a substantive difference between the two belief systems.
To appreciate Indigenous methodologies is to appreciate that they
arise from an Indigenous episteme.
   For contemporary scholars, acknowledging an Indigenous epis-
teme is not without its challenges. The English language limits a full
understanding of Indigenous knowledges. Oral tradition (as found
within an Indigenous episteme) raises eyebrows of how it might, if
ever, meet a standard of “truth” derived from a data-driven, text-based
30   Situating Indigenous Methodologies


     “objectivity.” Indigenous knowledges have a fluidity and motion that
     manifest in the distinctive structure of Indigenous languages meant to
     accentuate their animism (e.g., such as the use of ing in Nêhiyaw lan-
     guage). Oracy, as a form of knowledge sharing, relies upon the spoken
     word associated with a simultaneous witnessing other. Oral tradition
     is an expected knowledge dissemination approach within Indige-
     nous communities. The written expression of Indigenous knowledges
     does not supersede the oral tradition in Indigenous societies. Written
     knowledge dissemination relies upon scribed symbols where author
     and reader are largely not in same room.
        The two cultural paradigms approach knowledge creation, produc-
     tion, and dissemination in differing ways. Certainly, they intersect, but
     they are each distinctive knowledge paradigms. Because research is
     about knowledge creation and dissemination, how we communicate
     Indigenous knowledges in research is of import. For numerous rea-
     sons, those who attempt to fit Indigenous epistemologies into Western
     cultural conceptual rubrics expressed through the English language
     are destined to feel the squirm. Thus, we must consider the paradigm
     (and theory) factor in research methodologies.



     THE PARADIGM FACTOR IN RESEARCH
     METHODOLOGIES

     Research methodology includes the belief system and methods that
     guide the research, or more colloquially – “the thinking” and “the do-
     ing” of research. This “thinking” and “doing” constitute a research para-
     digm. In The Structure of Scientific Revolutions, Thomas Kuhn (1962/1996)
     defines paradigms as “some accepted examples of actual scientific
     practice – examples which include law, theory, application, and instru-
     mentation together – examples that provide models from which spring
     particular coherent traditions of scientific research” (p. 10). Whether we
     are considering quantitative or qualitative research, Kuhn’s description
     of paradigms encompasses both concept and method.
        There are distinctive qualitative research paradigms (Mertens,
     2005) that integrate specific conceptual framing and distinct method.
     As mentioned, many qualitative research paradigms align with the in-
     terpretative nature of Indigenous knowledges (Cajete, 1999; Deloria,
                               Indigenous Methodologies and Qualitative Inquiry   31


Figure 1.1. Situating Western and Indigenous Methodologies within
Interpretive Qualitative Research



    Western Methodologies                         Indigenous Methodologies
  (embedded within a Western                   (embedded within an Indigenous
    paradigm & conceptual                           paradigm & conceptual
           framing)                                        framing)




2002; Henderson, 2000; Little Bear, 2000). Indigenous researchers find
resonance with Heidegger’s constructivist phenomenology or associ-
ate with narrative inquiry approaches. From this perspective, Indig-
enous epistemologies align with a self-in-relationship approach that
marks a constructivist paradigm. Post-structural methodologies align
with a transformative qualitative research paradigm that critically
reflects on power dynamics and is a natural methodology through
which to conduct Indigenous research. One could argue that all Indig-
enous inquiries can, in some way, use a Western methodology with a
Western paradigm. This, however, is a problematic assumption that is
assimilative in its origin.
      The relationship between Indigenous methodologies and qualita-
tive research is best understood if one begins with clarity on the par-
adigm from which each has emerged (see Figure 1.1). I have ­revised
Figure 1.1 from the first edition of this book to show visually that
Western research arises from Western thought (noting that Western
thought is not a monolith). Indigenous methodologies arise from
­Indigenous thought. My stance is that all qualitative methodologies,
 even those with an empirical approach, are interpretative to some
 degree. I also conclude that Indigenous methodologies are interpre-
 tative. The interpretive nature of qualitative methodologies and In-
 digenous methodologies offers a bridge for understanding.
      In addressing Indigenous researchers, Indigenous scholar Shawn
 Wilson (2001) tells us that it is time to release our dependency on
 ­Western research traditions: “These are dominant western system
  ­research paradigms. Now as Indigenous researchers we need to move
   beyond these, beyond merely assuming an Indigenous perspective on
32   Situating Indigenous Methodologies


     these non-Indigenous paradigms” (p. 176). Shawn Wilson cautions us
     that knowledge is neither acultural nor apolitical. Indigenous research
     is embedded within a colonial history in which Indigenous peoples
     and culture have suffered. Thus, Indigenous methodologies form
     both a methodological and political project.



     A FOCUS ON INTERPRETIVE, RELATIONAL
     METHODOLOGIES

     In reflecting on qualitative research, Rossman and Rallis (2003) sub-
     mit that “qualitative research is quintessentially interactive” (p. 35).
     It is here, in this interpretative, relational meaning making, that
     ­qualitative and Indigenous research methodologies can find a bridge
      or crosswalk. What is meant by a qualitative relational m   ­ ethodology,
      in ­ general; and what is meant by Indigenous methodologies as
      ­constituting a relational approach, in particular? I would like to high-
       light aspects of a relational approach as considered through the lens
       of reflexivity, that many qualitative (e.g., feminist methodologies and
       appreciative inquiry) and Indigenous approaches share.
          In considering the interpretive, reflexivity dilemma, I begin to
       ponder my own immediate process of understanding, or Verstehen,
       in ­relation to my other. I think of the students who come into my
       research class. The words conceptual frameworks, epistemology, and
       ­
       theory send us off into different directions, creating a dialectical force
       field. ­Tensions arise from the need to attach meaning to lofty and ef-
       fervescent words like truth and knowledge. It seems that the interpreta-
       tive nature of understanding fastens itself to the most intimate aspects
       of our experience, connecting us enough to find both foe and brethren.
       The space between these two places is deeply political, where rep-
       resentation, method, and meaning vie to be heard, to be understood.

     The Reflexivity Dimension

     Reflexivity is the term often utilized within a variety of qualitative
     research approaches to reference the relational. Reflexivity is the
     ­researcher’s own self-reflection in the meaning-making process. For
      example, feminist inquiry is a methodological approach that is highly
                             Indigenous Methodologies and Qualitative Inquiry   33


reflexive. It provides a research methodology that allows feminist
­researchers to share the experience of conducting research and their
 own subjective experience with their research participants throughout
 the process from a women’s perspective. Integral is a gender a­ nalysis
 that contextualizes feminist research (Liamputtong, 2007, p. 11).
      Autoethnography (Diversi & Moriera, 2018; Spry, 2016) is an ap-
 proach with its foundations in ethnographical research and brings
 together the study of self (auto) in relation to culture (ethnography).
 Within this approach, self-reflection moves beyond field notes to hav-
 ing a more integral positioning within both the research process and
 the construction of knowledge itself. As Gergen and Gergen (2003)
 state, “rather than giving the reader pause to consider the biases, here
 the juxtaposition of self and subject matter is used to enrich the eth-
 nographical report” (p. 579). When postmodern theory is applied in
 qualitative research, reflexivity is a central component of the research
 process. It requires an awareness of the self, and multiple truths, in
 creating knowledge.
      In anti-oppressive research approaches (Brown & Strega, 2005;
 Strega & Brown, 2015), self-reflection is described as “critical reflex-
 ivity,” which purposefully gives space for the political examination
 of location and privilege (Herising, 2005, p. 136). In line with these
 research approaches, decolonizing methodologies demand a critical
 reflexive lens that ­acknowledges the politics of representation within
 ­Indigenous research. It is rare that qualitative research conducted in
  the past decade does not make mention of the self-reflective compo-
  nent in its methodology, whether referring to it as reflexivity, critical
  reflexivity, self-reflection, or field notes (Gitlin et al., 2002).
      Depending upon the specific methodology, the rationale for giv-
  ing reflexivity its due can vary. A methodology that flows from a
  theoretical perspective that highly values “self-in-relation,” such as
  autoethnography, will incorporate reflexivity as a necessary method
  to actualize its approach. For feminist and postmodern r­esearchers,
  critical reflexivity in anti-oppressive research (Potts & Brown, 2015)
  brings forward the political and representational dimension of
  ­research within the construction of knowledge that is inherent, specif-
   ically, to the theoretical standpoints of the feminist and postmodern
   researchers. In this sense, reflexivity is a method for theoretical trans-
