   parency and thus associated with trustworthiness of research.
34   Situating Indigenous Methodologies


        Alongside the theoretical transparency that reflexivity can help en-
     gender, reflexivity is associated with trustworthiness of the findings in
     qualitative research. Creswell (2003) situates reflexivity as an indicator of
     trustworthiness through the process of triangulation. He indicates that
     there are several strategies to demonstrate validity or trustworthiness in
     qualitative research, one of which is to clarify bias and make the research-
     er’s bias transparent. Evidence of self-reflexivity is an acknowledgment
     by the researcher of their subjectivity and its influence on the research
     findings. In the subjective world, we find a breadth of reasoning for how
     we come to know that ranges from deductive to inductive to abductive.
     I would like to focus on abductive reasoning as it is lesser known.

     The Relationship of Abductive Reasoning and Reflexivity

     Abductive reasoning is uniquely connected to personal experience,
     subjectivity, and reflexivity. Abductive reasoning was first put for-
     ward by Charles Sanders Peirce (1903/1998) in the late 1800s. During
     his life, Peirce, an American philosopher, was most associated with a
     philosophical approach later known as pragmatism (Råholm, 2010).
     But what is abductive reasoning? “[P]ut simply it can be envisaged as
     the creative, imaginative or insightful moment in which understand-
     ing is grasped – or is thought to be grasped” (Lipscomb, 2012, p. 244).
     Abductive knowledge comes to us in the a-ha moment – an insight
     that can be described as a flash of knowing. It is the creative insight.
          The insight or conjecture from abductive reasoning arises from
     ­experience (i.e., what one has known or seen to be the case before),
      and there are a number of possible explanations for the phenom-
      enon. ­  Abductive reasoning captures our attention (Råholm, 2010;
      ­Thorkildsen et al., 2013). In this generative process, there is a possi-
       bility of establishing a hypothesis. Abduction bridges observation, in-
       stinct, and experience. Because of its experiential basis, it allows for
       a holistic way of knowing. As Råholm (2010) states, “The abductive
       form of reasoning in nursing research and especially within theory de-
       velopment is an approach to theoretical inquiry that uses analogy as a
       method for devising theory” (p. 261). Along with analogy, metaphoric
       framing is a powerful method for bringing forth abductive reasoning.
          Of the three – deductive, inductive, and abductive – forms of reason-
       ing found within Western scientific discourses, abductive reasoning
                            Indigenous Methodologies and Qualitative Inquiry   35


is the least referenced in qualitative research. This is largely because
­abductive reasoning is considered most fallible (Peirce, 1903/1998).
 Critics point to its intangible quality as problematic in making sci-
 entific claims (Lipscomb, 2012). That being said, abductive reasoning
 allows for an inclusive, holistic consideration of knowledge. Respect-
 ing the forceful trio of deductive, inductive, and abductive knowing
 strengthens our ability to gain insight. Reflexivity is a tool for examin-
 ing how we come to know using all three.

Indigenous Methodologies and the Relational, Reflexive, and
Abductive

Indigenous scholar Vine Deloria (1999) explicates an Indigenous rela-
tional worldview. An Indigenous worldview is based upon an animis-
tic philosophy that views the human entity as but one entity within
the relational family. Deloria (1999) argues that a relational worldview,
from an Indigenous perspective, is one that assumes relationships be-
tween all life forms that exist within the natural world. Relationship
has a broad inclusive meaning within Indigenous understandings.
Indigenous scholar Gregory Cajete (2004) affirms the relational per-
spective of Indigenous knowledges: they are, he says, about “honor-
ing the primacy of direct experience, interconnectedness, relationship,
holism, quality, and value” (p. 66).
   We gather knowledge by observing the relationships within the
natural world (Deloria, 1999). Here the words relationship and observ-
ing are both equally significant. In making meaning, the relational
quality of an Indigenous worldview suggests a highly interpretative
approach. This is recognized among Indigenous peoples. In referring
to her doctoral work, Manu Aluli Meyer (2004) states that the episte-
mology that governs her dissertation “is an interpretative analysis of
my own epistemology, shaped by dream, taped narratives and a life-
time of interactions” (p. 86). Indigenous forms of inquiry find an ally
in the qualitative approaches that assume the relationally constructed
aspect of knowledge production.
   Relational research is concerned with doing research in a good
way. As Wilson (2001) affirms, as a researcher “you are answering to
all your relations when you are doing research” (p. 177). Indigenous
scholar Marie Battiste (2007) suggests that one of the most critical
36   Situating Indigenous Methodologies


     aspects of Indigenous research is the ethical responsibility to ensure
     that Indigenous knowledges and people are not exploited. I am re-
     minded that research is about collective responsibility: “we can only
     go so far before we see a face – our Elder cleaning fish, our sister living
     on the edge in East Vancouver … and hear a voice whispering, ‘Are
     you helping us?’” (Kovach, 2005, p. 31).
         An indicator of a relational approach in Indigenous methodologies
     can be found within process and product, and the reader must be able
     to identify both in the methodology. Indicators that this holistic episte-
     mology is present include explicit reference to personal preparations
     (Montgomery, 2012) involving motivations, purpose, inward knowing,
     observation, and the variety of ways that the researcher can relate her
     own process undertaken in the research. Another way to assess process
     is to look for the inclusion of story and narrative by both researcher and
     research participant. In an Indigenous context, story is methodologi-
     cally congruent with Indigenous knowledges. A product resulting from
     research using an Indigenous methodology ought to have a strong nar-
     rative component as part of its method and presentation of findings.
         Researchers wishing to use Indigenous methodologies may use
     them alongside methods developed from a Western tradition (e.g.,
     thematic analysis, photovoice). In a project that I conducted with a
     ­research team, entitled Indigenous Presence: Experiencing and ­Envisioning
      Indigenous Knowledges within Selected Post-Secondary Sites of Education
      and Social Work (Kovach et al., 2015), we utilized this approach. In
      such cases, the data can be coded, emergent themes analysed, and
      so forth, while transparently indicating that Indigenous theorizing is
      being ­applied. It is applying to interpretations an Indigenous theory
      grounded in Indigenous epistemology and connected to Indigenous
      community that makes the difference in Indigenous methodologies.



     COMMON QUESTIONS FROM QUALITATIVE
     RESEARCHERS ABOUT INDIGENOUS METHODOLOGIES

     As the number of qualitative researchers engaging with Indigenous
     methodologies increases, so have questions about Indigenous meth-
     odologies. To conclude this chapter, I pause to offer responses to sev-
     eral frequently asked questions.
                            Indigenous Methodologies and Qualitative Inquiry   37


Are Indigenous Methodologies Really Different from Western
Qualitative Approaches?

Yes. Indigenous methodologies are based on Indigenous knowledges
and referenced in research vernacular as Indigenous epistemology
and theory. In claiming the distinctiveness of any emergent method-
ology, the question arises: Is it really different? This is not surprising.
People tend to like to make sense of difference through a familiar,
comfortable lens. In fact, considering a different perspective can be
so unsettling as to stymie that which it seeks to create – new knowl-
edge. However, Indigenous methodologies are different from Western
methodologies because they are based on Indigenous knowledges
and Indigenous knowledges are different from Western knowledges.
Indigenous methodologies require an Indigenous interpretation
throughout. Adding an Indigenous “look” to research that is predom-
inately Western in its theorizing and methods creates methodological
floundering and can lead to tokenism. If we do not clearly recognize
Indigenous methodology for what it is – a distinctive methodology –
the political and practical quagmire will persist.

Is It Possible to Conduct Indigenous Methodologies without
an Indigenous Epistemology?

No. The result might be Indigenous research, but it is not arrived at
through Indigenous methodologies (see Chapter 2 for a definition of
each). The following is increasingly common in research labelled “In-
digenous”: the non-Indigenous principal investigator includes an In-
digenous co-investigator, collaborator, or community partner on the
research team. The research design includes methods such as research
circles and possibly cultural protocols (e.g., offerings, ceremony) and/
or Elder involvement. However, the research strategy is embedded in
a Western methodology (for example, a community-based approach or
phenomenology) and integrates a corresponding Western theory that
has credibility within the mainstream research community (for exam-
ple, critical theory or postmodernism). The research is institutionally
funded, typically with funds going to a non-Indigenous r­esearcher,
and has the intention of mentoring Indigenous researchers, so they can
gain the social and methodological capital to reproduce this strategy
38   Situating Indigenous Methodologies


     independently. And the beat goes on. This scenario is not all disheart-
     ening, and it is better than the extractive ethnographic research of times
     past. Nonetheless, the point remains that if the methodology does not
     flow from Indigenous epistemology (and theory), it is not Indigenous.

     Do Indigenous Methodologies Constitute a Pan-Indigenous
     Approach?

     No. In Canada, most individuals with a rudimentary knowledge of
     Indigenous cultures know that the coastal Tsimshian peoples have
     different practices than the land-based Nêhiyaw. The unique aspects
     of our Indigenous cultures are held in esteem because they emerge
     from our ancestral interrelationships found in place. Our commu-
     nity affiliations must be acknowledged – it is about identity and re-
     spect. However, as Indigenous people, we understand each other
     because we share a worldview that holds common, enduring beliefs.
     Indigenous scholar Leroy Little Bear (2000) states, “there is enough
     similarity among North American Indian philosophies to apply con-
     cepts generally” (p. 79). Thus, when considering Indigenous episte-
     mologies, Indigenous people contextualize to their community and
     cultural affiliation. We do this because our knowledges are bound to
     place. Therefore, if I indicate that, as a researcher, I will be follow-
     ing Nêhiyaw traditions (because that is my cultural affiliation), other
     ­Indigenous people will relate to the underlying Indigenous epistemo-
      logical positioning, though their specific customs and protocols may
      vary. Acknowledging Indigenous knowledges as world philosophy is
      not ascribing to a pan-Indigenous perspective.

     Who Can “Do” Indigenous Methodologies?

     It depends. From the non-Indigenous researcher or the Indigenous re-
     searcher with limited connection to community, I often hear this ques-
     tion: “Can I use an Indigenous methodology in my research?” My first
     response is that not all research involving Indigenous people requires In-
     digenous methodologies. It depends upon the research question. This is
     a methodology question. That being said, more often than not the ques-
     tion of who can “do” Indigenous methodologies arises from a questioner
     who is asking about identity, not methodology. From my perspective,
                            Indigenous Methodologies and Qualitative Inquiry   39


who can “do” Indigenous methodologies is less about identity than it is
about relationship and responsibility. I recognize that Indigenous iden-
tity will always be a factor. However, there are two questions that the
researcher must ask: Do I have a relationship with the Indigenous com-
munity with whom I seek to conduct research, the community I seek to
represent through my researcher’s voice? Am I trusted by that commu-
nity? These questions can apply to any type of Indigenous community
(e.g., territorial, organizational, or community of i­nterest). Researchers
need to “do the work” of honouring place and b       ­ eing in relationship
with the research community regardless of whether it is urban or rural.
At the end of the day, it’s all about relationships.
    The relational possibilities of interpretive research are a strong
bridge for appreciating Indigenous methodologies. Bridging differ-
ence requires the supportive involvement of academics who are not In-
digenous. No doubt this involvement will require Indigenous peoples
to educate their non-Indigenous counterparts. It is correct to assume
that Indigenous academics will be put in a position of educating their
non-Indigenous contemporaries about Indigenous methodologies. If
we do not take on this responsibility, Indigenous p    ­ ost-secondary stu-
dents wishing to employ Indigenous methodologies will continue to
be misunderstood and discouraged. However, we ought to have the
space and resources to support this educative process – and everyone,
and I mean all of us, has a role and responsibility here.
    I acknowledge the progressive work by many qualitative researchers
that has created space for Indigenous methodologies to flourish. Qual-
itative research can be a unifying force if, as researchers, we are knowl-
edgeable, respectful, empathic, and mindful of one another’s beliefs
and proceed through non-harming words and actions. If we seek to
animate Verstehen in our research through an empathetic understand-
ing and a respectful and responsive spirit, I think Apache Elder Uncle
Charlie from Keith Basso’s (1996) Wisdom Sits in Places would approve.

  to research.

     2 Explain, in accessible language, the role of Indigenous knowledges
       within Indigenous methodologies. This chapter asserts the role of
       knowledge (thinking) and method (doing) as integral in defining
       ­research methodology. Do you agree? Why? Why not?
     3 From your perspective, experience, and knowledge, in what ways do
        ­relationality and reflexivity arise in both Indigenous m
                                                                ­ ethodologies and
         qualitative research? On a flip chart, identify how our social, ­economic,
         and political world might be different if the underlying philosophy
         arose from an animate, non-humanistic, collectivist perspective.
     4 Can anyone “do” Indigenous methodologies, according to the text?
         ­Explain. What is your response? Why?
CHAPTER T WO

 Indigenous Conceptual Framing
 in Indigenous Methodologies




      Minnie was diligently working on a rug. I sat near Minnie and ob-
      served her hands patiently and meticulously pull charcoal colored
      wool through the warp. She shared with me stories about weaving,
      love, and relationships. In listening to her, I began to formulate clearer
      insights that connected weaving a rug to weaving a dissertation.
                                                                Tachine (2018)



 In this chapter (and book), I do not use the terms Indigenous research
 and Indigenous methodologies interchangeably. Research is about dis-
 covery, and researchers use differing methodologies to make those
 discoveries. If we think of research as discovering new knowledge,
 Indigenous research, then, is about discovering new understandings
 as these relate to Indigenous peoples.
     Indigenous research is an inclusive term, and it casts a wide net.
 Indigenous research comprises many different types of research
 methodologies, including quantitative (e.g., Walter & ­    Anderson’s
 (2013) work in Indigenous quantitative research). Qualitative meth-
 odologies applied in Indigenous research may include, but are not
 limited to, grounded theory, ethnography, and action research.
 ­Community-based research, a Western research tradition, is a method-
  ology found in many Indigenous research projects (Leeuw et al., 2012;
  Victor et al., 2016). Projects integrating Indigenous methodologies
42   Situating Indigenous Methodologies


     (­Debassige, 2010; Fidler, 2014; Kovach, 2009) are Indigenous research.
     There is a range of methodologies found under the umbrella of Indig-
     enous research. Determining the differing epistemic and theoretical
     roots of Indigenous research projects is methodologically important
     and will determine whether the approach is aligned with Indigenous
     methodologies or Indigenous research.
        Consistent with the above definition, the Social Sciences and Hu-
     manities Research Council of Canada (SSHRC) defines Indigenous
     research as research that may encompass different research method-
     ologies (SSHRC, 2020): “Whatever the methodologies or perspectives
     that apply in a given context, researchers who conduct Indigenous
     research, whether they are Indigenous or non-Indigenous themselves,
     commit to respectful relationships with all Indigenous peoples and
     communities” (para. 29). This definition links Indigenous research
     with respectful relations and ethical research. This is a useful defi-
     nition with a hopeful aspiration. However, a caution. We must not
     assume that because a study is labelled “Indigenous research,” it
     automatically means it is respectful research or that it appreciates
     respectful research from an Indigenous ethical stance. Given the colo-
     nial nostalgia that ­lingers in our contemporary midst, it is imperative
     that researchers guard against exploitive Indigenous research – assert-
     ing a label is not enough. I­ ndigenous ­research is not yet cleansed of its
     extractive roots.
        Indigenous methodologies, the focus of this book, are found under the
     umbrella of Indigenous research. For a methodology to be correctly
     identified as an Indigenous methodology, it must be anchored in In-
     digenous epistemology, theory, ethics, story, and community. This is
     not always the case in Indigenous research. Indigenous methodologies
     and Western research methodologies can both fall under the umbrella
     of Indigenous research but are methodologically distinct from each
     other. Metaphorically, one can think of a herring catch with the net
     being Indigenous research. If one visualizes a salmon in the net amid
     a whole lot of herring, Indigenous methodologies are represented by
     the salmon and Western methodologies by the many herring. Both are
     in a big, wide net of Indigenous research, but Indigenous methodolo-
     gies, epistemologically speaking, are a different kind of fish.
        As defined in the previous chapter, methodology includes both theory
     and methods. I use the term methodology broadly to describe both the
                 Indigenous Conceptual Framing in Indigenous Methodologies   43


thinking and doing aspect of conducting research. The thinking (phi-
losophy) and doing (method) in research methodology are guided by
the research purpose, question, and aim. Within qualitative research,
the methods may be similar across different methodologies; however,
philosophical and theoretical perspectives will often differ. For exam-
ple, both participatory action research and grounded theory may use
interviews as part of their data collection design (i.e., method); how-
ever, they may identify different theories (e.g., social constructivism or
critical race theory) as part of their approach. The theory choice will be
influential in all aspects of research but specifically so in analysis and
interpretation. Therefore, it is consequential to pay attention to theory
within a given methodology, including Indigenous methodologies.



WHY INDIGENOUS CONCEPTUAL FRAMING MATTERS
IN RESEARCH

Granted, it is difficult to navigate research language at best, and Indig-
enous methodologies are no different. This test of language exists amid
the overarching challenge of conducting Indigenous methodologies
within Western academic institutions not yet decolonized and with-
out near enough Indigenous professors to guide students. Relationally,
­Indigenous methodologies can be complicated, and operationally, it can
 get messy. Whenever people are involved in research, there is the po-
 tential for things to go sideways. Intellectually, Indigenous methodolo-
 gies require cerebral fortitude. They are founded upon a complex and
 nuanced philosophical paradigm and require rigour, skill, knowledge,
 and care in explicating the Indigenous conceptual basis of the specific
 methodology chosen (Foley, 2018). This work begins with Indigenous
 conceptual framing to focus, make visible, and uphold ­Indigenous
 knowledge systems. It is helpful to pause here to consider the function
 of conceptual frameworks in qualitative research in general.

Conceptual Frameworks within Research

Anfara and Mertz (2015) reflect on a variety of ways qualitative re-
searchers have defined conceptual framing in qualitative research.
They reference Miles and Huberman’s definition of a conceptual
44   Situating Indigenous Methodologies


     framework as being basic or sophisticated. The framework can be the-
     ory driven or casual, but it delineates the main things to be ­studied
     and the relationship between those things. The researcher’s c­ hosen
     theory and the researcher’s lived experience influence how the study
     is conceptualized. A clearly articulated conceptual framework in
     ­research makes visible our assumptions about the world and how we
      will proceed in conducting and interpreting our research from that
      perspective. Thus, self-referent knowledge will coexist alongside
      framework theories from existing research (e.g., Indigenous, feminist,
      critical, postmodern, and so forth).
          A conceptual framework will make, or ought to make, transpar-
      ent the beliefs (theories) that are guiding method choices and the
      interpretation and dissemination of the research. Potts and Brown
      (2015) make explicit the connection between conceptual frameworks
      and ­interpreting findings in anti-oppressive research: “We carry our
      framework, which is not inherently good or bad, around with us and
      it is through this framework that we view the data. Making visible the
      [conceptual] luggage is an individual and collective process” (p. 31).
      They state that an anti-oppressive interpretation and presentation of
      research findings inherently involves revealing the power dynamic in
      order to minimize power or, conversely, to conceal power dynamics
      so as to maintain existing power relations.
          In conducting research, the explicitness of our choices and the beliefs
      that influence them send a purposeful message about who we are as
      researchers. Potts and Brown (2015) argue that we bring this intention-
      ality to our research from day one. It does not magically appear when
      we present our findings but rather emerges throughout the research via
      our self-motivations, critical reflection, engagement with others, and
      overall research choices. Explicit conceptual frameworks allow us the
      opportunity to be honest about our perspective as researchers and to
      illustrate how this perspective impacts the methods chosen. How we
      speak about research – the form we give it through the metaphors we
      use to explain it – gives insight into our conceptual framing (on these
      metaphors see Goodyear-Ka‘ōpua, 2015; Michell, 2012; Tachine, 2018).
      A research conceptual framework is a road map that identifies the
      destination one is heading towards and communicates the direction,
      turns, and stops made along the way. Conceptual frameworks hold the
      ­theoretical choices of the researcher whether they are transparent or not.
                 Indigenous Conceptual Framing in Indigenous Methodologies   45


   In summary, a conceptual framework is a plan that guides the
researcher in developing a research question; contemplating episte-
mology, theory, and ethics; engaging with community; self-situating;
considering existing knowledge; hearing story; choosing methods
and analytical strategies; and presenting the research and arranging
for reciprocity in disseminating findings. A conceptual framework is
fluid with a cyclical sequencing – it is not a linear endeavour.



INDIGENOUS CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORKS

Jiménez Estrada (2005), an Indigenous scholar of Mayan ancestry,
­offers an example of a conceptual framework communicated through
 the metaphor of the Ceiba, or Tree of Life. This is a representation
 that honours Mayan cosmology and gives visual form to the thought
 behind the research design. In my doctoral research, when I was con-
 sidering several metaphoric possibilities to conceptually frame my
 overarching research design, I initially considered the symbolism of
 alder posts in a Nêhiyaw ceremonial teepee. The posts (or poles) offer
 a structural foundation for the hides or branches that enfold them. The
 ceremonial teepee gives shelter and holds ancient knowledges i­ nside.
 There are many examples for conceptual frameworks expressed
 through metaphor found in Indigenous methodological research.
    However useful conceptual frameworks are, we must proceed with
 caution. Applying the language of conceptual frameworks to Indige-
 nous inquiries requires “unpacking” both the term and its intention.
 Edward Buendía (2003) contends that conceptual systems tradition-
 ally utilized in Western higher education are culturally and racially
 loaded, privileging European thought. He argues (2003) that concep-
 tual frameworks, as a classification, put at the centre “acceptable”
 belief systems that make sense to dominant cultures. Buendía states
 that the result is “the imposition of a logic that has created particular
 types of coherencies in categorizing and explaining the real” (p. 51).
 When applied to Indigenous research, Western research conceptual
 frameworks heighten the potential for a Eurocentrism that reproduces
 deficit theorizing of Indigenous experience.
    In contemporary universities, Indigenous researchers are period-
 ically asked to make, as Māori scholar Graham Hingangaroa Smith
46   Situating Indigenous Methodologies


     (2006) tells us, “strategic concessions” (p. 161). For example, a­ pplying
     conceptual framework language to Nêhiyaw ways of knowing is
     a strategic concession. The term does not capture the relational,
     ­holistic flavour of this worldview, and the term conceptual privileges
         thought as the sole pathway to knowledge, placing intuition, spirit,
      and ­experience as secondary. Notwithstanding these limitations, the
      structure and form of Western research framework language can
      ­offer a portal for the inclusion of visual, symbolic, and m
                                                                 ­ etaphorical
       ­representations of a research design that mitigates the linearity of
        written text alone. Edward Buendía (2003) says that “researchers, be
        they White or researchers of color, can frame their research narra-
        tives to operationalize the epistemological standpoints of particular
        ­communities … to ‘see’ and write [knowledge] differently” (p. 50).



     THE FOUNDATIONS OF AN INDIGENOUS CONCEPTUAL
     FRAMEWORK

     When we consider Indigenous scholarship and community knowl-
     edge, there are at least four core foundations in an Indigenous con-
     ceptual framework: Indigenous epistemology, Indigenous ethics,
     Indigenous community (including land and place), and the self (see
     Figure 2.1). These four core foundations, as embedded in Indigenous
     thought, experience, and context, define an Indigenous conceptual
     framework and make Indigenous methodologies distinctive from
     other qualitative approaches.
        Figure 2.2 provides an illustration of an Indigenous conceptual
     framework that encompasses different aspects of a research design.
     It outlines a conceptual framework with interdependent parts. An
     Indigenous theoretical perspective is interwoven into all aspects
     of all research actions (questions, literature, analysis, and so forth).
     However, it is critical to be aware that this conceptual framework is
     a bundle holding all its parts, including the four core foundations of
     Indigenous epistemology (knowledges), ethics (axiology), commu-
     nity, and self (self-referent, experiential knowledge). It is a concep-
     tual framing approach that requires an interdependent balance. For
     example, if self-referent, experiential knowledge were divorced from
     Indigenous knowledges (i.e., epistemology and theory) and cultural
                       Indigenous Conceptual Framing in Indigenous Methodologies   47


Figure 2.1. The Four Foundations of an Indigenous Conceptual Framework



       Indigenous
                             Indigenous                           The
      Epistemology                            Indigenous
                                Ethics                        Experiencing
      (beliefs about                          Community
                             (axiological                        Self in
        knowledge                           (land & place)
                              premise)                        Relationship
         creation)




                              Foundations of an Indigenous
                                 Conceptual Framework




embeddedness, this would no longer be considered an Indigenous
conceptual framework as defined in this chapter. An Indigenous
conceptual framework is not one but all of its parts. The whole of it.
­Figure 2.2 is one way to present an Indigenous conceptual framework;
 there are others using culturally grounded metaphors (Debassige,
 2013; Tachine, 2018).
    Within Indigenous methodologies, metaphor and symbolism are
 used to express Indigenous conceptual frameworks. Conceptual ideas
 live in the realm of the intangible until expressed in tangible form.
 Metaphors are an effective technique for giving ideas form. Once
 conceptual frameworks are communicated through metaphors, and
 move from a concept to a precept, ideas are revealed in a concrete way,
 and the conceptual framework is imbued with energy and cultural
 resonance. Metaphors often arise from place and facilitate holistic, ab-
 ductive interpretations. Within Indigenous societies, metaphors and
 symbolism are common communication techniques. As Indigenous
 peoples, we use metaphors in our speech all the time.
    Using figurative language, such as metaphor, in expressing Indige-
 nous beliefs is relevant on two counts. First, as mentioned, metaphor
 aligns with the language structure of Indigenous societies. In his arti-
 cle on Indigenous knowledges and metaphors, Gregory Cajete (2000)
 writes of how, in Pueblo society, Elders commonly use metaphor in
 their communication. He says that Pueblo society is high-context,
 and the metaphors arise from the context of place. He provides the
 ­example of an earthenware traditional wedding vase of the Pueblo to
48   Situating Indigenous Methodologies


     Figure 2.2. Indigenous Conceptual Framework

                         The Four Foundations: Epistemology,
                             Ethics, Community (including
                                  land & place), & Self




     symbolize the unity of two spirits and one body. Because metaphoric
     expression is found in Indigenous languages and societies, it follows
     that communication techniques such as metaphors will be found in
     Indigenous methodologies.
         Metaphors help to reveal, and make visible, Indigenous ideas,
     thinking, and thought. The use of metaphor in Indigenous concep-
     tual framing allows for a variety of expressions representing diverse
     experiences and symbols from place. For example, Christine Fiddler
     (2014) utilized a braid of sweet grass in conceptually framing her
     ­research design; Absolon (2011), a petal flower; Debassige (2013), a
      turtle shaker; and Tachine (2018), a hand-woven rug. In designing
      and communicating Indigenous conceptual frameworks through
      word or metaphoric symbol, we must guard against the notion of
      “one size fits all.” Because so much of Indigenous ways of know-
      ing is internal, personal, and experiential, creating one standardized
      framework for Indigenous research is impossible and inevitably
      soul sapping for Indigenous people. However, personalizing an
      Indigenous conceptual framework, at the end of the day, must be
      based on Indigenous thought and imbued with an Indigenous sen-
      sibility and ethics.
                 Indigenous Conceptual Framing in Indigenous Methodologies   49


   Too often Indigenous methodologies have been equated with the
inclusion of particular methods, such as sharing circles, community-
based partners (e.g., Indigenous advisory circles), or autoethno-
graphic study, alongside a statement on ethical guidelines involving
research with Indigenous people and/or communities. While these
approaches attempt to consider Indigenous ways of knowing within
research, methodologically, they are problematic because the concep-
tual framing propelling these methods is often embedded in a nor-
mative, thus invisible, Western intellectual tradition. Certainly, using
Indigenous conceptual frameworks in research raises questions such
as how we express Indigenous understandings in a way that both
serves our communities and is accessible to the Western academy.
Does research language with terms such as conceptual frameworks chip
away at our philosophies? Can we carry out Indigenous embedded
research within the academy without this framework language?
   Ahenakew (2016) proposes that “using metaphor and poetry to
disrupt sense-making and prompt sense-sensing in the experience of
readers” (p. 337) is a helpful approach to revealing the contradictions
and limitations of introducing Indigenous knowledge (and methodol-
ogies) in Western contexts. Knowing the limitations but staying true to
Indigenous principles, we can use the “research framework” language
to bridge the divide and present an Indigenous conceptual framework
that makes visible Indigenous thought in our research actions. This
matters, for that which is not seen is not recognized or represented.
   The next section of this chapter outlines the Indigenous concep-
tual framing developed for my graduate research. Since then, I have
considered how Indigenous theory bridges Indigenous epistemology,
specifically in the process of interpreting findings (as discussed in
Chapter 8).



A NÊHIYAW CONCEPTUAL RESEARCH FRAMEWORK

For my doctoral research (Kovach, 2006), I asked whether Indige-
nous scholar-researchers were integrating cultural knowledges into
the methodologies of their research and, if so, how. My hypothesis
was that Indigenous researchers were doing this, if not in an explicit
50   Situating Indigenous Methodologies


     manner. In asking Indigenous scholars (insiders) for their insight into
     this question, I needed a methodology with an epistemology and
     axiology, along with a methods strategy, capable of exploring my
     research question – in other words I was asking Indigenous people
     about I­ ndigenous culture and so needed an Indigenous methodology.
     At the time, there were minimal Indigenous culturally informed meth-
     odologies to draw from, and I was left to call upon Western ­research
     approaches as a guide.
           Of the methodologies available, I initially chose phenomenology
     as grounded in a constructivist paradigm with its value placed upon
     an experiential, self-in-relation interpretative tradition. H   ­ owever,
     ­phenomenology did not encompass the decolonizing, social justice
      dimension of my question. After much thought, I turned to a trans-
      formative paradigm and a qualitative methodology anchored in
      ­critical theory, but I found that the primary focus on power discourses
       overtook a holistic, experiential approach. And so I tried to employ a
       constructivist and transformative paradigm in one singular ­design.
       Perhaps a combination of the two would work? More headache,
       something was still missing.
           I knew that both constructivist and transformative methodologies,
       though distinct from one another, would each result in interesting
       ­research findings in my study. Each could generate an interpretation
        aligned with its particular theoretical positioning and offer insight
        from that perspective. I could get solid research out of both. The pre-
        dicament for me was that both were embedded in Western intellectual
        tradition, and my aim was to delve into Indigenous cultural knowl-
        edges from the perspective of Indigenous people. Though unable to
        fully pinpoint the missing puzzle piece, I instinctively knew this was
        a methodological conundrum. I was exploring a question steeped in
        Indigenous culture and sought to learn about this question from an
        Indigenous insider perspective. No matter how simpatico the West-
        ern research design, a methodology couched in a Western conceptual
        framing would result in a Western gaze on the analysis. Western con-
        ceptual framing would take me down a road, but to a different des-
        tination than my research purpose intended. At the time, finding a
        suitable methodology was frustrating given that, for the most part,
        one wasn’t readily available.
                  Indigenous Conceptual Framing in Indigenous Methodologies   51


   Perplexed, I put aside Western qualitative research methodologies
with their abstract words and returned to Indigenous teachings. Based
upon the scholarly writing at the time, Indigenous scholars were
referencing Indigenous thought as an interpretive form of inquiry
(Atleo, 2004; Bastien, 1999; Bishop, 1997; Cole, 2002; Meyer, 2004).
Reflecting on their work and my culture, I began to conceptualize a
research framework based upon Nêhiyaw (Plains Cree) knowledges.
I thought, “If Western qualitative research conceptual frameworks
exist within specific approaches (e.g., constructivist, transformative)
with their corresponding epistemologies and theories (Mertens, 2005),
perhaps I can use similar ‘framework’ language to centre Indigenous
thought.” It was a concession, but it held possibility.
   I turned to Nêhiyaw scholar Shawn Wilson’s (2001) influential
writing on Indigenous methodologies to think through an Indigenous
conceptual framework for my research. Alongside this intellectual and
theoretical foraging, I was drawn to non-traditional, abductive forms
of knowledge found within Nêhiyaw teachings. I started dreaming
about my research. One night I dreamt of a sheet of paper with a cir-
cle nested in a circle. I knew it was about my research. I woke early
in the morning hours and quickly scrawled the dream image in my
research journal. In due time, this subconscious knowledge would re-
surface and manifest into a conceptual framework nested in Nêhiyaw
knowledges.
   The remainder of this chapter focuses on the Nêhiyaw conceptual
framework I developed for my doctoral research. The epistemic root
of this framework is Nêhiyaw Kiskêýihtamowin (Nêhiyaw knowledges).
The Nêhiyaw conceptual framework includes the following charac-
teristics (see Figure 2.3) with each part, as it originally appeared in the
first edition of Indigenous Methodologies, provided below:

a. Nêhiyaw Kiskêýihtamowin (epistemology),
b. decolonizing ethics,
c. researcher preparation (involving cultural protocols),
d. research preparation (involving the standardized qualitative
   ­research design),
e. meaning making (of knowledges gathered), and
f. giving back.
52   Situating Indigenous Methodologies


     Figure 2.3. Original Nêhiyaw Kiskêýihtamowin Conceptual Framework



                                     Researcher
                                     Preparation


                      Giving                              Research
                      Back                               Preparation


                               Nêhiyaw Kiskêýihtamowin
                                    (epistemology)

                     Making                              Decolonizing
                     Meaning                               Ethics



                                      Gathering
                                      Knowledge




     Nêhiyaw Knowledge Epistemology

     As I am defining it, epistemology includes beliefs held about knowl-
     edge, where it comes from and whom it involves (Kovach, 2018).
     Within research, epistemology addresses questions of what is con-
     sidered knowledge, such as whether subjective knowing can count
     as ­legitimate knowledge and what counts as legitimate knowledge
     (Kirby et al., 2006). Nêhiyaw epistemology guided my research
     methodology. When I speak of Nêhiyaw knowledges (e.g., values,
     language), it should be assumed that they are nested, created, and
     recreated within the context of relationships with other living beings.
     While these relational aspects of Nêhiyaw culture are represented
     through the linear constraints of written text, the elements are fluid,
     and they interact with each other in a web-like formation. Each value
     represents a strand in a web and is integrated and interdependent
     with the other strands. This is an important point, for I understand
     Nêhiyaw culture as being a non-fragmented, holistic approach to the
     world grounded in specific place (in my case, Treaty Four territory).
                 Indigenous Conceptual Framing in Indigenous Methodologies   53


Segregating values from ceremony or segregating either from place or
language is done at one’s own peril. Additionally, we must consider
the historical, political, and social context in which Indigenous meth-
odologies currently operate.

A Decolonizing Purpose with a Community-Informed Ethic

A decolonizing ethical imperative is integral to this Nêhiyaw concep-
tual framework. Because of the decolonizing terminology, there is an
assumption that its focus is on decolonizing relationships between
Indigenous peoples and settler societies, thus centring a critical or
postcolonial perspective. This is not the case. Nêhiyaw epistemol-
ogy, ethics, and theory are centred. Although this Nêhiyaw concep-
tual framework decentres the settler, the problematic nature of the
settler-Indigenous relationship is recognized. Thus, this research
framework incorporates an Indigenous theoretical positioning with a
decolonizing emphasis.
    A decolonizing emphasis is capable of understanding the felt expe-
rience of colonialism and its impact on Nêhiyaw peoples and knowl-
edges. There has been abuse of trust, with relational ethics cast aside
in the name of research expediency and a “get the job done” mentality.
Seen from a decolonizing lens, ethical infringement through research
is an extension of the Indigenous-settler colonial project. Much has
to do with divergent beliefs concerning the ownership of knowledge,
which stem from collectivist and individualist orientations that hold
deep philosophical assumptions about how a society should work.
As a guard against ethical infringement in Indigenous research, an
ethical vetting by Indigenous communities and establishments is
­recommended as a decolonizing imperative.
    Indigenous research in a specific geographical community may
 go to an Indigenous ethics board. However, this will not always be
 the case, so advisory committees or boards ought to be established
 to provide an ethical vetting of research from an Indigenous per-
 spective. Indigenous ethics review boards (and advisory commit-
 tees) act as stewards of Indigenous knowledges within a research
 context. H
          ­ owever, with much at stake, Indigenous ethics boards re-
 quire ­capacity and time to consider ethical compliance internal to
 the community and to interface with the non-Indigenous research
54   Situating Indigenous Methodologies


     establishment. From a Canadian standpoint, all research carried out
     within the auspices of Canadian universities must go through an eth-
     ics review process. Tri-Council funded research must go through an
     ethical review process in accordance with Tri-Council Policy Statement:
     Ethical Conduct for Research Involving Humans.1 These review processes
     will ask specific questions concerning ethical engagement with Indig-
     enous communities.

     Researcher Preparations

     The term researcher preparation describes the experiential aspect of the
     research, including space for the inward knowing arising from per-
     sonal experience. I refer to these personal preparations as m­ iskâsowin.
     Miskâsowin is a Nêhiyaw word that means to go to the centre of your-
     self to find your own belonging (Cardinal & H ­ ildebrandt, 2000, p. 21).
     From the oral teachings and writings of Indigenous peoples of different
     nations, the message is consistent – all we can know for certain is our
     own experience. The Nêhiyaw conceptual framework, as presented,
     includes methodological space for reflexivity and ­acknowledges in-
     ward knowledge.
        The inward reflection, or self-referent knowledges, of the researcher
     is not a new component of research. This aspect of researcher prepa-
     ration is accentuated in Indigenous conceptual framing because of
     the value Indigenous knowledges place upon experiential knowing.
     From a Nêhiyaw point of view, the attention to inward knowing is not
     optional. Inward knowing is about spiritual preparation. It implies
     a connection with our most sacred inner self. Spirituality, as defined
     here, is a connection to a larger force external to one’s self, whether
     this be a god force or the power of nature. (Spirituality, in this sense,
     is not interchangeable with organized religion.) From a traditional
     Nêhiyaw perspective, seeking out Elders, attending to holistic epis-
     temologies, and participating in cultural catalyst activities (dream,
     ceremony, prayer) are means for accessing inward knowledge and


     1 Tri-Council is the term for the three major research funding organizations in
       ­Canada. They include the Social Sciences and Humanities Research Council of
        ­Canada (SSHRC), the Canadian Institutes of Health Research (CIHR), and the
         ­Natural Sciences and Engineering Research Council (NSERC).
                 Indigenous Conceptual Framing in Indigenous Methodologies   55


the spirit force. According to Nêhiyaw culture, teachings come from
many places. We need to open ourselves to those teachings, pause for
reflection and integration; these teachings then assist us in being and
in helping our communities. Preparation of this nature in research,
by the researcher, is unique to each individual. It is a process that can
never lend itself to a checkbox, universal approach; rather, it is per-
sonal work, deeply contextual, that must be done by the researcher in
conjunction with her or his world (both inner and outer).
     In qualitative research that seeks to explore the lived experience of
marginalized groups, there is often reference to whether the researcher
is an insider or outsider (or a combination) to the research partici-
pants. Reflecting on insider/outsider status is a form of ­self-situating
and reflexivity. Integral to self-situating are motivation and purpose
in research. The work of Eber Hampton (1995) prompts us to be clear
about our position in relation to community and the research motiva-
tions, both academic and personal, guiding our inquiry.
     My doctoral research took me home to Saskatchewan, where
I connected with community and culture. Place and the everyday-
ness of our lives shape how we think and write. If I were located on
­Vancouver Island, the flavour and feel of my graduate research would
 have been different. It would have been the research and writing of an
 expatriate Cree/Saulteaux from the cafés of Victoria. I was destined
 to return home, to Nêhiyaw territory, to complete my PhD. Return-
 ing to a physical home territory might not be the case for everyone.
 However, if we are following the path of Indigenous methodologies,
 we each find home wherever that may be. Indigenous scholars speak
 about personal preparations in other ways, such as the inclusion of
 ceremony in guiding their research (Debassige, 2010; Kovach et al.,
 2015; Montgomery, 2012; Wilson, 2008).
     As a means of capturing personal reflections throughout the
 ­research journey, I elected to record my thoughts in a research jour-
  nal. Unlike field notes, which are recordings of observations made
  during field study, this journal captured reflections on thoughts, rela-
  tionships, dreams, anxieties, and aspirations in a holistic manner that
  related (if at times only tangentially) to my research. The journaling
  traced personal analysis and research insights through an abductive
  reasoning process. As such, journaling was a tool for making mean-
  ing and demonstrated evidence of integrating process and content. In
56   Situating Indigenous Methodologies


     whatever way we define them, personal preparations are about doing
     the work in a good way and being grounded. We must know that In-
     digenous methodologies will ask much of the self.

     Research Preparations

     My research asked that I have conversations with other Indigenous
     ­researchers about Indigenous research methods. Some of the specif-
      ics on gathering the data included selecting participants, deciding
      on methods for gathering knowledge, choosing interpretation ap-
      proaches, and considering ethics. In choosing participants, researchers
      undertaking qualitative studies are advised to recruit based on what
      people can bring to the study, as opposed to doing random sampling.
      This study sought depth rather than breadth, and so a small sample
      group of six research participants was chosen. Participants in my re-
      search included a strong representation of Nêhiyaw people (four of
      the six participants are Nêhiyaw). I also invited individuals who had
      a strong sense of their culture, experience in a Western academic doc-
      toral program, and a background in education and/or social work,
      this last because of the interdisciplinary nature of the study. I was
      an insider to the research in that I was Indigenous, an academic re-
      searcher, and knew the research participants from collegial networks
      of Indigenous researchers and university instructors. However, some
      individuals held pre-tenure or tenured faculty positions. I did not. In
      this sense, I was outsider.
         In line with Nêhiyaw epistemology, which honours sharing
      story as a means for knowing, conversation is a non-structured
      method of gathering knowledge. While this may seem like another
      way of saying “interview,” the term interview does not capture the
      full essence of this approach. This was very much a combination
      of reflection, story, and dialogue. It was a conversational method
      (Kovach, 2019). Thus, in my research framework, participating in
      the participants’ stories – their experiences with culture and meth-
      odologies – was a primary method of the research. The data collec-
      tion process for my research took place after I had relocated from
      Victoria, BC, to Saskatchewan. The conversations took place over
      a two-month period in four general localities (Regina, Winnipeg,
      Vancouver, and Victoria).
                 Indigenous Conceptual Framing in Indigenous Methodologies   57


   There is a Nêhiyaw word, tâpwê, which means to speak the truth.
This is about trustworthiness or, relationally speaking, credibility.
Presenting the conversations and talks through a condensed con-
versation format meets the Nêhiyaw criteria of tâpwê. For example,
participants reviewed and approved their transcripts. They were able
to make changes and be comfortable with both the raw transcripts
and the condensed conversations. In considering the trustworthiness
of research, I hear the Elders’ voices: “Are you doing this in a good
way?” To do this means to tend to the process in a good way, so that
no matter the outcome you can sleep at night because you did right
by the process.

Meaning Making of Knowledges Gathered

In interpreting the findings, I inwardly reflected on the stories
(i.e., i­nterview conversations), and I undertook a thematic analy-
sis. ­Working with story was consistent with a Nêhiyaw conceptual
framework. I chose to analyse data through a Western method of
thematic analysis. As Moustakas (1990) explains, I engaged in the
process of “identifying the qualities and themes manifested in the
data” (p. 51). I used a process that first identified themes into bun-
dles that aligned with my research question. This was not an Indige-
nous method, which I acknowledge. I experienced some uneasiness
in the analytical process at times because it felt like I was extracting
the findings from the context of people’s stories. In externalizing
the data, I was cautioned about the limitations of an Indigenous
conceptual framework for research in a textual universe. I am re-
minded of Vine Deloria’s account of Ruth Beebe Hill, a non-Sioux
researcher who “deliberately interpreted Sioux customs as an ex-
treme form of individualism … She utterly gutted all substance in
the kinship tradition and reduced the Sioux religion to bizarre mys-
ticism” (Deloria, 1991, p. 458). However, I maintained allegiance to
an Indigenous epistemology and theory and that served to be the
influencing variable.
    In my doctoral research, I also used the story form as a means of
both analysing and presenting findings. I documented the partici-
pants’ stories through conversations and chose to relate these conver-
sations in a condensed format yet staying as true to each story, to the
58   Situating Indigenous Methodologies


     voice, as possible. Each condensed conversation offers an introduc-
     tion and reflective commentary on the story, written by me, identify-
     ing the teachings that were particularly relevant to me. The goal is to
     show self in relation to others. Presenting the data in this way allows
     readers to interpret the conversations from their own particular van-
     tage points and take from the teachings what they need.
        In subsequent research writing, I have highlighted the function of
     Indigenous theory to make explicit how an Indigenous theoretical
     perspective is applied to meaning making within Indigenous method-
     ologies. It is Indigenous theorizing in the analysis that differentiates
     Indigenous and Western research findings from one another. This idea
     was not given its full due in the first edition of this book.

     Reciprocity and Dissemination: Giving Back

     In research and research dissemination that value miýo-wîcêhtowin or
     good relations, it is critical that research participants understand and
     accept how their teachings will be represented. To that end, they must
     be given an opportunity to review and approve transcripts of their
     words. Depending upon the ethical review policy and procedures,
     universities may allow research participants the option of letting
     their name stand, allowing for a form of accountability that is found
     within oral cultures. Disseminating Indigenous research findings re-
     quires guardianship responsibilities. It can be difficult for Indigenous
     researchers to determine how much cultural knowledge to include in
     a textual format. Fortunately, as a Nêhiyaw researcher, I had access to
     documented accounts of Plains Cree culture by Nêhiyaw Elders in a
     variety of published forms. As these Elders have allowed this knowl-
     edge to be shared in the public domain, it is appropriate to share.
     By observing and talking with Elders, family members, and other
     ­Nêhiyaw individuals with knowledge of the culture, I have gained an
      understanding of the ways and how they intersect with my life nar-
      rative. As a learner of Nêhiyaw epistemology, I have incorporated my
      understandings into my writing and have done so with respect and
      caution, particularly where aspects of my own story intersect with
      other people’s lives.
         I have been resolute in returning this research to the Indigenous
      community through oral presentations, publication, teaching, and
                  Indigenous Conceptual Framing in Indigenous Methodologies   59


meeting with Indigenous peoples, graduate students, and Indigenous
faculty. For in my heart, this research is born of the Indigenous col-
lective and to the culture it must return. I have been a conduit of this
knowledge for a brief time and will persist while I have this honour.
    In concluding this chapter, I am reminded of a quotation by
­Niitsitapi scholar Betty Bastien (1999): “To continue practicing ­research
 outside of one’s culture and attempting to develop research questions
 from experiences based on the western paradigm, continues to create
 dependency among tribal peoples” (p. 62). As Nisga’a Elder Maurice
 Squires tells us, “All problems must be solved within the context of
 the culture – otherwise you are just creating another form of assim-
 ilation” (as quoted in Bruyere, 1999). I am reminded of Susan Boyd
 (2005), a critical researcher, who points out that “knowledge is power”
 and the choosing of a methodology is a political act (p. 1).
    The language in this chapter uses contemporary research vernacu-
 lar to communicate Indigenous thought because words have power –
 research words have power. But the thing is, if we rub away the layer
 of powerful research lingo from Indigenous conceptual framing, what
 lies beneath is Indigenous knowledges – knowledges that have ­existed
 since time immemorial and will prevail so long as Indigenous peoples
 walk this earth. Over the past decade, Indigenous conceptual framing
 and Indigenous methodological research language have arrived in the
 academy. To me, this is miýo (a good thing). We create space by not
 being erased. We claim space by asserting our presence: let us claim
 ­Indigenous methodologies and name Indigenous methodologies!

  methodologies.

  illustrate an Indigenous conceptual framework? What would you in-
  clude? Draw this conceptual framework (use colour). Present it to an-
  other person or group of people.
3 Find time this week to walk outdoors (in a natural setting if possible)
  and note the different metaphors offered by nature. With the use of
  technology (such as your smartphone’s camera) or through journaling,
60   Situating Indigenous Methodologies


       determine a possible metaphor for an Indigenous conceptual
       ­framework. Share your metaphor, and the reason for your pick, with
        another person.
     4 As a graduate, faculty, or community researcher, what pushback might

          The Four Foundations:
