This page intentionally left blank
CHAPTER THREE

 Epistemology and Research: Centring
 Indigenous Knowledges




      In Aboriginal philosophy, existence consists of energy. All things are
      animate, imbued with spirit, and in constant motion. In this realm of
      energy and spirit, interrelationships between all entities are of para-
      mount importance, and space is a more important referent than time.
                                                            Little Bear (2000)



 Indigenous scholar Manu Aluli Meyer (2001) describes her relation-
 ship with epistemology like this: “Every little thing. I mean, I can see
 a dead frog on the road, and it relates to epistemology” (p. 192). It took
 me a while, but I understand Manu Aluli Meyer’s point of view now.
 Every decision, every move I have made during my N    ­ êhiyaw-centred
 research journey has asked me to consider how each decision fits
 with my beliefs about knowledge, the world, and Nêhiyaw ways of
 ­knowing. So much is about epistemology, but knowing this does not
  make the path clearer.
      The deeper I submerge myself in Indigenous knowledges, the more
  I resist Western ways of knowing as a given for all academic research,
  even though I know that this resistance demands a long swim against
  a strong current. I can appreciate the value of Western research meth-
  ods of coding, bundling, categorizing, and naming according to a set
  of beliefs and principles in knowledge creation. My concern is not
  about organizing knowledge, for Nêhiyaw society is quite adept at
64   The Four Foundations


     this, but rather it is the worldview, the epistemological underpinning
     of this organization with which I grapple. For me, epistemology is
     simultaneously elusive and ubiquitous, woven tightly with p     ­ ersonal
     identity that shifts over a life span, and though it is holistic, it is
     most often represented in my academic world through a cognitive,
     ­culturally embedded lens. Epistemology and research methodology
      are a tight, complex partnership. And as Manu Aluli Meyer (2004)
      states, the epistemological presence in research and life permeates our
      ­understandings in/of both. It is frogs everywhere.
           This chapter concerns itself with Indigenous epistemology through
       highlighting Nêhiyaw knowledges. The aim is to illustrate how
       ­Indigenous cultural epistemology, as a core foundation of Indigenous
        conceptual framing, guides research choices within Indigenous meth-
        odologies. To assist, Michael Hart, a Nêhiyaw scholar from the Fisher
        River Cree Nation, Manitoba, Canada, offers his thoughts. In my con-
        versation with him, he discusses the intersection between Nêhiyaw
        epistemology and research methodology. There is a resurgence of inter-
        est in Indigenous knowledges within the academic research establish-
        ment; however, Indigenous epistemology will remain on the periphery
        so long as it is squeezed to the sidelines. Therefore, before delving
        into the Indigenous epistemological discussion, a word on its colonial
        ­encounters in academic zones of contact where Eurocentrism prevails.



     EPISTEMIC PRIVILEGE AND CONTESTED SPACES

     Recognizing colonial influence in knowledge paradigms requires that
     we are ever mindful of the history of colonialism (Coburn, 2015). When
     the first European ships came to the Americas, followed soon there-
     after by the boats carrying settlers, they were packing aspects of their
     material culture that caused serious harm to Indigenous communities
     and cultures (Dunbar-Ortiz, 2014). They were also bringing an attitude
     about the world (and who owned it) that was in sharp contrast with
     Indigenous peoples’ worldviews. While colonization touched every
     ­aspect of Indigenous life, Western science has been instrumental in sub-
      jugating and discrediting Indigenous knowledge systems and peoples.
         Darwin’s evolutionary theory displaced a creationist approach
      and proposed that life evolved slowly and incrementally, with the
                                          Centring Indigenous Knowledges   65


superior forms of life prevailing and the inferior dying out. As Vine
Deloria (2002) points out, this perspective fit extremely well with the
goals of the elite in Europe who were engaged in the exploitive en-
deavours of colonialism and industrialism: “Survival of the fittest, the
popularization of a Darwinian concept, became a means for justifying
social piracy” (p. 17). The racism inherent in this evolutionary para-
digm contributed to the genocidal policy towards Indigenous peoples
in the Americas. Furthermore, science, in conjunction with a coloni-
alist agenda, was responsible for increasingly effective weaponry to
compel Indigenous peoples to capitulate.
    In a decolonizing analysis of the early precepts of a Western scien-
tific paradigm, Pam Colorado (1988) identifies assumptions that con-
flicted with an Indigenous philosophy: the universe is empty, static
space where atoms and particles live independent of each other and
do not shift or change; God, through Newtonian physics, does not
have a role in the cause and effect of the universe, and prophecy or
a greater purpose does not exist, as life is simply a cause-and-effect
dynamic; and all energy patterns can be accounted for by human in-
tellect with humans as all-knowing.
    Indigenous thought, unprepared to separate reason and spirit, did
not espouse an evolutionist theoretical perspective. As a result, Indig-
enous knowledge systems were viewed by Western scientists as mere
superstitions (Deloria, 2002). It was a rationalist, secular paradigm,
contesting the possibility of knowledge arising from happenings that
cannot be explained through reductionist means (Atleo, 2004; Delo-
ria & Wildcat, 2001). Then, as now, Indigenous knowledges cannot be
fragmented, externalized, and objectified and cannot be solely under-
stood from a reductionist analysis. Further, an Indigenous epistemic
foundation includes the axiological qualities of spirituality, ethics,
and aesthetics. Consequently, holistic, spiritually imbued Indigenous
thought was, and continues to be, considered by Western Eurocen-
trism as a belief system but not science in terms of what Western cul-
ture views science to be.
    More specific to research, the privileging of empiricism has side-
lined Indigenous knowledges. Tangible, observable evidence is the
standard in Western academic research traditions. In an archaeological
research context, Nicholas (2017) states, “[T]hey [archaeologists] tend
to be satisfied only when there is some physical evidence of religious
66   The Four Foundations


     practice associated with it [a sacred site], particularly in the context of
     land claims, resource extraction, or land development.” (p. 216). The
     empiricism integral to positivist research – with its emphasis on ex-
     ternal evidence, objectivity, neutrality, testing, and universal laws of
     generalizability – emerging out of a deductive rationalist paradigm
     has a particular approach to knowledge. The primacy of “objectivism”
     within positivism narrows a knowledge sphere to that which can be
     validated through a rationalist, secular approach. This contests the
     contextualized holism of an Indigenous approach to knowledge. It
     is thus no wonder Indigenous knowledges have been excluded from
     ­research paradigms and epistemology discourses. Historically, the con-
      ventional Western knowledge paradigm has not shown an ideological
      openness to understanding the holism of Indigenous knowledges. As
      Indigenous scholars advance Indigenous methodologies in academic
      research, there exists the possibility of a more inclusive science.



     EPISTEMOLOGY IN INDIGENOUS METHODOLOGIES

     In his influential book, The Foundations of Social Research, Crotty (1998)
     references epistemology, in part, as dealing with the scope and basis
     of knowledge. In conceptually framing research, an epistemological
     statement outlines the scope and basis of the knowledge and the iden-
     tity markers of the specific way of knowing for a study. Crotty (1998)
     provides a couple of examples of different epistemological stances. An
     objectivist epistemology, he says, is defined as a belief in an objective,
     singular truth. It is the belief in one truth waiting to be discovered not
     encumbered by the messiness of human interpretations. Positivist meth-
     odologies are objectivist. This is different from a constructivist episte-
     mology. From a constructivist perspective, truth is pluralized. Crotty
     states that, in constructivism, truths are constructed, not discovered.
     Truths arise from interactions between objects and subjects. A construc-
     tivist epistemology is the force of many interpretative methodologies.
         Crotty (1998) goes on to say that knowing the epistemological stance
     in research is critical. Our chosen epistemological stance (e.g., objec-
     tivist, constructivist) will imply “a profound difference in how we do
     our researching and how we present our research outcomes” (p. 9). It
     is similar to knowing that when we build a house, we (a) recognize
                                           Centring Indigenous Knowledges    67


we need a foundation and (b) know we will have to choose a type of
foundation (e.g., cement, wood). In qualitative research, we cannot
know our theoretical perspective, let alone our methods and analysis,
without first clarifying our foundational epistemological stance.
    The epistemological foundation of an Indigenous methodologies
study is based upon an Indigenous epistemology. Many books and
articles have been written on Indigenous philosophy and the Indige-
nous epistemic stance (Brant-Castellano, 2004; Cajete, 1999; Deloria &
Wildcat, 2001; Ermine, 1995; Little Bear, 2000), providing deep insight.
It is not my intent to try to encompass the totality or depth of this wis-
dom but rather to reference key features of Indigenous epistemologies
that are consistent throughout Indigenous thought and cultures in the
Americas and globally.
    Indigenous epistemologies are axiologically embedded. This
means that they include an ethical and spiritual base associated with
relationships between people, nature, and the cosmos. Guba and Lin-
coln (2005) retroflect on the role of axiology in qualitative research
paradigms: “If we had to do it all over again, we would make val-
ues, or, more correctly, axiology (the branch of philosophy dealing
with ethics, aesthetics, and religion) a part of the basic foundational
philosophical dimensions of paradigm proposal” (p. 200). Guba and
Lincoln (2005) go on to note that axiology has been extricated from sci-
entific inquiry because of its reference to religion. They say, however,
defining religion to allow for a conversation on spirituality broadly
(as opposed to defining religion as organized religion) – which is the
point for them – is to allow for a broader, more expansive understand-
ing of paradigm. Indigenous epistemologies do not contest an axio-
logical premise, and from their starting place assume that ethics and
spirituality are involved.
    What is the basis of Indigenous knowledges; what are the identity
markers of an Indigenous way of knowing? In responding to these
questions, we see that an Indigenous philosophy emphasizes a be-
lief system that espouses a non-fragmented, non-human-centric, ho-
lism focusing on the metaphysical and pragmatic brought alive by
an animate language structure and contextualized within place and
land-based knowing and teachings. These aspects of an Indigenous
philosophy are bound by the relational. Thus, Indigenous epistemol-
ogies uphold the virtues of interdependence (Cardinal, 2001; Ermine,
68   The Four Foundations


     1995) and must be understood from the vantage point of collectivism
     and relationality.
        When we reflect on the scope and basis of Indigenous knowledge
     systems, it is helpful to review the quotation by Niitsitapi scholar
     Leroy Little Bear (2000) found at the start of this chapter:

          In Aboriginal philosophy, existence consists of energy. All things
          are animate, imbued with spirit, and in constant motion. In this
          realm of energy and spirit, interrelationships between all entities
          are of paramount importance, and space is a more important ref-
          erent than time. (Little Bear, 2000, p. 77)


     In considering Leroy Little Bear’s statement on Indigenous philoso-
     phy, we can begin to identify the scope, basis, and identity markers
     of an Indigenous epistemological stance. The scope and basis of an
     Indigenous epistemology encompasses

     • multiple sources of knowledge, more commonly recognized as
       holism (scope);
     • a tangible and intangible animate world that is process orientated
       and cyclical, such as that expressed in verb-orientated languages
       (e.g., with ing endings), which comprise many Indigenous lan-
       guages (basis); and
     • a web of interdependent, contextual relationships over time, such
       as with place, family, and community (basis).

       If we see all the qualities of holistic, animate, and relationships embed-
     ded within community, place, and kin as being indelibly linked, couched
     within an axiological supposition, and contextualized within Indige-
     nous culture, we are on our way to identifying an Indigenous epis-
     temological stance. Let’s explore holism, animism, and relationality.



     HOLISM AND MULTIPLE SOURCES OF KNOWLEDGE

     Knowledge comes from multiple sources, such as humans, animals,
     birds, fish, insects, the earth, land, sky, and spirit. Indigenous episte-
     mologies are not human centric, meaning that all species, not solely
                                          Centring Indigenous Knowledges   69


the human species, are a source of knowledge. Neither is knowledge
limited solely to cognitive reasoning, though holism encompasses this
source of knowing. Knowledge arises from the intellectual realm of
the mind, the affective domain of the heart, the kinetic domain of the
body, and the spiritual domain of the soul. Knowledge is cognitive,
embodied, instinctual, and spiritual.
   Anishinaabe scholar Jan Hare (2012) tells us that “knowledge is
contained within cultural practices, where songs, dances and symbolic
representations are repositories for the history, genealogies, thoughts
and beliefs about the world and the relationships within it” (p. 392).
Marlene Brant-Castellano identifies Indigenous knowledges as com-
ing from a multitude of sources, including “traditional teachings,
empirical observations, and revelations,” and she goes on to suggest
that knowledge revelations emerge from a myriad of places, includ-
ing intangible knowledge sources such as “dreams, visions, cellular
memory, and intuition” (as quoted in Steinhauer, 2002, p. 74). When
considering Indigenous epistemologies, there are many entry points
for considering its holistic quality, including spiritual knowledge.
   In choosing Indigenous epistemologies respect must be given to the
intangible aspects of human experience as spiritual connectedness,
with an understanding that spirit is deeply personal and can be ex-
perienced in multiple ways. Michael Hart (see below in this c­ hapter)
references the role of ceremony within Nêhiyaw knowledges: “I ap-
proach it [­methodology] … [t]hrough a lot of reflection back on my
time with Elders, with traditional teachers, in ceremony – those are my
biggest influences … I say ceremonies because, to me, I don’t hunt, so
I can’t rely on that process.” Roxanne Struthers (2001) acknowledged
spirit by offering a traditional gift of tobacco to her research partic-
ipants, as well as a daily offering of tobacco to the Creator. In pre-
paring herself for the research – and gaining guidance as to whether
she should continue with her research – she relied on dream knowl-
edge that came to her in the form of three Ojibway grandmothers.
Guidance from dreams and spirit became a part of her research. In her
research, Jeannine Carriere (2006) references the advice given by her
Nêhiyaw colleague: “[H]old your tobacco and see what happens” (p.
174). These are examples of respect for holistic, spirit-guided knowl-
edges by researchers who view inward knowing imbued by spirit as
legitimate. I can identify this knowledge source in my own life. Early
70   The Four Foundations


     in my research, I had a powerful dream that was particularly relevant.
     I knew, culturally, not to dismiss the knowledge coming to me in this
     form, for within Nêhiyaw knowledges, dreams matter.
        When I was in conversation with Graham Hingangaroa Smith (2006)
     about the intersection of holistic philosophy, respect for intangible
     knowledges, and research, he said, “I just see that as part of I­ ndigenous
     knowledge frameworks. You need a way to write them in obviously,
     but it’s part of the cultural approach … I see dreams as being part of
     oral tradition” (p. 166). He went on to say that the Māori do not have
     the same traditional beliefs about dreams, but he would not dismiss
     this as a valid knowledge source. Scholarship on Indigenous science,
     in one manner or another, references the relationship with metaphysics
     through creation myths, philosophies on space and time, and an en-
     ergy source that Indigenous people describe as the sacred (Cajete, 1999;
     Cardinal & Hildebrandt, 2000; Little Bear, 2000). Suspension of judg-
     ment is required for the knowing to surface in its own time (­Deloria
     & Wildcat, 2001). Much insight comes to an individual inwardly and
     intuitively through processes such as dreams and ceremony. R        ­ ichard
     Atleo (2004) introduces the Nuu-chah-nulth method of oosumich,
     ­
     which is a spiritual methodology equivalent to that of a vision quest.
     He argues that Western methodologies and o­ osumich belong together
     because they are two proven methods of accessing information.
        Western research methodologies that privilege evidence-based,
     data-driven traditions thus remain, largely, committed to material
     proof for substantiation. Because of the role that intangible forms
     of knowing have within the knowledge generation process, Indig-
     enous ­holism tests Western research. Indigenous researchers are
     grappling with ways to explain how holism informs their research
     design so that the Western academic mind can understand. There
     continues to be a chasm between Indigenous holism and a tangible-
     centric Western science.



     ANIMATE AND INTANGIBLE

     From a Niitsitapi perspective, Leroy Little Bear (2000) explains the no-
     tion of an animated world as one of flux, motion, and ­interrelationship.
     Rupert Ross (1996) recounts a conversation with Danny ­Moonhawk
                                             Centring Indigenous Knowledges   71


­ lford in which Moonhawk describes the cyclical, animated, intan-
A
gible life force in Indigenous philosophy: “On a surfboard there is
the conviction of intimate and inescapable exposure to unfathoma-
ble powers which, while they might let you ride them, will never let
you gain control over them” (as quoted in Ross, 1996, p. 125). Ermine
(1995) suggests that Indigenous knowledges arise from an energy
moving between inner and outer space. The outer space is the physi-
cal world and the inner space is where metaphysical knowing resides.
This suggests that energy reveals itself as knowing stored deep within
a collective unconscious and surfaces through dreams, prayer, cere-
monial ritual, and happenings (Cardinal, 2001; Ermine, 1995).
   The concept of the animate and intangible relationship between
subject and object is of such significant value to Indigenous cultures
that, in response to Indigenous advocacy, UNESCO introduced, in
2003, the Convention for the Safeguarding of the Intangible Cul-
tural Heritage (UNESCO [United Nations Educational Scientific
and Cultural Organization], 2003). This convention can be consid-
ered a sister document of the United Nations Declaration on the
Rights of Indigenous Peoples (United Nations, 2007). Within the
convention (UNESCO, 2003), intangible culture heritage encom-
passes “oral traditions and expressions, including language as a
vehicle of the intangible cultural heritage; performing arts; social
practices, rituals and festive events; knowledge and practices con-
cerning nature and the universe; and, traditional craftmanship”
(p. 2). In considering knowledge generation from an Indigenous
perspective, both the tangible and intangible factors and forces
have equal weight in Indigenous knowledge construction. The tan-
gible is always animated. It is consistent with the Tlingit Elders’
message as expressed to curators of the Portland Museum of Art in
a 1989 meeting decades ago:

     It was clear that from the Elders’ viewpoint the collected objects
     were not primarily “art.” They were referred to as “records,”
     “history,” and “law,” inseparable from myths and stories ex-
     pressing ongoing moral lessons with current political force. The
     museum was clearly informed that the Elders’ voices should
     be presented to the public when the objects were displayed.
     ­(Clifford, 1997, p. 191)
72   The Four Foundations


     Knowledge generation ought not to be extractive but reciprocal to en-
     sure an ecological and cosmological balance and maintain a process-
     orientated, animated culture. The significance of the movement of
     ­energy and the intangible in knowledge generation from an Indige-
      nous perspective cannot be overestimated.

     Indigenous Languages

     I have included Indigenous languages under the heading “Animate
     and Intangible” as a way of illustrating their relationship. Language
     is pivotal in knowing and preserving Indigenous culture and philos-
     ophies (Bishop, 1997; Struthers, 2001; Waters, 2004; Weaver, 2001).
     We communicate knowing through language. Like inward knowing,
     language is powerful because it reminds us who we are; it is deeply
     entwined with personal and cultural identity. The language we speak
     influences how we know, and it holds within it a person’s (and peo-
     ple’s) worldview. We must be humble in this knowledge. Graham
     Hingangaroa Smith (2006) raises a concern about those who are
     “claiming to be working in the area of Indigenous theorizing” (p. 159)
     but do not appreciate that Indigenous cultural knowing lives in the
     language. Many Indigenous people are not fluent in their language
     but are attempting to relearn; however, it will take a lot of immersion
     to retrain our minds.
        In connecting language, culture, and knowledge, Anne Waters
     (2004) offers insights into the structure of Indigenous languages and
     how form gives rise to a way of thinking and being. Waters suggests
     that dualist constructs such as like/unlike have resulted in a binary
     language and thought pattern in European cultures. Conversely, in
     many Indigenous cultures the language constructs suggest a non-­
     binary, complementary philosophy of the world. Western research
     that serves to extract and externalize knowledges in categorical
     groupings aligns with the categorical premises of Western languages
     for they are of the same cultural epistemology. How to think and be in
     a non-­binary way is a challenge when we live in a binary world.
        Given the philosophical basis of a complementary, non-binary In-
     digenous language construct, it makes sense that narrative encased
     in the form of oral history would be the natural means to transmit
     knowledges (Struthers, 2001). Within the structure of story, there is a
                                           Centring Indigenous Knowledges   73


place for the fluidity of metaphor, symbolism, and interpretative com-
munications (both verbal and nonverbal), for a philosophy and lan-
guage synergy that is less definitive and categorical. Skilled orators,
then and now, can imbue energy through word choice and allow lis-
teners to walk inside the story to find their own teachings. The inter-
pretation and the teachings taken become the listener’s task. With the
listener’s involvement, the insight gained from the story is a highly
particular and relevant form of knowledge exchange.
    Another consideration of language is the research vernacular in
knowledge exchange. Transforming the abstract theoretical findings
of research into concrete, accessible language is a challenge. The skill
of making research methodology relevant and interesting to commu-
nity rests largely with language. Crafting our own research stories,
in our own voices, has the best chance of engaging others. One strat-
egy is to integrate into our research findings the stories that paint
the context of our research. In writing my dissertation, I returned to
Saskatchewan. Being on the Prairies infused my thinking and writ-
ing with a Great Plains landscape. That a magpie, a thunderstorm, a
teepee set against the rolling hills of the Qu’Appelle Valley could fill
the landscape of my writing seems most possible when there. The
visitation of anecdotes, metaphors, and stories about place make
cerebral, academic language accessible, and reflect holistic, animate
epistemologies.
    Given the role of language in shaping thought and culture, con-
flict between Indigenous and Western epistemology and research
approaches (and the involvement of each in knowledge generation)
rests deeply within language and the matter of dualistic thought
patterns. In Indigenous epistemologies we must assert the interrela-
tionship between Indigenous language structure and philosophy and
address how colonialism has interfered with this dynamic. One of the
first approaches to erasing a culture is to attack its language because
language holds deep insight into the social organization of a people.
Without language to affirm knowledge daily, it is easy to lose cultural
memory. Milan Kundera, the well-known Czech novelist and philoso-
pher on cultural evolution, has written: “The first step in liquidating a
people … is to erase its memory … Before long a nation will begin to
forget what it is and what it was. The world around it will forget even
faster” (as quoted in Dyck, 1986, p. 132).
74   The Four Foundations


     COMMUNITY, PLACE, AND LAND-BASED
     RELATIONSHIPS

     Similar to the constructivist epistemology within Western tradition, as
     referenced earlier, an Indigenous epistemological stance is all about
     relationship between subject and object, tangible and intangible, self
     and others, self and the world. Indigenous epistemology ­expands the
     scope of relationships. As my doctoral committee member Leroy L       ­ ittle
     Bear advised me, “It’s all about relationships.” From an I­ndigenous
     point of view, relational means self in relationship with the natural
     world, the human world, kin, community, place, and land; relation-
     ships over time; and relationships that are interdependent and collec-
     tivist. This is why community engagement is so critical in Indigenous
     research. It is the significance of relationships that allow us to say that
     Indigenous culture is high-context. In the web of relationships, we
     find attachment in the places of our lives, whether under the cosmos,
     with nature (land, sea, sky), or among community and kin.
         Daniel Wildcat considers how place informs knowing: “You see and
     hear things by being in a forest, on a river, or at an ocean coastline; you
     gain real experiential knowledge that you cannot see by looking at the
     beings that live in those environments under a microscope or in a lab-
     oratory experiment” (Deloria & Wildcat, 2001, p. 36). As I­ndigenous
     peoples, we understand how to proceed based upon a long history of
     interrelationship with a particular territory or place. Our connection
     with land and place is what distinguishes Indigenous peoples from one
     another and what differentiates us from settler societies. Our places
     and connection to land give us identity. A Saskatchewan Cree poet and
     scholar, Neal McLeod (2005), writes about place and how it allows us
     to transverse time, giving us an immediate connection to the ancestors
     and reminding us who we are: “to the circle of old men speaking /
     echo of generations / gave form to the moment of my birth” (p. 23).
         Place, land, community – this concrescence links present with past
     and our personal self with kinship groups. What we know flows
     through us from the “echo of generations,” and our knowledges
     cannot be universalized because they arise from our unique experi-
     ence with our places. This is why name-place stories matter: they are
     ­repositories of science, they tell of relationships, they reveal history,
      and they hold our identity. In southern Saskatchewan, I am reminded
                                              Centring Indigenous Knowledges     75


of a well-known name-place legend of how the Qu’Appelle Valley re-
ceived its name. The most familiar version tells of a love story between
a Cree man and woman who were soon to wed. Away from home on
a hunting trip, he paddles home to her, for they are to marry the next
day. As he is nearing her home, he hears a voice calling out his name.
He responds Kâ-têpwêt (who calls?) in Cree, then Qu’appelle? in French,
but there is no reply, so he travels on. He arrives at her home and finds
her family grieving. They tell him that she has left for the spirit world,
but add, “Twice did she call for thee last night.” Pauline Johnson, a
Mohawk poet, wrote the poem “The Legend of Fort Qu’Appelle,”
based on a story handed down from the old people of this region.
      There are different versions of the Kâ-têpwêt legend, and with its tinge
of frontier romanticism, it is “likely misinterpreted from a story told by
the Indians” (Lerat & Ungar, 2005, p. 17). Even so, its haunting sadness
casts a line back through time to the ancestors. There is an alternate
version of how Qu’Appelle got its name. According to this story, “two
groups of people arrived on opposite sides of the Q    ­ u’Appelle and since
they could not get across to visit, they shouted news across the water,
and that is how the river got its name” (Lerat & Ungar, 2005, p. 17).
­Either way, these stories situate us in place and time; they localize his-
 tory and maintain an oral tradition of passing on knowledge.
      Place names make theoretical notions concrete; they offer us tacit
 meaning. Stories, like name-place legends, give comfort and ground-
 ing and offer the warmth of belonging. It is from these relationships
 that we can reach out to the world. Stories connected to place and
 land are about collectivist Indigenous orientation, and they are
 ­located within our personal knowing and conceptual framework of
  the world. In a talk I attended by Niitsitapi scholar Narcisse Blood,
  he reminded us of places as being alive, that they are imbued with
  spirit. Place and land are our teacher. The teaching of Black Elk alerts
  us that we, meaning all entities that dwell on the earth, are family
  and that nature sustains us: “O You, sacred Earth, from whence we
  have come, You are humble, nourishing all things; we know that
  You are wakan and that with You we are all relatives” (as quoted in
  Epes Brown, 1989, p. 20). Michael Hart (2006) echoes the teachings of
  ­relationality between people, place, land, language, and animals and
   describes how these relationships influence our coming to know in a
   non-human-centric world.
76   The Four Foundations


         From a holistic epistemology, one relationship is not more signif-
     icant than another. Rather, it is a balance of all. Relational balance is
     holistic epistemology. Given the interdependency of the relational
     from an Indigenous perspective, differing relational aspects of know-
     ing (thought, spirit, inward knowing) are difficult to define, abstract
     out, or compartmentalize – reductionist tools seem to not work here.
     As Battiste and Henderson (2000) indicate, “universal definitions of
     Indigenous knowledge” do not work well either because the knowl-
     edge, particularly the knowledge that originates from the extraordi-
     nary, is in deep relationship to the personal and particular (p. 36).
         Indigenous epistemologies are holistic, honour the intangible, and
     are relational with places, land, sky, and water – alive with life in a
     non-human-centric world. Indigenous epistemologies are process
     orientated. They are about living life every day seeking knowledge
     according to certain values. Reflecting on an Indigenous mind-set,
     Leroy Little Bear characterizes the ideal Native American personal-
     ity as one that is kind, puts the group first, is friendly, “is steeped in
     spiritual and ritual knowledge,” is easy-going, and has a good sense
     of humour (as quoted in Alfred, 2005, p. 10). Inherent within this
     ­perspective is knowledge and action in relationship with the world.
      This ideal reflects a holistic, value-based knowledge system that con-
      sistently returns to the responsibilities of maintaining good relations
      or miýo-wîcêhtowin. Moving from Indigenous epistemologies broadly,
      the remainder of this chapter highlights Nêhiyaw knowledges.



     A NÊHIYAW EPISTEMIC APPROACH

     Miýo-wîcêhtowin (good relations) is the heartbeat of the Nêhiyaw cul-
     ture. Miýo, Nêhiyaw for good, is an integral quality and a manifes-
     tation of holistic, relational epistemology. Miýo is about sharing and
     generosity, respecting the earth and all its inhabitants, working hard,
     and caring for other people. Irene Calliou, a Métis Elder, remembers
     her Cree grandmother speaking of how these values were part of
     daily practices: “My grandmother used to dig up medicinal roots; and
     once she dug them up, she placed tobacco there [in the hole]. I did
     not know then why she put tobacco in” (as quoted in Ahenakew &
     ­Wolfart, 1998, p. 157). Calliou tells us of showing respect for the earth,
                                             Centring Indigenous Knowledges   77


of reciprocity, and of the importance of observation and attentiveness
in learning because knowledge is transmitted through kinship rela-
tionships. The importance of land and place is tied with the value of
collective responsibility and stewardship. A prevailing teaching is that
an Indigenous research framework must not solely be an intellectual
construct, for it cannot be understood in the absence of its practical
manifestations, which involve living life in a way that reflects good-
ness, that reflects miýo.
   The buffalo hunt exemplifies Nêhiyaw knowledges and offers a met-
aphor for a Nêhiyaw methodology. The buffalo – paskâwo-­mostosw –
were the mainstay of the Plains Cree economy. “In 1870, there were
hundreds of thousands of buffalo in the Saskatchewan country; by
1881, there were only a few head, widely scattered” (Mandelbaum,
1979, p. 51). The slaughter of the buffalo due to the encroachment of
European settlement led to the starvation and destruction of the tra-
ditional Cree economy. In its prime, when the buffalo were plentiful,
the hunt was a central part of Plains Cree life. There were two ways
of procuring buffalo, the hunt and the chase. In the autumn and early
winter, tribes used a buffalo chute or pound, but in the spring and
summer, as the herds moved southward, they used the chase. In read-
ing stories about the hunt, it is apparent how place, values, and cer-
emony are integral to this act. Peyasiw-awasis (Chief Thunderchild)
shares a land- and place-based story of the hunt:

     In the days when the buffalo were many, there were Old Men
     who had the gift of “making pounds.” Poundmaker’s father
     was such a one, and he gave the name to his son. Another was
     Eyi-pâ-chi-nas, and when it was known that he was “sitting at
     pound” – that he was seeking the supernatural power to bring
     the buffalo – hunters would gather.
        One winter there were ten teepees, just for these hunters.
     Working all together, they cut trees to make a circular pound
     about seventy yards across … The gate was fourteen feet wide,
     and out from it they laid two long lines of tufted willows that
     spread farther and farther apart, to channel the buffalo into the
     pound. In the centre they set a great lobbed tree.
        When everything was ready, other Old Men joined Eyi-pâ-chi-
     nas and sang the buffalo song. Far on the plain, a herd of buffalo
78   The Four Foundations


           was sighted, and two young men rode out to watch. They were
           to blow their whistles as soon as the buffalo started to move in
           the early morning … The buffalo came on between the lines of
           the wall and through the gate … Then the hunters closed in, and
           stopped the gateway with poles and buffalo robes.
              We would cut up the meat till late at night, and haul it with
           dogs to the encampment … Other bands came to join us and to
           feast. (as quoted in Ahenakew, 1995, p. 36)


        Underlying the hunt was a way, a methodology, that Nêhiyaw
     used to undertake a sacred act that kept the community and its people
     alive. The hunt involved preparation for the hunt, a method, proto-
     col, ceremony, and respectfulness for going about the procurement of
     these animals and sharing the bounty. In many ways, the story of the
     buffalo hunt is a research teaching story, an allegory for a Nêhiyaw
     conceptual framework for research:

     •   preparation (for the research),
     •   preparation (of the researcher),
     •   recognition of protocol (cultural and ethical),
     •   carrying out the action (researcher in connection with community),
     •   reciprocity (social sharing of knowledge and food), and
     •   respectfulness.

     The buffalo hunt provides an epistemological teaching, a reference
     point for how to do things in a good way. It is born of the land, place,
     and context specific to Plains Cree. Driving through the Qu’Appelle
     Valley today, one can easily imagine the hunt in this place, and I can
     ground my research framework in the place markers of my ancestors.
        Knowledges from the ancestors are alive in Nêhiyaw communities.
     The most sacred form comes through dreams, fasts, sweats, and vision
     quests and during sacred ceremonies. Historically, there were differ-
     ent sacred gatherings among Plains Cree people, many of which still
     occur today. One of the most sacred is the Rain Dance, which is held at
     a specific time each year, and individuals who participate (stall danc-
     ers) do so for solemn and personal reasons. “Traditionally, Cree and
     Saulteaux votaries often made vows in time of great stress such as
     when a family member was very ill or when an individual confronted
                                           Centring Indigenous Knowledges    79


immediate danger” (Brown, 1996, p. 44). Often, they vowed to be-
come stall dancers. The lodge is constructed in a specific manner, and
there is a specific protocol around the dance, dress, and ritual practice
of the Rain Dance. The Saulteaux and Cree of the Qu’Appelle Val-
ley share similar rituals in carrying out the Rain Dance. Brown (1996)
documents Tommy Anequad’s explanation of the Rain Dance, which
points to the complexity of ritual and method within this ceremony:
“The drums, the whistles, the chanting, the sweet-grass i­ncense, fast-
ing, the Thunderbird’s nest, the ritual and ceremony are used to create
the proper atmosphere … to help the person under vow who partici-
pates … to attain cosmic consciousness” (p. 150).
    From teachings conveyed through oral tradition, these practices are
said to be timeless, and through the generations, the internal integrity
of the Rain Dance has remained. Through ritual and ceremony, indi-
viduals give of themselves for another, and in this sacrifice, the danc-
ers are able to make a connection with the spirit powers to receive
spirit blessings for loved ones on whose behalf they dance. During the
ceremony, a dancer may receive a vision or dream that offers guidance
or assistance. As Brown (1996) recounts, if someone is unsure about
a vision, he or she can offer tobacco to the Rain Dance sponsor or a
known sweat-lodge leader who assists in interpreting the meaning
of the vision. Not only are ceremonies sources of knowing, they also
sanction acts of great importance to the people.
    The pipe, the drum, the songs, and prayer are integral parts of
Nêhiyaw ceremony, and ways in which to honour the Creator and
seek blessing. Once an act is carried out with the sanction of the cer-
emonial pipe, it is considered sacred. In Saskatchewan, according to
Elders, the treaties are a sacred accord that was sanctioned by the
Nêhiyaw people through one of the most sacred of ceremonies, the
pipe ceremony. According to Plains Cree Elder George Rider of Carry
the Kettle First Nation, Treaty Four tells us that “the pipe is holy and
it’s a way of life for Indian people … The treaty was made with a pipe
and that is sacred, that is never to be broken … never to be put away”
(as quoted in Cardinal & Hildebrandt, 2000, p. 28). Sacred knowledge
is dismissed in Western research, other than in an anecdotal, anthro-
pological, exotic kind of way. This can create a difficulty for the Indig-
enous researcher, for if one chooses to embrace Nêhiyaw knowledges
one must honour the whole of it.
80   The Four Foundations


          Just as language is integral to a broader Indigenous epistemology
     (as mentioned earlier in this chapter), language is integral to Nêhiyaw
     knowledges. There are six different dialects of the Nêhiyaw language:
     (a) East Cree (Montagnais and Naskapi); (b) Attikamek Cree (R dia-
     lect), which is also spoken in Québec; (c) Moose Cree (or L dialect),
     found in Ontario; (d) Swampy Cree (N dialect), found in Ontario and
     Manitoba; (e) Woods Cree (or TH dialect), spoken in Manitoba and in
     north central Saskatchewan; and (f) the Plains Cree (Y dialect), found
     in south and central Saskatchewan and throughout central ­Alberta.
     There are additional Cree languages and communities found in
     ­British Columbia, Northwest Territories, and Montana (Wolvengrey,
      2001). I am of the Y dialect or Nêhiyawewin.
          When taking a Nêhiyawewin language course, I was intrigued to
      learn how the language constructs fit with my understandings of a
      Nêhiyaw worldview. It reinforced for me why language is so impor-
      tant when considering a Nêhiyaw epistemology. The linguistic para-
      digms that we studied included the imperative, delayed imperative,
      the indicative, and the subjunctive. The subjunctive paradigm makes
      use of an auxiliary verb with an “ing” gerund. If I were writing, “I am
      sleeping” in Cree, it would look like this: ê-nipayân (Ahenakew, 1987).
          Not being fluent myself, I am told that fluent Nêhiyaw speakers
      most often speak in the subjunctive, or “ing,” mode. The subjunctive
      is the opposite of declarative and suggests a worldview that honours
      the present, what we know now. It gives us insight into the concept of
      time. It also suggests a worldview that focuses as much, if not more,
      attention on process than on product or outcome. Cree Elder Joseph E.
      Couture explains this concept: “Everybody has a song to sing which
      is no song at all; it is a process of singing, and when you sing you are
      where you are” (as quoted in Friesen, 1998, p. 28).
          When Nêhiyaw and Nahkawininiwak (Saulteaux) Elders talk
      about the world as being animate, as of spirit, it makes sense because
      this is reinforced on a daily basis in the language. Animals, tobacco,
      trees, rocks are animate, and hence they merit respect. Learning about
      the structure of Nêhiyawewin gives me a sense of the way that ­fluent
      Nêhiyaw speakers would have related with their world. Although
      one may not be a fluent Nêhiyaw speaker, having an understanding
      of how language influences Nêhiyaw knowledge is a key aspect of a
      research framework based on Nêhiyaw epistemology.
                                                    Centring Indigenous Knowledges         81


    Nêhiyaw ways of knowing emphasize the importance of respect,
reciprocity, relation, protocol, holistic knowing, relevancy, story, in-
terpretative meaning, and the experiential nested in place, land, and
kinship systems – all of which ought to be in a research process that
encompasses this way of knowing. My ancestors were highly strategic
peoples in both the practical aspects of life as well as within ceremo-
nies and rituals contextualized in place and manifested in ways of
knowing. Nêhiyaw knowledges are bound with and exist within a
­relational universe, and research choices flowing from this position-
 ing must be congruent with these foundational, holistic beliefs.
    The proposition of integrating spiritual knowings and processes, like
 ceremonies, dreams, or synchronicities, which act as portals for gaining
 knowledge, makes members of mainstream academia uncomfortable,
 especially when brought into the discussion of research. This is because
 of the outward knowing versus inward knowing dichotomy – and the
 spirit-science split. It has much to do with Western science’s uneasy
 relationship with the metaphysical. The Elders knew about inward
 knowing and valued it highly (they still do). In fact, this inward know-
 ing was a central, integral component of how they approached the
 buffalo hunt and their most deeply sacred ceremonies. They were able
 to share teachings through stories about their experiences, passed on
 using the oral tradition, and this knowing was respected as legitimate.
 The Nêhiyaw ancestors knew that all ways of knowing (empirical, ex-
 plorational, abductive) are needed from all cultures (e.g., Indigenous,
 Western, Eastern). It is about needing what when and where. Why
 would research based on Indigenous epistemologies be different?



A CONVERSATION WITH MICHAEL HART

To complete this chapter, Nêhiyaw (Cree) scholar Michael Hart shares
his reflections on Cree knowledge.1 As mentioned, Michael Hart is
Nêhiyaw from the Fisher River Cree Nation in Manitoba. He has a
doctoral degree in social work. I have been familiar with Michael’s


1 This excerpt from my interview with Michael Hart (2006) is taken from Kovach (2009).
  The interview itself was conducted for my dissertation published in 2006. The institu-
  tional affiliation of the interviewees for my dissertation reflects that time period.
82   The Four Foundations


     work for several years. As a post-secondary instructor and curriculum
     developer, I have used Michael’s book, Seeking Mino-Pimatisiwin: An
     Aboriginal Approach to Helping (2002), often. It has been useful in illus-
     trating an Indigenous cultural approach to contemporary social work
     practice. I am scheduled to meet Michael at his home in Winnipeg in
     the afternoon, so I hit the Trans-Canada Highway heading east from
     Regina in the early hours of the morning. By the time I get to Michael’s
     place, I am a little tired, frazzled, and nervous, but I immediately feel
     comfortable. He offers me coffee and food. We sit at his kitchen table
     and start to talk about what is becoming a very familiar topic to me –
     Indigenous research methodologies.

       MAGGIE: What do you think are some of the challenges that we are
            having in trying to explain our methodologies to academia?
       MICHAEL: I would step back even further. I would go back to looking
            at – again it’s a Western concept – worldviews. The challenge with
            bringing out worldviews is language, overall. There are concepts
            that we have in Cree that don’t have English translations. Right off
            the bat we are going to lose some of the meanings, and we are also
            going to change some of the meanings. At the same time, we need to
            do that because we don’t have the choice at this point. I have been
            trying to learn Cree for a long time, but I have a long, long way to go.
               I was just remembering something … about protocol. The way
            I’ve been dealing with things – let’s say I will come to a ceremony,
            come to understand protocols, but it’s not the ceremony that gets
            transferred, it’s the underlying meanings. What I am doing now is
            looking at the different things that I have been through with Elders,
            with traditional teachers, and try to understand the underlying
            teaching, what values are being demonstrated. What am I supposed
            to do, and how am I supposed to act? I will try to transfer those
            pieces into the new context, which to me reflects what we have to
            do in life. What we have to do right now in terms of decolonizing –
            I wish there was [another] term because decolonization focuses on
            colonization. My intent is to focus on our own ways. What ends up
            coming to me again is that it goes back to language and place.
               I am Indigenous, I speak English, and that’s where I come from.
            I am trying to understand that perspective because it reflects my
            reality. My mother was fluent in Cree. I have listened to her growing
                                             Centring Indigenous Knowledges       83


    up, speaking Cree, but when she spoke to us, she spoke English.
    My Mom said that we didn’t want to learn, so there is always that
    piece. It doesn’t mean that I am not Cree, but I have a different
    understanding than a Cree speaker. The journey for the fluent Cree
    speaker or the Saulteaux speaker isn’t the same journey that you or
    I would take. The journey you and I would take wouldn’t necessarily
    be the same, but they are all part of being Cree. If we deny that, then
    we have to deny ourselves, and my understanding about our peoples
    is that we don’t do that. We are inclusive, we bring people in. They
    may come from a different place, have a different journey, but they
    are still part of us, and they are still brought in. It’s only when it’s to
    the detriment of the group.
       For me and how I value language, it can be hurtful not to speak
    the language in terms of the peoples. If it gets to the point, if there
    is not enough [Cree] speaking, then we [have] lost that aspect for
    the future, not just within us, but as peoples. I know it needs to be
    retained.
MAGGIE: How do Indigenous researchers approach the cultural aspects
    of Indigenous knowledges when making methodological choices?
    [We talk about dream knowledge.]
MICHAEL: In spending time with these Elders, they [may help you]
    come to understand a dream, but it’s knowledge when you put
    those dreams, or that dream, into the physical reality. I am trying
    to explain this without speaking of a particular dream … Let’s say
    I dream about a smoke lodge, a dream about a particular aspect of a
    smoke lodge, the way you have to go to the smoke lodge. The dream
    in and of itself has informed me, but the knowledge process is just
    more than me having that dream. It is more than me taking that
    dream and talking with an Elder about it. It includes that process
    of doing whatever I have to do for that dream to become reality.
    The methodology isn’t just the dream, it isn’t just you sitting back
    and coming to understand the dream, but what you do with that
    dream, how you put it into reality. So for me, when I think about
    how I approach research, the issue of research methodology – I never
    thought of it as an issue but that’s an interesting point in and of
    itself – that’s how I understand methodology. When I talked about
    there being no single methodology, this is another example. I see
    people focusing just on the one, maybe on the dream. I had this
84   The Four Foundations


            dream and therefore I came to know, which I won’t dispute, but I
            think there is more to it.
               There’s a longer process that needs to be involved in bringing the
            dream to life. It’s already alive but bringing that dream into this world.
            So that whole piece – how you came back, how you ended up home –
            to me, that is speaking about the methodology. It would be like doing
            interviews and saying the interviews are the methodology. There is
            more, there is a whole bunch of other pieces that are tied to that.
               The dreaming would almost be part of the method. Methodology
            is bigger than that. So how do I approach it? Through a lot of
            reflection back on my time with Elders, with traditional teachers,
            in ceremony – those are my biggest influences. I do readings on
            other areas and talk with other people about their experiences, but
            I approach it more from there [Elders, ceremonies] because I want
            to try [to] understand it the best I can in a way that reflects how we
            do things. I say ceremonies because, to me, I don’t hunt, so I can’t
            rely on that process. A key piece for us that reflects our culture is
            the ceremonies. To me, they have probably been influenced the least
            [by other cultures] as opposed to other things.
               As an Indigenous researcher, how do you understand an
            Indigenous approach to research? Well, like I said, it depends upon
            which approach you take and which understanding you have. I think
            it takes a lot of self-reflection, not just self, but self in relation to the
            Elders, the ceremonies, your academic life. I mean, when we go out
            fasting, we are opening ourselves and inviting the spirits to be with
            us. We are reflecting on ourselves as well, so, to me, both of those
            aspects would be present in Cree ways of approaching research.



     POSTSCRIPT: A WRITTEN CORRESPONDENCE FROM
     MICHAEL HART AFTER THE INTERVIEW

     What follows is an email that Michael sent me after the interview, and
     its inclusion, with his permission, is an important continuation of our
     conversation.

          I know there is much more to conducting Indigenous research,
          or more specifically Cree research since I am writing as a Cree
                                         Centring Indigenous Knowledges   85


man who has and is learning from Cree Elders across Alberta,
Saskatchewan and Manitoba. We have several tools to help us
enter a place, a sacred, beyond physical place (I am realizing
place does not capture what I want to say, since place is too often
limited to physicality or the more post-modern/post-structural
conceptualization of location) and these tools are aspects of our
means to access knowledge. I guess even before we get to that
we need to consider “what is knowledge?” For me, knowledge
is that which helps people move forward in their lives. It may
help one person, or it may help many. I guess that is one of the
differences I see in my understanding of Indigenous knowledge
and Amer-European knowledge, particularly Amer-European
knowledge based in positivistic empiricism: That knowledge can
be applicable to one person. However, it [knowledge] is beyond
one person in that it is between that person and the sacred world.
Anyway, back to my commentary.
   It seems to me that tools are significant. These tools include
our pipes, our songs, our rattles, and our sacred items that we
care about, including plant and animal medicines. These items
are catalysts in our processes. While by themselves, they may
mean very little. But these items have arisen through at least one
of several processes. These processes include dreams of the items
before they arrive, the interpretation of the dreams of these items,
the acceptance of these items as catalysts, and the passing of these
items from one person to another. As I was reading your proposal
and thinking on Indigenous ways of coming to know and what is
it that we know, I was listening to some stickgame songs. While
these songs are not on the [same] level (which suggests a whole
other discussion since level implies a very significant considera-
tion) as sundance, smoke lodge, chicken dance, or sweat songs,
they did remind me that part of our processes, hence method-
ologies, including a reliance on such catalysts (I do not know
what words to use to express my meaning other than catalysts).
They are physical manifestations of sacred experiences. So when
I have prepared for my research for my Ph.D., my methodology
­includes the use of these items, particularly a pipe and songs.
I have also partaken in other activities to seek guidance, specif-
ically ceremonies. Finally, I will continue to rely on these sacred
86   The Four Foundations


          items for support as I complete my degree. Hence, our method-
          ologies are bigger than we can easily explain. I think your task is
          an honourable, but large one as it is bigger than we can imagine.
          I should speak for myself: Bigger than I can imagine.


        As a listener, I was able to take teachings from Michael Hart’s
     words. He reaffirmed Indigenous methodologies. In doing so, he
     stressed that Indigenous methodologies are anchored in Indigenous
     epistemologies and that though we, as Indigenous peoples, may share
     methods, it is the distinctive cultural epistemology (e.g., Nêhiyaw,
     Coast Salish, and so forth) that provides the specific underpinning for
     the Indigenous methodology being applied. Michael Hart proposed
     that epistemology is found in language. He talked about the relation-
     ship between language, place, land, and people.
        As Michael Hart spoke, I reflected on my desire to learn the Nêhi-
     yaw language while living on Vancouver Island and the motivational
     challenges that arose for me. It was not that I had any philosophical
     problems with the notion of learning Cree on Coast Salish territory.
     Instead, I just could not get traction – I am not sure why, but maybe
     this helps to explain. When I returned to Saskatchewan and began
     Nêhiyawiwin instruction, one of the first phrases my classmates
     and I learned was to introduce ourselves. It goes like this: Tânisi,
     Maggie, n  ­itisiyihkâson, Kovach, nitaspiyikasôn, Pasqua iskonikanik
     nipê-ohcin. The iskonikanik, identifying the community of belonging,
     loosely translated means little piece of land that the white settlers
     didn’t want. This one word is so full with connection to Plains In-
     digenous people, speaking of their relationships with white settlers,
     treaty lands, farmland, and buffalos – it is a single word loaded with
     the historical context of my homeland. My conversation with Mi-
     chael Hart helped me understand why learning this word from a
     Cree instructor amid other students, who were Cree, Saulteaux, and
     non-Indigenous S    ­ askatchewanians, fit. His thoughts on the connec-
     tion between place, language, and relationships as the basis of In-
     digenous knowing resonated with me. Each nation has its iskonikanik
     that tells a big story in a little word. I believe that is what Michael
     Hart was saying.
        Michael Hart urged care in sharing knowledges coming from the
     sacred, especially in settings such as universities where this form of
                                             Centring Indigenous Knowledges     87


knowledge risks diminishment. This is an important caution, and it
left me questioning how much to share without sharing too much.
Indigenous researchers have guardianship responsibility for the re-
search flowing from their ancestral epistemology. This remains an
ethical consideration of Indigenous research. Ceremonies, protocols,
and ways of Nêhiyaw people cannot be separated from their under-
lying relational values. Being kind, being inclusive, being community
minded in combination with ceremonies, protocols, and cultural ways
constitute the power of Indigenous culture.
     Indigenous epistemologies are imbued with complexity; however,
Indigenous researchers are finding ways to integrate Indigenous epis-
temologies within their conceptual frameworks. In doing so, they
are asserting the expectation that research relations involving the
­Indigenous community will incorporate accountability, responsibility,
 ­respect, compassion, and trust.

CHAPTER FOUR

 Indigenous Ethics and Axiology: Miýo
 (A Good Way)




      The combination of reciprocity and relationships within Kirkness and
      Barnhardt’s (1991) original 4Rs brings emphasizes that the relation-
      ships between researcher and rightsholders is just as important as the
      research itself.
                                                             Pidgeon (2019)



 “Expert continued to use native blood without consent. Blood taken
 from a Vancouver Island native group and used for unauthorized
 genetic studies in the U.S. and England has been returned to B.C.”
 (Munro, 2005, p. A18). This quotation was taken from a Vancouver
 newspaper article in The Province that appeared in January 2005.
 The article reported on a 1986 research project that studied the ge-
 netic incidence of arthritis. The research was carried out through the
 University of British Columbia and funded by Health Canada. Eight
 hundred and eighty-two Nuu-chah-nulth individuals donated blood
 for the project. The project reported inconclusive findings. When the
 researcher left the University of British Columbia, he took the blood
 samples with him without informing the Nuu-chah-nulth community.
 This well-known case is one amid too many others.
    Ethical infringements in research impacting Indigenous communi-
 ties are not hard to find. From 1942 to 1954, nutrition experiments,
 in cooperation with the Canadian government, were conducted in
90   The Four Foundations


     Canadian Aboriginal communities in northern Manitoba and in six
     Indian residential schools. These studies included controlled exper-
     iments with the goal of assessing whether nutritional interventions
     would offset diseases such as tuberculosis, a disease which was
     ­increasingly afflicting Aboriginal communities at the time. “To test
      this hypothesis fully, they [researchers] proposed that an intensive
      one-to-two-year study be conducted ‘on a limited number of Indians’
      to demonstrate the effects of nutritional interventions into the diet of
      an already malnourished population” (Mosby, 2013, p. 147). These
      dehumanizing experiments were exploiting an already ­malnourished
      community, and among these nutritional studies, Mosby states that
      there were “two separate long-term studies that went so far as to
      ­include controlled experiments conducted, apparently, without the
       subjects’ informed consent or knowledge” (p. 148).
           Beyond Canadian borders, the late twentieth century saw the re-
       currence of ethical violations in pharma research. Brazilian-based eth-
       nopharmacologist Elaine Elisabetsky (1991) points to the practice of
       extracting Indigenous plant knowledge from the Amazon to develop
       pharmaceutical products. She notes that this was typically done with-
       out benefit going to the local Indigenous community and typifies the
       scientific imperialism of Indigenous resource extraction for profit.
       Elisabetsky (1991) states that trust between researcher and community
       ought to be established during the research, so “it is only fair to expect
       that this trust is extended after research results are collected, especially
       if results should eventually present commercial value” (p. 236).
           In health research, there have been ethical infringements that have
       been redressed only in the past decade. In 2003, Havasupai Tribe mem-
       bers in Arizona “discovered that their DNA samples, collected between
       1990 and 1994 for genetic studies on type II diabetes by ­researchers at
       Arizona State University, had also been used without their knowledge
       or explicit approval for studies on schizophrenia, migration, and in-
       breeding” (Garrison & Cho, 2013, p. 55). The tribal members filed a
       lawsuit in 2004 over lack of informed consent and reached a settle-
       ment in 2010. The Havasupai members involved in the study received
       financial compensation, and the Havasupai Tribe r­eceived funds for
       a community clinic and school. The DNA samples were returned to
       the community (Garrison & Cho, 2013). ­Research on ­Indigenous peo-
       ples has been marked by dehumanizing methodologies, with ethical
                                            Indigenous Ethics and Axiology   91


infringement being a clear indicator of a deficit of respect towards In-
digenous peoples. Research has done much to sever trust.
   In 2005, when the Nuu-chah-nulth story broke, I was living on
Vancouver Island. I was jolted by this extractive and exploitive inci-
dence of Western research into Indigenous life so near to where I lived.
I was, however, not surprised. In reading this article and subsequent
newspaper items on researchers extracting biological samples from
the Nuu-chah-nulth and not returning them to the community, I was
struck by how this was a blatant example of the long history of re-
search on – not with – Indigenous communities. It became evident that
there was an urgent need to clarify overall ethical regulations concern-
ing the genetic and biological material gathered for research purposes.
I knew this extended to qualitative research – and to all research in all
disciplines. I was deeply affected by the Nuu-chah-nulth experience
because it represented another research betrayal of Indigenous people.
   An Indigenous ethical perspective is the focus of this chapter; it
begins by recognizing the backdrop of Indigenous research ethics pro-
tocols in Canada and beyond. The crux of the chapter, though, high-
lights an Indigenous ethical framing couched within trust and good
relations. We know much about Western research ethics; however, as
Bull (2010) notes, it is necessary to consider ethics from both a West-
ern and an Indigenous worldview. An Indigenous perspective encom-
passes a collectivist, big “E” ethical framework. A holistic, place-based
approach to ethics is a necessity within Indigenous methodologies.
This is articulated by Kathy Absolon, Anishinaabe scholar from the
Flying Post First Nation, in my conversation with her that concludes
this chapter. Kathy stresses the big “E” (ethics) and big “R” (reciproc-
ity) in an Indigenous ethical framework.
   The landscape of Indigenous research ethics outlined in this chap-
ter is painted with a broad brush. I acknowledge that ethical research
within Indigenous communities necessitates a finer bristle to attend
to relational nuances. As such, the next chapter, Chapter 5, is meant
to be read with this chapter. Chapter 4 is a panorama of the Indige-
nous research ethics while Chapter 5 moves into the nuances of ethical
tensions that arise when differing cultural communities converge. It
expands upon Chapter 4 to discuss research ethics specific to commu-
nity engagement. I struggled with whether to merge these two chap-
ters; however, I chose not to go that route. Community engagement,
92   The Four Foundations


     given its centrality to Indigenous methodologies and ethical research,
     requires its own chapter.



     AN AXIOLOGICAL PREMISE

     The term axiology is less frequently used than the term ethics in this
     chapter although this chapter, the whole of it, is about axiology. Axiol-
     ogy is a term that signals to the researcher to pause, take a breath, and
     consider questions such as these: What knowledge is of value? What
     knowledge is worthwhile to study? Axiology is associated with value
     theory, which is a moral philosophy and encompasses “social and po-
     litical philosophy, aesthetics, and sometimes feminist philosophy and
     the philosophy of religion … In this narrow sense, ‘value theory’ and
     ‘axiology’ are related. Axiology can be thought of as primarily con-
     cerned with classifying what things are good, and how good they are”
     (Schroeder, 2016, para. 1). Newhouse (2008) articulates an axiological
     premise from a Haudenosaunee perspective, defining it as a “good
     mind”:

          What does it mean to be of good mind? A good mind is balanced
          of reason and passion, ever negotiating the dance that the two
          undertake. A good mind is ever thinking of how to foster peace
          between peoples, the world, and all its inhabitants. (p. 188)


     Axiology is about ethics. Nêhiyaw scholar Shawn Wilson (2008)
     states, “Axiology is thus asking, ‘What part of this reality is worth
     finding out more about’ and ‘What is it ethical to do in order to gain
     this knowledge, and what will this knowledge be used for?’” (p. 34).
     This applies to our relationship with other people along with our rela-
     tionship to the natural environment. Axiology and epistemology are
     interwoven in Indigenous methodologies and can be revealed through
     two ­questions: “What knowledge is being called upon?” (epistemol-
     ogy) and “What is the plan for accessing that knowledge?” (axiology).
     There is no way around it; the ethics of research lands us squarely in
     the zone of axiology.
        When defining Indigenous ethics in research, we must consider the
     axiological premise arising from Indigenous culture. The Canadian
                                                     Indigenous Ethics and Axiology     93


national funding body for research in the social sciences and humani-
ties, the Social Sciences and Humanities Research Council of Canada,
has defined Indigenous research as respectful research and explicitly
states that any researcher conducting Indigenous research, under its
auspices, must “commit to respectful relationships with all Indige-
nous peoples and communities” (SSHRC, 2020, para. 29). Further,
all SSHRC-funded research involving Indigenous peoples must be
compliant with the C  ­ anadian Tri-Council Policy Statement: Ethical Con-
duct for Research Involving Humans (TCPS2).1 TCPS2 is the research
ethics policy of C ­ anada’s three federal research funding agencies,
known as the Tri-Council, which includes the SSHRC. The council’s
policy statement on ethics was launched in 2010 (known as TCPS)
with a second and currently active edition (TCPS2) released in 2018.
One might assume that if research is funded by SSHRC and vetted
through a university TCPS2-compliant ethical review, all axiological
considerations are met for Indigenous research. This is where there
needs to be a pause. TCPS2 is a valuable policy with Indigenous in-
put, but it is a Western ethics policy construct. It may or may not meet
the big “E” axiological criterion of an Indigenous ethical framework
from a local Indigenous community level. Further, as Morton Ninom-
iya and Pollock (2017) state in referencing university-driven research,
“[T]here is no consistent ­accountability structure built into research
ethics boards” (p. 32).



A CANADIAN BACKDROP: INDIGENOUS RESEARCH
ETHICS GUIDELINES AND PROTOCOLS

Attention to axiological considerations and research ethics protocols
in conducting research with Indigenous peoples has flourished since
the first publication of this book in 2009. I will not attempt to ­provide
a listing of all recent statements and resources available to assist
­researchers in the ethical engagement of research within ­Indigenous

  ­Research Council (SSHRC).

     communities. However, I wish to make an illustrative, if not exhaus-
     tive, listing of several community-initiated ethical guidelines and pro-
     tocols that have arisen internationally since 2009:

     • 2010: The Te Ara Tika Guidelines for Māori Research Ethics: A ­Framework
