        In Canada, the following are examples of ethical guidelines for con-
     ducting research with Indigenous communities. Not all are currently
     active, but all have been influential.

     • 1996: The Royal Commission on Aboriginal Peoples produced
       a document entitled “Ethical Guidelines for Research” as an
       ­appendix to volume five of its report (RCAP, 1996b).
     • 1999: The Mi’kmaq Ethics Watch (1999) produced a document out-
        lining principles and protocols for conducting research with and/
        or among Mi’kmaq people.
                                                  Indigenous Ethics and Axiology   95


• 2003: Local, collaborative protocols were developed, such as the
  Standard of Conduct for Research in Clayoquot and Northern Barkley
  Sound Communities (Clayoquot Alliance for Research, Education
  and Training, 2003).
• 2007: The Canadian Institutes of Health Research (CIHR, 2007) issued
  its initial Guidelines for Health Research Involving Aboriginal People.
• 2014: The First Nations Information Governance Centre (2021)
  filed trademark applications for OCAP (“Ownership, Control,
  Access, and Possession”) in 2014. Earlier, Schnarch (2004) detailed
  this well-known statement of the four principles that, if followed,
  can offset extractive research practices.
• 2018: The current ethical policy document guiding most research2
  in Canada is the Tri-Council Policy Statement: Ethical Conduct for
  Research Involving Humans (TCPS2). TCPS2, released in 2018,
  ­includes “Chapter Nine: Research Involving Aboriginal Peoples.”

   Released in 1996, the report of the Royal Commission on Aboriginal
Peoples (RCAP) is the most substantive study to date of conditions in
Indigenous communities. Researchers from across Canada were invited
to submit proposals to conduct research on various aspects of Indig-
enous experience. To ensure ethical conduct, RCAP published guide-
lines to which all research conducted under RCAP was subject. The
guidelines were applicable to both individual and community-based
research. In community research projects, researchers were required to
spell out how Indigenous communities would participate, how they
would provide consent, and how they would benefit from the research.
   The RCAP guidelines asked researchers to clarify that their re-
search and subsequent findings were, indeed, coming from an Indig-
enous perspective and following an Indigenous axiological premise.
Furthermore, researchers had to show how their research design in-
corporated local protocols on knowledge sharing and how findings
involving Indigenous knowledges would be validated. The RCAP
members monitored work to ensure that these guidelines were fol-
lowed (RCAP, 1993). This was the first Canadian government-funded
research initiative to develop ethical research guidelines specifically


2 All research that is funded by the Tri-Council (CIHR, NSERC, SSHRC) must be
  compliant with TCPS2.
96   The Four Foundations


      with the Indigenous community in mind. While the RCAP’s report
      did not generate a broad awareness among the larger Canadian
      research community at the time, it planted seeds in the collective
      ­Canadian consciousness. RCAP was a leader in Indigenous research
       ethics protocol development and provided a practical tool for con-
       ducting ethical research in Indigenous communities at the time
       (Brant-Castellano, 2004).
             RCAP propelled momentum on the Indigenous research eth-
       ics front in Canada. A significant document of mention, though no
       longer ­active, emerged in the mid-2000s from the Canadian Institutes
       of Health Research, entitled the CIHR Guidelines for Health Research
       ­Involving Aboriginal Peoples (CIHR, 2007). This guideline document
        meant that the CIHR became the first mainstream Canadian re-
        search funding agency to develop ethical guidelines in Indigenous
        research. The guidelines spoke to the protection of Indigenous knowl-
        edges, community control, benefit sharing, recognition of cultural
        ­protocols, and the involvement of Indigenous peoples in interpret-
         ing ­research findings (CIHR, 2007). The CIHR Guidelines for Health
         ­Research ­Involving Aboriginal Peoples took several years to develop and
          was finalized in 2007. But the document is no longer active: “Since
          2010, health ­research involving First Nations, Inuit and Metis peo-
          ple in Canada is governed by the provisions outlined in Chapter 9 of
          the Tri-Council Policy Statement: Ethical Conduct for Research Involving
     ­Humans (TCPS)” (CIHR, 2013, para. 1).
             These CIHR guidelines have been influential in the development of
      the Tri-Council Policy Statement: Ethical Conduct for Research Involving
      Humans, which emerged from a collaboration between the three na-
      tional research funding bodies in Canada, known as the Tri-Council.
      The Tri-Council includes the Canadian Institutes of Health Research
      (CIHR), the Natural Sciences and Engineering Research Council of
      Canada (NSERC), and the Social Sciences and Humanities Research
      Council of Canada (SSHRC). The first Tri-Council policy (TCPS) was
      adopted in 1998 and has since gone through subsequent editions. The
      most recent edition, released in 2018, is known as TCPS2. TCPS2 has
      a chapter (i.e., Chapter 9) devoted to Indigenous research. The proto-
      cols and principles outlined in this chapter have been developed in
      dialogue with Indigenous voices, though some have critiqued TCPS2
                                            Indigenous Ethics and Axiology   97


as having incomplete consultation. Stiegman and Castleden (2015,
p. 1) critically reflect on the updating process of TCPS: “Soon after, it
became clear that the TCPS[1] needed updating. As a result (though
some have criticized the process, arguing that insufficient commu-
nity engagement or researcher consultation took place), the TCPS2
included an entirely new chapter (Chapter 9).”
    Like the CIHR guidelines predating it, Chapter 9 of the TCPS2 is
to be read in the context of an Indigenous axiology (or big “E” e­ thics)
honouring qualities such as relationship (with people and place), rec-
iprocity, collectivism, and sacred knowledges. There is an expectation
that researchers who hold alternative worldviews will respect these
values. When used in conjunction with local protocols, these TCPS2
guidelines provide researchers with well-defined benchmarks for
ethical research within Indigenous contexts. A central focus of ethical
engagement in Indigenous research as outlined in TCPS2 is embed-
ded in the concept and practice of “community engagement.” Further,
“Ownership, Control, Access, and Possession” (OCAP) is referenced
in TCPS2. Both OCAP and TCPS2, as ethical protocols, are embed-
ded in Indigenous community engagement and have implications
for research funding. Because of the significance of OCAP within
Indigenous communities and the challenges of applying OCAP in
­post-secondary settings, an expanded discussion on the complexities
 of the practical application of OCAP occurs in Chapter 5.
    Guidelines for conducting ethical Indigenous research, be they in
 protocol form or as a statement of principles, are there to stave off
 objectionable research practices that might otherwise occur, such as
 those that run counter to Indigenous governance, consent, ownership,
 or use. Research protocols and cultural protocols work to strengthen
 the overall ethical foundation of an Indigenous research project. Any
 researcher wishing to carry out research with Indigenous commu-
 nities must possess an awareness of both research and cultural pro-
 tocols. (This also applies to those who evaluate Indigenous research
 such as academic faculty and publication and grant reviewers.) While
 most protocols are not specific to a particular research project, they
 will offer guidance on overarching ethical principles and assistance
 with how to assess, and respond to, the ethical implications of con-
 ducting research.
98   The Four Foundations


     INDIGENOUS ETHICAL FRAMEWORKS: THE BIG “E”
     ETHICS

     In Cree, the word miýo means “good, well, beautiful, valuable.” The
     word ethics is not differentiated from good relations (Wolvengrey,
     2001, vol. 1, p. 109). Indigenous-informed ethics on research find com-
     monalities of shared values of respect for relationship with the hu-
     man and natural world. They echo the 4Rs of research as identified
     by Kirkness and Barnhardt (1991) in their foundational work “First
     Nations and Higher Education: The Four R’s – Respect, Relevance,
     Reciprocity, Responsibility.” Pidgeon (2019), in her recent writing on
     Indigenous research methodologies, adds reverence as a fifth R. This is
     an important addition as it explicitly indicates a physical and meta-
     physical dimension that lives within the tangible and intangible eth-
     ical practices arising from Indigenous knowledges. Relational ethics,
     as defined within Indigenous communities, are more expansive than
     the liability focus that marks university ethics standards. At its crux,
     an Indigenous ethical framing starts with respecting Indigenous peo-
     ples, places, and knowledges, and so we must consider tâpwê or truth
     and trust in research from an ethics point of view.



     TÂPWÊ (TRUTH AND TRUST) FROM AN INDIGENOUS
     ETHICAL FRAMING

     Denigration of Indigenous knowledges often occurs when a Western-
     trained researcher poses questions concerning research legitimacy
     and validity. Too often, these questions call forth a Western pro forma
     response on research validity that dismisses Indigenous knowledges.
     Indigenous knowledges are holistic, contextual, allow for subjective
     interpretations, and consider sources that Western science may not
     acknowledge as scientifically legitimate or trustworthy. Betty Bastien
     (1999) states that Niitsitapi teachings speak about trust and validity
     of knowledge: “Grandfather is saying, the validity and ethics of the
     epistemologies and pedagogy of Niitsitapi knowing lives through the
     manner in which I live my life” (p. 97). From a Nêhiyaw perspective,
     Winona Stevenson (2000) states that truth (validity), or tâpwê, is bound
     with the integrity of the person sharing knowledge. Stevenson makes
                                            Indigenous Ethics and Axiology   99


the following suggestion to the Indigenous historian: “The tasks of
tribal historians are to recover the past and to present it to the public
in a form that meets the approval of the people whose histories and
lives it represents” (p. 298). Our stories, our research, are our tâpwê.
It is about standing behind one’s words and recognizing collective
protocol, about showing that one is accountable for one’s words. The
story is not separate from the teller or the audience. This is an ethi-
cal and axiological premise of Indigenous practice and often conflicts
with the objective, decontextualized notion of truth and trustworthi-
ness found within Western intellectual tradition.
   Betty Bastien (2005) states that Niitsitapi understandings of trust-
worthiness are meant to be comprehended in the (oral) language of
the oral tradition of each Indigenous culture and community. These
teachings say that trustworthiness of research is tied to the personal
credibility of a researcher who is accountable to community in a rele-
vant way. Trustworthiness, then, cannot be abstracted from methodol-
ogy, community, and personal ethical choices. When we consider the
subjective nature of trustworthiness within Indigenous research, it is
wise to note that the reader of Indigenous research may not have the
background to appreciate a holistic Indigenous knowledge perspec-
tive. Indigenous Elder and scholar Marlene Brant-Castellano (2004)
comments that Indigenous ethics can never be limited to a defined
set of rules. They are about knowing who you are and the values you
hold. They are about how one fits within a spiritual world. In a ­recent
interview, she reflected, “We need a critical mass of Aboriginal re-
searchers who are educated in the ethics of their own traditions and
also open to seeing the common ground that they share with other
traditions, including settler society” (Gentelet et al., 2018, p. 835).



GUARDIANSHIP OF KNOWLEDGES

Ethics, cultural tradition, and guardianship of knowledge go hand
in hand. Article 31 of the United Nations Declaration on the Rights
of I­ndigenous Peoples (United Nations, 2007) speaks specifically to
guardianship of Indigenous knowledges: “Indigenous peoples have
the right to maintain, control, protect and develop their cultural herit-
age, traditional knowledge and traditional cultural expressions” (p. 11).
100   The Four Foundations


      Article 31 exists because of the colonial oppression of Indigenous peo-
      ples and knowledges. Guardianship is challenging because, once In-
      digenous people agree to share Indigenous knowledges in the public
      realm, it is difficult to protect the knowledges from being misunder-
      stood and/or misused. Thus, ethics and guardianship of knowledge
      are intertwined, and we must pose the questions asked by Shawn Wil-
      son (2008, p. 34): “What is it ethical to do in order to gain this knowl-
      edge, and what will this knowledge be used for?”
            In her research, Betty Bastien (1999) received help from the
      ­Niitsitapi Elders to determine which cultural knowledges she could
       share. In my research, I did not want to exploit community or familial
       knowledge or have this knowledge subject to dismissal (or appropri-
       ation). I made the choice to reference only minimal knowledge from
       my own experience when writing specifically about Nêhiyaw sacred
       knowledge. I relied upon sources by Elders and others already in
       the public realm. When I spoke with Indigenous researchers for my
       study, I was confident that they were aware of the risks associated
       with bringing cultural knowledges into Western research projects and
       would monitor their contributions. Thus, this group’s astute apprecia-
       tion for guardianship of Indigenous knowledges situated them as less
       vulnerable research participants.
            Michael Hart (2006) urges care in sharing sacred ways of ­knowing,
       like dreams, when their appropriateness in research might be ques-
       tioned by Western-trained academics. When one conducts ­Indigenous
       research, Elders, tribal ethics boards, community relationships,
       ­research advisory boards, local protocols, and Indigenous scholars
        can be particularly helpful in determining which cultural knowledges
        to share. Determining how much cultural knowledge to share ­requires
        an astute assessment given that for some Indigenous researchers there
        is an ethical imperative to share knowledge. Betty Bastien (2005),
        ­Niitsitapi scholar, notes that she was ethically bound to use any cul-
         tural information if it was to be used in a helpful way.
            In actualizing a researching life that is aligned with the spirit of
         Indigenous relational ethics, one must take as an overarching theme
         conducting oneself in a way that reflects miýo. Being on the front line
         of knowledge creation, individual researchers who learn and commit
         to ethical protocols for research within Indigenous communities are in
         a privileged position to not only create change from the bottom up but
                                                       Indigenous Ethics and Axiology        101


also further broaden their understandings of ethical relationships on a
deeply personal level. To be relationally accountable is to ­continually
ask of one’s self, as a researcher, questions that are about trust and re-
spect and the axiological premise of the culture where one is ­conducting
research. Has my research hindered or helped to e­ ngender a trusting
relationship with the Indigenous peoples i­nvolved? Does the research
assist the community? Do the Indigenous research participants and
stakeholders feel respected? Can I, as a researcher, sleep at night?



A CONVERSATION WITH KATHY ABSOLON

The first step in contemplating an Indigenous ethical framing is un-
derstanding its unique relational ethos. Kathy Absolon3 offers com-
ments on the expansive relationality of Indigenous experiences and
the ethics associated with a relational worldview that values reciproc-
ity and mutuality at its core. Kathy Absolon is Anishinaabe from the
Flying Post First Nation. She is first degree Midewiwin of the Three
Fires ­Society. She completed her doctorate through the Department
of Adult Education, Community Development and Counselling
­Psychology, at the Ontario Institute for Studies in Education (OISE),
 University of Toronto. When I talked with Kathy, I was feeling alone
 with a lot of uncertainties. Having a talk with a friend about a subject
 that is near to our hearts was the boost that I needed.

   MAGGIE: What prompted you to enter into PhD studies? What was it that
        made you decide that it was time to go back?
   KATHY: For me, I don’t think I can say that it was one thing. It was a
        number of things. I am still not even sure if this is a useful thing
        to do as an Aboriginal person, as an Anishinaabe. I am not sure if
        it’s contributing to the community or our overall healing, recovery,
        and self-determination. But I guess it’s simply that I’ve been an
        educator. My community said it was okay, and it was timely in that



3 This excerpt from my interview with Kathy Absolon (2006) is taken from Kovach
  (2009). The interview itself was conducted for my dissertation published in 2006.
  The institutional affiliation of the interviewees for my dissertation reflects that time
  period.
102   The Four Foundations


             I was feeling I needed to change my job. It was all winding up to
             say this is the doorway. I don’t completely understand why we go
             through those doorways or why the universe or the spirits present
             those doorways, but I think not all of us are medicine people, not
             all of us conduct sweats, Rain Dances, or Sun Dances. Some of us
             are educators, some of us healers, some of us are a little bit of this
             and a little bit of that. I think I can accept that I am an educator. At a
             structural level, one of the roles of the educator is to bring validation
             and help widen the path for other Aboriginal people to be okay
             with who they are. To know that if you choose the path of education
             you can still come in and be who you are. This one student, her
             methodology is water walks across the [Great] Lakes, her reflection
             on her water walk, and the understanding and insights that come
             out of that. If doing this PhD gives me the power to say to somebody,
             “It’s okay for you to do your research that way,” then that’s an
             important reason to do it.
                A big part of it is a lifelong journey, and I know that when I am
             talking about my research it has to feel like it’s meaningful. It means
             something to me that I know how to sing the songs in my language.
             When I go to ceremony and my Elders are saying the ceremonies in
             Anishinaabe, that I know what they are talking about. So, the PhD has
             to be meaningful; it has to help – that there is a purpose in terms of
             the bigger picture of who we are as Anishinaabe. When my daughter
             does the ceremonies, I tell her that she is bringing those lines back
             into the world. That’s more important to me than my PhD. So, yeah,
             it’s important, but there are other things that are more important, but
             it’s [the PhD] also the catalyst, the doorway. I believe that if I wasn’t
             meant to do my PhD, that doorway wouldn’t have been there, that
             path wouldn’t have been created. That’s what I believe.
                [In doing this PhD] my challenges have been around how
             does this learning, a PhD in the academy, support me to become
             more who I am. The answer is that it doesn’t. Where I have been
             supported – to be who I am, know what I know, and have that
             grow – is in our lodge. In our lodge, in our communities, I can talk
             the way I talk, and people get me and they understand where I am
             at. The other thing is that I get them. They teach me. Working at
             the community level has helped me know myself and to feel the
             strength, pride, and identity as an Anishinaabe person. The struggle,
                                            Indigenous Ethics and Axiology   103


if that’s what you need, is not in academia. You have to get that from
your family, your community, your own teachings, and your culture.
You have to go there …
   What I feel frustrated or constrained by [in] doing research in the
academy is that you are forced to begin from a colonized place, and
we are forced to begin from that place for two reasons. One is that we
are colonized – Indigenous people are colonized. The second reason
is that the academy reinforces that, and we are in that place. Yet,
within me, there is an Anishinaabe thinker, person, and my European
side. It’s not just Anishinaabe, it’s not just European. It’s about
what I am. It’s also what a lot of other Aboriginal people are, too.
We haven’t had an education system that has helped us to reconcile
those different parts within ourselves, so I begin with that kind of
frustration …
   My topic is around how do we search for knowledge as
Indigenous people. I believe that if we look at some of the
methodologies along the continuum that are less oppressive, they
might be supportive of Indigenous methodologies or they might
even be in alliance with Indigenous methodologies, but they are still
not Indigenous methodologies. They happen to come from their
paradigm and their reference point, and while they might fit, they are
not based in Indigenous thinking. They are not based in spirit and
where spirit comes from. A couple of people said that to me, and so
that’s been imprinted in my notions. More recently, a colleague who
has finished his PhD said, “Indigenous research is in the language.
All you have to do is learn language.” If we learn our language,
the methodology is in learning your language because then you’ll
understand what the Elders are talking about. The knowledge is in
the language.
   When I was writing up my methodology and thinking about
it, the first thing is that it has to be Indigenous, but that’s a
contradiction and kind of a state of anguish right off the bat. All
of the things I’ve learned about in academia don’t come from an
Indigenous paradigm. Even the idea of doing interviews, I was
writing about that and thought, “Well, we don’t do interviews in
Aboriginal culture. We have discussions and talks.” I talked to my
committee advisor and said, “I think I am going to reword it to
say this is what we are doing.” She said the committee might not
104   The Four Foundations


             get that, but I reworded it anyway. We don’t have focus groups,
             we have circles. I am trying to rethink the methodology, but that’s
             a real hard thing because none of us can say we know what is
             authentically Indigenous.
                We are all colonized. The people who do know are not in the
             academy … [T]he people who understand are the Aboriginal people
             who are poor, who are living in rez housing. If you want to know
             your culture, they will do the ceremony for you or they will help
             you understand the roles. If you need help, they will help you. They
             will teach you about friendship and community and about what it
             means – what those teachings really mean versus just rhetoric. I don’t
             know if I am articulating that clearly, but our teachers are not really
             in the academy. Our teachers are in community.
                I think of our methodologies. We have to be able to start from a
             slate that is not influenced by the other, so that it is okay to have your
             dreams be your methodology. If you go on a water walk or quest,
             that is your methodology. I was reflecting when you were talking
             about yours [your methodology]. If I said I am doing my PhD and
             my methodology is my dreams, and I am going to go on a fast every
             year, and after that fast I had somebody come and visit me and talk
             to me about my fast and take [teachings] with them. I wouldn’t
             propose that because I wouldn’t want that to [be] measured. I know
             that is Indigenous methodologies, but I wouldn’t propose it as a
             methodology within a mainstream setting because I don’t want them
             to have the power to say that that’s not research. But it is. What my
             friend was saying to me five years ago was true, that your research is
             through your dreams – that is where we go for our answers, and that
             is where we go for our knowledge.
                What I have done with my methodology – I know it’s a long-
             winded answer – is that my methodology has become a combination
             of looking at what other Indigenous researchers have used in their
             process, knowing that they are only speaking about a small piece.
             It’s like an iceberg. Secondly, doing what you are doing, talking to
             other Indigenous researchers about their methodologies and then
             trying to create. The third is creating a learning circle and facilitating
             Indigenous scholars to come together, to talk about what we are
             talking about right now and what they consider to be Indigenous
             methodologies. The big dilemma and struggle is doing that in a
                                          Indigenous Ethics and Axiology   105


Western methodological research context, trying to grow something
Indigenous there. Out of a box, you’re morphing a circle and there is
something kind of wacky about that, but there is something kind of
challenging about that, too [laughter].
   I think our methodology is process. I don’t know what kind
of boundaries I am going to be pushing at the university, but you
know that you will be pushing boundaries. My ultimate goal is that
I want to privilege an Anishinaabe worldview and Anishinaabe
methodology. I am here to uphold and uplift Indigenous
methodologies, not for the world to see but for my children and their
children and other Anishinaabe children. To say to them that they
[Indigenous methodologies] exist, and they are beautiful and that
they are amazing ways of growing and learning and being.
   I often question, “Should we be doing these methodologies?”
Even this conversation is making me think, “Maybe I should scrap
my methodology.” Maybe I should just be saying, “I’m doing my
methodology, using my dreams, and I am going to do what I already
believe is Indigenous.” Why do I need to go and talk to people about
it when I already believe it? If you experience a fast, I know what
that is like, and so I know what comes for you. It’s different for
everyone. I know that experience is sacred. What do you call it, like
in sci-fi movies, a portal? I know that there are portals, and when you
go into a sweat-lodge there are portals. When you go into different
ceremonies, there are portals for knowledge to come through. You
take that knowledge and you go into a ceremony and tell people
what you saw and what you experienced. That is research. That is
bringing in knowledge …
   It’s what we will hear people saying over and over again, in my
research [and] in your research, we will hear people say that our
methodologies exist in our dreams, in our fast. They will say that
we traditionally knew about the portal, the doorway, how to get
knowledge, and that it was brought to the people by sharing, by
community forums, by sitting in circles, by engaging in ceremony, by
honouring your relationship to the spirit. When we do that, the spirit
will reciprocate, and we will be given what we are needing. The
universe will provide for us if we honour the great circle and cycle of
being, and that can only happen if we know how to do that. So why
am I not doing the methodology based on what I already know? …
106   The Four Foundations


             If I was at home in my own community, I would be bantering
             back and forth with people in my community and saying, “Well,
             Indigenous methodologies, what do you think I should be doing?”
             I would be doing more discussions. That is something that we
             talked about in terms of our own methodology, and we haven’t done
             very well to date. We do it when we are doing a conference, but on
             our own we should be doing more sitting and talking about our
             research. Like here, we are together doing our research; we need to
             be bouncing our ideas off each other because we are both in the same
             boat. We need to be talking about these things and creating a space
             for us to write, read, and get feedback from each other. I think part
             of it is that you get so used to working in isolation, you just don’t
             think to ask. We can talk about this and how we ought to do that,
             and I find I create things where the process seems like a lot of work.
             I know that with Indigenous methodologies or traditional practices,
             it is more work.


            Kathy Absolon’s perspective on Indigenous methodologies reflects
      miýo. As I listened and she spoke, I was learning how she was nest-
      ing her research practice within an Indigenous ethical framework of
      ­relationship, purpose, sacred knowledge, and giving back. ­Reciprocity
       was evident throughout, as she was steadfast in her assertion that
       her research must be meaningful to her family, her community, and
       ­Anishinaabe people. As a professor, she found purpose in supporting
        Indigenous students in their research and in assuring them that their
        cultural knowledges, be they received through dreamtime or water
        walks, were respected as legitimate sources of knowing. In this way,
        as an Indigenous professor, she is giving back to the larger Indige-
        nous community. Kathy spent time reflecting upon cultural knowl-
        edge sources. Cultural knowledges are powerful, and their sacredness
        must be respected. Although she was strong in Anishinaabe ways and
        teachings, carrying out an Indigenous methodological study within
        the Eurocentric contact zone of the academy was a struggle. She
        spoke of the ethical anguish of bringing cultural knowledges into our
        ­research while knowing they will be inevitably judged by those unfa-
         miliar with them.
            Ethical infringement in research occurs where power is not
         checked. Why this happens varies. A neo-liberal analysis suggests
                                              Indigenous Ethics and Axiology   107


ethical infringement thrives where dominant ideology and privilege
sustained by societal and institutional structures allows for an ethos
of take, take, take. The homogeneity of large research centres makes
these institutions fertile ground for a monocultural, exploitive indi-
vidualism. While unethical research practices in Indigenous com-
munities have been and are being addressed, we cannot yet situate
these as purely historical phenomena. In both principles and practice,
­Indigenous ethical research lives in relationship and requires vigi-
 lance and honesty, it requires truth for trust, and it requires a continu-
 ance that goes beyond attaching the label of Indigenous to research and
 calling it done. In many ways accepting ethical principles is largely
 straightforward in the abstractedness of the theoretical. The complex-
 ity arises in the research relationships that animate Indigenous com-
 munity engagement.

CHAPTER FIVE

 Engaging with the Indigenous
 Community in Research




      [T]he problems with research stem from who is in control – and
      thus what gets done and how it is done – and who knows about it.
      The question of whose interests are served is central. And of course,
      there is an unambiguous relationship between control and benefit.
      Ceal Tournier, former co-chairperson of the First Nations Information
      ­Governance Committee (FNIGC) put it this way: “He who controls the
      data, controls the gold.”
                                                           Schnarch (2004)



 When ethical principles animate ethical research practices and ac-
 countability mechanisms are in place, researchers stand on firm
 ground; researchers experience healthy, productive relationships
 with Indigenous communities. Theoretically, we, as researchers,
 ­understand the expectations of community accountability associated
  with ethical Indigenous research, yet we grapple with whether we
  have met this mark. Too often, relationships between researchers and
  ­Indigenous communities are not nourishing, and researchers are left
   to ponder, “What happened?” “Why is this so?”
      Researchers are required to treat research ethics with care because
   of liability implications, as should be the case. Yet research ethics
   ought to be about more than liability; they ought to be about rela-
   tionships. However, in research training, relationship-building skills
                       Engaging with the Indigenous Community in Research   109


are largely considered soft skills. This is indicated by the absence of
specific courses on research relationships alongside research method-
ology courses. Insufficient preparation of the differing facets of the
research relationship, including trust building and conflict resolution,
is problematic in sites of research where ethics and community rela-
tionships are integral, the one to the other. Too many researchers enter
into Indigenous community research without the relational skills for
an axiological, relational big “E” ethics that Indigenous research re-
quires. Given that ethics and community engagement share the same
bundle, Chapter 5 on community engagement is written to compli-
ment ­Chapter 4 on ethics. Chapter 4 offers an Indigenous ethical and
axiological framing for research as facilitated through an interview
with Kathy Absolon. Chapter 5 directs its attention to ­Indigenous
community relationships and explores the terminology associated
with community engagement, problematizes the notion of a homog-
enous Indigenous community, asserts the ethical principles of OCAP
(ownership, control, access, and possession) and the complexities
of implementing it, and contemplates human agency in the trust/­
distrust continuum that defines Indigenous community engagement.



INDIGENOUS COMMUNITY ENGAGEMENT

Indigenous research works best when the community asks for the
­research. However, often this is not the case, and research, itself,
 becomes a catalyst for reaching out and establishing relationships.
 Many graduate students wishing to conduct research with Indig-
 enous ­communities, and who do not have a pre-existing relation-
 ship with a community, ask, “Who is the Indigenous community for
 my research?” This is followed by, “How do I connect and build a
 relationship with the community?” Faculty, graduate students, and
 non-university ­researchers seeking partnership with a community
 contemplate, “How do I navigate community needs in light of colonial
 funding bodies and corporate research culture?” “What are the con-
 cessions?” “What are the non-negotiables?” “Are the ­non-negotiables
 the same for me and the community?” Indigenous community mem-
 bers question, “Can we trust researchers?” To answer these q
                                                            ­ uestions,
 we, as researchers, must be honest about our motivations and know
110   The Four Foundations


      the Indigenous community we are engaging with. As a starting place,
      researchers must accept that the Indigenous community is not mono-
      lithic. While there is a shared belief system among Indigenous ­peoples,
      Indigenous people live in diverse Indigenous communities with each
      community having its own identity and nuance.
          In Canada, the term community engagement in Indigenous research
      has become a nomenclature signalling an ethical research relationship
      with Indigenous peoples and has become formally linked with ethical
      conduct in Indigenous research. While the term community engagement
      is a common expression in Canada, it does not mean it is the best term.
      More aptly, it is a term that Smith (2006) might reference as a “strate-
      gic concession” (p. 161) for both Indigenous and settler groups. The
      term community engagement is written into Indigenous research plans
      and rolls easily off the tongue at planning meetings – then it rolls into
      town, and suddenly it’s real. The approach and practice of Indigenous
      community engagement ranges from the desire for genuine relational
      building to viewing community engagement as a thing to be checked
      off, as a box in a research ethics application, a needed step for the release
      of funds, or the requirement of a peer-reviewed publication. Too often,
      community engagement, as a term, signifies building relationship but
      downplays engagement stress arising from conflicting ethical ­tensions.
      We assume community engagement to be a soft rug. We are startled
      when threads break loose and poke our skin. Still, notwithstanding im-
      perfect language among imperfect people in an imperfect world, com-
      munity engagement is the heartbeat of Indigenous research.
          What follows, then, is a walkabout in the land of Indigenous com-
      munity engagement, one that lands us squarely in the politics of Indig-
      enous research. When human subject research and research funding
      are part of research, the political nature of research reveals itself in a
      way it does not in theoretical research. However, I stress that though
      this book and chapter focuses on human subject research, Indigenous
      research includes theory-driven intellectual work associated with sec-
      ondary research into scholarly and artistic work.
          Not all Indigenous research involves community-engaged primary
      data collection. Theoretical research, based upon secondary sources,
      is an example of Indigenous research that does not involve data col-
      lection and might not involve direct community partnerships. Indig-
      enous research is broad. It’s a big tent. This scope must be respected
                        Engaging with the Indigenous Community in Research   111


else we limit the possibilities of Indigenous research and scholar-
ship. However, whether researchers are engaged in research that is
data driven, theoretical, creative, applied, or a combination thereof,
an appreciative consciousness of Indigenous community is critical so
that we do not slip back to deficit theorizing about Indigenous peo-
ples. As researchers know, or ought to know, words matter. In this
spirit, it is useful to give attention to the terminology found within
Indigenous community-engaged research.



INDIGENOUS COMMUNITY ENGAGEMENT: TERMS AND
DEFINITIONS

 Defining terms associated with community engagement, terms such
 as Indigenous research, Indigenous peoples, and Indigenous community, is
risky business. Each term clarifies and complicates in its defining, as
researchers attempt to squeeze nuanced contextual ascriptions into
standardized descriptions. However, it is not possible to begin a dis-
cussion of community engagement in Indigenous research without
first defining and then troubling several of these terms. I note that
definitions work to objectify, thingify, and abstract concepts from
­relational contexts, and I post a “read with caution” sign as we travel
 through the precarious terrain of attempting to define experience.

Keeping a Cautious Eye on Definitions of Indigenous Research

I intentionally reference a Canadian ethical framework for research
known as the 2018 Tri-Council Policy Statement: Ethical Conduct for Re-
search Involving Humans (TCPS2; the first version of this policy, launched
in 2010, was known as TCPS). The Tri-Council is the largest research
funding body in Canada and includes the Canadian Social Sciences
and Humanities Research Council (SSHRC) referenced in this book. To
receive funding from the Tri-Council, all research grant applications
must comply with the TCPS2, as vetted through university ethics ap-
proval processes, hence the policy linkage between ethics and funding.
Because of its scope, TCPS2 significantly impacts research conducted
in Indigenous communities in Canada. Community engagement (i.e.,
not consultation or community consent) is the term used in TCPS2 and
112   The Four Foundations


      is defined in a specific way. Though the terminology the policy uses
      arises primarily from a Canadian context, I am hopeful that what fol-
      lows will resonate within an international context, even if language
      differs. (As best I can, I have tried to keep the acronyms to a minimum.)
         Indigenous research encompasses myriad research methodologies,
      arising from data-driven, applied, creative, and theoretical research
      associated with Indigenous peoples including Indigenous methodol-
      ogies that integrate Indigenous conceptual frameworks. (Indigenous
      research and Indigenous methodologies are not interchangeable terms.
      Indigenous methodologies are rooted in Indigenous systems of
      knowledges guided by an Indigenous conceptual framing). A formal
      definition of Indigenous research is put forward by the Social Sciences
      and Humanities Research Council (SSHRC), one of the Tri-Council
      funding bodies. It states,

           Research in any field or discipline that is conducted by, grounded
           in or engaged with First Nations, Inuit, Métis or other Indigenous
           nations, communities, societies or individuals, and their wis-
           dom, cultures, experiences or knowledge systems, as expressed
           in their dynamic forms, past and present. Indigenous research
           can embrace the intellectual, physical, emotional and/or spiritual
           dimensions of knowledge in creative and interconnected relation-
           ships with people, places and the natural environment.
              Whatever the methodologies or perspectives that apply in a
           given context, researchers who conduct Indigenous research,
           whether they are Indigenous or non-Indigenous themselves,
           commit to respectful relationships with all Indigenous peoples
           and communities. (SSHRC, 2020)


      The definition goes on to state that Indigenous research “reaffirms
      SSHRC’s support of research by and with” Indigenous peoples
      and communities, which emphasizes the Indigenous community’s
      strengths, values, and knowledge systems. The definition states that
      all research involving Indigenous peoples must be in accordance with
      Chapter 9 of the 2018 TCPS2 (SSHRC, 2020). It is a definition that casts
      a wide net.
          The SSHRC definition of Indigenous research, and those simi-
      lar, is a useful working definition. However, it can create confusion
                       Engaging with the Indigenous Community in Research   113


depending upon how it is interpreted. For example, this definition
allows for methodological latitude in Indigenous research. This is
important because there is a range of research questions pertinent to
Indigenous communities, and we need a range of methodologies to
explore those questions. However, some may interpret the SSHRC
definition of Indigenous research to allow for a methodological rela-
tivism, to say that any conceptual framing – Indigenous, Western, or
otherwise – can apply equally to all aspects of Indigenous experience,
including Indigenous knowledge systems. We must trouble this no-
tion. If the research question deals with Indigenous philosophy, we
must consider our methodological choices carefully. An Indigenous
methodology, because it is epistemologically constructed to do so, is
most able to respond to inquiries that have an Indigenous knowledge
focus. Methodology facilitates knowledge production, and, as re-
searchers, we must ask ourselves, “Do I know the scope and capabil-
ity of my methodology?” “Do I have clarity on my research purpose?”
“Am I confident that the chosen methodology or conceptual framing
will serve the research question posed? In other words, will my meth-
odology get me where I want to go?”
    In the case of inquiries that seek insights into an Indigenous phi-
losophy, not all methodologies work equally, even if they involve
Indigenous peoples and are termed Indigenous research. We must not
push aside Indigenous methodologies for established, favoured West-
ern methodologies because a definition of Indigenous research by a
mainstream funding body gives us that latitude. We must not totally
whitewash Indigenous research by pushing aside Indigenous meth-
odologies or calling research “Indigenous methodologies” when it
is, in actuality, Indigenous research employing a methodology from
Western intellectual tradition.
    Alongside the matter of methodology, Indigenous research de-
mands bicultural acuity on ethical codes and an awareness that ethi-
cal standards are cultural constructs. A grounding feature of SSHRC’s
definition of Indigenous research focuses on relationship, “research-
ers who conduct Indigenous research, whether they are Indigenous
or non-Indigenous themselves, commit to respectful relationships”
alongside a stated requirement that Indigenous research be congruent
with TCPS2, Chapter 9, in which community engagement is a foun-
dational guideline. As defined, Indigenous research subsequent to the
114   The Four Foundations


      2018 TCPS2 must be ethical research. This aspiration cannot be con-
      tested. However, this can lead to a generalization that all post-2018,
      Canadian-based funded research labelled Indigenous research with
      university ethical approval is now ethical research according to Indig-
      enous relational ethics. The big “E” Indigenous ethics is more expan-
      sive than the individual consent process tied to institutional liability
      that is foundational to university ethical guidelines. Infringement
      upon an Indigenous collectivist ethical code, or axiology, can occur
      even if formal university ethical procedures are observed.
         The jeopardy of defining Indigenous research too constrictively
      limits research possibilities. On the other hand, defining Indigenous
      research too loosely or perfunctorily is a slippery slope. What is clear
      is that defining Indigenous research requires the appreciation of In-
      digenous peoples as a distinct cultural group. The question then be-
      comes, “Who are Indigenous peoples?”

      Who Are Indigenous Peoples?

      Depending upon the jurisdiction and country, the term Indigenous
      peoples has differing nuances. Attempting to find a standardized defi-
      nition of Indigenous peoples, in the English language, is a complex
      endeavour. The United Nations, for example, has not adopted a for-
      mal definition of Indigenous peoples because of the multiplicity of
      experiences associated with Indigenous cultures and communities,
      experiences that directly shape Indigenous lives. The United Nations
      has, however, relied upon the definitional criteria associated with In-
      digenous peoples put forward by Special Rapporteur José Martinez
      Cobo of the Sub-Commission on Prevention of Discrimination and
      Protection of Minorities in the 1982 Study of the Problem of Discrimi-
      nation against Indigenous Populations. In this study, Cobo (1982) iden-
      tifies shared criteria of Indigenous peoples to include the occupation
      of ancestral lands; common ancestry with the original occupiers of
      those lands; culture in general, with specific manifestations depend-
      ing upon the community; Indigenous language; residence in certain
      parts of a country or in certain regions in the world; and other relevant
      factors. This definitional criterion suggests that Indigenous peoples
      are a community of individuals with shared cultural beliefs embed-
      ded within an ancestral connection to land.
                        Engaging with the Indigenous Community in Research   115


    For the purposes of research ethical guidelines, TCPS2 uses the
term Indigenous but situates it within a Canadian context. Its defini-
tion states, “Self-identification is a fundamental criterion for defining
Indigenous peoples.” The policy goes on to say that “The term ‘Indig-
enous’ does not reflect the distinctions among First Nations, Inuit and
Métis peoples, who have their own histories, cultures and languages,
so an attempt has been made to limit use of the term in this policy to in-
stances where a global term is appropriate” (CIHR et al., 2018, p. 110).
This definition clearly indicates that it is using the term Indigenous so
as to be as inclusive as possible and arguably to avoid the political and
cultural contestations that frame Indigenous identity in Canada.
    In Canada, the term Aboriginal is used to define Indigenous peo-
ples within the Constitution Act, 1982. The Constitution Act, through
Section 35, affirms Aboriginal rights but does not define them. The
term Indian status is a legal term associated with the Indian Act and
references individuals who hold Indian status. This means individ-
uals with Indian status are registered to a First Nation band (i.e.,
­reserve community). The terms Indian status and treaty status are not
 fully interchangeable. Treaty status means that a First Nation is a sig-
 natory of a treaty with the Canadian Crown and holds certain rights
 associated with that treaty. Not all First Nations communities have
 signed a treaty with the Canadian Crown, such as in the case of the
 Musqueam people for example. Musqueam territory is on unceded
 lands in the lower mainland of British Columbia. Musqueam is a
 First Nations community, and members of Musqueam First Nation
 are considered “Indian” for the purposes of the Indian Act but are
 not signatories to a treaty with the Canadian Crown. The term First
 Nation has replaced the colonial term Indian in common usage while
 the term First Nations community is now commonly used to replace
 the colonial term band or reserve. First Nations peoples will often de-
 fine themselves by their nation, such as Haida, Saulteaux, Mi’kmaq.
 Alongside First Nations people, Canadian Indigenous people include
 the Inuit, Métis, and urban Indigenous.
    As significant as the term Indigenous peoples, the term Indigenous
 community is equally weighty. Because of the collectivist nature of
 Indigenous culture, the term Indigenous peoples and Indigenous com-
 munity are defined in the context of each other. I argue that, within
 contemporary Indigenous research, either of two actions ought to be
116   The Four Foundations


      required – direct Indigenous community engagement or benefit to an
      Indigenous community in some way.

      Indigenous Community or Communities?

      Historically in Canada, the definition of Indigenous community has
      largely focused on First Nations, the Métis peoples, and the Inuit. ­Often
      the Indigenous community has been defined as a community of Indig-
      enous peoples who live in a specific geographic area (e.g., tribal lands,
      reservations) to deflect a pan-Indigenous approach. A more complex,
      nuanced understanding of the term Indigenous community has arisen
      as Indigenous peoples have urbanized and increasingly form com-
      munities both on and off tribal jurisdictions. In a response to outsider,
      extractive, unethical research practices, Indigenous peoples have
      ­advocated for ethical guidelines in research that impacts ­Indigenous
       communities wherever they exist. Through persistent a­ dvocacy, this
       has resulted in documents such as the TCPS2 (2018) clearly identify-
       ing community and community engagement as central to ethical r­ esearch
       with Indigenous peoples. “This policy [TCPS2] ­acknowledges the
       role of community in shaping the conduct of research that affects
       First ­Nations, Inuit, and Métis communities” (p. 109). As such, this
       ­policy puts forward an articulation of community for the purposes of
        ­research with Indigenous peoples that is helpful.
            Chapter 9 of TCPS2 outlines three different types of Indigenous
         community collectives: “territorial, organizational, and or a com-
         munity of interest” (CIHR et al., 2018, p. 109). While these differing
         definition of community arise from a Canadian context, they may be
         useful to countries outside of Canada:

      1 Territorial bodies are those that are associated with a jurisdictional
        governing structure such as First Nations (i.e., tribal) or Inuit com-
        munities. In the instance of a territorial community, the researcher
        “shall seek the engagement of leaders of the community” (CIHR
        et al., 2018, p. 115). Local authorities may require that the research
        goes through an approval process involving the community’s
        ­local governance process, such as a band council resolution.
      2 An organizational community has a formal organizational ­mandate
         and governance structure with designated leadership. An
                        Engaging with the Indigenous Community in Research   117


  Indigenous friendship centre or Métis association would be an
  example. Research involving an Indigenous organization would
  involve engagement with said organization.
3 An Indigenous community of interest is a third form of community
  defined in TCPS2. A community of interest is without a formal
  structure and is less straightforward to define. According to the
  TCPS2, a community of interest “may be formed by individu-
  als or organizations who come together for a common purpose
  or undertakings, such as a commitment to conserving a First
  ­Nations language. Communities of interest are informal commu-
   nities whose boundaries and leadership may be fluid and less
   ­well-defined” (CIHR et al., 2018, p. 109). An Indigenous research
    advisory group is useful when it comes to gaining advice on re-
    search within or defining a community of interest.

     In articulating a definition of community, the 2018 TCPS2 advi-
sory working group acknowledged the contextual nuances of Indig-
enous communities. For example, members noted that a geographic
community (i.e., a territorial group) may hold within it several ­sub-
communities, or communities nested in communities. In some in-
stances, they noted that seeking approval and engagement with the
formal leaders may not be appropriate and cited the example of women
community members seeking research on domestic violence. Such re-
search may not receive support from formal leadership. They raised
the issue that within First Nation communities, there might be tension
between traditional cultural leadership authority and leadership au-
thority deriving from the Indian Act (Aboriginal Research Ethics Ini-
tiative, 2008). In these instances, there may be a need for engagement
with a sub-community (or community of interest). Within urban con-
texts, where Indigenous people comprise a community without a for-
mal organizational structure, such as urban Indigenous youth, a group
may be termed a community of interest if its members choose to define
themselves in this collective way. Of note, it is not the researcher’s de-
cision to determine membership in a community of interest.
     Individuals may identify as Indigenous but not belong to an In-
digenous community. Simply because one or two individuals of
­Indigenous identity are involved in a research project does not make
 it Indigenous research. However, if the majority of individuals are
118   The Four Foundations


      Indigenous, then the researcher must assess if, in fact, this constitutes
      an Indigenous community of interest to the extent that the project
      would be considered Indigenous research. In theoretical research,
      there might not be human subject research, but the focus could be
      on an Indigenous community in some manner. Determining the In-
      digenous community to engage with will largely depend on the re-
      searcher’s relationship with community in tandem with the subject
      and aim of the research project. As mentioned, it is less complicated
      if the Indigenous community requests the research and articulates
      with whom, where, and how engagement needs to take place. When
      all is said, knowing community means reaching out, learning about
      the community, hoping for connection, being vulnerable to rejection,
      building relationships, being humble, communicating, and keeping
      on keeping on.

      Defining Community Engagement in Indigenous Research

      TCPS2 defines community engagement for the purpose of Indigenous re-
      search as “a process that establishes an interaction between a researcher
      (or a research team) and the Indigenous community relevant to the
      ­research project” (CIHR et al., 2018, p. 110). The definition states that
       community engagement is the intention to form a collaborative relation-
       ship between researcher(s) and community. Although the policy does
       not quantify the level of collaboration, it does indicate that this will vary.
       Indicators of engagement, according to this definition, may ­include “re-
       view and approval from formal leadership to conduct research in the
       community, joint planning with a responsible agency, commitment to
       a partnership formalized in a research agreement, or dialogue with an
       advisory group expert in the customs governing the knowledge being
       sought” (p. 110). TCPS2 concludes the definition by stating that the en-
       gagement or partnership may be on a continuum from “active partic-
       ipation and collaboration, to empowerment and shared leadership of
       the research project.” It includes the provision that communities may
       not participate in the research project but simply “­acknowledge it and
       register no objection to it” (CIHR et al., 2018, p. 110).
          In considering the term community engagement linked to ethics
       in an Indigenous research context, I recognize any interpretation is
       fraught with challenges. Brunger and Wall (2016) point to colonial
                         Engaging with the Indigenous Community in Research   119


research methodologies that haunt Indigenous communities. As
they say, “­research ethics guidelines that speak of partnerships and
­relationships but promote contract-style consents and volunteer obli-
 gations, these are connected not ( just) by metaphors of power, but by
 the immediacy of ongoing colonialism” (p. 26). Western research has a
 bad reputation in Indigenous communities – for good reason. Unethi-
 cal research of the past has given rise to ethical protocols for research
 involving Indigenous communities. These ethical protocols have been
 developed through community dialogue and substantive scholarship
 (Battiste & Henderson, 2000; Brant-Castellano, 2004). While defini-
 tions (and their criteria), generally speaking, are blunt instruments,
 they help point us to the same book if not always the same page. An
 understanding of the terms Indigenous peoples, Indigenous research and
 Indigenous community is the necessary first step in appreciating the
 term community engagement in Indigenous research.



ENGAGING COMMUNITY: PRINCIPLES, PRACTICES,
AND PRICKLY ISSUES

     In the spaces between research methodologies, ethical principles,
     institutional regulations, and human subjects as individuals and
     as socially organized actors and communities is tricky ground.
     The ground is tricky because it is complicated and changeable,
     and it is tricky also because it can play tricks on research and re-
     searchers. (Smith, 2005, p. 85)


Indigenous community engagement signals relationship. It holds com-
plexity because it involves people, and where people are involved
connections can nourish our spirits, or, conversely, things can go side-
ways fast. Effective community engagement is both a process and an
outcome that requires relational attentiveness, communication, care,
and planning. The latter half of this chapter considers the complexi-
ties of community engagement in Indigenous research, as Indigenous
research moves forward and back on a trust/distrust continuum.
I start this next section intentionally with OCAP (ownership, control,
access, and possession) principles. OCAP principles, developed in
Canada, are a response by the Indigenous community to extractive
120   The Four Foundations


      research in Indigenous communities. As a set of principles, OCAP is
      a decolonizing force in Indigenous research practices, but a challenge
      to implement.

      The OCAP Principles

      Researchers in Canada ought to be familiar with OCAP (ownership,
      control, access, and possession). Most Indigenous research carried out
      in Canada is done with some consideration of the OCAP principles.
      OCAP is an Indigenous community code of ethics. OCAP is refer-
      enced in TCPS2 (a Canadian government policy). Like TCPS2, OCAP
      originates in Canada; however, the two statements emerge from two
      differing cultural perspectives that exist in Canada and thus, at times,
      chafe against each other. “Since community codes of ethics and the
      TCPS have evolved from different cultures and histories, interpreta-
      tion, negotiation and flexibility will often be needed to reach agreement
      on ethical practice” (Aboriginal Research Ethics Initiative, 2008, p. 12).
      Both TCPS2 and OCAP are influential within Indigenous research;
      however, OCAP principles are Indigenous community d             ­ eveloped
      and driven. OCAP gives language to reclaiming control of research by
      Indigenous communities and establishing a self-­determining frame-
       work that sets boundaries on outsider data collection.
          The term OCAP was first coined in 1998 by the First Nations
      ­Regional Longitudinal Health Survey Working Committee. This com-
       mittee has since evolved into the First Nations Information ­Governance
       Centre. OCAP was first conceived as OCA – ownership, control,
       and ­access – with the original acronym OCA “attributed to Cathryn
       George, a member of the committee representing the ­Association of
       Iroquois and Allied Nations” (Schnarch, 2004, p. 81). However, a “P”
       was subsequently added to denote the importance of possession of
       data; thus OCA evolved into OCAP. OCAP was developed in 2004,
       after the Royal Commission on Aboriginal Peoples (RCAP) report
       was published. As mentioned in Chapter 4, RCAP was the first to
       develop ethical guidelines for research with Aboriginal communities
       in Canada. OCAP was brought into consciousness through a publi-
       cation by Brian Schnarch. At the time, Schnarch (2004) was with the
       First Nations ­Centre, National Health Organization. While the article
       was written with Inuit and First Nations communities in mind, OCAP
                        Engaging with the Indigenous Community in Research    121


principles have been a source of guidance for, if not direct application
to, Indigenous research generally.
   Since its introduction, OCAP has been of significant influence as
a set of principles for conducting research in Canadian Indigenous
communities. Schnarch (2004) summarizes a non-exhaustive but
significant list of ways in which Western research has aggrieved the
Indigenous community and from which the OCAP document has
emerged. Examples from the list include researchers pre-empting
community involvement by presenting research designs constructed
without community participation yet requesting a community stamp
of approval; research funding controlled by external agents and not
accessible to Indigenous communities and organizations; community
participation without full, informed consent; and the use of biological
samples (e.g., blood) for further research without community consent.
   Ethical infringements can occur in any research site; however, the
potential for ethical infringement within Indigenous communities, par-
ticularly by outsider research, is increased within Indigenous communi-
ties because of their relatively small size and inability to leverage power
within dominant society (Schnarch, 2004). To counter such exploitive
research practices, the four key principles of OCAP have been set forth.

• Ownership assumes that a community owns cultural knowledge
  or data collectively, in the same manner that an individual owns
  personal information, and so the community’s consent is required
  to use said knowledge.
• Control asserts that First Nations people have a right to have
  ­control in various aspects of research that involves them, i­ ncluding
   the formulation of research frameworks, data management, and
   dissemination.
• Access is the ability for Indigenous people to retrieve and examine
   data that concern them and their communities.
• Possession refers to the actual possession of data. “Although
   not a condition of ownership per se, possession (of data) is a
   mechanism by which ownership can be asserted and protected”
   (­Schnarch, 2004, p. 81).

   Since the introduction of OCAP, there have been responses by the
larger Canadian research community. For example, as mentioned,
122   The Four Foundations


      OCAP is referenced in TCSP2. As a set of principles, OCAP is valuable
      because it signals researchers to pause and reflect upon the Indige-
      nous communities’ point of view when it comes to control of research
      involving them. The OCAP principles require researchers to think
      ­beyond individual consent in research – to a collective, community
       consent. It provides a conversational portal through which research-
       ers can unravel the tangle of normative assumptions about ethics,
       funding control, and power relations in research by demonstrating
       how these assumptions are viewed when embedded in an Indigenous
       context. As Schnarch (2004) states, “OCAP is not a doctrine or a pre-
       scription. It is a set of principles in evolution” (p. 81). As a set of princi-
       ples, OCAP is powerful. However, the practical application of OCAP
       is complicated and difficult to fully implement, with some aspects of
       OCAP easier to apply than others depending upon the research.
           The practical application of OCAP as a framework has the best
       chance within Indigenous communities identified as territorial or
       organizational (e.g., First Nations or tribal jurisdictions, friendship
       centres, Métis associations) and when funding flows directly to the
       Indigenous body or organization. When conducting research with
       these Indigenous communities, researchers face a governing struc-
       ture through which mechanisms for implementing OCAP and OCAP
       oversight can occur. When research involves Indigenous communities
       of interest, where no formal Indigenous jurisdiction over the research
       exists, the practical application of OCAP as a policy, beyond a set of
       guiding principles, is challenging given that there is no formal commu-
       nity to implement and monitor it. Alongside community governance
       implications, researchers must often consider how to apply OCAP to
       university-funded human subject research that must go through an
       ethical review process. When a stated principal investigator is a uni-
       versity faculty member, OCAP application is an equal challenge.
           In most Canadian university-based research, a principal investi-
       gator model exists for human subject research. Further, one person
       (i.e., the principal investigator) is responsible for applying for ethics
       approval. Funding is contingent upon ethical compliance. For a pro-
       ject flowing through a Canadian university, the principal investigator
       must be an employee of the university. This means that a university-
       employed researcher has responsibility for and control of the fund-
       ing allocated for a research project. This gives the researcher, not the
                        Engaging with the Indigenous Community in Research   123


community, control of the funding and ultimately the decisions as-
sociated with the research. TCPS2 acknowledges OCAP but clearly
states that “Researchers should consult their own institutions to en-
sure that the application of OCAP or other community-based eth-
ics codes is consistent with institutional policies” (CIHR et al., 2018,
p. 119). Consistent means being compliant with institutional financial
administration policies.
    Indigenous possession of data, a specific OCAP standard, is equally
challenging to implement. In human subject research, the principal in-
vestigator employed by the university and receiving ethical approval
is responsible for arranging storage of data and the subsequent dis-
posal of data after a set period of time. Possession agreements, distinct
from research agreements, are currently in consideration to address
OCAP concerns. A possession agreement as articulated in TCPS2
“covers the control and use of data and human biological material
collected over the course of the research” (CIHR et al., 2018, p. 119).
Even if possession agreements consign to Indigenous communities
and organizations the authority to store data, their capacity and infra-
structure to do so is often limited. The physical infrastructure in Indig-
enous organizations and communities for research data warehousing
do not yet largely exist in Canada.
    Research agreements are identified and defined within TCPS2 as a
way to insure that, at some level, the spirit of OCAP is being respected.
A research agreement as identified in TCPS2 is a formal agreement that
clarifies and affirms mutual expectations arising from the research. It
is a place where commitment of the researcher and community can be
identified. Research agreements include, but are not limited to, ethi-
cal protections, consent, community participations, decision-making
processes, dissemination, benefits of the research, publications, data
collection, and conflict resolutions processes. Researchers with fund-
ing should also be prepared to sign a transfer agreement to allocate
funding to the Indigenous community where possible.
    OCAP intersects with intellectual property rights law, property
rights in human biological materials, and Indigenous rights. Indige-
nous rights, internationally, must also be considered in the context of
the United Nations Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous P      ­ eoples
as well as world international property rights. Research cannot
­divorce itself from the legalities and politics surrounding Indigenous
124   The Four Foundations


      community and experience. The manner in which university research
      is administered, which fastens funding to data control, creates com-
      plexities for fully implementing OCAP. As Ceal Tournier, quoted at
      the beginning of this chapter, says, “who controls the data, controls
      the gold” (Schnarch, 2004). However, as a principle-driven approach
      that underscores collectivist ethics, OCAP has been changing colo-
      nial research culture, attitude, and practice as it applies to Indigenous
      research. OCAP is a defence against “smash and grab” extractive
      research, and it articulates what meaningful respect looks like in
      ­Indigenous research (Martin & Frost, 1996, p. 606). For all studies,
       large or small, the OCAP principles are a force even when the practi-
       cal application is complicated. OCAP reveals one of several inflection
       points within Indigenous and settler community engaged research
       ­relations – a relationship defined largely by a push-pull dynamic.

      Engagement Stress

      The familiar adage “nothing about us without us” is a worthy rallying
      cry when it comes to research with Indigenous peoples. The positive
      shift towards involving community in Indigenous research, prompted
      by principles from RCAP and OCAP, is undeniable. ­Ethical guide-
      lines, such as TCPS2, create tangible policy portals for a semblance
      of community control to come through. As mentioned, in Canada,
      ­university-based ethics approval for Indigenous research must be com-
       pliant with TCPS2. This ethics approval process requires community
       engagement for conducting Indigenous research. Thus, researchers
       are highly motivated to meet a community engagement requirement.
       Communities, in a desire to take back research, are equally compelled
       to involve themselves in research.
          As research with Indigenous peoples intensifies, ethical guidelines
       that require community participation are often the determining fac-
       tor between principled, socially just research and extractive research.
       However, community engagement, worthy as it is, creates stress for all
       partners, and specifically for Indigenous peoples, no matter the form
       of community (e.g., territorial, organizational, or community of inter-
       est). Engagement stress arises from prickly pragmatics associated with
       differing cultural understandings of research ethics, the increasing
                        Engaging with the Indigenous Community in Research    125


presence of engagement scope creep, the constraints of funding policy,
time pressures, and the relational presence/absence of trust.

Prickly Pragmatics

Community engagement is subject to outside ethics and funding pol-
icies alongside timeline constraints, all of which create constraints on
Indigenous jurisdiction. (Jurisdiction, here, means the community’s
ability to make decisions.) Such factors cause prickly issues compli-
cating community partnering and evoking engagement tensions. In
a study with the NunatukKavut on Indigenous research community
engagement, Brunger and Wall (2016) critically examined the inter-
secting role of community engagement and research ethical guide-
lines, specifically TCPS2. In their research, they highlight two aspects
of engagement stress that converge and create strain for the commu-
nity. They include research fatigue and an ever-widening engagement
scope creep.
   In the Brunger and Wall (2016) study, research fatigue is described by
a community member in this way: “I mentioned the word, ‘research
fatigue.’ I think that evolves out of almost like the fly-by-nighters that
go in, you know, and they’re just there to get the data and move out”
(p. 1868). As this individual reflects, research fatigue arises from a his-
tory of extractive research. However, as Brunger and Wall (2016) state,
contemporary research fatigue moves beyond being researched to
death and now implicates a cyclical swathe of researchers, each with a
particular research agenda and little awareness of how many similar
project have taken place or are currently underway. This creates hid-
den harm and hardship for the community.
   The phenomenon of research fatigue is associated with an increase
in what Brunger and Wall (2016) term “engagement creep” (p. 1871).
Engagement creep, akin to project scope creep, denotes the widening
demand for community engagement in all aspects of research. This
includes, but is not limited to, the increasing research demand for In-
digenous research participants, Indigenous research facilitators, ethi-
cal reviewers, research advisory members, research event organizers,
Elder consultation, and dissemination activities. Brunger and Wall
(2016) state that as the scope of community engagement widens, it is
126   The Four Foundations


      increasingly based upon community voluntarism. And they make this
      concerning point:

           Interestingly, the least amount of engagement in research is
           through actual participation (being a “subject” of research); the
           bulk of the engagement in research occurs in the form of volun-
           teer work by older women in the community or by staff working
           outside of their regular paid employment to assist researchers.
           (p. 1872)


      Depending upon the community capacity, the level of engagement
      stress can vary. However, for many Indigenous communities, ­research
      fatigue alongside a widening engagement scope manifests in the
      ­inability, as a result of limits in human, financial, or infrastructure
       support or capacity, to keep pace with the demands of the outsider
       researcher’s need for Indigenous community engagement.
           Lack of research engagement funding arising from systemic
       choices leads to significant engagement stress. This starts at the base
       level of forms. In many Indigenous research funding application
       forms, there is no specific category or budget line item for community
       ­engagement expenses. This is a systemic blind spot that becomes par-
        ticularly relevant when researchers who hold funds for Indigenous
        research underestimate the cost of community engagement – or try to
        pursue research without adequate funding. Community engagement
        is a clear principle of Indigenous research and ought to be reflected
        in a research budget line item or category. Brunger and Wall (2016)
        suggest a 10 per cent administrative overhead cost, paid directly to a
        community for engagement costs.
           The principal investigator approach of university-based funding
        models is also problematic because it concentrates power, and decision-
        making authority, in the researcher’s jurisdiction with minimal op-
