        portunity for community regulation. When ethical responsibilities
        and funding control lie with a sole university-based principal inves-
        tigator, the Indigenous community is dependent on the researcher
        making pro-community choices. When institutional funding policy
        conflicts with justifiable community engagement needs, researchers
        will find themselves advocating for an Indigenous community within
        their institution. If there is disagreement, as with much in our society,
                        Engaging with the Indigenous Community in Research   127


the power holder triumphs, and this is usually not the community
(Stiegman & Castleden, 2015). As Tournier says, “He who controls the
data, controls the gold” (as quoted in Schnarch, 2004, p. 83).
   Graduate students, without funded research, may not recognize
the amount of research fatigue Indigenous communities are experi-
encing. Graduate students may not realize that this might be why it
is hard to make inroads. It appears that extractive research, corrected
through the ethical requirements of community engagement, has, in
too many instances, shape-shifted into stress for Indigenous commu-
nities, stress that comes not from community engagement itself but
from the fact that this engagement is overly demanding and under
resourced. Brunger and Wall (2016) offer three suggestions that would
make a significant difference and are applicable to researchers with
funded or non-funded research:

• get funding for community engagement;
• liaise with the community on an ethical review to ensure that re-
  searchers and university research ethics boards (REBs) are doing
  their share and not relegating ethics review to the community; and
• seek an introduction to the community – “no cold calls.”

These recommendations tie directly to funding, ethics, and relation-
ships that are often at the root of engagement stress. Brunger and
Wall’s recommendation of “no cold calls” is couched in relationality
and moves us from the prickly pragmatics to the relational dynamic
that community engagement evokes – specifically, the presence/­
absence of trust.

The Trust/Distrust Continuum

At its crux, ethical protocol for Indigenous peoples is about relation-
ality, and ethics is about trust. Pidgeon (2019) notes that ethical guide-
lines – and protocols by nature – are general principles of conduct,
“and a researcher has a responsibility to understand the spiritual
and cultural teachings behind the protocols” (p. 243). This requires
a relationship with the Indigenous community, be it territorial, or-
ganizational, or a community of interest. Although relationality pep-
pers Indigenous research literature, Morton Ninomiya and Pollock
128   The Four Foundations


      (2017) point out, “it cannot be taken for granted that researchers or
      research institutions know how to be relational or support relational
      ­approaches” (p. 35). They acknowledge delineated principles out-
       lined in ethical documents; however, they state that it is “everyday”
       ­decisions of daily action that define an ethical relationship. And, it
        is in daily actions of relationship that trust can be built or broken.
        ­Burnette and Sanders (2014) make this point:

           Because trust was present in each major cluster, it seemed that
           activities between researchers and indigenous communities were
           on a continuum between trust-building and trust breaking. For
           example, exhibiting transparency and beneficence fostered trust
           development, whereas conducting researcher-driven work with-
           out the input of tribe impaired trust development. (p. 14)


          In their study of Indigenous communities in the United States,
      Burnette and Sanders (2014) explored trust development in
      ­
      Indigenous community engagement for the purpose of research.
      ­
      Through a qualitative study, they interviewed thirteen Indigenous
      and ­non-Indigenous researchers, situated in the United States, with
      experience conducting research in Indigenous communities. In the
      study, Burnette and Sanders (2014) defined trust as a concept that
      ­figures prominently in interactions where there is uncertainty about
       the intentions of another because one party is unknown or because
       there is a clear power differential between the two parties.
          In defining trust, Burnette and Sanders (2014) reviewed literature
       comparing tendencies of collectivist traditions, as in Japanese ­culture,
       in contrast to individually oriented societies, such as the United
       States. They found that in collectively orientated societies, individuals
       tend to remain in stable relationships where the other party is known;
       they place group over individual interests; they are relationally orien-
       tated and rely upon shared connections, such as friendship, in trust
       building; they rely upon contextual indicators such as benevolence,
       predictability, and group membership in forming trust relationships;
       and they value qualities such as empathy, loyalty, and support as part
       of socially cohesive norms. Individually orientated societies (such as
       those stemming from Western culture) are more likely to take more
       trust risks when in trust-building situations and to rely upon personal
                       Engaging with the Indigenous Community in Research   129


character, reputation, and achievement (e.g., professional credentials)
as indicators of trustworthiness.
   From their research, Burnette and Sanders (2014) reported several
factors, as identified by experienced researchers working with Indig-
enous communities, that impact trust building including: “historical
oppression, risk and reputation, power balancing across multiple
levels, reciprocity and benevolence, and cross-cultural collaboration”
(p. 11). Alongside these significant factors, Burnette and Sanders
(2014) raise a phenomenon connected to risk and reputation, one that
arises in the subtext of engagement discourses although it is not often
overtly addressed. That is the experience of credibility stress (p. 11).
   Credibility stress is the vulnerability that community members open
themselves to when bringing in researchers who are not community
members or known to the community. If researchers do not conduct
themselves in a way that is respectful, and the research does not give
back, it is the community member who experiences the blowback: “If
you [the outside researcher] do something wrong, they [­Indigenous
peoples] are not gonna look at you. They’re gonna say, ‘Look at that
woman, who brought those folks in here, and they’re exploiting us’”
(Burnette & Sanders, 2014, p. 11). An example, not in the Brunette
and Sanders study but related to the theme of risk and reputation,
is the risk to the individual community member who introduces to
a community a researcher (or research team) with a proposed study;
the study does not get funded, and, next thing, the researcher disap-
pears. The community is left wondering what happened. Credibility
stress also weighs heavily for woke and aware researchers. Situating
themselves as university-based researchers, Stiegman and Castleden
(2015, p. 4) make this point about being caught between jurisdictions,
“we find ourselves in a ‘catch-22’ where we are ‘damned if we do’ the
minutia (privileging academic protocols over respect for Indigenous
jurisdiction and community autonomy) or ‘damned if we don’t’ (thus
not getting REB approval to proceed with our research).”
   Burnette and Sanders (2014) denote that Indigenous research ap-
pears to be a relational continuum that moves back and forth between
trust and distrust. Where the researcher-community relationship finds
itself on the trust/distrust continuum reflects the health of the rela-
tionship as well as the skill of the parties in navigating the prickly
terrain of community engagement.
130   The Four Foundations


      Research Relationship Skills Matter

      A study by Morton Ninomiya and Pollock (2017) is useful in highlight-
      ing both situations of ethical or engagement stress and possibilities
      for resolving tensions. In collaboration with Sheshatshiu Innu First
      Nation in Labrador, Canada, they conducted a research study explor-
      ing the experiences of children and youth living with foetal alcohol
      spectrum disorder (FASD). In reflecting upon the research relation-
      ship, they outline several “on-the-ground” ethical dilemmas, rooted
      in trust, associated with their research. They offer salient points as to
      how these ethical dilemmas were resolved. One common prickly is-
      sue, as Morton Ninomiya and Pollock (2017) point out, is the financial
      administrative control of a project.
         Morton Ninomiya and Pollock (2017) note that this lack of control
      creates grief for community and researcher alike – both are aggravated
      by institutional financial policies that frustrate community e­ ngagement.
      They describe a too-common scenario of a sticky situation:

           [C]ommunity members involved in the research suggested re-
           freshments for a meeting be ordered from a community Elder
           who was selling local sweet bread. As funding for the meeting
           was provided by a federal grant and administered by the univer-
           sity, an official receipt was required to claim the expenses related
           to food. However, the Elder did not have an official business as
           she was fundraising for a traditional winter trek, and typically
           did not issue receipts. (Morton Ninomiya & Pollock, 2017, p. 34)


      About this situation, Morton Ninomiya and Pollock note that ethical
      guidelines like TCPS2 or OCAP do not provide explicit guidelines for
      universities or research institutions in partnership with Indigenous
      communities when conflicting financial guidelines cause tension.
      Seemingly a minor issue, this disconnect over who gets paid and how
      can create significant relational tension, particularly in communities
      where financial resources are not in huge supply.
         Morton Ninomiya and Pollock identify another prickly issue, which
      centred on data. They had a written community-researcher agreement
      with the Sheshatshiu Innu First Nation to conduct their FASD research
      study. Shortly thereafter, the community commissioned a consulting
                         Engaging with the Indigenous Community in Research   131


agency to conduct a FASD service-related needs assessment in the
community. The studies were different, but there were some overlap-
ping data of interest to both parties and studies. The data collected
in the FASD study by Morton Ninomiya and Pollock, as indicated in
the community-researcher agreement, were to belong to the commu-
nity through OCAP. Interview informants provided interviews based
upon this understanding. Morton Ninomiya and Pollock (2017) were
confronted with this data dilemma:

     As per TCPS 2, a community-researcher agreement for the FASD
     study was developed and signed, but there was no consideration
     for how to share data with a separate, community-commissioned
     investigation. Both the consultant and the research team were
     committed to producing work for and with the SIFN, however it
     was unclear about how much ownership or control either the con-
     sultant or research team had over the data or the analyses. (p. 32)


Both bodies were focused on learning from the same individuals.
This created ethical dilemmas associated with data sharing. “This sit-
uation raised concerns for the research team including potential re-
search fatigue among informants, unclear protocol for data ownership
and sharing, and the release of results prior to the study’s reporting”
(Morton Ninomiya & Pollock, 2017, p. 32).
     In the Morton Ninomiya and Pollock study, relational tensions
­occurred in decisions on how to disseminate findings. The commu-
 nity required a multiform dissemination strategy including text, vis-
 uals, and graphs with face-to-face knowledge sharing. The university
 required academic peer-reviewed publication and written reports
 (benefiting the university and researchers). Morton Ninomiya and
 Pollock (2017) state, “Even if a research study is meant to benefit an
 Indigenous community, it frequently benefits researchers” (p. 34).
 They go on to say, “In cases where scholarly writing does not directly
 benefit an Indigenous community, we suggest that researchers must
 explicitly discuss and negotiate the volume of writing with commu-
 nity members” (p. 36).
     In all instances, communication amongst the community, the
 ­researchers, and the consultants (when matters concerned them) was
  instrumental in resolving the tension. Through transparency, listening,
132   The Four Foundations


      and responding, Morton Ninomiya and Pollock were able to develop
      a collaborative action plan to respond to the ethical dilemmas that
      the research evoked. It required communication that was at times,
      no doubt, awkward. Decisions about the extent of a community’s in-
      volvement in scholarly research products require discussion upfront.
      Knowing the collective trust ethos of Indigenous communities helps
      researchers understand that being upfront about limitations, being
      prepared to listen, being willing to communicate, and being cogni-
      zant of conflict resolution strategies (or being humble enough to know
      when a conflict resolution facilitator is required) provide preparation
      to mediate engagement stress when it arises. Inevitably, researchers
      and community must have the relational research skills effective for
      trust building.
           Indigenous community engagement is marked by the reverber-
      ations of colonial trauma, jurisdictional intersections, crisscrossing
      cultural paradigms, the pragmatics of timelines, and funding policy.
      These coexist alongside diverse motivations compelling human be-
      haviour that are foundational to the research relationship. ­Experienced
      researchers know that when the research relationship works well
      it is because there is trust among all parties. They know that trust
      takes time and effort. Given the omnipresence of colonial history
      amid conscious and unconscious bias, creating a trusting Indigenous
      ­community-researcher partnership requires the relational attentivity
       and skill to sustain itself when trickster appears.
           Morton Ninomiya and Pollock (2017) make a point at the end of
       their article that indicates the importance of the miýo ethic in research:
       “While universities offer courses on research methods, there is no guar-
       antee students will be taught or evaluated on their competencies and
       skills in communication and conflict resolution skills” (p. 35). R
                                                                        ­ elational
       competency in research is critical because even though the TCPS2 out-
       lines ethical requirements, there is, they say, “no mechanism to consist-
       ently enforce or hold researchers and their institutions ­accountable to
       Indigenous communities” (Morton Ninomiya & ­Pollock, 2017, p. 32).
       It is up to the researcher to have not only the ethical sensibility but also
       the relational skills to sustain an ethical approach.
           This chapter concludes with a community engagement “something
       to think about” list. It is not a checklist, but it offers some thoughts
       that may be of use when engaging in relationship with an Indigenous
                        Engaging with the Indigenous Community in Research   133


community, whether it be territorial, organizational, or a community
of interest. A question I hear most often from graduate students is,
“How do I engage with community?” My response is that engaging
with community is about forming a relationship. I ask my students to
reflect on the relationships in their life. How did they enter into these
relationships?
   As humans, we have a desire to connect, and we reach out to each
other in an instinctual way. We know that respect and mutuality,
alongside humour and shared experiences, go a long way to building
relationship. We also recognize that in relationships, there is engage-
ment stress, and if the relationship matters to us, we find healthy ways
to work through the tensions. For community engagement to work
well, researchers need an engagement plan. Making one cannot be
done in an ad hoc manner. The desire, or hope, to connect with com-
munity is a strong ethos, but hope is not a plan. On the flip side, build-
ing relationship is not a “checklist” endeavour either. It is contextual.
That being said, there are some preparations for effective community
engagement in Indigenous research. Below I offer illustrative sugges-
tions (they are not definitive or in any particular order).

• Ask yourself about your knowledge and intentions: “Do I know the
  community I would like to partner with?” “Do I have c­ onnections
  or not?” “If I don’t, what is my plan to reach out (be specific)?” “Do
  I know if the community welcomes my research?” “Have I formed
  a relationship with the community before I have committed to the
  research?” “Is the community in my corner?”
• Educate yourself about the community (e.g., history, context,
  cultural protocols, and roles of community members) and the re-
  search projects that have affected the community (e.g., its history
  of research participation).
• Educate yourself on research fatigue and engagement stress.
• Inform yourself of what a collectivist trust ethos involves.
• Consider whether your research is relevant to the community and
  whether it will contribute to research fatigue.
• Inform yourself about the notion of credibility stress and what it
  means in light of you and your specific research.
• Facilitate a research funding sub-transfer (if research is funded)
  for engagement budget items.
134   The Four Foundations


      • Prepare for policy hurdles surrounding financial administration
        and ethics application. Communicate with the community about
        hurdles and prickly issues.
      • Plan and budget for engagement.
      • Plan for reciprocity (i.e., knowledge dissemination) even if these
        costs are not reimbursed.
      • Ensure you do not over-promise what you cannot deliver.
      • Ensure that you, as researcher, connect with the community (e.g.,
        do not have graduate students or research assistants “cold call”
        communities).
      • Adopt an attitude and expression of humbleness, and accept feed-
        back even if it is hard to hear.
      • Develop relationships with the community prior to research.
      • Educate yourself about conflict resolution strategies, or be pre-
        pared to enlist a conflict resolution facilitator.
      • Communicate, communicate, communicate.
      • Consider a community engagement plan for your research, seek
        community input, and review the plan with the community.
      • Be accountable to the community.

         This chapter charts the complexities of community engagement
      given the ontological, institutional, and personal power leverage
      bound up with the researcher identity that is complicit within a colo-
      nial research model. It is an attempt to provoke thought on how we
      might reposition ourselves as researchers worthy of trust and agents
      of community service. How might we be researchers capable of vul-
      nerability – be as unsettled as we are unsettling for an Indigenous
      community? Too close, I offer a scenario that is familiar to many.
         The researcher walks into the community hall. The researcher
      has been invited to the community, but no trust relationship yet
      exists. Everyone knows the researcher is an outsider from the uni-
      versity. There is food, tea, coffee, a welcome, and the researcher qui-
      etly finds a seat in a circle of chairs. The circle starts. The researcher
      and the project are introduced by a community member who has
      organized the event. A feather is passed, and the community mem-
      bers share. The research evokes memories, and the stories poke at
      painful places. The words waken heartache. It is the Elder’s turn to
      talk. She, maybe he, looks at the researcher. Suddenly, the Elder is
                         Engaging with the Indigenous Community in Research     135


asking epistemological, axiological, and methodological questions:
“Who are you? What are you asking for? Why do you want to know?
How will you proceed?” As the Elder talks the researcher thinks,
“The methodology books aren’t going to help me here.” The feather
is passed to the researcher. The researcher sinks a little in the chair
feeling the unbearable weight of history in this moment. Dark eyes
watch; this is a witnessing. The researcher starts to speak. Much will
be decided in the next few ­moments …



DISCUSSION QUESTIONS

1 Discuss in a group, or with another individual, your understanding
  of the terms research fatigue, engagement scope creep, and credibility
  stress. Why might these cause engagement stress?
2 The chapter discusses the differences in the trust-building ethos of
  two traditions: a collectivist versus an individually orientated tradition.
  What are the differences? How does the collectivist ethos fit within an
  Indigenous ethical framework, as discussed in Chapter 4?
3 In your small group or dyad, discuss the three different types of com-
  munity identified in the chapter. Consider a community of interest.
  How might this designation apply specifically to an urban Indigenous
  context? How might you engage a community of interest?
4 In your small group or dyad, develop an outline for a community
  engagement plan. What would it include? Make sure to identify an
  engagement budget and clearly articulate what financial resources
  would be required. Remember, hope is not a plan.
CHAPTER SIX

 Preparations: Situating Self, Culture, and
 Purpose in Indigenous Methodologies




       Write with your eyes like painters, with your ears like musicians, with
       your feet like dancers. You are the truthsayer with quill and torch. Write
       with your tongues of fire. Don’t let the pen banish you from yourself.
                                                                Anzaldúa (2015)



 I move back through time and am transported. It is an annual Fed-
 eration of Saskatchewan Indian Nations1 powwow. I listen, watch,
 and quiet my mind as an Elder raises the pipe and offers a prayer.
 Soon, the dancers are entering the stadium in a Grand Entry. I hear
 the drums, see the colours of their regalia flicker as they move, and
 I viscerally know this ceremony sustains my culture. I think about my
 Nêhiyaw and Saulteaux identity and why this desire to belong aches
 deep in my soul. My research is an extension of this personal, inward
 journey. I want my research to uphold my culture; it is purposeful,
 and I have been given the space to do this. I am thankful. And though
 I have known days when the emotional work of being an Indigenous
 researcher has tempered my gratitude, I know that purpose has kept
 me going when my spirits have been low.



 1 In 2016, the Federation of Saskatchewan Indian Nations changed its name to the
   Federation of Sovereign Indigenous Nations.
               Situating Self, Culture, and Purpose in Indigenous Methodologies   137


    In Indigenous methodologies, the experiencing self is intentionally
and indelibly invested in Indigenous epistemology, Indigenous eth-
ics, and Indigenous community. Indigenous thought and ­custom do
not situate the self as separate or antecedent to community. ­Arguably,
community, at times, may be antecedent to the self. Typically, a con-
sciousness of self and community exist simultaneously in Indige-
nous methodologies, and we must view the self as interwoven into
a larger tapestry not as a singular thread beyond the weave. Self,
epistemology, ethics (and axiology), and community converge in
­
­carrying out ­personal preparation within Indigenous methodologies.
    The need for personal preparation is documented by Indigenous
 methodologists (Bastien, 2005; Lambert, 2014; Montgomery, 2012;
 Steinhauer, 2001; Windchief et al., 2018). This writing tells us that per-
 sonal preparations are multilayered, particular, and varied: visiting
 for tea, connecting with kindred spirits on the research road, attend-
 ing ceremony, and introspective journaling, for example. There is no
 formula (nor could there be) for this is personal work, and it does not
 need to be subject to academic evaluation – it is personal. However,
 narrative devices such as a prologue statement ought to be formally
 integrated into an Indigenous methodological research design, as
 these provide a place for the researcher to clarify research purposes
 and motivations for doing research. My conversation, at the end of
 this chapter, with Indigenous researcher Cam Willett, from Little Pine
 First Nation in Saskatchewan, gives an example of what a prologue
 statement might include. Personal preparation is protective when
 research stress happens (and it will). These preparations will matter
 when an Elder asks, “Why did you do that research?” and “Why did
 you do it in that way?”



PURPOSE

While writing my dissertation, I attended a workshop led by a set-
tler scholar presenting research on the socio-economic conditions of
Indigenous people. I thought the study was insightful and would
provide helpful research for Indigenous communities. I was curious
about his motivation for carrying out this study, especially given his
situatedness as a settler scholar. During the “Q and A,” I asked him
138   The Four Foundations


      what encouraged him to carry out this study, what compelled him.
      To my mind, it was a straightforward question about personal mo-
      tivation and purpose, from a personal perspective, asking about his
      hopes for the study. He seemed unsure of how to respond. Afterward,
      I thought my question was either ambiguous or odd to him. I was
      confused, but the interchange got me thinking. By this time in my
      ­research, I was steadily reminded of a holistic conceptualization of
       purpose in Indigenous research – that our research must have personal
       and societal significance – so I was perplexed as to why my question
       “threw” the workshop leader. Perhaps different cultural traditions
       defined research purpose differently? I knew that the Indigenous
       community would ask me about my research question, my motiva-
       tions for the study, and what purpose I hoped would be achieved. The
       ­Indigenous community would expect a layered response, and I had
        best be prepared.
             Research purpose and research questions are inseparable. Purpose
        statements anchor and direct research questions. Put another way,
        ­research questions narrow the more elongated, complex, holistic pur-
         pose statement. There is much attention to the formulation of research
         questions within academic research, for they can be difficult to craft.
         The research question ought to be specific enough to render focused
         findings but at the same time allow for discovery. The research ques-
         tion can emerge from a personal curiosity or be tailored to a specific
         need as identified by the public or private sector. It can evoke explor-
         atory or applied research. A research question can develop through
         conducting a literature review or through collaboration with a com-
         munity or a stakeholder group (such as a research advisory commit-
         tee). There ought to be a gap in research that the proposed study can
         fill. In other words, a research question ought to inspire research that
         arises from a purpose statement.
             Instructions on crafting research questions and purpose statements
         are often found together in research textbooks. When a researcher de-
         velops a research proposal, the purpose statement outlines the ration-
         ale for the research and research question. The rationale, including the
         proposed need for the research, will be subsequently affirmed through
         a literature review of the existing research on the topic. Because the
         purpose statement outlines the research curiosity and signals how to
         gain further insight on the topic, it foreshadows a research design.
               Situating Self, Culture, and Purpose in Indigenous Methodologies   139


Within Indigenous methodologies, crafting a purpose statement (and
research question) is similar though more organic, holistic, and elasti-
cized, and it requires reflection on personal motivation in connection
with community.
    Among the six Indigenous scholars whose conversations are pre-
sented in this book, there was a consistent belief that research, and
research questions, should be collectively relevant and personally pur-
poseful. In their own research, this personal meaning was bundled
with community relevancy. Kathy Absolon (2006) expresses a decolo-
nizing purpose of her research found in community relations: “[O]ne
of our core missions in decolonizing and in our life is to figure out who
are we, and in order to do that you are not going to find that out in a
university, in the absence of your peers and your culture” (p. 218). In
picking her research focus, Jeannine Carriere (Chapter 7) struggled to
find a topic. After considering several options, then reconsidering, she
found her research purpose (and subsequent question) by considering
her own experiences; she would explore the lives of Indigenous adop-
tees. A friend helped Jeannine focus her inquiry on the experience of
Aboriginal adoptees: “This is who you are, this is your story, and this is
what you should be contributing.” Michael Hart (2006) articulates the
role of current research in giving back to community. He says, “I am
doing this because we can’t all live in teepees forever … our realities
have changed, and they are going to continue to change” (p. 217). All
three speak to purpose, either explicitly or implicitly, as bound to com-
munity relevance, as coming from a place of personal situatedness.
    As with cultural grounding, there are different paths arriving at a
personally inscribed purpose statement within Indigenous method-
ologies. Often in thesis or dissertations, the personal and academic
purpose statement will be interwoven into an introductory chapter, or
the personal purpose will be separated and presented as a prologue
statement (Burning, 2017; Hobenshield, 2016; Stewart, 2015; Thomp-
son, 2012). It is no surprise that when this happens, individuals feel
passionately about their research. Passion and purpose combined
are strong motivators for the daunting research journey in graduate
programs. However, it is important to note that, though interrelated,
passion and purpose are not the same thing. Hansen (2018), in his
research on the motivating factors contributing to work productiv-
ity, states that passion is what you love while purpose is about doing
140   The Four Foundations


      work that makes a contribution. He says, “You have a sense of pur-
      pose when you make valuable contributions to others (individuals or
      organizations) or to society that you find personally meaningful and
      that don’t harm anyone” (pp. 90–91). This makes sense from an Indig-
      enous point of view.
         Connecting our personal self with the community we serve is inte-
      gral to an Indigenous conceptual framework. In this sense, ­Indigenous
      methodologies bind passion and purpose together through an Indige-
      nous epistemic logic (Kuokkanen, 2007) and structure. Here are some
      questions that assist in crafting a purpose statement in Indigenous
      methodologies. What is your purpose for this research? Why and how
      does this research give back to the community? How did you arrive at
      this research curiosity? What is your story? What is your connection
      to your culture?



      PREPARATIONS

      What does Indigenous cultural grounding mean for Indigenous
      conceptual framing in research? Cultural grounding means differ-
      ent things for different Indigenous people. Much depends on one’s
      life context. Some Indigenous customs are shared openly, others pri-
      vately. This needs to be respected. Cultural ceremony is a common
      ritual to show respect and imbue balance. Some Indigenous research-
      ers may incorporate ceremonial practices to coalesce research stories
      with an ethos of respect, guardianship, and reciprocity. Indigenous
      methodologies will record different approaches to cultural prepara-
      tion. In his research, Harpell Montgomery (2012) identified “four dis-
      crete facets of preparation, namely: 1) preparing ourselves, 2) having
      a plan, 3) checking our materiel, and 4) ensuring supports are there
      when needed” (p. 40).
          In her doctoral dissertation “Haq wil la hlo is sim: Walk Slowly on
      the Breath of Your Ancestors, an Examination of Gift Giving within
      Post-Secondary Education,” Gitxsan scholar Sharon Hobenshield (2016)
      identifies Gitxsan knowledges in the conceptual planning of her re-
      search. “Gitxsan culture is the predominant setting in which I locate
      myself and ground my Indigenous perspective. I initiated this study
              Situating Self, Culture, and Purpose in Indigenous Methodologies   141


acknowledging my ancestry, where I come from and identifying the
git’mgan (Crest Pole) as the centrepiece to contextualize Gitxsan world-
view” (p. 78). Within the findings of her research, Sharon Hobenshield
points to an Indigenous ethos of balancing a good heart and mind.
     Niitsitapi scholar Betty Bastien (1999) highlights the role of Elder
guidance in conducting research into Niitsitapi ways of knowing in a
Niitsitapi, community-based bachelor of social work curricula. ­Asking
for the grandparents’ guidance involved cultural protocols, gift offer-
ings, food, ceremony, and prayer. The “Kaahasinnon – G    ­ randfather[s]
for the Sacred Horn Society” (p. 88) were a consistent source of guid-
ance on Niitsitapi knowledge protocols in Betty Bastien’s research
and were a source of Niitsitapi knowledges available to the “public
domain.” She tells us, “The guidance and advice shared by the grand-
parents about common knowledge of the Niitsitapi I share here is
meant to be shared with the uninitiated” (p. 95). A Nêhiyaw custom
is to offer tobacco to Elders and teachers for guidance. This is not the
protocol with all Indigenous communities. Protocols for ceremony
will depend upon the community. Thus, it is necessary to know the
culture, community, and specific protocol.
     Laara Fitznor (2006) recalls a story of a more personal nature about
how, when she was in a tight spot, her Indigenous spiritual practice
sustained her. When she was working on her doctoral research, her
computer crashed with all her research on it. Stomach in knots, she
evoked ceremony. “I smudged my papers, I smudged my computer
and I said, ‘you know Creator I need help, help to get me onto the
next stage.’ All of a sudden I had this burst of energy and I just wrote”
(p. 151). I, too, found myself asking for help from spirit. At a critical
time in my research, I did not know whether to return to Saskatche-
wan from British Columbia to complete my research. I found my an-
swer through spirit in dream. I returned home and once there was
able to connect with family and the land of my ancestors.
     The extent to which researchers share the story of their prepara-
tions is contingent upon their judgment in accordance with cultural
­protocol – each will face unique personal and community-based con-
 texts. There is a requirement for thick descriptions here. What matters
 is that there is room within Indigenous methodologies to acknowledge
 the self in relation to Indigenous culture in an intentional way. There
142   The Four Foundations


      is no dogma, as cultural preparation will be defined, in a p
                                                                 ­ ersonal
      way, by the researcher.



      SELF-LOCATING

      Max van Manen (2001), a social constructivist phenomenologist, tells
      us: “How can we pursue the question of what constitutes (phenome-
      nological) knowledge in such a way that our way of addressing this
      question may become an example of what the question in its ques-
      tioning seeks to bring to clarity?” (p. 46). Van Manen underscores the
      significance of exploring the self as an epistemic deliverable in which
      we reveal the beliefs that shape our lives and what we take as “truth”
      and knowledge. It is not only the questions we ask and how we go
      about asking them but who we are in the asking. Van Manen goes on
      to say that “the question of knowledge always refers us back to our
      world, to who we are … It is what stands iconically behind the words,
      the speaking and the language” (p. 46). Self-locating in research is
      common among many qualitative approaches. The extent to which
      a researcher is required to integrate and/or interrogate the subjective
      self in the research process, and for what reasons, can vary.
          Within critically reflective feminist methodologies, researchers are
      encouraged to locate themselves. This is a means of building “rec-
      iprocity, rapport and trust between the researcher and researched”
      (­Liamputtong, 2007, p. 13). Anti-oppressive inquiries integrate self-­
      location to identify and then mitigate power differentials in research.
      Anti-­oppressive researchers Susan Strega and Leslie Brown (2015)
      propose that “It [critical reflexive research] requires that we inten-
      tionally, consciously, and repeatedly bring our awareness to the ques-
      tion of what influences our perceptions, conceptions, and responses
      (internal and external).” They go on to indicate that this ought to
      happen throughout the research – “from inception to dissemination”
      (p. 9). From this perspective, self-locating is a cogent practice for ana-
      lysing power differentials in society and a strategic action for further-
      ing social justice.
          Postmodern theory highlights the existence of multiple truths.
      In applying postmodern theory to autoethnography and self-study
      methodologies, researchers reveal how the subjective and experiential
              Situating Self, Culture, and Purpose in Indigenous Methodologies   143


work to construct knowledge. The self is performative. In this research,
what is “central in autobiographical narratives is ‘I,’ our accounts of
the world, which are constructions, made up of language and mean-
ings, and our own histories” (Kimpson, 2005, p. 75). This form of re-
flexivity allows the researching self to participate as co-constructor of
knowledge in specific and defined ways. There are a number of dif-
ferent methodologies arising from Western intellectual tradition that
both feature self-location prominently and require reflexivity on the
performative, researching self. Likewise, Indigenous methodologies
require reflection on the self.
      Many are drawn to Indigenous methodologies because, as a whole,
the approach allows for self-exploration. Indigenous belief systems
uphold inward knowing arising from self in relation to society, na-
ture, the larger cosmos, and spirit. Like critically reflective method-
ologies (e.g., autoethnography), Indigenous methodologies honour
self-knowing surfacing from lived experience found in personal story.
For example, Debassige (2013) uses a “self-referent approach” (p. 16)
within his Indigenous methodological study on Aboriginal literacy.
In using a ­self-referent approach within Indigenous methodologies,
Debassige clearly, and persistently, links the Indigenous self with
Indigenous knowledges. Debassige’s careful Indigenous approach
­
­reminds us, however, to be cautious – not all reflective methodologies
 are Indigenous methodologies. If the desire is to concentrate solely on
 personal history and the performative self without any connection to
 Indigeneity, then this is not research according to Indigenous method-
 ologies. There are other ways. One might be better served by autoeth-
 nography, self-study, or life history research – all of which are better
 equipped for this exploration because they may not require the same
 epistemological attention that an Indigenous ­self-referent approach
 will require. I assert this caution not to diminish Western-imbued,
 ­self-orientated methodologies that centre personal story as the stance
  from which to gain social, political, historical, and cultural mean-
  ing but to state that these approaches are different from Indigenous
  methodologies. They are different because they do not derive from an
  ­Indigenous epistemological tradition. Indigenous methodologies ar-
   rive from an Indigenous tradition and, at minimum, require explora-
   tory study between the self and Indigenous ­epistemology, I­ ndigenous
   axiological premises, and the Indigenous community.
144   The Four Foundations


      THE PROLOGUE STATEMENT

      A prologue statement introduces the researcher, situates the re-
      searcher in relation to the Indigenous community, and functions as
      a knowledge dissemination protocol for sharing research to a wider
      audience (notably the Indigenous community). Graham Hinganga-
      roa Smith first introduced the function of a prologue in Indigenous
      methodologies to me. He integrated a prologue as a form of intro-
      duction in his doctoral research (Smith, 1997). In my culture, this pro-
      logue is akin to a protocol of introduction. The prologue is where the
      writing can shift from an “objective accounting” to a holistic narra-
      tive revealing how one’s personal experience, purpose, and prepara-
      tion influence research choices and interpretations. Each individual’s
      prologue or protocol of introduction will be expressed in a personal-
      ized way. Thus, there is no template. It is personally contextualized.
      The aim of this prologue is to introduce oneself and situate oneself in
      relation to the community and to one’s research. Through prologue,
      personal story is given value and recognized as integral to knowl-
      edge construction.
          Indigenous epistemologies do not get absorbed in the subjectivity-
      objectivity debate or give sway to fragmentation in knowledge
      ­production – subjectivity is a given. Jeannette Armstrong (2007) makes
       this point in linking methodology, culture, and self:

           We have, what I today call, a methodology. I think it’s an impor-
           tant perspective in terms of putting a perspective on what my
           role is as a human, and thinking about the Okanagan perspective
           of that, and looking at that question … The idea of the word that
           we use to describe ourselves, Syilx people, is a part of that. In a
           sense, if I were to translate that word for you, contained in that
           word is the foundational instruction, or paradigm, that expresses
           that idea of being so indigenous, and so a part of the natural word
           that our humanness is an expression of that natural world. (p. 6)


      Nêhiyaw scholar Winona Stevenson (2000) tells us that Nêhiyaw
      ­Elders will often preface their words by saying, “I believe it to be
       true” (p. 19). Through this statement, the Elders are acknowledging
       life experience; recognizing a multiplicity of truths in a shared world;
               Situating Self, Culture, and Purpose in Indigenous Methodologies   145


espousing unpretentiousness; and connecting the truth of one’s words
to the trustworthiness of one’s character.
   Eber Hampton in Memory Comes before Knowledge: Research May Im-
prove if Researchers Remember Their Motives (1995) describes the rela-
tionship between memory, self, and research. He advises researchers
to unfold the sacred medicine bundle that holds memory and con-
sider how memory has shaped personal truth. Absolon and Willett
(2005) tell us that memory binds us to place and people. “Location
ensures that individual realities are not misrepresented as generaliz-
able collectives. Our ancestors gave us membership into nations and
traditions; location both remembers and ‘re-members’ us to those
things” (p. 123). Styres (2017) reminds us that the land is alive and
holds knowledge. By anchoring ourselves in place, land, culture, and
community, we show how personal experience and community con-
nections are interwoven into our researching.
   As Indigenous researchers, we self-situate in different ways
(­
 Fiddler, 2014; Hobenshield, 2016; Windchief & San Pedro, 2019).
I used the method of keeping a reflective journal (“field notes”) dur-
ing my research. It was a helpful reference in preparing the prologue
for my dissertation. My prologue, situated at the start of the docu-
ment, reflected my personal purpose for the research in that my writ-
ing comes from my heart and my need to say to my academic world
that my Nêhiyaw and Saulteaux culture counts.
   In Dee(a)r Spine: Dance, Dramaturgy, and the Repatriation of Indigenous
Memory, Mitchell and Burelle (2016) describe and reflect upon the Yaqui
Deer Dance performed by Mitchell on Kumeyaay territory at the Univer-
sity of California, San Diego. It interweaves the performances of dance,
dramaturgy, and methodology with memory, kinship, and belonging.
In the article, Sam Mitchell writes, “Dee(a)r Spine has taught me to trust
the stories within my body.” He goes on to say, “Dee(a)r Spine has given
me a way to repatriate what adoption interrupted: what I once knew,
what I am beginning to know, and what I have never known” (p. 52).
   A Nêhiyaw kôkom shares a memory of her personal history and “the
fascination which the fabled attractions of modern life – such as lipstick
or mail-order catalogues – held for her” (Ahenakew & ­Wolfart, 1998,
p. 24). Like the Nêhiyaw kôkom, researchers are encouraged to recog-
nize all of who we are. There are many ways (poetry, story, expository)
to write a prologue. To resist pan-Indianism, identifying our specific
146   The Four Foundations


      Indigenous connection (e.g., in my case Nêhiyaw and Saulteaux) is
      custom. For many Indigenous people, the act of self-location is intu-
      itive, born of a practice embedded in Indigenous cultural traditions.
          Introducing ourselves shows respect to the ancestors and allows com-
      munity to locate us through cues such as name, kinship, culture, and
      territory. Alongside introducing ourselves, situating self in research al-
      lows us to clarify our perspective on the world (Hampton, 1995; Meyer,
      2004). Self-location connects the personal, cultural, and social aspects
      of the self. In valuing the act of self-locating, we are given occasion to
      analyse the power dynamic flowing back and forth between researcher
      and participant. Through prompting awareness of the extractive nature
      of research, self-locating acts as a decolonizing strategy.
          Being truthful about one’s identity does not mean cultural disenfran-
      chisement, nor does it preclude a collective belonging. In my conver-
      sation with Michael Hart (Chapter 3), he points out, “The journey you
      and I would take wouldn’t necessarily be the same, but they are all part
      of being Cree.” Inclusiveness is a Nêhiyaw value. Being honest about
      who we are gives us grounding when the trials of research loom. As
      Indigenous scholar Laara Fitznor (2006) reflects, “I was raised in north-
      ern Manitoba not on the reserve, not in the First Nations community …
      I became status through Bill C-31 amendment to the Indian Act. I am
      Cree with German/Scottish ancestry” (p. 193). She goes on to say, “For
      me, it’s always important to acknowledge those, the parts of who I am”
      (p. 193). In reflecting on the value of self-situating for Indigenous stu-
      dents entering post-secondary studies, Kathy Absolon (2006) advises
      that if you “went and found out what it was like and if you feel that you
      can’t be yourself, you can’t be who you are, then you can leave” (p. 193).
          The experience of a cross-racial adoptee raised outside of Indig-
      enous culture frames my perspective, and I write from this identity
      positioning. During my graduate research, I kept a journal to capture
      my reflections on identity. It is a mix of research observations, reading
      analyses, road trip reflections, and annotations about family dinners,
      ceremony, vibrant dreams, and an on-off relationship with French
      fries. It was this process that assisted me in saying, “I believe this to be
      true.” Below is an excerpt from my research journal:

           I am taking Cree; it is my first class today. Walking into the First
           Nations University there are Indians everywhere with shiny hair
                  Situating Self, Culture, and Purpose in Indigenous Methodologies           147


       flying as both instructors and students race down the hall to class.
       The instructor is Cree, Plains Cree, he was raised with the lan-
       guage, says he has been teaching here forever. This class is full
       of young students, not just Indigenous but of a variety of colours
       and cultural offerings. Though I am comforted as the instructor
       reads off the class list – Tootoosis, Cyr – these are names I know.
       These are the Crees. The instructor asks, “Who is Cree?” We put
       up our hands. He asks why we do not know our language. He
       points to me. I say adopted. He nods.


In rereading this excerpt, I see a raw and vulnerable ache palpable in
these written words – a layered purpose found in my story.



A CONVERSATION WITH CAM WILLETT

My conversation with Cam Willett2 provides an example of what a
self-situating statement or prologue might look like. Cam Willett re-
flects upon his motivation to enter into doctoral studies and research.
Cam Willett is Nêhiyaw from Little Pine First Nation in Saskatche-
wan. He received his doctoral degree from the Ontario Institute for
Studies in Education (OISE), University of Toronto. I offered Cam to-
bacco for his teachings and started by asking Cam some general ques-
tions about his background. First, I asked Cam why he went back for
a doctoral degree. This is his response:

   MAGGIE: Cam, what precipitated you going back to get your doctorate?
   CAM: Is it about community for me? I guess, but it seems more personal.
        I was thinking about that as you were talking and remembering.
        I think about my elementary education and my secondary education,
        and which were fine until Grade 12. I dropped out of school because
        of a lot of life pressures. I was working full time and had an hour-
        and-a-half bus ride to school and back. One day, these three teachers
        ambushed me in the hallway – going from one to the next to the

2 This excerpt from my interview with Cam Willett (2006) is taken from Kovach
  (2009). The interview itself was conducted for my dissertation published in 2006.
  The institutional affiliation of the interviewees for my dissertation reflects that time
  period.
148   The Four Foundations


             next – saying things about me, “You sleep in my class, you’re really
             lazy.” I stood up and said, “Well, fine, I quit,” or whatever, and
             walked down the hallway. The vice-principal was walking down
             the hallway, and I said to him, “I quit right now.” He said, “Well,
             you should think about it.” And I said, “No.” I got on the phone and
             I called my dad. He came and drove an hour to the school, and we
             sat down with the vice-principal. He said I should think about this
             and that, but I couldn’t be convinced. That was it for me. I went to
             Saskatoon and started delivering pizza and worked for a while, then
             went back to school.
                I think the reason I am talking about this is because at that time
             I was thinking to myself, “Gee, am I really capable of graduating
             from Grade 12? Can I do this?” I was really questioning my
             intellectual ability and whatever. I used to think, “Yeah, I could,”
             because I remembered my experiences from before where I did
             pretty well at school. I am a fairly smart guy; I could do it. So, after
             going through a lot, I didn’t really have any choice. I went back to
             school. I did great and my confidence was restored, and so I went
             into university. It was just sort of the logical thing. What else was
             I going to do?
                So why did I start my PhD program? Because it’s related to all
             that. I was coming out of my master’s program and the opportunity
             was there. I knew it wouldn’t always be there, and I felt I should
             just do it. At this point, I was starting to wonder what the heck I am
             doing this for. How will this benefit my community? How does it
             benefit me, even? I think my major reason for continuing is to finish
             what I have started. You don’t want to just bail. I know I can do this
             and that’s my motivation. As a young person, thinking back about
             my experience in high school, a lot of that was pure racism. Today,
             I know that, and at the time I was trying to figure it out. Has it made
             me happier to know that racism is so powerful in this province, in
             this country, today? I don’t know. I find now that I can’t read the
             newspaper. I just sort of skim through it. My mind deconstructs
             everything very quickly and it’s frustrating. I deconstruct everything,
             my mind is less – I wouldn’t say it’s totally decolonized – but it’s
             certainly a lot less colonized than it was. I don’t know. What good
             does that do? I guess there is a benefit in teaching for the students
             that are there. It’s not just me instructing them to teach the same old
              Situating Self, Culture, and Purpose in Indigenous Methodologies   149


      colonial curriculum. I don’t want my students to go off into Black
      Lake and teach colonial curriculum. That’s not what I am teaching
      them; I want them to be critical.
         When I was taking a course in a master’s program, I was talking
      to my professor during a break, and we were talking about how
      far I would go in my program. Would I become a faculty member
      or would I get a PhD? Of course, when you are a student you have
      the privilege of being radical. You can go into a classroom and say,
      “I defy school. I think we should tear down all the universities
      because it’s all bullshit.” But then he pointed out to me, “Well, you
      know, if you don’t get the PhD, will people ever listen to you? Will
      Western-minded people ever value what you have to say?” That’s
      the thing. It’s these two worlds that we are living in. The one world
      you are honoured with the eagle feather and the other world you
      are honoured with the doctoral degree. Maybe that’s one of the big
      reasons why I wanted to finish the PhD. I guess finishing is partly for
      validation, though it’s not really that important to me. But if that’s
      what it takes for people to listen to you, well then …
         You know, we were saying something about feeling alone and that
      was a pivotal point in my research. The first year you’re learning all
      this Western stuff, reading all these books that don’t have anything
      Aboriginal, and you’re the only Aboriginal person in these classes
      with no Aboriginal faculty. You start to feel really alone. I think when
      you do any ceremony, you do reach some of these portals, and you
      do realize that you’re not alone. People are always talking about
      the language disappearing, cultures disappearing. I think that’s a
      load of bull, because if you put your tobacco down and you go to
      ceremonies, I believe that all transcends time and space. You can’t
      be lost or killed because all you have to do is sweat and ask, and
      the songs will come back to you. That’s the power of them. Our
      knowledge and legacy can never be erased. We are very strong and
      that makes me proud. The legacy of our people is this land.


   During our conversation, Cam alerted me to several significant
qualities of Indigenous research. He affirmed Eber Hampton’s advice
that knowing purpose is wise and that we find purpose within our
personal narrative. When Cam spoke of his high school experience,
I was taken back to memories of my schooling and how negative
150   The Four Foundations


      residue from that experience left me questioning my abilities. What
      were my motivations for returning to school? Would I have pursued
      doctoral studies if I did not feel that I had something left to prove to
      the educational system? And when does that stop? The conversation
      got me thinking.
          We talked about story, purpose, self, and the relevance of being
      ­holistically true to one’s worldview. Was it okay to apply an episte-
       mology (e.g., feminist, Indigenous) to our research but not practice the
       same beliefs in our lives? It was not really a question about the merits
       of purity or applying an impossible orthodoxy to one’s life; rather,
       Cam’s point was that epistemology ought to be congruent with life
       choices, not just research choices.
          I was reminded of the holism inherent in Indigenous epistemolo-
       gies and thought of how we teach research methodology classes in
       Western universities. We assume that people can select methodolo-
       gies solely in relation to a research curiosity without a reflection on
       the self. No wonder it can be a “head trip.” Cam spoke about the tra-
       ditional knowledges and the value of dream knowledge, grounding
       from the ancestors, and the timelessness of ceremonies. I knew that
       whether we identified these in our methodologies, they were guiding
       our search for knowledge. Indigenous methodologies require a pur-
       pose statement about self in relation to the world. This meant pausing
       to honour the kôkoms and mosôms by remembering them. Right off
       the bat, this was no small order. Knowing why we are carrying out
       research – our motive, a knowledge found in our story – transports us
       to head and heart places. It gives us purpose. Preparations steady us.
       I am reminded anew that it is through rousing personal story, giving
       voice to the words stuck in our throats, that we begin to answer the
       Elders’ questions: “Why did you do that research?” and “Why did
       you do it in that way?”



      DISCUSSION QUESTIONS

      1 Write a 500-word prologue statement. Begin the prologue statement
        by introducing yourself to the reader. Integrate whatever identity
        markers you wish. Please note that you will rely upon these ideas in
        ­introducing yourself when an Indigenous Elder is in the room.

2 In a small group, discuss the terms research purpose and research
  ­motivation. What do they mean? How are they related? How are they
   different? Why do they matter in putting together a research plan?
   How might different cultural epistemologies approach research
   ­purpose and research motivation?
3 In your small group, imagine that the members of the group are
    an ­Indigenous community of interest. You are presenting on an
    ­Indigenous research project you are involved in. Take turns ­presenting
     and respond to these questions: “Why are you involved in that
     ­research?” and “Why does this research matter?” Attempt to respond
      to these questions by giving a layered, multilevel (e.g., personal,
      ­intellectual, social) response. Go whole.
4 Reflect upon who you are now in this moment of your “me.” Then
       ­consider those individuals or beings that comprise your “we.” Take a
        photograph or find a drawing the symbolically represents the “me” and
        “we” of your life. Pause. Breathe. Give thanks.

       PART THREE

Applying Indigenous
