CHAPTER EIGHT

 Indigenous Theorizing




      My contribution was to add the word “theory” to Kaupapa Māori …
      The phrase “Kaupapa Māori theory” was my attempt not simply to try
      and capture the high knowledge status associated with the word the-
      ory; it was a strategic move to open up a powerful space for Māori
      in the academy. That has been a successful strategy; the space has
      largely been won. Kaupapa Māori theory, as well as related terms such
      as “Kaupapa Māori methodology,” are now seen as legitimate phrases
      shaping forms of academic inquiry.
                                           Graham Hingangaroa Smith (2012)



 Theory matters in research whether that research is qualitative, quan-
 titative, Indigenous, primary, secondary, human subject, theoretical, or
 applied research. Collins and Stockton (2018) offer insight into the func-
 tion of theoretical frameworks in research: “we distill them [theoreti-
 cal frameworks] to mean an explanation of the way things work. The
 source, size, and power of those explanations vary, but they all link back
 to an attempt to understand some phenomena” (p. 1). C      ­ onsciousness
 of theory in all research, including qualitative, quantitative, and Indig-
 enous, is vital. However, it is particularly key when it comes to Indige-
 nous research, as it can help the researcher avoid the deficit theorizing
 of Indigenous people, which happens regularly, intentionally or not,
 when Western theory is applied to Indigenous experience.
178   Applying Indigenous Methodologies


         This chapter begins by sketching, in broad brush strokes, the func-
      tion of theory in qualitative research as a prelude to the chapter’s
      focus on Indigenous theory. In exploring Indigenous theory from dif-
      ferent vantage points, this chapter highlights

      • Indigenous theory in Indigenous conceptual framing;
      • Indigenous theory’s emphasis areas that intersect and cross polli-
        nate, including an axiological-cultural focus (e.g., as found in an
        ­Anishinaabe mino-bimaadiziwin methodology), a decolonizing empha-
         sis (e.g., Indigenous resurgent approach), and a shared space–liminal
         space theorizing approach (e.g., two-eyed seeing approach); and
      • a conversation with Māori scholar Graham Hingangaroa Smith1
         on Kaupapa Māori theorizing within academic zones of contact.

      To communicate how theory functions in research methodology requires
      attention to terminology choice. Qualitative research is fraught with ter-
      minology that can obscure as much as it can illuminate. For example,
      terms such as conceptual framework and theoretical framework are abstract
      and can create confusion when we attempt to define them. Some people
      differentiate these terms while some folks use these terms interchange-
      ably. It can be a muddle. For example, Miles and ­Huberman (1994) state
      that a conceptual framework “can be rudimentary or elaborate, theory-
      driven or commonsensical, d    ­ escriptive or causal” (p. 18), but a conceptual
      framework implies a framework that reveals the relationship between
      all parts of the study. According to Miles and Huberman, a conceptual
      framework in research methodology is the whole of it. This definition is
      similar to how some ­methodologists define theoretical frameworks. The
      literature (­Anfara & Mertz, 2015; Bradbury-Jones et al., 2014; Collins &
      ­Stockton, 2018) tells us that there are at least two ways in which a theoret-
       ical framework is defined and functions in qualitative research:

      a. Influencing all aspects of the study. Anfara and Mertz (2015) describe
         a theoretical framework “as any empirical or quasi-empirical the-
         ory of social and/or psychological processes, at a variety of levels

      1   All of the interviews in this book were conducted for my doctoral research, Search-
          ing for Arrowheads: An Inquiry into Approaches to Indigenous Research Using a Tribal
          Methodology with a Nêhiyaw Kiskêýihtamowin Worldview (Kovach, 2006). Individuals
          requested that their names be associated with their interviews.
                                                        Indigenous Theorizing    179


   (e.g., grand, midrange, explanatory), that can be applied to the
   understanding of phenomena” (p. 27). While Anfara and Mertz
   define theory as narrower than a paradigm (and epistemology),
   they define theoretical frameworks as more far reaching than the-
   ory limited to specific methodological functions, such as for data
   analysis in a research design. Here, a theoretical framework is in
   operation from start to finish of the research (operating much like
   Miles and Huberman’s conceptual framework).
b. Limiting theory to specific or discrete aspects of methodology. For exam-
   ple, this occurs when scholars apply a theoretical lens to data ana­
   lysis. Applying theory to analysis is contested by some on the basis
   of subjectivity and contaminating the data. However, as Watling
   and Lingard (2012) pose, “Can existing theory be integrated into
   grounded theory research without ‘contaminating’ the analytic
   process? We believe that it can and should be integrated, but the
   approach to using existing theory remains controversial” (p. 858).

I see a theoretical framework as relevant in all aspects of research,
including epistemology, ethics, community, self, theory, methods,
meaning making, and dissemination. Because my definitions of con-
ceptual frameworks and theoretical frameworks are similar, in an
­effort to avoid confusion, I do not use the term theoretical framework in
 this chapter, but rely upon the terms theory or theorizing.
     In using the terms Indigenous theory and Indigenous theorizing, I note
 Kathy Charmaz’s (2010) distinction between theory and theorizing.
 She says, “My preference for theorizing – and it is for theorizing, not
 ­theory – is unabashedly interpretive. Theorizing is a practice” (p. 128).
  Like Charmaz, I have a preference for the term theorizing because
  it suggests an animate and active agency that transforms over the
  course of a life. Indigenous theorizing with its “ing” is active, alive, and
  evolving. The term theory tends to thingify.



THE FUNCTION OF THEORY IN QUALITATIVE RESEARCH

Theory in qualitative research is elusive. Qualitative health research-
ers Bradbury-Jones et al. (2014) conducted a study revealing the pres-
ence and absence of theory in qualitative research. They developed a
180   Applying Indigenous Methodologies


      typology (or framework) illustrating the extent to which researchers
      referenced theory in their publications of research findings. Through
      a scoping exercise of fifty-five qualitative research studies published
      in five different journals,2 they identified five different levels of theo-
      retical presence in the studies:

      1 theory is not mentioned at all;
      2 theory is implied but the influence of theory is not explicitly stated
        in the study;
      3 theory is partially implied in reference to research aims, research
        questions, and data analysis;
      4 theory is retrospectively implied as an afterthought; and
      5 theory is consistently applied throughout and guides the entire
        research process.

      Of the fifty-five articles, Bradbury-Jones et al. found twenty instances
      where theory was absent and nine articles where theory was consist-
      ently applied. The researchers indicate that this was a scoping, not a
      systematic, review and that it was based upon information the authors
      could identify from the published articles. Still, the authors’ “theoret-
      ical visibility typology” (Bradbury et al., 2014, p. 137) solidly shows
      that theory is more absent than present in qualitative research. When
      theory does appear, as revealed in this scoping exercise, it often does
      so in an ad hoc, inconsistent manner. Theory in qualitative research, it
      would seem, is a trickster and hard to tame.
         To tackle theory effectively in research requires making theory ex-
      plicit in a research project right at the “get-go.” Crotty (1998) states
      that “we bring a number of assumptions to our chosen methodology.
      We need, as best we can, to state what these assumptions are” (p. 7).
      Theory is a set of suppositions, or beliefs, about why something hap-
      pens in a certain way. Theory can also be predictive, explaining why
      something might happen in a certain way. As sentient beings, we de-
      velop theories about the world from living in the world through our
      experiences with family, schooling, social media, and the like. Formal


      2   The journals included Health and Social Care in the Community (HSCC), the
          ­International Journal of Nursing Studies (IJNS), Qualitative Health Research (QHR),
           Social Science & Medicine (SS&M), and BMJ.
                                                    Indigenous Theorizing   181


theories, as found in academia, are argued through scholarship. For
example, research with women by women about their experience has
given rise to feminist theory. Feminist theory has been applied to pre-
dict phenomena such as “the glass ceiling.” Theory is philosophical,
conceptual, and intellectual. Consciously or unconsciously, theory re-
veals our beliefs and influences our actions.
    Theory is not neutral because it is implicated in societal beliefs
(and biases), in how people relate to each other, and in the policies
influencing programs that impact people’s lives. Theory is political,
sociological, and ideological. Theory is an identity marker. People
dig their heels in when it comes to theory. People clash over theory.
­Contestations over whether theory matters and what a theory should
 entail happen all the time. On the upside, theory connects people.
 Theory can change worlds. People are passionate about their theories.
 If not polarizing, theory helps us feel alive and connected.
    Articulating theory can feel like unravelling a tightly tangled knot.
 However, it is through this untangling that we see how theory operates
 and how one theory is different from another (and where they might
 overlap). Maddening as this might be, taking a deep dive into theory
 is a signal that we are complicating the analysis by contemplating the
 distinctions between, and intersections of, different theoretical posi-
 tionings. Digging down into theory is the intellectual work required
 to achieve congruence between theory and method (“­thinking” and
 “doing”) in qualitative research design. This is no different in Indige-
 nous methodologies.



INDIGENOUS THEORIZING

Philosophically, Indigenous theory arises from an Indigenous episte-
mology and is couched within Indigenous ethics, community (includ-
ing land and place), and self. In reference to epistemology, Graham
Hingangaroa Smith (2006) states, “There is a critical need to under-
stand the epistemological roots that set the parameters around the no-
tion of Indigenous research – or to be more specific, Māori ­research,
Māori theory, Navajo theory, Cree research and so on” (p. 159).
­Epistemology, simply defined, is concerned with the questions of
 “what constitutes knowledge” and “how we acquire knowledge.” An
182   Applying Indigenous Methodologies


      Indigenous epistemology integrates several distinguishing character-
      istics functioning in a unified manner to answer these questions about
      knowledge. An Indigenous epistemology assumes that knowledges
      arise from multiple sources, a perspective commonly referenced as
      holism (scope); from an interconnected tangible and intangible ani-
      mate world that is process orientated and cyclical; and from a web
      of interdependent, contextual relationships. A delicate relationship
      exists between thinking and experience within Indigenous culture.
      ­Epistemological and experiential knowing travel hand in hand – often
       they are each other – from an Indigenous philosophical perspective.
       ­Experiential knowledge is organic and kinetic and embedded in the
        subjectivity of lived experience. As such, thought, feeling, and action
        are all respected. In the presence of a respect-based holism, dichot-
        omies dissolve. Because being and doing are intricately related, we
        can say that Indigenous epistemologies are holistic, animate episte-
        mologies that are axiological in nature (i.e., based on values, ethics,
        ­spirituality, and aesthetics). Indigenous theory flows from this episte-
         mological a­ ssumption concerning Indigenous knowledge production.
            Asserting Indigenous theory in Indigenous methodologies means
         that researchers are able to

      • distinguish between Indigenous epistemology and Indigenous
        theory (epistemology is focused on beliefs about what constitutes
        knowledge while theory is an individual’s belief about a phenom-
        enon, such as a research topic, and includes beliefs about inequi-
        ties, society, family, schooling, and so forth);
      • articulate Indigenous theory in their conceptual framing;
      • describe how Indigenous theory functions in the conceptual
        framing (e.g., does it influence ethical decisions, method choices,
        ­analysis, dissemination?);
      • define and articulate Indigenous theory through referencing
         ­Indigenous voices and scholarship; and
      • explain which aspects are being emphasized (axiological, decolo-
          nizing, shared) for the purposes of the research.

         A visual illustration (see Figure 8.1), revised from the more gen-
      eral one presented in Chapter 2, situates Indigenous theory in an
                                                        Indigenous Theorizing   183


Figure 8.1. Indigenous Conceptual Framework Illustrating the Role of Theory

           Indigenous Conceptual Framework in Research Study




                                                   Story & Methods
       Question & Purpose
                                             (including ethics approvals)


           Preparations
                                                    Interpretation
      (protocol & ceremony)    Indigenous
                                 Theory
                                                 Reciprocity through
           Literature                              Oral, Visual, &
                                                 Text Dissemination


                   Indigenous Theorizing Is Embedded in
                     the Four Foundations of Indigenous
                      Epistemology, Ethics, Community
                        (including land & place), & Self



Indigenous conceptual framework. In this example, theory is centrally
positioned to illustrate its influence on each aspect of the research.
    In reviewing the literature, Indigenous theorists often define the-
ory by both what it says and what it does. This is congruent with an
animate epistemology that does not sever the relationship between
the doer, the doing, and that which is done. I am reminded of Michel
­Foucault’s well-known quotation: “People know what they do; fre-
 quently they know why they do what they do; but what they don’t
 know is what they do does” (as quoted in Dreyfus & Rabinow, 1983,
 p. 187). Indigenous theorizing tends to underscore what the doing
 does. Considered below are insights from leading scholars who artic-
 ulate Indigenous theory.



VIEWS FROM INDIGENOUS THEORISTS

As Graham Hingangaroa Smith (2012) states, “‘Kaupapa Māori the-
ory’ and ‘Kaupapa Māori methodology’ are now seen as legitimate
phrases shaping forms of academic inquiry” (pp. 10–11). As a lead
thinker in Indigenous theorizing, Smith outlines Kaupapa Māori
184   Applying Indigenous Methodologies


      theory within a schooling context (although this theory is transferable
      to other disciplines and sites) and identifies its functions:

          i. It [Kaupapa Māori theory] promotes the validity and legitimacy
             of Māori language, knowledge and culture.
         ii. It creates the political space to enable the legitimate study and
             continuance of Māori language, knowledge and culture …
       iii. It is positioned as Māori centred, but also speaks c­ ross-culturally
             in the sense of making space for Māori ­language, knowledge
             and culture existence.
        iv. It is concerned with economics and structural change.
         v. It attempts to take account of unequal “power relations” and
             dominant/subordinate politics.
        vi. It is transformative in its aims.
       vii. It attempts to challenge existing theory as being culturally and
             interest laden.
      viii. It supports the use of all existing theory (by Māori) providing
             that it can positively support Māori advancement.
        ix. It recognizes that indigenous struggle is neither singular nor ho-
             mogenous; and that there is a need to “struggle” on several lev-
             els and in several sites, often simultaneously. (Smith, 2003, p. 11)

          Kaupapa Māori theory has culturally located origins, and it is
      change orientated, not universal but portable to other sites, flexible, not
      isolationist (i.e., engaged with other theoretical positionings), trans-
      formative, transferable to a variety of sites of struggle, accessible in its
      communication, and community engaged. Application of a K         ­ aupapa
      Māori theory requires a theorist steeped in Indigenous epistemic foun-
      dations broadly and in Kaupapa Māori knowledges specifically.
          Enfolded in theory, Lester-Irabinna Rigney (1999) identifies several
      guiding principles based upon what he terms as “liberation episte-
      mologies” (p. 114). In explicating liberation epistemologies, Rigney
      identifies a feminist methodology as a helpful point of reference to
      illustrate how a liberation epistemology is situated in a research meth-
      odology. Feminist epistemologies include women as researchers and
      subjects, and they incorporate women’s experiences, knowledges, his-
      tories, and epistemologies. From this theory perspective, women are
      defining research for themselves. Rigney indicates that, as in feminist
                                                    Indigenous Theorizing   185


resurgence research, Indigenist research is similarly informed by a
liberation epistemology. Except, of course, in Indigenous resurgent
research, the “liberation epistemology” is Indigenous. For Indigenous
resurgent research, Indigenous peoples are the focus for the purposes
of advancing self-determination, of achieving the political agenda of
liberation, and of making known the experiences and truths of an op-
pressed group. In his words, “I understand Indigenist research to be
informed by three fundamental and interrelated principles: resistance
as the emancipatory imperative in Indigenous research, political in-
tegrity in Indigenous research, [and] privileging Indigenous voices in
Indigenist research” (p. 116).
    Karen Martin (2003) builds upon Rigney’s principles in her artic-
ulation of theory for Indigenous and Indigenist research. Martin ex-
pands Rigney’s “liberation epistemology” approach by adding these
qualities: recognition of the Indigenous worldview, knowledges, and
reality as vital to Indigenous survival; recognition of social mores of
Indigenous life and learning; emphasis on the social, historical, and
political contexts that shape Indigenous experience; and the privi-
leging of Indigenous voice and experience as situated in Indigenous
lives and lands.
    Kathy Absolon (2010) stresses the Indigenous epistemologi-
cal foundations of Indigenous theory. Absolon states, “Indigenous
wholistic theory is wholistic and multi-layered, which encompasses
the spiritual, emotional, mental and physical elements of being. We
also acknowledge our past, present and future” (p. 74). In her re-
search, Sharon Hobenshield (2016) highlights the interrelationship of
theory and practice in Gitxsan culture through introducing the reader
to the hawaal. The hawaal binds the “the financial contribution to one’s
wilp to compensate for the use of the lax’yip (land).” As Hobenshield
shares, a hawaal is a gifting process – encompassing both belief and
practice – that exists within her community. “In this sense, the hawaal,
with its contributions from the wilp and other relative, clan and com-
munity members … models the responsibility and kinship ties the
community holds in adhering to a structure that solidifies a way of
life” (p. 72).
    Indigenous theorizing, as articulated by Indigenous scholars, re-
flects in some manner the following qualities. (This list is illustrative
not exhaustive.)
186   Applying Indigenous Methodologies


        Integration of Indigenous Epistemology (beliefs about knowledge
        creation)

        An Indigenous epistemology

        • is a non-fragmentary, relationist holism arising from multiple
          sources, including humans, non-human species, land, water,
          and sky;
        • does not ascribe to an anthropocentric-nature divide or a
          ­spirit-science disconnect;
        • values tangible and intangible knowledge sources; and
        • presupposes an animate relationship between knowledge
           ­product and knowledge producer, flux and balance.


        Community Collectivist Social Mores (ethical beliefs and
        ­axiological conditions)

        Indigenous theorizing exhibits

        • Indigenous ethical assumptions and a relational value base
          (e.g., respect, reciprocity, relevance, responsibility);
        • high empathy quotient practices (compassion, kindness, hu-
          mour, love);
        • high diplomacy quotients;
        • Indigenous sociocultural formations as articulated in commu-
          nity protocols (e.g., gifting, the protocol of introduction); and
        • relationist oracy in the presence of witnessing, including spo-
          ken, visual, and performative storytelling practice (e.g., song,
          dance, story, art).


        Colonial History (marginalization of Indigenous peoples)

        Indigenous theorizing addresses this history by

        • analysing power relations that have led to the marginalization
          of Indigenous peoples;
        • aiming for a transformative and resurgent accounting of power
          relations;
                                                      Indigenous Theorizing   187


  • asserting rights though non-polarizing means and relationships;
  • advancing a research perspective that is non-isolationist in dis-
    position and transferable to different disciplines and sites, both
    inside and outside the academy; and
  • addressing Indigenous historical, political, social, cultural,
    ­legal, and economic conditions.

  Experiential Knowledge (self in relation)

  Indigenous theorizing is inclusive of

  •   personal beliefs and perspectives;
  •   lived experience and life history;
  •   voice and community participation;
  •   self in relation to kin, community, and the larger world; and
  •   self in relation to the earth, sky, water, other sentient beings,
      and spirit.

    As a whole, Indigenous theory is vastly inclusive and can seem
overwhelming. The line between epistemology and theory blurs all
the time, and that is okay. Epistemology and theory are Western re-
search terms, and they bend towards compartmentalization and
fragmentation. That being said, it is useful to know theory because
theory is an attempt to “pull from” epistemology and give more spe-
cific focus. In an effort to provide theoretical focus, researchers and
scholars will often emphasize one or two qualities in their Indigenous
theorizing depending upon the purpose of the study, its site (i.e., dis-
cipline or sector), the community, and/or a researcher’s beliefs. This
makes sense because, like Indigenous identity, Indigenous theory is
not ­homogenously expressed.
    When we consider different articulations of Indigenous theory, it
holds that Indigenous theorizing integrates a set of foundational or
shared assumptions (as mentioned above), assumptions that a re-
searcher brings to interpreting a phenomenon. However, Indigenous
theorists – depending upon who they are and depending upon their
research question – will express an Indigenous theory with different
emphasis areas. Metaphorically, if Indigenous theory is a tree, the
shared or foundational assumptions of Indigenous theory are the tree
188   Applying Indigenous Methodologies


      trunk. The tree branches symbolize the emphasis areas, and so, a note
      or two about branches.



      EMPHASIS AREAS IN INDIGENOUS THEORIZING

      As Indigenous scholars highlight different emphasis areas in their
      theorizing, there is an expectation that emergent researchers will also
      focus their Indigenous theorizing. Upon reviewing Indigenous schol-
      arship, I discern at least three different emphasis areas, or branches, in
      Indigenous theorizing. These emphasis areas can be fluidly grouped
      as an Indigenous axiological-cultural focus, a decolonizing emphasis, and a
      shared space–liminal space theorizing approach. I am focusing on these ar-
      eas, but there are others, for example, Indigenous queer theory, Indige-
      nous land and place-based emphasis, and Indigenous feminist theory.
          I intentionally use the word emphasis because it implies a focus with-
      out denying the existence of a fluid, fluxing, intersectionality b­ etween
      the different emphasis areas (an Indigenous episteme u    ­ pholds interre-
      lationships and holism). For example, one emphasis area may empha-
      size Indigenous spirituality; however, this does not mean it is quiet
      on colonial oppression. All emphasis areas can be considered as part
      of a larger, unified Indigenous theory. It becomes the responsibility
      of researchers to identify clearly how they approach and explicate
      their Indigenous theorizing and why it assists in responding to their
      ­research questions.

      Axiological-Cultural Emphasis

      Indigenous theorizing with an Indigenous axiological-cultural em-
      phasis has, as a focus, Indigenous beliefs, values, and practices found
      within Indigenous cultures. From an Anishinaabe perspective, Kathy
      Absolon (2011) argues for Indigenous theory with an emphasis on
      Indigenous values. In reflecting upon Indigenous methodologies,
      Anishinaabe scholar Brent Debassige (2010) identifies an Anishi-
      naabe mino-bimaadiziwin (i.e., the good life) philosophical orientation
      that “calls on individuals to seriously pursue Anishinaabe mino-­
      bimaadiziwin (or tribal equivalent) within the context of ceremonies
      and academic studies simultaneously.” He goes on to say, “Traditional
                                                   Indigenous Theorizing   189


preparation and ongoing Traditional participation be at the heart of
the Indigenous researcher’s way of knowing” (p. 24). This ­theoretical/
philosophical orientation aligns with the Haudenosaunee concept of a
good mind consciousness as articulated by ­David Newhouse (2008).
    In referencing Canada’s report of the National Inquiry into
­Missing and Murdered Indigenous Women and Girls, Annita Het-
 oevėhotohke’e Lucchesi (2019) identifies Indigenous “spirit-based”
 research as upholding an Indigenous value-based theoretical ap-
 proach. Spirit-based research encompasses “heart-work,” which is
 “guided by compassion, empathy, prayer, and love” (p. 2). In their
 study on the value of relationships in pedagogy, Bishop et al. (2014)
 applied a Kaupapa Maōri approach and used whanaungatanga as a
 theoretical framing; the term is a metaphor for a research strategy
 that mirrors a family and extended family relational structure. The
 research team of Sasakamoose et al. (2017) applies an Indigenous
 culturally responsive theory based on miyo-pimātisiwin (i.e., in a
 good way) to their research into Indigenous health and well-being.
 In their framework, they highlight approaches to well-being that are
 trauma informed, strengths based, community engaged, and spirit-
 ually grounded.

Decolonizing, Anti-Colonial Emphasis

In Indigenous theorizing with a decolonizing anti-colonial emphasis,
the colonial power dynamic between Indigeneity and Eurocentrism,
as it exists within contemporary zones of contact, is the focus. For
example, from early contact the Eurocentric reaction was to dismiss
traditional Indigenous philosophies as superstitions (Deloria, 2002).
However, Steinhauer (2002), an early writer in the area of Indigenous
research, notes that this dismissal evolved into the labelling of In-
digenous philosophy as a culturally exotic fringe belief system rele-
gated to the purview of folklore scholars. Through such labelling, an
Indigenous belief system has been dismissed from the “real” work
of knowledge construction. Whether through outright dismissal or
a paternalistic diminishment, Eurocentric responses have led to the
systemic marginalization of Indigenous peoples. This is one example
of colonial oppression. A decolonizing emphasis in Indigenous the-
orizing interrupts the normative relations of power arising from the
190   Applying Indigenous Methodologies


      colonial enterprise, which continues to sideline Indigenous experi-
      ence wherever it occurs.
         Graham Hingangaroa Smith (2012) affirms the influence of critical
      theory, specifically the writings of Jürgen Habermas, in his articulation
      of Kaupapa Maōri theorizing. Métis scholar Adam Gaudry’s (2011)
      articulates how he positions an insurgent research approach embed-
      ded within a larger, unified Indigenous theory space: “(1) by explicitly
      employing Indigenous worldviews; (2) by orienting knowledge crea-
      tion towards Indigenous peoples and their communities; and (3) by
      seeing our responsibility as researchers as directed almost exclusively
      towards the community and participants” (p. 114). In Dian Million’s
      (2008) conceptualization of felt theory, she provides a critical analy-
      sis “within a dense web of gendered colonialism” (p. 268). Through
      gender analysis, she highlights the colonial violence on Indigenous
      women’s lives.
         Whether through unintentional, tokenizing exploitation or explicit
      hostility, deficit theorizing of Indigenous life has been disastrous for
      Indigenous peoples. Deficit theorizing is a colonial curating of Indig-
      enous life that serves nothing but Indigenous subjugation. A decolo-
      nial emphasis in Indigenous theorizing reveals the Indigenous-settler
      power relationship so as to advance the aim of social justice for Indig-
      enous peoples (Brayboy, 2005; Coulthard, 2014; Tuck & Yang, 2012).

      Shared Space–Liminal Space Emphasis

      Shared space–liminal space emphasis focuses on how differing
      worldviews might coexist together in a non-assimilative, respectful
      manner. It emphasizes the dynamic that exists between two cultural
      philosophies. ­Indigenous theorizing focusing on the peaceful coex-
      istence between Indigenous and Western worlds is highlighted here
      through these ­Indigenous philosophers and their works: Gloria Anza-
      ldúa (­Borderlands/La ­Frontera: The New Mestiza), Albert and Murdena
      ­Marshall and Cheryl ­Bartlett (“Two-Eyed Seeing”), and Willie Ermine
       (“The Ethical Space of Engagement”). It is a space of becoming.
          Gloria Anzaldúa was born in Texas on the southern border ­between
       the United States and Mexico. A Chicana scholar of mestizaje ancestry,
       Anzaldúa has undertaken intellectual work that casts light on the mul-
       tiplicity of marginalized identity positionings, such as race, gender,
                                                        Indigenous Theorizing    191


and sexuality, embodied within a singular self. In her theorizing, it
is the fusion of contradictory forces that creates angst while simul-
taneously powering transformation. In her 1987 book, B           ­ orderlands/
La Frontera: The New Mestiza, she introduces the notion of Coatlicue.
“Coatlicue is a rupture in our everyday world. As the Earth, she opens
and swallows us plunging us into the underworld where the soul
­resides, allowing us to dwell in darkness … ­Coatlicue depicts the con-
 tradictory” and “the fusion of opposites: the eagle and the serpent,
 heaven and the underworld, life and death, mobility and immobility,
 beauty and horror” (Anzaldúa, 1987, p. 46). While she ­focused on a
 gendered colonialism, Anzaldúa (2012), writing in later life, returned
 to a spirit-infused theory of how opposites merge and transform. She
 called this space Nepantla from the Nahuatl (i.e., ­Aztec) language.
 ­Nepantla is a place, she says, where you “haven’t got into the new
  identity yet and haven’t left the old identity behind either – you are
  in a kind of transition” (p. 276). Anzaldúa’s theorizing, ­influenced by
  Aztec philosophy, articulates a conceptual borderland of simultane-
  ous contradictory and affiliating coexistence.
      Weaving Indigenous and mainstream knowledges into an integra-
  tive science, Cheryl Bartlett and Micmaw Elders Murdena and Albert
  Marshall (2012) introduce the “Two-Eyed Seeing” theoretical approach,
  in their article of the same name. They offer an integrative science frame-
  work with several principles and practices. These principles i­nclude
  the vitalness of a co-learning journey; an openness to different ways
  of approaching science; an experiential approach; critical reflection on
  values, actions, and knowledges; and multiple ways of communicating
  science knowledge. The framework includes developing an advisory
  council. Within an Indigenous context, Elder Albert Marshall (2012)
  ­explains two-eyed seeing as the gift of being able to see many different
   perspectives: “it refers to learning to see from one eye with the strengths
   of Indigenous knowledges and ways of knowing, and from the other
   eye with the strengths of Western knowledges and ways of knowing,
   and to using both these eyes together, for the benefit of all” (p. 335). In
   their article, the authors propose an Indigenous theory to guide rela-
   tional encounters that involve working across difference. It is a theory
   emphasizing empathetic mores in service of mutual benefit.
      Akin to Anzaldúa, Bartlett, Marshall, and Marshall, Nêhiyaw
   scholar Willie Ermine (2007) offers an ethical space theory. Focusing
192   Applying Indigenous Methodologies


      on the coexistence of Indigenous and Western culture in a shared
      space, Ermine evokes an Indigenous cognizance in his theorizing: “It
      is the social, political and historical consciousness about existence,
      and a place in the universe that is valid and imbued with purpose
      and hence our cultural/political claims revolve around identity and
      issues of knowledge and power.” He goes on to say that it is a “mind-
      ful gaze informed by values, a moral structure, and a sincere interest
      for justice” (p. 199). From this vantage, he articulates an ethical space
      wherein difference can overcome the polarizing fear of the other. This
      is a space, he argues, that will allow us to “step out of our allegiances,
      to detach from the cages of our mental worlds and assume a position
      where human-to-human dialogue can occur” (p. 202). In this space of
      becoming we are on the threshold of peaceful coexistence.
           When it comes to research with Indigenous peoples, a methodo-
      logical challenge is to determine whether one’s theory is founda-
      tionally embedded in Indigenous epistemology and borrows from a
      ­Western tradition (e.g., critical theory) or whether one is applying the-
       ory arising from Western thought. If the theory syncs with an Indige-
       nous epistemic foundation and Indigenous voices are included in the
       ­articulation, it is Indigenous theorizing. Like Indigenous communities,
        Indigenous theory is complex, multifaceted, and nuanced by local con-
        text. Indigenous theory manifests through custom and moves through
        the intangible quality of story. Above all, Indigenous theory is.



      A CONVERSATION WITH GRAHAM HINGANGAROA
      SMITH

      What follows in an excerpt from my conversation with Māori scholar
      Graham Hingangaroa Smith.3 I was first introduced to Graham Smith,
      a distinguished professor of education, in 2005 through the SAGE
      (Supporting Aboriginal Graduate Enhancement) program that he
      was heading at UBC (University of British Columbia), a program that


      3   This excerpt from my interview with Graham Hingangaroa Smith (2006) is taken
          from Kovach (2009). The interview itself was conducted for my dissertation pub-
          lished in 2006. The institutional affiliation of the interviewees for my dissertation
          reflects that time period.
                                                       Indigenous Theorizing   193


continues to be an active, vibrant support network for graduate stu-
dents at UBC. Graham Smith has been influential in the establishment
and creation of Kaupapa Māori elementary schools and in advanc-
ing the discourse on Indigenous higher education internationally. He
has been central in developing a Kaupapa Māori research framework.
When I spoke with Graham, he reflected upon the trials confronting
Indigenous theorizing in Western academic contact zones.

  MAGGIE: Graham, what was your research approach to your PhD studies
      [i.e., what were the methodological and theoretical issues]?
  GRAHAM: I used an eclectic methodology; that is, I drew methodological
      insights from many streams. It’s probably important to understand
      my philosophy on this particular point about both theory and
      method. In my view, the Western academy has a number of
      theoretical models and tools embedded within it. Many of those
      tools come from different cultural roots, and many of those tools may
      well be useful in a universal way. My argument is that Indigenous
      peoples need to struggle over the academy. In this sense, there is a
      need to critically understand knowledge and pedagogy, for example,
      how “theory” is selected and privileged in the academy.
         I am particularly concerned to challenge the narrow,
      monocultural, interest-laden way in which particular theories and
      methodologies are produced, reproduced, and privileged inside
      the Western-oriented academy. Equally, I am also interested in how
      particular cultural knowledge and forms are excluded, marginalized,
      and denigrated within these same contexts of the public institution
      that is ostensibly addressing all forms of higher learning. More
      often than not, we would find in respect of our Māori situation that
      particular academic issues that we might have been working on,
      studying, or researching were unable to be addressed adequately
      by the existing range of theoretical tools and knowledge. That is,
      when we looked to the existing theoretical and methodological
      tools hanging on the wall of the university to help us understand
      and to deal with these Māori-specific issues, we would sometimes
      discover that the available tools sometimes did not completely fit
      the circumstances that we were dealing with. If you extend this
      idea a little bit further, my view was that we needed to put some
      Indigenous theory tools or, in a New Zealand sense, Māori tools on
194   Applying Indigenous Methodologies


             the wall of the university alongside of all the other theoretical tools
             and all the other research methodologies, so that we would have a
             more effective and wider choice of options. What I am arguing for
             is that there needs to be a space for our knowledge and our tools as
             well inside the academy. More often than not, we are using Western
             ideas, lenses, and tools to help us engage with our own culturally
             shaped issues. We also now have the added value and option of
             being able to use our own tools and our own ways of doing things.
             It is not an either/or situation, and I think this is a really important
             point to emphasize.
                There are several points that inform my perspective here. First, we
             need to struggle over the academy and to perceive the academy as a
             site of struggle – this calls us to think and act critically about the social
             “constructedness” of knowledge and the curriculum. Second, we as
             Indigenous peoples walk away from and disengage from the academy
             at our peril given that the academy performs the vital societal role of
             producing the elite knowledge in society. And thirdly, I would make
             the point that we need to add our own Indigenous knowledges and
             understandings to the existing range of theoretical and methodological
             “tools” that are currently available inside these institutions.
        MAGGIE: What has been the interface with the Western universities?
        GRAHAM: Oh, it’s been a struggle. Fundamentally, we are contesting
             at the level of knowledge, but we are also contesting a history
             of colonization and colonizing processes. If you understand
             schooling and education as “selection of knowledge” that is taught
             in institutions, and that dominant cultural groups can determine
             what knowledge is selected to be taught, then you will understand
             how schooling and education become sites for colonization and
             assimilation. The interests of the dominant white society at the
             university are able to be reproduced within the structures of
             social, political, and economic dominance, and so forth. In order to
             overcome Indigenous complicity in the reproduction of white social,
             economic, and political privilege, Indigenous academics need a
             philosophy that allows us to engage within the academy, within the
             ambit of what I call “the politics of truth.”
                The politics of truth is about knowing the limits and the capacities
             of what we as Indigenous scholars can and cannot achieve in the
             university context – it challenges us to stop bullshitting. There are
                                                     Indigenous Theorizing   195


many of our own Indigenous academic faculty, staff, and students
who argue for space for self-determination and sovereignty within
the academy. While the sentiment is laudable, there is a huge
contradiction here. When you look critically at such struggle, there
is an obvious contradiction of being simultaneously inside an
institution that is dominated and controlled by non-Indigenous
interests. That is, we need to appreciate the limits and capacities of
what can be achieved and, indeed, what should be achieved within
an institution that we do not own or have much power in. Thus,
doing a degree in a dominantly white institution requires Indigenous
scholars and faculty to make compromises all over the place. The
problem arises when Indigenous people do not own up to this fact.
Developing sovereignty and self-determination in an institution
where we do not have the power just doesn’t ring true. We need to
know the terrain on which we are struggling. We need to know the
limits and capacities of what can be achieved in particular sites. I
think we need to make strategic concessions to win what we can,
but the critical understanding here is that this is only one site of
struggle – we ought to be developing transformation in many sites.
   My own methodology has become quite significant in its own
right, and many students and researchers follow this pattern. My
methodology was to put myself as a Māori researcher at the centre
of the project and all that this entails. I argue for subjectivity as
being a more honest position. I declare openly that I am arguing
for my language, knowledge, and culture and against reproducing
colonizing forces in my research. I name these things overtly. I wrote
my personal story at the front of my thesis in order to lay bare my
biases and cultural nuances, preferences, [and] prejudices. That is, all
the things that make me up as a Māori academic, and that contribute
to constructing my worldview and my perspective. In this way, I am
saying, “Well, this is me, I am trying to be neutral, but I can’t be
because I am Māori. I am trying to be objective, but I can’t be. So, this
is me, warts and all. The onus for issues of objectivity and neutrality
are for you, the reader, to work out. You can read my text against
knowing my personal background and then make up your own
mind about the validity and legitimacy of my arguments.”
   Every thesis is written laden with the author’s own interests that
are often submerged in the text, yet researchers continue to make
196   Applying Indigenous Methodologies


             arguments that they are objective and neutral and therefore carry out
             this pretence that their own interests have not tainted their work. It’s
             a load of bull, of course. As a Māori, my methodology should be true
             to my own traditions, and these deserve to be laid out up front. In
             the end, I was using Western and Indigenous theory and method as
             appropriate.
                I want to elucidate this issue of using Western and Māori tools a
             bit more. The example of Gramsci’s “hegemony” helps me clarify
             this point here. The critical notion of hegemony is a great tool for
             understanding how Indigenous peoples become complicit in forming
             their own oppression and exploitation. It helps us to understand
             how common sense itself can become co-opted. As such, it is a useful
             tool to allow this insight. It helps Māori understand the processes
             of colonization, and that’s all. I am not going to say Western theory
             is useless, that it’s white man’s knowledge and we shouldn’t use it
             and all that stuff. That’s a load of bull – we need to use all the very
             best available theoretical and methodical tools, and where necessary
             develop new approaches when these tools are inadequate.
                Kaupapa Māori is Māori-centred. It is Māori cultural, Māori
             political, Māori social – a Māori-centric positioning. It reinforces
             Māori academic work because it takes for granted Māori language,
             knowledge, and culture as not only being valid but also being
             important. It acknowledges that Māori language, knowledge, and
             cultural interests are at the centre of the project. It also connects with
             the epistemological foundations and basis in the way we think, act,
             and live out our culturally preferred values.
                The politics is another dimension that is important here because
             within the Kaupapa Māori framework, we really need to understand
             the politics of colonization, although I don’t like to talk about
             colonization. This is because such expression (colonization) puts
             the colonizer at the centre of the discourse, and we are positioned
             to become reactive. I prefer to use the term and talk about
             conscientization rather than colonization because such a term is more
             positive. It puts a focus on us at the centre rather than the colonizers,
             and it also centres concerns about our development.
                The point here is that we need to learn the critical illiteracies that
             are required to unpack all of these colonizing processes. Doing a
             thesis and doing intellectual work is a political process if you are
                                                          Indigenous Theorizing   197


      from a minority cultural group because you are often working in an
      institution that is ruled by the dominant societal groups. There is a
      need to have a way in which to defend yourself with some critical
      and political understandings.
  MAGGIE: When we bring Indigenous methodologies into the academy,
      what is the risk?
  GRAHAM: There is risk, and we definitely should be careful around
      this. But what I say to my students is that you have to question
      why you are bringing it in. There is some Indigenous knowledge
      which is already out there. In this sense, it’s already public domain
      knowledge in our own communities and perhaps even known and
      accessible in non-Indigenous contexts. On the other hand, there is
      some knowledge which is regarded as being sacred or restricted,
      and if bringing such knowledge into the academy is going to cause
      angst to others, then it shouldn’t be brought in. The reason I say
      this is because I can’t guarantee that this particular institution,
      for example, can look after it, treat it with respect, and preserve it
      in the way that you need it to be preserved, taking regard of the
      community’s expectations. It’s unfortunate, but I would rather
      that you keep such knowledge safe by keeping it outside. There
      are plenty of other things to write about, research, and study for
      a thesis that do not open up Indigenous knowledge to disrespect,
      exploitation, and colonizing.
         [As Indigenous researchers,] you have to make some decisions
      as well, take some responsibility about guardianship. Why would
      you put sacred knowledge at risk within an academic institution?
      Indigenous researchers need to act responsibly, as well. Why put
      at risk our culture and knowledge for the sake of an individual
      thesis or research project? Are you just opening it up and making it
      vulnerable? I think there is some onus on ourselves here. There is a
      need for us to exercise some agency. Then again, critical theory gets
      me to think like that, so this is an example of how critical theory can
      assist us. The point being that it [critical theory] helps us make space
      for ourselves, our culture, our ways of thinking.


  Graham Smith affirms that theoretical tools like critical theory are
useful to Indigenous researchers, but it is not enough to stop there.
Deciding amongst theories from Indigenous and Western traditions is
198   Applying Indigenous Methodologies


      not, as Graham states, an either/or proposition; rather, he advocates
      for a more inclusive approach to research within academic zones of
      contact that respects Indigenous theorizing and methodology. There
      must be Indigenous methodological tools and Indigenous ­theorizing.
      At this juncture, it helps to have a “good think” on whether one is doing
      Indigenous theorizing or theorizing about Indigenous ­peoples. With-
      out Indigenous theory, are researchers consciously or s­ ubconsciously
      engaging in deficit theorizing? A truth-talk with the researching self
      requires courage, but this is a necessary conversation. With theory, the
      adage “do no harm” is relevant.
          Inevitably, the question arises, “Who can do Indigenous theoriz-
      ing?” Indigenous theory is bigger than identity politics – but con-
      scious of it. Therefore, it is not one’s Indigenous bloodline that defines
      whether one can proceed with Indigenous theory but rather one’s
      relationship with Indigenous epistemology, ethics, and community.
      That being said, one must be sensitive to the politics of Indigenous
      theorizing and clarify the self-identity positioning from which one
      does research and under what constraints. For those engaging in In-
      digenous methodologies, the message is clear: Know your theory,
      what your theory does, and who you are in your theorizing.
          In considering Graham Smith’s articulation of Indigenous t­ heory
      in light of the academy, I view Indigenous scholars’ desire to trans-
      form the academy as being motivated by at least three factors:
      (a) to carry on, borne by historical momentum, the anti-colonial
      struggle against settler sentiment and action; (b) to make visible
      the c­ onnection ­between cultural sustainability, Indigenous rights,
      and higher e­ ducation; and (c) to bring Indigenous bodies, episte-
      mology, and theory into the ­academic spaces. Graham Smith gives
      voice to the “politics of truth” in my conversation with him. He
      spells out the contradictory nature of Indigenous higher education,
      a zone where trust waxes and wanes. Yet, amid these contradictions,
      the i­ntellectual work of Indigenous theorizing offers Indigenous
      ­peoples one more powerful “tool” in the pursuit of transformative
       praxis and social justice.
          As I close this chapter, a warm memory of a rainy day resurfaces,
       and suddenly I am back in time. I am working at the Caring for First
       Nations Children Society. It is a damp winter day in Victoria. I am
       at a Fort Street restaurant lunching with a friend. Eating a chicken
                                                         Indigenous Theorizing   199

Cesar salad, I am in a liminal space and say to my friend that I feel
a shift coming in. I tell her, “Maybe I’ll go back to school to get my
PhD … I am not sure what I’ll do.” I tell her that I need a respite – to
read books, contemplate, do some theory work. I look outside the
window. The rain falls through the grey skies washing the green
shrubbery and leaves. Trees absorb the raindrops while water splat-
ters off concrete buildings and asphalt streets. Out the window, it is
shades of grey with green. In this moment, I am lost in a jumble of
thoughts. Crunching lettuce, I think of the history and theories that
have shaped this urban space. The cityscape conjures a past of min-
ers, adventurers, and settlers feverishly extracting resources from this
place with the Salish, Songhees, Saanich, and Sooke holding fast to
the sacredness of trees. Two truths, two theories destined to clash.
I swallow my salad, and the rain splashes down outside. My lunch-
mate and I return from our thoughts. My pal smiles at me as I talk
about going back to school. She says, “Academia, that’s all theory
smeary. Why do you want to go back to that place?” I laugh and say,
“No doubt.” The memory recedes, and it is a Tuesday morning in
Vancouver. I am back to my world, and the rain is falling outside
my window as theories spark and illuminate my sky and I am spell-
bound in their wake.

CHAPTER NINE

 Analysing, Interpreting, and Meaning
 Making




      Ways of knowing are like a pebble thrown into a pond: Once I began
      my journey of inquiry, I began making connections. These connec-
      tions eventually all converged in one place – my place or being in the
      universe. My being is dependent upon my participation; my existence
      is determined by my participation. I am an interdependent being.
      I am a pebble and my participation in the universe has a ripple effect
      outward. At the same time, my existence is determined in an inward
      move by my participation.
                                                              Bastien (2005)



 Indigenous epistemology and theory are inseparable and influence
 all research choices and actions in Indigenous methodologies. As a
 unified force, they create a ripple outward. In interpreting, analysing,
 and making meaning in research, Indigenous theorizing, as articu-
 lated through individual understanding, permeates and influences
 the ­impact and relevancy of Indigenous research findings. Chapter 9
 starts by discussing the relationship between theory and analysis and
 the function of Indigenous theory in analysing findings. The chap-
 ter then turns to how Indigenous theorizing might be applied to a
 thematic analysis strategy using a coding process. The second half of
 Chapter 9 highlights truth and trustworthiness in research, and then
 the chapter closes with words from Indigenous scholar Laara Fitznor.
202   Applying Indigenous Methodologies


      Laara Fitznor is of Nêhiyaw ancestry from northern Manitoba and a
      faculty member in the Faculty of Education, University of Manitoba.



      THEORY, THEORY, THEORY

      If a theory frames a research study at the outset, it should influence
      all phases of the study. When a project is complete, we ought to see
      how the theory choice makes sense given the research question,
      how ­theory guides the data collection, and how theory is applied
      to ­analysis. ­Dissemination products (e.g., reports) should discuss
      the role of ­theory. For instance, a report should describe how theory
      is integrated into analysis, informs the presentation, and contextu-
      alizes the findings within relevant theoretical literature (Wu et al.,
      2016, p. 498).
           Theories, in a nutshell, help us to interpret data. Our chosen theory
      is the grounding from which to say, “I have interpreted the findings
      from this theoretical lens (e.g., Indigenous, feminist), and these are
      my assumptions; this is my interpretation.” It is as deceptively simple
      as that. Working with theory to analyse findings requires attention
      and intention. Working with theory can be maddening and magical.
      As ­Charmaz (2010) says, “Theories flash illuminating insights and
      make sense of murky musings and knotty problems. The ideas fit.
      ­Phenomena and relationships between them you only sensed before-
        hand become visible.” Charmaz goes on to say, “Still theories can do
        more. A theory can alter your viewpoint and change your conscious-
       ness” (p. 128). B­ ecause applying theory in analysis can be inferential
       and enigmatic, Box 9.1 outlines several questions and considerations
       that may assist you to follow a systematic process when applying
       theory in analysis.
           Attention to theory in research is important. Deficit theorizing
       of ­ Indigenous people and culture has, in part, occurred because
       ­researchers are not paying enough attention to the theorizing in their
        research and how it might impact Indigenous peoples. Those involved
        in Indigenous methodologies, and in Indigenous research generally,
        must pay attention to theory. A theoretically framed analysis gives us
        a way to do this.
                               Analysing, Interpreting, and Meaning Making   203



BOX 9.1 APPLYING THEORY TO ANALYSIS

• Have you thought through how a theoretical perspective (e.g.,
  critical race theory, Indigenous theory) informs the analysis of
  your research findings? If not, why not? Through a free writing
  activity, journal your thoughts on your resistance to theory.
• Once you are ready to consider theorizing, do you know what
  theoretical perspective you are applying in your research
  ­analysis? Is this theoretical approach consistent throughout your
   research?
• In one or two sentences, identify the key focus of the theory you
   are using for your analysis. (For example, if your chosen theory is
   critical race theory, a statement may look like this: A key focus of
   critical race theory is to examine why racism exists and its impact
   on individual experience and larger society.)
• Write a concise overview (a bulleted list of several aspects or
   ­values) on your chosen theory, and select three different asser-
    tions associated with that theoretical perspective. For exam-
    ple, Plummer offers a concise and specific overview of several
    assertions arising from queer theory. I have highlighted a few
    specifics:
         “Both the heterosexual/homosexual binary and the sex/­
         gender split are challenged.
         All sexual categories (lesbian, gay, bisexual, transgender,
         heterosexual) are open, fluid, and nonfixed” (as quoted in
         Creswell, 2007, p. 29).
• After you have produced a concise overview of your theoretical
    stance (assertions), briefly explain why you have chosen this the-
    oretical stance/lens to analyse your research findings. Can you
    state clearly why you think this theory is the most suitable to the
    context of your research? Can you identify its gaps (all theorizing
    has gaps)?
• Once you have identified a concise and specific overview of your
    chosen theory, explain how you will keep this concise and specific
    overview in mind while you are organizing and analysing your
    findings. How will your theory influence your interpretations?
204   Applying Indigenous Methodologies


      INDIGENOUS THEORY IN ANALYSING FINDINGS

      Indigenous theorist Dian Million (2014) argues that Indigenous epis-
      temic systems give rise to an Indigenous theory and that Indigenous
      theory exists. She says,

           As I have written elsewhere, American Indian, First Nations, and
           Indigenous scholars recognize orally based communal knowledge
           as organized epistemic systems that do exist and whose influence
           is active even though they might not be recognized as legitimized
           by academia. These systems are theories since they posit a propo-
           sition and a paradigm on how the world works. (p. 35)


      The Indigenous conceptual framework presented in this book (see
      Chapter 2) illustrates the influential role of Indigenous theory. Where
      Indigenous theory is influential, the researcher will provide an artic-
      ulation of Indigenous theory couched in community voice and schol-
      arly works associated with that theoretical perspective. There will
      be a strategy or method for interpreting the stories and identifying
      patterns (e.g., coding, categorizing, documenting epiphanies, find-
      ing themes) and for employing Indigenous theory (i.e., Indigenous
      ­thinking) to gain further insight and understanding of the research
       topic. However, there are at least a couple of key considerations for
       researchers working with theory, especially in meaning making.
           A primary consideration is that Indigenous theorizing in interpret-
       ing research stories (or data) is a contextualized analysis that is influ-
       enced by personal, particular, and experiential nuance. Spradley charts
       a six-level grid moving from the general/abstract to the ­particular/
       contextual. Level one of this six-level grid denotes statements that
       are equal to universal statements while level six encompasses “spe-
       cific incident statements: writing that takes the reader immediately
       to a particular behaviour or particular events, demonstrating cultural
       knowledge in action” (as quoted in Coffey & Atkinson, 1996, p. 117).
       In using Spradley’s grid, Indigenous theorizing intersects with each
       level but underscores a level-six analysis because of the value placed
       upon the personal and particular in an Indigenous episteme.
           Another important consideration is that before we proceed with any
       theory in Indigenous research, it is pertinent to ask ourselves about
                                  Analysing, Interpreting, and Meaning Making   205


community. In analysing findings, am I consulting with community?
Have I reached out to a community advisory committee as I arrive at
findings? If not, why not? Is it time to ask for an “update” meeting
with the committee? The community might not actually wish (or have
the time available) to take a “hands-on” role associated with analysing
data, but one’s community partners ought to have the choice to partic-
ipate and advise. Communication is the key. Through connection with
community and asserting Indigenous theory, I­ ndigenous peoples find
ground to stand upon in a shifting world of knowledge production.
    Waking up to a new fully decolonized day is still in the future,
and the process is more fluid and incremental, with “strategic con-
cessions” all over the place. After all, co-dependency is one wicked
little web from which to disentangle oneself. However, we are mak-
ing headway by asserting Indigenous thinking through Indigenous
theorizing in arriving at research findings. As we nod to Indigenous
theory and do the hard work of coming to know why we are doing
what we are doing, it feels good. Pause and enjoy the feeing. Because
next comes the how.
    Research analysis within qualitative approaches typically requires
organizing data for the purpose of revealing patterns. In many in-
stances, this process is assisted by a theoretical perspective. (There
are many theoretical perspectives available, such as critical theory,
Indigenous theory, postmodern theory, and so forth.) The challenge
with analysis is that it can decontextualize knowledge through the
organizational act of sorting data. In analytic methods or strategies
that involve thematic grouping, sorting often involves working with
transcripts to arrive at a “meaning unit” (Ely et al., 2001, p. 162).
­Depending upon the qualitative methodology, there may be more
 or less emphasis on contextual interpretation. The long and short
 of it – research, by its nature, can be extractive and bend more to-
 wards the deductive than the inductive. Yet it matters that, whatever
 our strategy, we, as researchers, arrive at findings – whether that be
 through thematic analysis, coding, or otherwise – with care and in-
 tention. Further, it is critical that researchers carry out analysis with a
 critically reflexive consciousness that has community context in mind,
 that allows for the personal and particular of the experiential, that has
 room for abductive knowledges, and that is committed to a search for
 knowledge that will serve.
206   Applying Indigenous Methodologies


      A SPOTLIGHT ON THEMATIC ANALYSIS IN INDIGENOUS
      METHODOLOGIES

      A note: The analytical strategy introduced in this chapter is a the-
      matic analysis. Within Indigenous methodologies, finding meaning
      in story does not have to rely upon a thematic analysis. For example,
      in analysing findings for my dissertation, I integrated vignettes (i.e.,
      excerpts) from the stories I heard. I then articulated teachings from
      the stories that resonated with me. I also identified themes (through
      coding). Since that time, I have worked consistently with thematic
      analysis. My experience tells me that thematic analysis through a
      coding process is a common approach for many Indigenous qualita-
      tive researchers, that this is the path to their findings. When one bases
      research on Indigenous theorizing (philosophy), a thematic analysis,
      even though it is an established academic tradition, can be consist-
      ent with an Indigenous conceptual framing. A thematic analysis is
      one approach, not the approach. For example, the “self-referent” ap-
      proach of Debassige (2013, p. 16), the self as embedded in Indigenous
      knowledge systems, is one used within Indigenous methodologies.
      As mentioned, there are many paths to the centre of the circle. I look
      forward to learning of new ways to think about meaning making
      with oral history and story.
         Thematic analysis is a common analytical strategy in qualitative
      research. Braun and Clark (2006) describe thematic analysis as a
      ­
      method of working with data via transcripts, a method that analyses,
      identifies, and reports patterns in that data. They argue that thematic
      analysis is widely used in research, and while there is no prescribed
      rule book for how to go about a thematic analysis, what is impor-
      tant is that it is applied in a systematic way, that the chosen applica-
      tion is consistent in a research project, and that the researcher clarify
      the chosen thematic analysis technique. They indicate that thematic
      analysis can be “data-driven” (i.e., emergent), in other words, based
      on themes arising from the data, or theoretically framed (a priori),
      that is, based on preconceived themes. Braun and Clark (2006) iden-
      tify a helpful six-step process for thematic analysis. This begins with
      an initial phase of familiarizing oneself with the data. The next steps
      are generating initial codes, searching for themes, reviewing themes,
      ­
      defining and naming the names, and producing a report.
                                   Analysing, Interpreting, and Meaning Making    207


    In focusing on the analytical aspect of research, Kirby et al. (2006)
say that within social science research there is an analytical ladder
that includes epistemology, theory, contemporary and social contexts,
and lived experience. They further suggest that inductive qualitative
research utilizes this conceptual framework to organize, understand,
and record data. Ely et al. (2001) stress that researchers are consist-
ently describing, analysing, and interpreting data throughout the en-
tirety of the research process. Figure 9.1 presents this reflexivity as
applied to thematic analysis within Indigenous methodologies (IM).
In that figure, “Thematic Analysis Strategy for IM,” note that the ap-
proach is more circular and less like a ladder; it is not a linear process
but cyclical, and moves back and forth with portals for experiential
and abductive insights. Any thematic strategy that integrates Indig-
enous theorizing (such as the one outlined in Figure 9.1) gives space,
credence, and opportunity for Indigenous thinking and theory that is
of relevance to Indigenous communities in specific ways. This is an
ethos throughout all aspects of an Indigenous methodology project.
Box 9.2 provides a brief explanation of the terms used in the thematic
analysis strategy for IM (Figure 9.1).
    Braun and Clark (2006) say that while thematic analysis is rela-
tively straightforward, there are at least four pitfalls that can befall the
researcher in carrying out a thematic analysis. They are worth men-
tioning. The first pitfall is the failure to analyse the data. This happens
when researchers present quotations and data without completing an
analysis. In this situation, the researcher is describing the findings,
not analysing them. The second pitfall is related to a failure to analyse
as well. This happens when a researcher uses prompt questions in
an interview and then presents the prompt questions (as identified
in the interview schedule or instrument) as themes. The third p       ­ itfall,
according to Braun and Clark (2006) “is a weak or unconvincing
analysis, where the themes do not appear to work, where there is too
much overlap between themes, or where the themes are not internally
­coherent and consistent” (p. 94). A final pitfall that Braun and Clark
 mention occurs when there is a disconnect between the data and the
 analysis, or, in other words, when the claims, arising from the ­thematic
 analysis, are not supported by the data.
    I have typically undertaken thematic analysis with a coding pro-
 cess. Coding is, at its core, about assigning a code, such as a word

         BOX 9.2 EXPLANATION OF TERMS IN THEMATIC ANALYSIS FOR IM

         A Priori Framing: The process begins by establishing key touchstones
         from an Indigenous theoretical perspective. These touchstones will
         be called upon for the theoretical framing throughout the analytical
         process.
            1st Level Analysis: Transcripts of stories are reviewed keeping
         Indigenous theory touchstones in mind. In this first-level analysis,
         we are seeing what is there. When using a coding process, the
         researcher begins to identify concepts and assigns codes through
         open coding. (More information about open coding is in Box 9.3
         in this chapter.)
                                 Analysing, Interpreting, and Meaning Making   209



       Relational Analysis: This is a second level of meaning making in
which we begin to see, and establish, relationships between the
findings of the first-level analysis. Winnowing occurs here. The sto-
ries are gifting you. The researcher moves back and forth between
transcripts, initial findings, Indigenous theorizing, field notes, and
emerging patterns. If the researcher is using a coding process, this
step is called axial coding. (More information about axial coding is
in Box 9.3 in this chapter.)
       Intentional Interpretation with Theory (a priori): Review the
patterns emerging in light of the key touchstones of Indigenous
theoretical framing. Are you thinking Indigenous? This is a good
time to pause, to attend ceremony, or go for tea with an Elder – to
connect with community. Allow for the inductive and ­abductive.
This is an opportune time to return to the teachings. In an
­Indigenous higher education study (Kovach et al., 2015), our team
 invited Nêhiyaw Elder Joseph Naytowhow to guide us in work-
 ing with stories. Elder Joseph led us in a sweat-lodge ceremony,
 and it was here that the conceptual framing for the findings took
 root (see Chapter 10). Find your way to embed your analysis in
 an ­Indigenous consciousness and Indigenous thinking. Begin to
 think about non-textual ways to present your findings: ­metaphors
 and symbolic imagery (we will discuss the presentation of
 ­findings in Chapter 10).
       Arriving at Themes: From the categories and patterns, identify
  the several key themes of your research. The themes will not
  be ­specific, and there may only be four or five central themes
  or ­patterns. However, the themes will arise from the specificity
  of analysis (or through codes if you are using them). If you are
  ­intentionally staying close to Indigenous theory, community
   guidance, and the personal and particular, the themes will have
   relevance to Indigenous communities. Once you have arrived at
   themes, this is the time to consider how you will present your
   ­findings in a way that serves your research and community.
       “Coyote Codes”: Remember coyote codes. They are the outlier
    findings. Outlier findings can give significant insight. Find a way to
    include or recognize them.
210   Applying Indigenous Methodologies


      or phrase, to communicate the relationship between two things. To
      code is to interpret and catalogue objects, ideas, and events and assign
      meaning. Coding is relational, but it can fragment knowledge. Coding
      is about organizing information in a specific way to communicate in-
      formation for a specific intent. The next section briefly discusses codes.



      CODING FOR THEMES IN INDIGENOUS
      METHODOLOGIES

      Reflective Pause. The following act of coding story in research gives
      me pause. The term coding is antithetical to working with story, and
      I struggle with language as I write this section. In my mind, Indige-
      nous story work is on one street with coding and analysis on another
      street diametrically across from it. There is one wide space in between.
      I seem to be standing at an intersection on Red Road waiting for a
      sign to cross – how to proceed with writing about codes in Indige-
      nous methodologies. No sign is coming, and I leave my writing for the
      day. At night, I let the predicament sink into my subconscious during
      dreamtime. The next morning, the alarm clock goes off, and I wake
      up, brush my teeth, pour a cup of coffee, and settle at my computer.
      I don’t want to write today, but I know I have to face the blank screen
      on my computer because the only way through is through. Next thing,
      without consciousness of the shift, a switch has flipped. I do not know
      what has happened, but I am in motion. My desktop search engine is
      open, and my fingers are swiftly keyboarding “Navajo Code Talkers”
      into Google search.
         The Navajo Code Talkers assisted the United States Marines dur-
      ing World War II in fighting battles in the Pacific theatre, including
      at the Battle of Iwo Jima. English words were communicated through
      an unbreakable Navajo language coding system. The codes were
      then interpreted by Navajo Code Talkers on the frontline. For exam-
      ple, the military word “ships” was translated into the Navajo word
      “toh-dine-ih.” The Navajo code of “toh-dine-ih” was then transmitted to
      the front line where it was translated back into English as “sea force.”
      “Submarine” was translated into “besh-lo” in Navajo and then back into
      English as “iron fish” (Silversmith, 2018). The Navajo codes were trans-
      mitted through Morse code and rapidly interpreted by Code Talkers.
                                  Analysing, Interpreting, and Meaning Making   211


Broderick (2011) wrote how this process was embedded in a Navajo
holistic episteme that honoured both empirical and spiritual wisdom:

     In interviews, Code Talkers relate how their code worked: A
     message was given to them in written English, which they trans-
     lated into the unique Navajo code, and then safely transmitted
     in Morse Code. At the end of his training, a Code Talker could
     receive, translate and send a three-line message in a mere twenty
     seconds. The Code Talkers explained that they did not just mem-
     orize one code; they had to commit to memory three separate
     codes. After training, these young soon-to-be warriors returned
     home where they took part in traditional Protection Way cere-
     monies designed to protect them in battle. True Whisperers [about
     Navajo Code Talkers] reenacts one of these ceremonies, as the
     voices of the Code Talkers relate how the elders in their commu-
     nities performed songs, prayers and rituals for the young man’s
     safe return. Each young marine was given symbolic objects to
     wear on his body as a talisman. (p. 90)


    There is a history of Indigenous peoples using codes with agility.
As a process for organizing and communicating, codes have been
used in Indigenous communities. Coding is one way of making mean-
ing, but it is, indeed, a way that has historically aligned with an Indig-
enous ethos. As I contemplate coding in Indigenous methodologies,
the weathered faces and spirit of the Diné grandfathers fill my writing
room: “Carry on Nôsisim,” they say.
    In considering codes as they factor into thematic analysis in Indig-
