enous methodologies, I have selected key terms from the extensive
scope of qualitative analysis terminology. These terms can assist you if
you decide to follow a thematic analysis strategy (Figure 9.1) that uses
a coding process. Box 9.3: “Terminology in Coding” presents defini-
tions of key coding terms as emergent and a priori. If you use coding,
it is helpful to keep Figure 9.1, Box 9.2, and Box 9.3 nearby.
    Blair (2015) writes in his article “A Reflexive Exploration of Two
Qualitative Data Coding Techniques”: “In the end there was no ‘best’
method and I adopted a combined approach as this seemed more
pragmatic, and because I was more comfortable with what I felt it
offered” (p. 26). Coding techniques and approaches in service of a
212   Applying Indigenous Methodologies



         BOX 9.3 TERMINOLOGY IN CODING

          Term         Description
          Code*        According to Saldaña (2009), “A code in qualitative
                       inquiry is most often a word or short phrase that
                       symbolically assigns a summative, salient, essence-
                       capturing, and/or evocative attribute for a portion
                       of language-based or visual data” (p. 3). To code is to
                       say, “This means that.” If we think of the Navajo Code
                       Talkers, code talking is the use of one word (Navajo
                       code word) to show the relationship between
                       two other words. Effective coding is intentional,
                       purposeful, clear-minded, and communicative.
                       There are two distinctive approaches to coding:
                       emergent and a priori (Blair, 2015).
          Emergent     Emergent coding is the term used for codes that
          Coding       emerge or are drawn from data in the form of
                       text, which are most commonly transcripts from
                       interviews. A phrase associated with emergent
                       coding is “from the ground up” (I like to think “from
                       the earth up”). This means the code arises from
                       the data with researcher subjectivity and theory
                       bracketed out as much as possible. Emergent
                       coding has been associated with objectivism and
                       empirical qualitative research and is most frequently
                       associated with the grounded theory method.
                       Open and axial coding are also associated with both
                       emergent coding and the grounded theory method.
          A Priori     A priori coding is the term used when a theoretical
          Coding       lens is determined prior to analysing data texts
                       (e.g., transcripts). The theoretical lens determined
                       beforehand is considered a starting place for analysis.
                       The a priori theoretical lens is based on concepts
                       or codes arising from previous studies, literature, or
                       existing theoretical assumptions. For example, an a
                       priori provisional (beforehand) guide arises from, and
                             Analysing, Interpreting, and Meaning Making   213



            aligns with, the theoretical paradigm (e.g., touchstones)
            for the study. The provisional guide provides a theoret-
            ical framing in determining the codes. A phrase associ-
            ated with a priori coding is “from above” or “a view from
            the sky.” A priori coding is associated with subjectivism
            and interpretivist qualitative research. Types of coding
            that are associated with a priori coding include structural
            and provisional coding (Saldaña, 2009). I have tended to
            use a priori coding in my ­research analysis.
Open        When we first code a transcript, this first phase is
Coding      most often termed open coding. Open coding can
            be conceptual or literal. Conceptual coding means
            identifying the main ideas, or concepts, that arise
            from data. Literal coding means coding each line of
            the data in a transcript. There is a range of different
            types of coding approaches (e.g., descriptive
            coding, narrative coding, values coding, and so
            forth). During open coding, many specific codes are
            assigned.
Axial       This second phase of coding, during which
Coding      relationships and categories emerge, is commonly
            termed axial coding. The process of grouping codes
            into categories requires the researcher to assess and
            decide which codes will form a category. Often, this
            process will involve re-sorting and re-categorizing
            your data. Patterns in the data begin to reveal
            themselves. By assigning codes to categories, a
            researcher streamlines the data. There are no hard
            and fast rules, but researchers must be able to
            explain their systems and reasons for determining
            categorized codes. It is through axial coding that we
            get closer to seeing themes emerge.
Category*   A category is formed when several codes that have
            a distinct relationship to each other are given a
            label or name; in other words, when the codes
                                                            (Continued )
214   Applying Indigenous Methodologies




                         assigned in an open coding process are grouped
                         into a category through axial coding. For example,
                         imagine you are coding transcripts describing
                         the activities that Indigenous parents associate
                         with their child’s learning process. You assign the
                         following codes: taking children to cultural events
                         with knowledge-holders, sharing cultural teaching
                         stories, communicating with schoolteachers,
                         ensuring homework is completed. Categories
                         arising from these codes could be cultural events,
                         community, communication, teachers, parental
                         oversight, teaching stories, and knowledge-holders.
          Theme*         Saldaña (2009) defines a theme as an outcome of
                         coding and categorizing. He goes on to say that in
                         reading, reviewing, and working with the data “we
                         can’t help but notice a theme or two (or a pattern,
                         trend, or concept)” (p. 13). Themes arise from the
                         codes and categories. In the example above, themes
                         that might emerge from asking Indigenous parents
                         about activities they associate with their child’s
                         learning process might include cultural education,
                         parental involved schooling activities, and formal
                         schooling. Themes are more abstract, less specific,
                         than categories and codes. Within a qualitative
                         research study, there might be only four or five main
                         themes, but they are built upon previous categories
                         and codes. A theme is, as Saldaña (2009) states, a
                         trend or pattern arising from the data that gives
                         further insight into a research question.

         * It can be difficult distinguishing between codes, categories, and
         themes. Remember when coding, categorizing, and assigning themes
         that there will be many codes, fewer categories, and fewer themes. This
         process is interpretative, at times intuitive, but requires that researchers
         can explain their rationale for analysing (or grouping) the data in the way
         they did.
                                Analysing, Interpreting, and Meaning Making   215


thematic analysis can take different forms, but the coding has to make
sense and be explainable to another.
   Whatever approach is taken in working with findings (be it coding
or otherwise), it is ethically essential that participants have an oppor-
tunity to check and approve the transcripts of the stories. Allowing
participants the opportunity to review the preliminary analysis and
interpretations is good practice. Participants must also be given the
opportunity to withdraw from the research. Throughout research,
checking in with participants and community regarding consent is
pivotal. Without informed consent, the research must halt until in-
formed consent is provided. A researcher must share the summary
findings of the research with participants and their community prior
to the research being publicly released. This ought to be standard
practice within research generally, but because of the misrepresenta-
tion of Indigenous cultures and communities within research, it is
crucial within Indigenous methodologies. Within Indigenous meth-
odologies, trustworthiness of research cannot exist without both in-
formed consent and the knowledge that the participants are secure, in
their own minds, that informed consent has been granted. This brings
us to tâpwê: truth and trustworthiness in research.



TÂPWÊ: TRUTH AND TRUSTWORTHINESS

Trustworthiness is about the legitimacy, dependability, and reliability
of research findings. Our choice of analytical strategies for reaching
findings (e.g., thematic grouping, epiphanies, statistical analysis) dis-
closes to the world the kind of knowledge we trust. It is with and
through these analytical strategies that a researcher is able to say,
“These are my findings, this is how I arrived at them, and this is
why you, world, can trust them.” At this point in the research, the
researcher has decided to use statistics, stories, or a combination of
sources and reveals which voices have been included and excluded.
To show that findings are consistent with what else is known about
the subject, researchers will tell how they have triangulated the data
with other chosen sources (e.g., literature reviews, field conversations,
and research memos). Theories, decisions, actions – indeed, all the
turns and switchbacks made during the research – bring the research
216   Applying Indigenous Methodologies


      to the findings phase. As researchers assert and unveil their results, it
      all comes out in the open. The researcher says, “Yes, I believe this to
      be true, and this is why.” Research analysis shines a spotlight on truth
      and trust in research.
           Certainly, in earlier stages of research, researchers contemplate the
      larger epistemological question of what constitutes truth and trust-
      worthiness in knowledge creation. However, in the analysis phase,
      these beliefs reveal themselves. In research analysis, researchers
      re-enter the complex truth-trust-research labyrinth. Here, we ask im-
      portant questions to reaffirm our research path. How do I show that
      my ­research findings are trustworthy? What constitutes trustworthy
      findings? Empirical or interpretive analysis? Constant comparative?
      Heuristic? What analytical strategy? Coding, categories, patterns,
      or themes? How do I present themes? A metaphor or a table? As In-
      digenous methodologists, we ready ourselves, once again, for the
      ­subjectivity-objectivity debate in qualitative research.
           An ongoing debate on trustworthiness in qualitative research con-
       cerns itself with an objective-subjective dichotomy. Orthodox pro-
       ponents of objectivism believe that a truth claim can be trustworthy
       only if backed by external, physical evidence of observable sensory
       sources with data-driven research as the modus operandi. For some,
       empirical research is perceived as the most trustworthy. Empiricism
       is ­associated with objectivism, positivism, and generalizability in
       ­research. ­Empiricism “is the name for a family of epistemological the-
        ories that generally accept the premise that knowledge begins with
        sense experience” (Schwandt, 2007, p. 83). Within Western research
        discourses, empiricism points to observable evidence that can be
        measured, usually through numbers, and it is most commonly asso-
        ciated with quantitative studies that involve large statistical samples
        that can be generalized.
           On the other hand, proponents of subjective knowledge value
        the interpretations embedded within individual internal conscious-
        ness (i.e., theory and beliefs) and arising from lived experience. For
        them, truths arise from and are interpreted through the contextu-
        alized experience of life. Often, individual researchers locate them-
        selves somewhere on a continuum between objectivism, on one end,
        and subjectivism, on the other. Strategies for research analysis, such
                                Analysing, Interpreting, and Meaning Making   217


as empirical data-driven approaches or theory-guided analysis, will
depend upon one’s beliefs about what constitutes truth.
   One might think there is a clear binary: quantitative-empirical/
objectivist and qualitative-interpretative/subjectivist. There is not.
Though researchers may assert stark binaries and either/or choices,
they tend to move back and forth on the objectivist-subjectivist con-
tinuum. Further, research methodologies themselves have shifted on
the topic. The early days of qualitative research reflected empiricism,
as found in Glaser and Strauss’s 1967 work The Discovery of Grounded
Theory (Charmaz, 2010). Since then, qualitative researchers, of differ-
ing theoretical ilk, have held differing perspectives on how, or if, em-
piricism factors into their qualitative research practices.
   Indigenous methodologies are, arguably, a different kettle of fish
because they are anchored in Indigenous knowledges. An Indig-
enous episteme holds a differing perspective on binaries such as
objectivism-subjectivism or empiricism-interpretivism. An Indige-
nous episteme views knowledge as non-fragmentary, interdepend-
ent, and holistic (Atleo, 2004). Choices about what knowledge to
call upon are dependent upon the context and purpose of a problem
or situation at hand. It is not that one type of knowledge is more
valuable than the other; it is more about what type of knowledge
is most helpful at a given time. Further, a holistic Indigenous belief
system views the e­ xistence and intersection of tangible (i.e., phys-
ical) and intangible (i.e., metaphysical) knowledge as integral to
knowledge creation.
   Indigenous intangible knowledges, as recognized in the UNESCO
Convention for the Safeguarding of the Intangible Cultural H   ­ eritage,
encompass oral traditions and expressions, performing arts, social
practices, rituals and festive events, knowledge and practices concern-
ing nature and the universe, and traditional craftsmanship ­(UNESCO,
2003). Indigenous knowledges factor physical-metaphysical, tangible-
intangible knowledges in truth claims. Subjective and intangible
truth, or inward knowing, is given respect in truth claims because it is
integral to an Indigenous theory of the world. Of all ­intangible knowl-
edges, inward knowing is not only a legitimate knowledge source
(Bastien, 2005; Brant-Castellano, 2004; Ermine, 1995) but pivotal
within Indigenous epistemology and theorizing, and it f­actors into
218   Applying Indigenous Methodologies


      the trustworthiness of knowledge. Kathy Absolon (2006) describes
      how inward knowing exists in ceremony, through fasts, and is com-
      municated through dreams and by sitting in circle. Inward knowing
      is intangible, interpretative soul work.
          Research findings arising from an Indigenous methodology will
      recognize, interpret, and, typically, document the intangible. Doc-
      umenting inward knowing requires reflexivity methods. Examples
      include methods such as textual and audio journaling or maintain-
      ing a visual portfolio. In her research, Jeannine Carriere (2006) used
      a portfolio to document her feelings, intuitions, and reflections on
      the research process. Many personal stories and observations in my
      graduate research were taken from my research journal. Depending
      upon the methodology a researcher selects, inward knowing may
      emerge in myriad ways, for example, in thematic analysis, critical
      incident analysis, or integrated through field memos. For the Indig-
      enous methodologist, inward knowing is not throwaway knowl-
      edge nor is it licence for self-absorption or egotism. Calling upon
      inward knowing in research analysis is purposeful so as to serve
      the collective good.
          While intangible knowledge, as inward knowing, is valued and
      trusted in Indigenous culture, Indigenous peoples also rely upon
      tangible, empirical knowledge gained through observing patterns,
      events, and behaviours in the physical world and drawing conclu-
      sions from those observations. In many Indigenous communities,
      individuals with the training and experience to analyse patterns
      are knowledge-keepers and are highly esteemed. Scholars such as
      Deloria (1991) and Cajete (1999) document the value placed upon
      empirical knowledge within Indigenous cultures. However, they
      note that observations (and conclusions) were highly contextualized,
      particular, and purposeful for the need at hand. Also, these obser-
      vations were made with judiciousness and caution, especially when
      it came to generalizing knowledge extracted from context – or what
      researchers term generalizability. No matter the method, if research is
      conducted according to Indigenous traditions, truth claims are tied
      to the character of the truthteller. My conversation with Indigenous
      scholar Laara Fitznor concludes this chapter. Laara reflects upon
      truth and trust and highlights the pragmatics of arriving at the find-
      ings phase of research.
                                       Analysing, Interpreting, and Meaning Making        219


A CONVERSATION WITH LAARA FITZNOR

Laara Fitznor1 is Nêhiyaw from northern Manitoba and a faculty mem-
ber at the University of Manitoba. Laara has been a leader in Indige-
nous education for many years, and I welcomed the opportunity to
speak with a female Nêhiyaw scholar. I was intrigued by her Nêhiyaw
approach to research sampling and to other methods of gathering data.

    MAGGIE: Laara, what was your research topic? Could you talk a little bit
        about that?
    LAARA: At the time, there was an Aboriginal circle of educators [in
        Winnipeg], which was called the Aboriginal Teachers Circle. I got
        involved with the Teachers Circle, and as I sat with [members],
        I began observing what was unfolding. [They were talking about]
        issues around advocacy, racism, isolation in the workplace, wanting
        to incorporate Aboriginal perspectives. [I was] trying to understand
        them, trying to work in an Aboriginal way with Aboriginal knowings
        and processes. In observing a number of things, I started to think,
        “Geez, maybe I can do something with my research that involves
        this group, to look at their stories.” In that sense, you look at a
        methodology. I wasn’t wondering what methodology I would use.
        I would be getting the stories from them with the basic question
        being, “How do Aboriginal teachers or educators see their experience
        as Aboriginal professionals who work in primarily Eurocentric
        systems?”
            They [members of the Aboriginal Teachers Circle] were
        excited. I was excited about the idea, and everybody wanted to be
        interviewed. They were telling me that they had stories to tell. I had
        worked with my thesis advisor. I said I would like to work with
        the educator’s circle. Of course, then you are up against all kinds
        of things that they talk about in research, such as saying that you
        can’t be too close to your subjects. One of the things that he [the
        supervisor] recommended was a pilot study with the group. Instead
        of doing that, I had these initial observations, which were in lieu of a

1   This excerpt from my interview with Laara Fitznor (2006) is taken from Kovach
    (2009). The interview itself was conducted for my dissertation published in 2006.
    The institutional affiliation of the interviewees for my dissertation reflects that
    time period.
220   Applying Indigenous Methodologies


             pilot study, [that] I developed in my proposal from what I observed.
             There were five themes, which ended up being themes of the stories.
             I worked with my committee, saying, “Now I have got something
             here, [I want] to interview the teachers one on one and to do sharing
             circles.” Those were methodologies. The sharing circles, the one-on-
             one interviews, and working with our medicines, such as tobacco.
             Offering tobacco, much like you did, to the participants, explaining
             what that is all about, and if people were not comfortable to take it,
             I wouldn’t expect them to take it – that it’s just a little gift. The people
             who were part of the Teachers Circle already knew that smudging
             was part of opening meetings there. There wasn’t anything new that
             I was doing. It would be unusual for somebody in the circle not to
             know about these practices.
                The sharing circles were a common thing happening in the
             community and in the schools, so a lot of people knew about sharing
             circles. I was talking of a research circle/talking circle where I could
             ask questions and people would share what they had to share. I was
             actually doing this in terms of methods because I knew circles would
             be known and familiar for the group, as opposed to, “I am going to
             come and ask you questions.” Following the circle, I had intended to
             interview maybe one person from each circle, just to further clarify …
                I was hired to teach at OISE [the Ontario Institute for Studies
             in Education in Toronto], and I knew that I gotta collect my data
             before I go [leave Winnipeg]. That pushed me to do data collection
             with the circles, because I had already done the biographical survey.
             I called the members, as many as possible, particularly the ones that
             submitted their surveys. They were all willing. I think everybody
             said yes. I panicked because I wanted to do a simple random sample,
             selecting only so many of them, but I thought if I am working in a
             Cree way, this is forfeiting my Cree perspective.
                So, what do I do? I included everybody, which meant that I would
             have as many circles as people want, because I am getting data from
             people that you can always merge anyway. I thought, okay, sharing
             circles are not written up in any research methodology, in a methods
             book. I saw focus groups, so I wrote a long rationale in the initial part
             of my research explaining the importance for sharing circles. They
             both get at maybe the same information, but in focus groups people
             are just talking and in sharing circles it is quite different. It is more of
                             Analysing, Interpreting, and Meaning Making    221


a ceremonial, sacred space where I am building in the cultural pieces
of it, [such] as opening and closing with an acknowledgment or a
prayer and opening with a smudge. I used tobacco offerings.
   In two of my five circles, I had an Elder that was involved. I had
the Elder do an opening or closing for two of my circles. The premise
was to bring an Elder into each of my circles, but I couldn’t organize
it for three of them. When I couldn’t [have an Elder in attendance],
I did the opening and the closing. I have done so much work with
circles in my own teaching and in my own development through
sitting in Elders circles, whether it’s in the lodge, sweat-lodges, or
just sitting around listening to Elders talk and having an opportunity
for people to respond and talk about themselves and who they are,
their own developments. I have team-taught with Elders, so I have
learned a lot from Elders.
   When I was doing the circles, when you ask about the quality
difference from the focus group, [it] is that everybody gets a chance
for input; everybody has that airtime; they can take as long as they
want. I was prepared to be there for a good one, two, three, five hours.
I reminded each of them about that, because a lot of them knew
sharing circles. I said, “Keep in mind that although I am saying this
could be around two hours, likely it might be more.” I think they were
all about two to three hours. When each person spoke, sometimes it
seemed ten minutes, twenty minutes, sometimes a half an hour. When
we went through another round, it would be the same. The smallest
size circle that I had was three, which was interesting because there
was supposed to be five or six of us, but three couldn’t show up, and it
so happened that the Elder was there, a young man, and myself.
   It’s still a circle. I saw one [of] your comments, the part on
intuition. I know this happens to me a lot in terms of being guided
by the Creator. I think I made some mention of it, that it [this circle]
was needed for the young man, just for the three of us to be there.
The Elder did the opening, listened while this person spoke, and
[then] did the closing. The things that the person spoke about were
really fascinating, so it was almost like it was needed for that to
happen. I did the usual thing in terms of tobacco, and everybody was
okay with recording because I told them ahead of time. I said that
I could either take notes or try to remember after, but I said, to really
get the essence of your voice, I think recording would be the next
222   Applying Indigenous Methodologies


             best thing. That is what we did. The other thing I did was – let’s say
             that one educator might not feel comfortable being in a circle with
             another – I would make sure they were not in the same circle. Or say,
             well, there is safety in there; it’s your voice and the person knows it’s
             your voice. They know me, I have a good reputation and they know
             that I would be trustworthy.
                The relationship is very important, and I did a lot of follow-up,
             too. What I did in terms of making space – creating the space to
             reflect on our traditions, our culture, our ways, and our knowledge –
             was before the circle, I would have a mini-feast. I prepared the food
             myself, whether it was bannock and stew or bannock and chilli.
             I would prepare food, also keeping it open; if they needed to bring
             their kids they could. That happened in a couple of instances. In one
             instance, the child was old enough that she could play off on her own
             while we were in circle. And in the other instance, the kid was in the
             circle, running around, [laughter] driving us crazy. That was kind of
             neat. I didn’t want to say, “Well, this is my work, don’t interrupt me,”
             because that is not our way. Your kids are part of it, so how can I then
             say, “Oh no, don’t bring them.”
                You find a way to work it in, [even if] it takes longer. I mean, that
             took a long time, about four hours, but that was okay. I was talking
             about the opening and the closing. I think that there was one circle
             where one of the participants was known to do Elder or traditional
             work, so I offered her the tobacco and asked her to open the circle for
             us, and that was okay.
        MAGGIE: How did you handle things like consent forms?
        LAARA: Well, these were all educators. I didn’t go into a community,
             which I think might have been a different sense. I mean, they are
             used to stuff being written and signed and everything. They were
             okay with this; they were signing it ahead of time … Again, it’s the
             trust, the relationships – they know who I am. In the one-on-one
             interview, I did the same thing: I prepared food. In one case, I went to
             somebody’s office and interviewed her and went through the same
             tobacco offering. This is where [consent forms] with methodologies
             or research ethics that you want to protect people’s anonymity for
             various reason, for good reason, because people know each other.
             Although people will know that I interviewed one of eighty-eight
             people that are listed, they will not know who I interviewed.
                                 Analysing, Interpreting, and Meaning Making   223


       In terms of a methodology, I didn’t really name a methodology.
    What I did was talk about the inquiry, listening to the stories and
    providing meaning to the stories, what the teachers [were giving
    as] meaning to the stories through description and an analysis of
    their stories. I wanted to compare these to what Indigenous scholars
    were saying. The difficulty I was having with methodologies was
    getting them to fit what I was doing. I noticed you talk about allied
    methodologies. In one sense, that is kind of what I did. Then I looked
    at the Indigenous scholars. This is where the permission came for me
    to do the storying in lieu of research questions.
       In the write-up, I think things start to unfold as you are doing it,
    things you hadn’t thought of ahead of time. For example, what do
    I do with the Elders’ words because you also transcribe them? That’s
    why, for me, it was good to transcribe as opposed to somebody else
    because they might have excluded it because that’s not part of the
    [research] participants. They might exclude the Elders’ words. As
    I was going through, I would transcribe their [the Elders’] words,
    too, and I thought, “Wow, it’s so right-on,” and then I thought,
    “What do I do with the Elders’ words in the stories?” You talk
    about, how do you work with intuition. There were many times
    when I would feel stuck. I would smudge, I would pray, and then
    I would get an energy flow. I even smudged my computer. So, I was
    wondering what to do with the Elders’ words. You know, how do
    I work this in?
       I thought it would just be a major omission if I didn’t
    acknowledge the Elders and say thank you. How do I write it up?
    When he [the Elder] was present, each time he opened he didn’t
    say exactly the same words, but similar words. What I did was
    take the bulk of what he said for the bigger circle, because he is
    acknowledging everybody. When I was writing it up, I put his
    opening before the stories and his closing after the stories. Anyway,
    that meant that I had to backtrack and phone him because I can’t do
    it without his permission.
MAGGIE: Overall, I think you have responded to this, but do you believe
    there is an Indigenous methodology and distinctive way we
    approach research?
LAARA: I think it’s a lot more mindful, respectful of the bigger picture and
    the individuals within the bigger picture. It’s not just the institution
224   Applying Indigenous Methodologies


             that matters or what publications can come out of it. It is about how
             it [research] can benefit the community.


          When I reflect on my conversation with Laara, I see that, at the
      time, it felt like I was partaking in a Nêhiyaw research seminar. I was
      responding with questions about Laara’s process: What did you do
      next? How did you handle that? What approach did you use there?
      What kept you going? I found this conversation to be very instruc-
      tive on the pragmatics of gathering and interpreting data. Laara noted
      that she frequently confronted Western and Nêhiyaw approaches to
      research that were at odds (e.g., exclusivity in sampling versus in-
      clusiveness, privileging the rationale versus honouring the intuitive,
      and utilizing a focus group versus a circle method). But identifying
      places of divergence without compromising a Nêhiyaw approach
      illustrated the possibility of Indigenous methodologies. From using
      research-sharing circles as method to finding supportive allies in the
      institution, having Elders opening circle gatherings, making offerings
      of tobacco, and using smudging and calling upon spirit, Laara was
      working with Indigenous protocols and holistic knowledges. Meaning
      making in Indigenous methodologies includes observation, sensory
      experience, contextual knowledge, and the recognition of p     ­ atterns.
      It draws upon external and internal sources, is highly interpretative,
      and involves inductive analysis.
          Laara said that she did not write much about intuitions, dreams, or
      energy, and much of the sacredness in my research did not appear in
      the written document – family gatherings, kind words, friends, smiles,
      teasing, tears, teachers, a deer sprinting across the open prairie, or
      Manitou giving energy when I could go no further. These experiences
      constitute meaning that cannot be written, only felt, remembered,
      and, at best, spoken. These experiences return to the particular, for
      our truths are found in our places.
          So it goes. Tâpwê. With different cultural paradigms, different
      people, and different perspectives on deciding what counts as truth,
      individuals are active agents. In interpreting findings, researchers
      confront the truth-trustworthiness question. It is a murky “truth-trust-
      research” pool with first a splash then a ripple as we make choices on
      how we will analyse, interpret, and present findings from the gifts
      (i.e., data) we have been given. For the Indigenous methodologists,
                                  Analysing, Interpreting, and Meaning Making   225


there are a couple of pools, and double the number of ripples, because
we research at the intersection of Indigenous and Western research
thought. Clarifying the role of Indigenous theory in research analysis
and interpretations will help us to wade on through and say to the
world, “This is what I believe to be true.”



DISCUSSION QUESTIONS

1 Discuss with another individual your research interest and what
  ­Indigenous theory (including emphasis area) might work best for your
   analysis.
2 The chapter focuses on a thematic analysis. What analytical strategy
   might you apply in your research? Would this include a coding process
   or another approach? How would Indigenous theory apply?
3 Discuss with another person the difference between first level and
   ­relational analysis in a thematic analysis strategy.
4 Write a letter to yourself. What knowledge do you trust? Why? How
    does the knowledge source (e.g., the person sharing the knowledge)
    factor into your decision? Where are you on the objectivity-subjectivity
    continuum?
CHAPTER TEN

 Presenting Findings, Metaphoric Framing,
 and Representation




      Hidden in our bodies is the memory of our ancestors who know that
      the fastest road to healing is to metaphorically express the memory
      in poetic form and draw lessons from it. Our languages are lyrical,
      ­image-filled and rich in metaphor. The language of poetry lives in the
      body and we are always hungry for it. Our Elders, even when ­speaking
      English, open with prayers that are poems that bring peace to our
      bodies, and we know, we just know, that we are at one with the world.
                                                              Maracle (2015)



 Throughout the research project, strategies for presenting findings in
 a cogent manner percolate in the researcher’s mind. It is now time
 to give these germinating ideas a south-facing exposure, so they can
 lean towards the light. Chapter 9 and Chapter 10 work together as a
 unit. Chapter 9 focuses on analytical strategies to arrive at patterns
 and meaning. Chapter 10 introduces analytical frameworks, with a
 focus on metaphor, as ways of revealing discoveries and presenting
 findings in Indigenous methodologies.
    Having done the preliminary work of analysing transcripts, identi-
 fying patterns, and arriving at themes, now what? How do we show
 the relationship between those themes? What text, images, and figu-
 rative expressions will we enlist to frame our research findings? With
 whom will we share those findings and how? How will we present
                 Presenting Findings, Metaphoric Framing, and Representation   227


findings in a relevant way to the Indigenous community? For those
new to qualitative research, these tasks may be challenging. In a study
on the challenges of writing qualitative research, Wang (2013) found
“that the common mistakes students make include the lack of an
­explicit argument, the lack of strong claims before offering the quo-
 tations, and the lack of sufficient interpretation after the quotations”
 (p. 12). The power of research lies in the researcher’s ability to com-
 municate findings clearly and relevantly. Analytical frameworks are
 useful in helping us reveal the message of our research.



ANALYTICAL FRAMING

Analytical framing means presenting findings. Upon reaching the
“findings” phase, data have now been organized through a system-
atic interpretative process (e.g., thematic analysis). Depending upon
the analytical strategy, the data may be organized in different ways –
themes, patterns, or critical events – but it ought to give insight into
the overarching research question. At this point, there ought to be
sufficient discoveries to respond to the “so what?” question of one’s
­research. Now it is time to frame the research findings analytically
 and communicate the research discoveries.
      The analytical framework is the structure, form, or frame arising
 from the data to focus the findings, and it will help in reporting rec-
 ommendations. Analytical framing in qualitative research moves with
 a “to and fro” spiralling energy rather than on a linear trajectory. De-
 termining an analytical framework means listening to the data, keep-
 ing one’s chosen theoretical positioning in mind, and being conscious
 of cultural and personal context. Determining an analytical frame-
 work has an interpretive quality but is also about organizing the data.
 An analytical framework reveals the relationship between data, and,
 in doing so, gives the data contour and shape, which helps commu-
 nicate what has been discovered in the research findings. It’s a deli-
 cate balance between listening to the data (i.e., interpreting), keeping
 ­Indigenous theorizing in mind, and holding a predetermined plan for
  a framework (i.e., organizing) without coercing or forcing the data.
  ­Finally, the researcher must be able to explain how the specific ap-
   proach for presenting the findings emerged.
228   Applying Indigenous Methodologies


          A jigsaw puzzle is a common metaphor for describing phases of
      qualitative research (LeCompte, 2000). It is also a useful metaphor to
      illustrate the process of arriving at findings in thematic analysis. Each
      theme is a puzzle piece with its own distinctiveness, but it is also a
      part of a bigger picture. Theorizing or theory is our picture on the
      puzzle box that we use as a reference point to assist in assembling
      the puzzle. As the researcher interprets how themes fit together, using
      both a systematic and organic thought process, the puzzle takes form,
      and a cohesive picture comes into view. As in a jigsaw puzzle, an an-
      alytical framework is about making connections between small pieces
      so we can see a bigger picture.
          There is a range of methodologies – quantitative research, action
      research, grounded theory, Indigenous methodologies – and so it fol-
      lows that there are myriad ways to present findings. While this is true,
      there are at least two important considerations:

      a. The researcher needs to be able to explain the chosen analytical
         framework: How did the framework emerge? Why does it make
         sense given the research questions and preliminary findings?
      b. The analytical framework needs to serve the research findings:
         How does the analytical framework help to reveal and communi-
         cate the findings of the research?

      Because it arises from the data, which are gathered and analysed in
      concert with theory, an analytical framework gives shape to findings
      and helps the researcher communicate these findings in a comprehen-
      sible way. In qualitative research, there is a history of the use of meta-
      phor as analytical framework. Within Indigenous methodologies, the
      use of metaphor is common. Let’s turn our focus to metaphor.



      METAPHOR AND MEANING

      As James Geary (2011) says, “Metaphor is most familiar as the liter-
      ary device through which we describe one thing in terms of another”
      (p. 2). However, he says that metaphors are much more than this:
      “Metaphor lives a secret life all around us. We utter about one met-
      aphor for every ten to twenty-five words or about six metaphors a
                  Presenting Findings, Metaphoric Framing, and Representation   229


minute” (p. 5). ­According to Geary, when Barack Obama gave his
second presidential inaugural speech, the fourth paragraph of his
speech included four metaphors, two of which were the “rising tides
of prosperity” and the “still waters of peace” (p. 7). This worked out to
­approximately one metaphor for every eighteen words. Geary argues
 that metaphors are much more than literary devices to distinguish
 literal from figurative speech. In using metaphor, one is painting
 ­pictures with words to ­express ideas to a wide range of people.
      Figurative language, such as metaphor, is a device for integrating
  imagery into text. There are several different types of figurative lan-
  guage. They include, but are not limited to, analogy, cliché, person-
  ification, hyperbole, metonymy, synecdoche, allegory, symbology,
  simile, and metaphor. Within this abundance, metaphors and similes
  are the worker bees in everyday life as we seek to connect with each
  other through our talk and writing. The terms metaphor and simile are
  often used interchangeably. This is because they both require com-
  parisons between different things to explain a point. McClanahan
  (2014) clarifies a basic difference between these two figures of speech.
  A simile compares two things with connectors that include “like” or
  “as” while a metaphor does not. McClanahan provides this exam-
  ple: “Simile: Your hair is like a dark river. Metaphor: Your hair is a
  dark river” (p. 95). She says that simile “is usually limited to one like-
  ness, whereas metaphor often invites a whole range of like qualities”
  (p. 96). Both metaphor and simile are used within Indigenous com-
  munity vernacular. For our purposes, both metaphor and simile will
  be in the bundle as we proceed, although the chapter more frequently
  references metaphor.
      Figurative language compares one thing to another to communi-
  cate an idea beyond a literal meaning. Metaphor is rich and expands
  insights. Metaphor is forceful when communicating concepts within
  and across cultures. Even though the choice of images and compara-
  tives arises from personal experience and cultural context, the figura-
  tive language of metaphor is a servant of universal connection. In an
  effort to communicate, individuals will choose their figures of speech,
  such as which metaphors to use, with an audience in mind. On either
  a subconscious level or with intention, individuals assess with whom
  they wish to communicate and determine which figure of speech (e.g.,
  metaphor) will be the most effective. Without getting into the weeds,
230   Applying Indigenous Methodologies


      people use metaphors to conceptualize, contextualize, and communi-
      cate knowledge – all the time.
         From the early 1980s, scholarship has emerged on the use of meta-
      phors as conceptual and as contextual within knowledge discourse. In
      their original 1980 publication, Metaphors We Live By, Lakoff and John-
      son (2003) argue that thought itself is metaphorical in nature and that
      we think figuratively. They put forth the claim that “metaphor is per-
      vasive in everyday life, not just in language but in thought and action.
      Our ordinary conceptual system, in terms of which we both think and
      act, is fundamentally metaphorical in nature” (p. 3). The authors orig-
      inally argued that thought is constructed through the metaphors we
      use. They coined the term conceptual metaphor. In the 2003 edition of
      their original work, Lakoff and Johnson acknowledge that their defi-
      nition of a conceptual metaphor has created controversy. That contro-
      versy is linked to the debate about subjectivism (i.e., ­knowledge as
      constructed) versus objectivism (i.e., knowledge as empirical and uni-
      versal). In the following quotation, they explain the “sticking point”
      and where they now find themselves:

           Above all, the key sticking point is the existence of conceptual
           metaphor. If conceptual metaphors are real, then all literalist and
           objectivist views of meaning and knowledge are false. We can no
           longer pretend to build an account of concepts and knowledge
           on objective, literal foundations … At the same time, what we
           have discovered is fundamentally at odds with certain key tenets
           of postmodernist thought, especially those that claim meaning
           is ungrounded and simply an arbitrary cultural construction …
           There appear to be both universal metaphors and cultural varia-
           tion. (Lakoff & Johnson, 2003, pp. 273–274)


         It would seem that Lakoff and Johnson (2003) are stating that
      metaphors have both subjective and objective properties. Kövecses
      (2015), a professor of linguistics, writes that the experimental study
      of metaphor “indicates that metaphor interpretation can take place
      only in context; that is, metaphor interpretation varies with context
      and, thus, m
                 ­ etaphor and context are closely linked” (pp. 6–7). This
      perspective highlights the connection between the experiential and
                 Presenting Findings, Metaphoric Framing, and Representation   231


metaphoric within context. The scholarship suggests that metaphor
serves in many ways.
   Metaphors build imagery around story. Metaphors invite the par-
ticular to enter while bridging contextual divides. Metaphors facil-
itate knowledge sharing across disparate communities. Metaphoric
framing is a catalyst for generating and expressing ideas in a holistic
way. Bruan and Clark (2006) tell us that building imagery is a pow-
erful way to make meaning out of research results “when you start
thinking about the relationship between codes, between themes, and
between different levels of themes” (p. 89). Visual representation
shows the thread that connects the data (e.g., themes). In thinking
about relationships through metaphor, it is, as Kövecses (2015) states,
that we bring in context. Conceptual or contextual? Metaphor, it seems,
does not wish to be pigeonholed. While there are differing perspec-
tives on the metaphor, the use of metaphor as a structural device to
give ideas form is persistent, and thus its use in analytically framing
findings in qualitative research is common – though arguably, meta-
phoric framing is more typically found in studies where interpretive
theorizing is present.



METAPHORIC FRAMING IN QUALITATIVE RESEARCH

Metaphors are powerful tools for conceptual and contextual think-
ers, and qualitative researchers are both. In research methodology,
metaphors offer an analytical framework that brings cohesion to dis-
parate themes and grounds meaning in shared experience. Carpenter
(2008) tells us of the utility of metaphor in organizing data: “An over-
arching metaphor provides a central theme to the text, introducing
the data and explaining the relationships among concepts” (p. 275).
Along with its organizing function of bringing themes together, ap-
plying metaphoric framing to research findings assists in ensuring
the relevancy of the research outcomes and recommendations. Aita
and colleagues (2003) tell us that metaphor has a unique ability to
connect research impact with community values, which is important
because “engaging with values, meanings, and expectations is essen-
tial for the design of interventions for change” (p. 1430). Qualitative
232   Applying Indigenous Methodologies


      researchers have used metaphors from gardening (Cook & Gordon,
      2004) to family (Aita et al., 2003) to wrestling (Dexter & LaMagde-
      leine, 2002) in their metaphoric framing so as to visually demonstrate
      relationships between purpose, data, stakeholders, and impact. Met-
      aphoric framing is powerful; however, be careful, because metaphor
      can be a trickster.
         Given the power of metaphoric framing, it is wise to proceed with
      respect and caution. Cook and Gordon (2004) apply a metaphoric
      structure of gardening to qualitative research and attest to its value.
      They note that metaphors can highlight some things while they hide
      others and say that “the meaning a metaphor may have for any in-
      dividual will be partly determined by culture and past experiences”
      (p. 654). The advice is that when working with metaphors, watch out
      for blind spots. Dexter and LaMagdeleine (2002) alert us to the temp-
      tation of imposing metaphor without allowing the data to speak to us:

           To minimize the potential risk of conceptually “cooking the ana­
           lysis” when using conceptual metaphors, a researcher should use
           a variation on the traditional mode of triangulating various types
           of data to strengthen confidence in the trends being uncovered
           and to constantly seek for specific examples of the metaphor in
           the data. (p. 376)


          It is common for a researcher to decide upon a metaphor in the
      early stages of research, perhaps in the research proposal stage, and
      to desire to use this metaphor for framing the analysis. It is equally
      common to become attached to that first metaphor, to want to hang
      on to it. However, the data may not feel the same way about the met-
      aphor. The advice: let it go. The data will guide us to a new metaphor.
      It is not necessary to fully abandon a first metaphor. There is possi-
      bility of applying it to another aspect of the research. Or, should we
      set the original metaphor aside, we can reference it as part of our an-
      alytical thinking when we contextually narrate the research project’s
      process in its entirety. As with all powerful forces, there is a need for
      respect and responsibility when working with metaphor. In reflect-
      ing on the adeptness of metaphor in qualitative research, including
      its trickster nature, we now shift to metaphoric framing in Indigenous
      methodologies.
                  Presenting Findings, Metaphoric Framing, and Representation   233


METAPHORIC FRAMING IN INDIGENOUS
METHODOLOGIES

To explain why framing findings through metaphor fits within Indig-
enous methodologies, let’s revisit the Indigenous conceptual frame-
work (ICF) introduced earlier in this text (see Chapter 2). The ICF has
four foundational aspects: Indigenous epistemology, Indigenous eth-
ics, Indigenous community, and self in relationship. All four founda-
tions apply, but let’s focus on Indigenous epistemology. Indigenous
epistemology is holistic, animate, and relational (as with community,
place, and kin). The relational signals clear attention to context. Context
and metaphor are closely connected (Kövecses, 2015). Concepts are
then communicated through metaphoric reference points (Lakoff &
Johnson, 2003). The language that breathes life into oral tradition
relies upon imagery and figurative speech. For these reasons, meta-
phors, a type of figurative speech, frequently appear in Indigenous
storytelling and community vernacular.
   Pueblo scholar Gregory Cajete reminds us that figurative language,
specifically metaphors, are threaded through Indigenous language to
express a relationality that underpins Indigenous societies: Allegories,
parables, riddles, visualizations, symbols, poems, rituals, and myths
all provide specific expressions of metaphorical thinking. He goes on
to say, “Metaphors are also highly flexible in their uses and can be
adapted to virtually all cognitive levels, from child to adult. Our met-
aphors show the depth of relationships humans have with nature and
how all beings participate in life” (Cajete & Williams, 2019, p. 7). In
his earlier writing, Cajete (2000) offers the example of Kokopelli the
Pueblo mythic figure who symbolically represents “the procreative
process and energy in nature” (p. 182). Kokopelli, Cajete tells us, is
a metaphor for all individuals. Like Kokopelli, all individuals bring
unique gifts, “seeds,” that we plant in our life travels; in turn, we
benefit from the gifts of others. In communicating context and concept
within Indigenous societies, metaphors matter. Thus, it is not a stretch
that the use of metaphor as an analytical framework finds a home
within Indigenous methodologies.
   In Indigenous methodologies, metaphoric framing can be found in
at least two places: a) the Indigenous conceptual framework (i.e., re-
search design) and b) the analytical framework. Chapter 2 introduced
234   Applying Indigenous Methodologies


      metaphor as a valuable device to give expression to the Indigenous
      conceptual framework or overarching research design. In Chapter 2,
      I identified several examples of metaphoric framing for research
      ­design (Absolon, 2011; Fiddler, 2014; Tachine, 2018). Tachine (2018), a
       Navajo researcher, explains why she chose a Navajo rug as the meta-
       phoric framing for her research design:

           Metaphors were integral to this research, as metaphors evoke im-
           agination and ancient wisdom. I found the use of metaphors a
           natural part of my thinking and writing as metaphors provided
           a space to visualize and connect difficult thought processes and
           assert the Navajo way of life. (p. 66)


      Typically, one metaphor is used per study – as a means of framing the
      research design or the findings or both. However, this is not a hard
      and fast rule. If a researcher uses more than one metaphor in more
      than one place, it is essential to explain the “why, when, where, and
      how” of both metaphors to avoid confusion. Additionally, if there
      is more than one chosen metaphor for more than one aspect of the
      ­research process, these metaphors should complement rather than
       crash into each other.
          The remainder of this section emphasizes metaphoric framing as
       an analytical framework (or concept mapping device) for presenting
       findings. To do this, I offer the example of an Indigenous research
       ­project – made public in a report entitled Indigenous Presence – that
      uses metaphor to analytically frame and present findings.



      THE LANDSCAPE OF A WINTER SWEAT

      The following study by Kovach et al. (2015) offers an example of an
      Indigenous study – Indigenous Presence: Experiencing and Envisioning
      Indigenous Knowledges within Selected Post-Secondary Sites of Education
      and Social Work – that highlights metaphoric framing. In this research
      project, our research team asked a culturally diverse group of uni-
      versity professors in the fields of education and social work about
      their experience with Indigenous knowledges in their academic
      and teaching lives. To hear story, our team used a conversational
                  Presenting Findings, Metaphoric Framing, and Representation   235


approach (Kovach, 2019), akin to storytelling, yarning, talk story, or
re-storying (­Absolon & Willett, 2004; Bishop, 1999; Geia et al., 2013;
Thomas, 2015). To gain insight and analyse our findings, we applied
an Indigenous theoretical lens that included the touchstones of holism
(Brant-­Castellano, 2000), relationality, conditions and circumstance
contextualized in colonial history, story (Archibald, 2008), and move-
ment and change (Smith, 2006). We called upon a thematic analysis
strategy to reveal patterns from the stories and were assisted by a
coding process. Upon a first-level and second-level relational analysis,
we arrived at four overarching themes: Indigenous knowledges them-
selves; a decolonizing desire by faculty; relationships with students,
other faculty, staff, and the administration; and the university context.
    To ensure we were consistent with Indigenous theory and think-
ing, Nêhiyaw Elder Joseph Naytowhow led our team in a Nêhiyaw
ceremony prior to our analysis. In 2013, on a cold snowy spring in
­Saskatchewan, our research team participated in a sweat-lodge cer-
 emony to meditate upon the responsibility, respect, relevancy, reci-
 procity, and reverence that our research required of us. From this
 cultural, community, and experiential ceremony arose a metaphoric
 framing – the landscape of a winter sweat – that helped us to present
 our findings. To animate our metaphoric framing, our research team
 commissioned Métis artist Leah Dorion to assist us in visually repre-
 senting and presenting our finding (see Figure 10.1). Several elements
 of the sweat were called upon to represent themes:

  LODGE: Working with Indigenous knowledges
  A FIRE: Decolonizing desire by faculty
  THE SMOKE: Relationships with students, other faculty, and the
       administration
  THE SNOW: University context itself including university systems
  THE PATH: Recommendations from the findings that ranged from the
       employment of more Elders and knowledge-holders to broadening
       what counts as scholarship in post-secondary sites


   The metaphoric framing of findings can serve Indigenous research,
but it must be treated with respect. To clarify our theorizing, we must
be intentional about how we are organizing knowledge and certain
that our research is framed in a way that is responsive and relevant to
236   Applying Indigenous Methodologies


      Figure 10.1. Analytical Framework Using Metaphor – Landscape of a Winter
      Sweat, painting by Métis artist Leah Dorion




      the community. When we do these things, the research path becomes
      less obscure and overwhelming. Still, no matter the metaphor or lan-
      guage we use to present our findings, it is crucial to be vigilant about
      how we are representing the voices, and stories, of our research.



      VOICE AND REPRESENTATION IN THE RESEARCH
      FINDINGS PHASE

      Guba and Lincoln (2005) explain axiology as that which deals with
      ethics. An Indigenous conceptual framework identifies ethics as one of
      its four core foundations. Ethics is embedded throughout the research
      design of Indigenous methodologies – from forming the research
      question to involving the community, participant consent, collection
      of data, analysis, presenting the findings, and giving back. Thus, it
      follows that an ethics watch extends to research representation.
                  Presenting Findings, Metaphoric Framing, and Representation   237


    Attention to voice and representation has relevance within research
regardless of the experience being represented or the methodology
chosen. Schwandt states (2007), “Representation in its many forms –
as resemblance, replication, repetition, description, and d ­ uplication –
is central to the modernist project of understanding the world”
(p. 264). Schwandt goes on to emphasize the postmodernist contesta-
tion of representation. Postmodernists see representation as a fallible
construct of objectivism, arguing that there can be no authentic du-
plication of experience. According to postmodern theorizing, there is
an infinite capacity for differing representations in qualitative s­ tudies.
Representation in research is about the researcher invoking a subjec-
tive understanding of another’s experience rather than objectively
representing or duplicating another’s experience (Schwandt, 2007).
    Discussing participatory methodologies, Lincoln et al. (2018) ad-
dress voice and representation. They comment on how the responsi-
bility to represent identity and voice faithfully applies to researcher
and research participants. Lincoln et al. (2018) point to the emerging
practice, in some methodologies, of research participants represent-
ing themselves in research rather than having their experience fil-
tered through the researcher’s eyes and interpretations. They point to
a growing tradition of “letting research participants speak for them-
selves, either in text form or through plays, forums, ‘town meetings,’ or
other oral and performance-orientated media or communication forms
designed by the research participants themselves” (p. 142). In her book
Researching the Vulnerable, Pranee Liamputtong poses this question:
“How do researchers write their interpretation without ‘othering’ their
research participants, exploiting them, or leaving them voiceless in the
telling of their own stories?” (Liamputtong, 2007, p. 165).
    Representation has significant implications because of the h    ­ istory
of research in Indigenous communities. Linda Tuhiwai Smith (2012)
points to the criticality of representation in relevant, responsive
­research: “Representation of indigenous peoples by indigenous peo-
 ples is about countering the dominant society’s image of indigenous
 peoples, their lifestyles and belief systems” (p. 152). To engage in
 ethical representation within Indigenous methodologies, we must
 address the colonial history of deficit theorizing and consider how
 Indigenous peoples have been misrepresented in research – so as not
 to repeat these transgressions.
238   Applying Indigenous Methodologies


      DITCHING A DEFICIT THEORIZING APPROACH

      Indigenous methodologies require researchers to be conscious of the
      theoretical lens through which they are interpreting Indigenous ex-
      perience. This requirement is in place to prevent deficit theorizing
      about Indigenous lives. Deficit theorizing sees only individual not
      systemic deficits, blames only individuals not social systems. Born of
      a ­neo-liberal and victim-blaming sentiment and raised to a theorizing
      level in research, deficit assumptions employ theory in research rep-
      resentation to strategically diminish marginalized peoples. The result
      is deficit theorizing.
          In their study on risk in education and human services, Pica-Smith
      and Veloria (2012) define a deficit perspective as that which assigns
      deficits to individuals from marginalized groups rather than to the
      shortcomings of a societal structure that creates inequities and hard-
      ships for marginalized groups. Pica-Smith and Veloria (2012) write,
      “Deficit theories assume that some children, because of genetic, cul-
      tural, or experiential differences, are inferior to other children. This
      ideology is problematic as it places complete responsibility on the in-
      dividual and ignores a systemic analysis” (pp. 2–3). Pitzer (2015), in
      her study of urban teachers navigating deficit and neo-liberal logics,
      defines a deficit discourse as “a racialized approach that constructs
      urban students as lacking; that faults Black and Latin@ students, com-
      munities, and their cultures for the state of their schools.” Pitzer (2015)
      goes on to say that, as a result of deficit thinking, “there is a “tattoo-
      ing … of ‘lack’ onto most Black, Latino, immigrant, and/or poor stu-
      dents” (p. 2).
          Indigenous experience has long been subject to deficit theorizing.
      Deficit theorizing has occurred all too often in the representation of
      ­Indigenous lives by outsider research. In a social science and humani-
       ties context, Lynne Davis (2004) writes of Haida texts published with-
       out Haida permission, in Robert Bringhurst’s 1999 work A Story as
       Sharp as a Knife: The Classical Haida Mythtellers and Their World. Along-
       side the absence of community consent, this research raises ethical
       questions about representing Indigenous life without an authorized
       interpretation and vetting. Davis (2004) links research representation,
       ethics, and power: “With ‘research’ and ‘representation’ so deeply
       implicated in replicating relationships of dominance rooted in our
                 Presenting Findings, Metaphoric Framing, and Representation   239


shared colonial past, contemporary researchers striving to engage in
an ethical relationship with Indigenous communities are challenged
to decolonize their thinking and research practices” (Davis, 2004,
p. 5). The detached, outsider, colonial interpretations of Indigenous
life have historically had the loudest voice in representing Indigenous
experience. As researchers who aspire to a morally ethical representa-
tion within Indigenous research, we must be vigilant about shutting
out deficit theorizing and, indeed, clear about the implications of any
form of theorizing.
    Deficit theorizing can occur intentionally or through lack of vigi-
lance. In an effort to serve both Indigenous and Western audiences,
researchers may not clearly identify their theoretical positioning, and
this results in an ad hoc methodology (Deloria, 1999, p. 33). Ad hoc
methodologies imply a lack of attention to theory. Too often, theory
itself is blamed in deficit theorizing. From my perspective, the use of
theory, itself, is not the problem. The problem arises from one’s choice
of theory, of course, but also from one’s position towards theory. Arro-
gance about theory assumes one’s theoretical approach is normative
or above reproach and does not need “unpacking.” Inattention to, ac-
tive avoidance of, or obliviousness towards theory leads to its reckless
use. The good news is that if we pay enough attention to theory in
the interpretation, presentation, and representation of story, research-
ers can ditch the deficit theorizing of Indigenous peoples in research
representation.
    We cannot decolonize research without broaching the subject of
who is representing Indigenous experience and how. An ideological
and political history exists when it comes to voicing and representing
Indigenous story – a history of Western researchers weaponizing re-
search to diminish Indigenous culture. Outsider research has histori-
cally misrepresented Indigenous experience for reasons ranging from
ignorance of Indigenous culture to racist assumptions about who In-
digenous people are. Western research is littered with evidence of this.
Thus, we must pay careful attention to how we represent Indigenous
story and experience in presenting our research findings. We must be
ethical in the representations we make. Before mobilizing research
findings that impact Indigenous peoples, it is ethically responsible to
ask – “Does my research respect the dignity of Indigenous peoples?”
Be honest. If you are not certain, get a second opinion.
240   Applying Indigenous Methodologies


         Disseminating findings is the next phase of the research design.
      Within Indigenous methodologies, findings can be communicated
      orally, textually, visually, and performatively through testimony, song,
      art, or a combination of forms. What ensues is a call-out to oracy, to
      oral tradition, as a form of knowledge dissemination within Indige-
      nous methodologies.



      DISCUSSION QUESTIONS

      1 Having done the preliminary work of analysing transcripts, ­identifying
        patterns, and arriving at themes, now what? So what? How might
        you present findings to community? Ask an experienced researcher
        some questions about approaches to or thoughts on presenting
        ­findings. Did that researcher use an analytical framework? How did the
         ­researcher arrive at this framework?
      2 To show relationships between themes and patterns, how might you
          frame your research findings? Ask an Indigenous community member
          for thoughts on how to present research in a relevant way.
      3 Identify at least three and up to five different Indigenous research
          studies that use metaphor. Compare how these studies have used
          ­metaphor in research.
      4 What is the relationship between unexamined theory and deficit
           theorizing? Why does knowing this relationship matter in Indigenous
           research?
CHAPTER ELEVEN

 Oral Dissemination and Capacity Building
 in Indigenous Methodologies




      There’s the continuing oral tradition which is fundamental to everything,
      really to everything, whether it’s scholarly writing, or poetry or art, or
      any expressive and perceptive form of human art and endeavor.
                                                                   Ortiz (1990)



 Research that is responsive, relevant, and serves those whom it pro-
 claims to serve is the heartbeat of Indigenous methodologies. To sustain
 this pulsing beat, there must be Indigenous voice, ethical representa-
 tion, and a research outcome that is useful from the point of view of the
 research participant and community. Mindful practice and community
 connection oxygenate each research action. Returning knowledge to
 the community is consistent with the ethos of reciprocity in Indigenous
 methodologies. Through the multichannel, dialogic, performance of
 oracy (Clements, 2002), research enacts. It is a relationship that does
 not expire with the project’s formal closure. Mobilizing findings within
 Indigenous methodologies can take many forms but what it does not do,
 what it is not about is “smash and grab” (Martin & Frost, 1996), write
 and rush out, or a one and done. As Linda Tuhiwai Smith (2012) tells us,

      Reporting back to the people is never ever a one-off exercise
      or a task that can be signed off on completion of the written
      ­report. Some of my students have presented their work in formal
242   Applying Indigenous Methodologies


            ­ceremonies to family and tribal councils; one has had his work
            positioned amongst the wreaths which have surrounded the
            ­casket to a deceased relation. (p. 16)


      From an Indigenous point of view, research is relationship.
         In spotlighting research dissemination, this chapter reviews the
      function and form of research knowledge mobilization broadly with a
      reminder that Indigenous methodologies, like all methodologies, will
      include written product. It then turns its attention to the centrality of the
      oral dissemination of knowledge and the practice of oratory and oracy
      within Indigenous culture, methodologies, and scholarship. Chapter 11
      concludes Part 3 (Animating an Indigenous M    ­ ethodological Approach)
      by affirming that knowledge mobilization is not solely about dissemi-
      nation – it is about capacity building. From the get-go, purposeful In-
      digenous research is about discovery, but it is also about shaping policy
      and practice so as to benefit Indigenous lives. Research is far-reaching.



      MOBILIZING THE FINDINGS IN RESEARCH

      Tying the bundle of a research project, in general, means arriving at
      findings and representing findings to be shared with a larger commu-
      nity. Research findings can be written, performed, or spoken. Like a
      story, research has a narrative arc that starts with a beginning, transi-
      tions to a middle (or apex), and then pushes towards a resolution. In the
      narrative arc of the singular research project, the findings phase is the
      resolution, where we share the research outcomes. The resolution does
      not mean a termination of the relationships nurtured by the research.
      It may, indeed, lead to another collaborative project arising from the
      recommendations of the current project. However, projects themselves
      do reach an end, and it is through sharing findings that a researcher
      will say, “This is the research I have conducted, and this is its message.”
         Mobilizing knowledge is about sharing knowledge for a purpose.
      The Social Sciences and Humanities Research Council (SSHRC) out-
      lines knowledge mobilization as

            The reciprocal and complementary flow and uptake of re-
            search knowledge between researchers, knowledge brokers and
         Oral Dissemination and Capacity Building in Indigenous Methodologies   243


      knowledge users – both within and beyond academia – in such
      a way that may benefit users and create positive impacts within
      Canada and/or internationally, and, ultimately, has the potential
      to enhance the profile, reach and impact of social sciences and
      humanities research. (SSHRC, 2020, n.p.)


The definition goes on to say that knowledge mobilization activities
must advance research agendas, theory, or methods within academia
or inform public debate, policy, or practice beyond academia. Knowl-
edge mobilization, as defined by SSHRC, is about communicating
research activities and outcomes to stakeholder communities, the
media, and civil society at large. The SSHRC definition does not pre-
scribe what constitutes impact, for whom, or how knowledge should
be communicated.
    The expectation in conventional research is that the findings will be
disseminated typically through a written report. Within Indigenous
methodologies, there is a balance between written and oral dissemina-
tion. As such, Indigenous methodologies will yield a written product
in some form (e.g., report, article, briefing). In written reports, find-
ings in qualitative research can be displayed through differing struc-
tural techniques, such as text, tables, charts, concept mapping, short
story, vignettes, or poetry. Chapter 10 explores a metaphoric framing
approach that augments the written with the visual. According to Wu
et al. (2016), the results and discussion (i.e., findings and reporting) sec-
tion of a qualitative report is the place to “[p]rovide summaries and
interpretations of the data (e.g., themes, conceptual models) and ­select
illustrative quotes” (p. 495). They go on to say that findings ought
to be couched within relevant literature, and they stress that it is the
­researcher’s analysis coupled with the use of select quotations support-
 ing the analysis that will determine the trustworthiness of the research.
    In modern Western research culture, knowledge narratives are
 written and often decontextualized. Abstracted and removed, writ-
 ten narratives take the form of expository writing with less context,
 intimacy, and immediacy. On reporting research, Charmaz (2010)
 cautions against parsing the life from written research findings and
 tells us to “Go beyond an analysis of acts and facts.” She asks us
 to pose the questions that give vitality to the reporting: “Cultural
 context? Historical antecedents? Organizational climate? Emotional
244   Applying Indigenous Methodologies


      ambiance? See how rendering it explicitly in the text affects your
      writing – and moves your analysis beyond reporting” (p. 172). If you
      are writing a report, write so that the report paints a picture with
      words and evokes action.
           Even though written reports, theses, and communiqués are main-
      stays for those researching at the intersection of Indigenous and
      Western knowledge creation, the choices for mobilizing the findings
      can vary. In their article on non-traditional strategies for mobilizing
      knowledge in qualitative research, Keen and Todres (2006) identify
      different ways that findings can be communicated. They itemize sev-
      eral ways to mobilize knowledge, including research-based theatre
      or ethno-dramas, workshops, patchwork quilts, art, performance
      texts or scripts (non-preformed), storytelling, poetry, film, dance, so-
      cial and electronic media (e.g., websites), and brochures. Mobilizing
      knowledge involves creativity plus communication so as to inform
      and engage. In oral tradition, knowledge moves through spoken
      word, dance, and song. These methods have particular relevance
      when a researcher has focused on collecting data and representing
      findings that uphold the centrality of oral communications and tradi-
      tions. In Indigenous methodologies, we will be called to write up our
      ­research, but we must balance the written with oral or performative
       ­knowledge sharing: oral dissemination in Indigenous methodologies
        is non-negotiable.



      KNOWLEDGE MOBILIZATION IN INDIGENOUS
      METHODOLOGIES

      Story and storytelling are inseparable within Indigenous societies.
      Stories hold knowledge. Stories hold information about familial rights
      associated with territorial stewardship. In 1997, resulting from the Del-
      gamuukw case, the Supreme Court of Canada ruled that oral testimony
      held the same weight as written evidence in land entitlement cases.
      The significance of story in maintaining generational responsibilities
      is ancient, though it has only recently been recognized in contempo-
      rary Western jurisprudence. From an oral tradition, teaching stories
      do not align with a linear sequence of time; rather, teaching stories
         Oral Dissemination and Capacity Building in Indigenous Methodologies   245


transcend chronometric timekeeping and fasten themselves to places
(McLeod, 2007).
    Within Indigenous cultures, stories holistically engage us. Nêhiyaw
