scholar-researcher Winona Stevenson states that Indigenous oral his-
tories do not share conventional categorical boundaries that separate
knowledges: “the package is holistic – they include religious teach-
ings, metaphysical links, cultural insights, history, linguistic struc-
tures, literary and aesthetic form, and Indigenous ‘truths’” (p. 79).
Oral story does not exist in isolation; it cannot be outside of the act
of telling to the story listener. When discussing story and meaning
making, Stevenson (2000) tells us, “All stories are didactic to vary-
ing ­degrees, but they hardly ever have built-in analysis – analysis is
the job of the listener” (p. 233). The teller and the listener are equally
active; the listener is not passive. Oral storytelling is a non-directive
process, and the story listener is self-determining.
    In commenting upon a Western scholarly approach to collecting
data through story, Stevenson says this often means that “once a story
is shared and recorded, ‘facts’ are extracted, and the remaining ‘super-
fluous’ data set aside … the bundle is plundered, the voice silenced,
bits are extracted to meet empirical academic needs, and the story
dies” (p. 79). This differs in Indigenous oral tradition where the oral
story, contextualized in the now, witnessed in the synchronistic mo-
ment of its telling, can never be fully recaptured in written research
because the writing follows the hearing. The knowledges that we
gather in the ephemeral moment of oral story, as told by a teller, as we
sit in a specific spiritual, physical, and emotional space and place, are
immediate, sensory, and felt.
    The holistically felt immediacy of the spoken word delivered live
cannot be replaced by (or communicated through) a conventional,
conceptually written research product. However, to bring in the felt
experience of spoken story, Indigenous methodologists can balance
written research dissemination through an “in the moment” sharing
with community. This is the oral tradition. This is the Indigenous way.
Thus, oral dissemination will matter in Indigenous methodologies
and scholarship. When one considers oral tradition in research meth-
odology, it helps to unpack terms such as oratory and oracy and oral
dissemination.
246   Applying Indigenous Methodologies


      ORATORY

      William Clements (2002) offers us a definition of oratory in his book
      Oratory in Native America. He describes oratory as word choice
      threaded with imagery in service of verbal expression alongside a
      sophisticated knowledge about the audience hearing the oratory:
      “The art of oratory is more than metaphors and figures of speech. By
      definition, oratory is a very public art form, and the orator had to
      recognize the public situation as he or she spoke” (p. 102). Clements
      (2002) points out that a strong orator was mindful of audience, of lis-
      tener, and could keep the oration accessible and real: “The effective
      orator succeeded often as much by how the speech was rendered as
      by what the speech said” (p. 102). Oratory, at is core, is social. Oratory
      is purposeful and of place. Oratory is testimonial. Oratory requires a
      witnessing other. In historic and contemporary Indigenous culture,
      oratory is a valorised function of Indigenous societies because it is
      through oratory that a powerful orator communicates a message that
      is remembered.
          Oratory is spoken word that is often associated with leadership. In
      Western culture, for example, early deliberations on oratory are found
      in Aristotle’s Rhetoric, which defines oratory as the skill of persua-
      sive public speaking. Oratory, memory, and messaging were linked
      in early Indigenous societies because knowledge and transactions
      needed to be remembered so as to be retold. Working in unison, both
      the words and the speaker’s ability to deliver words that would stick
      were critical in communicating a message that would stay in memory.
      Chief Abel McLeod, of the James Smith Cree First Nation, explains
      that “our ancestors had no other way to keep the sacred promises
      [that treaties had] given to them, only by memory. They said then
      their brains were like paper” (Wheeler, 2014, pp. 53–54).
          Blackfoot Elder Jim Black offers an example of oratory in service
      of sending a warning call, as recorded in a 1974 land claims inter-
      view. In giving evidence to the Office of Specific Claims and Research
      (Canada), Elder Jim recounts a story of the Blackfoot hunting party
      alerting Chief Crowfoot of the Canadian railroad track being laid on
      the prairies. The hunting party messaged “there is something coming
      straight towards us. It is iron with a flooring, there is fire with iron
      that smokes walking on it” (Office of Specific Claims and Research,
         Oral Dissemination and Capacity Building in Indigenous Methodologies   247


1974, p. 3). Calling upon local knowledge to communicate a message
to community, the Blackfoot hunting party compared the railroad, of
which they did not yet have experience, to that of a fire burning. The
words, as translated from Blackfoot into English, suggest how im-
agery was built into oratory to communicate a warning call. To relay
a message or concept and “have it stick,” Indigenous orators need the
skill and ability to paint a picture, relevant to place, in detail. Skilled
orators would select imagery connected to place using figurative lan-
guage, such as metaphor, for this communicative function. Because
oral tradition was critical in safeguarding Indigenous societies, the
training of orators (i.e., storytellers) was part of an Indigenous tradi-
tional education.
    Oratory is one term for communicating through spoken word.
­Oratory and orator, as mentioned, are terms typically ascribed to lead-
 ers who are able to inspire and persuade through the spoken word.
 According to this definition not everyone is an orator or has been
 bestowed with the training and talent for oratory. However, the vast
 majority of the human population practices oracy every day. Oracy is
 prevalent and valued in contemporary Indigenous culture. Oracy in
 Indigenous research and scholarship is pivotal given the role of oral
 tradition in Indigenous knowledge sharing.



ORACY

In Kôhkominawak Otâcuniwiniwâwa – Our Grandmothers’ Lives as Told
in Their Own Words, Ahenakew and Wolfart (1998) write down the
stories of the kôkoms speaking in Cree, presenting each story as closely
as possible to the oral spoken story. Introducing the stories, they tell
the reader, “The style of these reminiscences is casual, familiar, and
marked by numerous interruptions and exchanges” (p. 19). Ahenakew
and Wolfart’s written record of the reminiscences of kôkoms and
mosôms gives an insider’s perspective on everyday oracy in Nêhiyaw
life. Their message is that oral culture is alive through the practice of
storying – where knowledge spirals through the galvanized energy of
a speaker and listener in a synchronous moment.
    In contemporary Western scholarship, the term oracy was coined
in the early 1960s and associated with Andrew Wilkinson and a
248   Applying Indigenous Methodologies


      Birmingham University School of Education research team. “It was
      felt necessary to have a term for the skills of listening and speaking
      which would be parallel to ‘literacy’ for the skills of reading and writ-
      ing” (Wilkinson, 1968, p. 743). In a subsequent article entitled “The
      ­Concept of Oracy,” Wilkinson (1970) further defined oracy and de-
       scribed why the concept was necessary. A teacher of English, Wilkinson
       argued that the English language is about the “verbalization of experi-
       ence” and the “experience of verbalization,” which required both the
       written and spoken word. During the late 1960s, when Wilkinson was
       teaching, written language instruction or what we know as literacy,
       ruled the day (arguably, it still does), and there was not, at that time, a
       parallel term to describe the practice or teaching of oral skills.
          In the early 1970s, Wilkinson (1970) and his research team differ-
       entiated oracy from oratory: “What we mean by speech is not inter-
       pretative language, it’s not reciting a poem, or even giving a public
       speech (very few people ever have to give public speeches).” They go
       on to say that oracy is the act and skill of “putting one word of our
       own, next to another word of our own, next to another word of our
       own, and so on in the creative utterance which we are all using all
       the time while we are speaking” (p. 74). They say that oracy, or the
       spoken word, flourishes in an environment where values of relevancy,
       relationship, and reciprocity are present. In an early contribution to
       the scholarship of teaching, Wilkinson (1968) describes the import of
       relationship and reciprocity when it comes to fostering oracy in the
       learning environment:

           [T]he climate of oracy within the school … is clearly connected
           with the relationships existing between teachers and taught. If
           these are authoritarian then the occasions for reciprocity, and the
           growth of confidence on the part of the pupils, are less than if they
           are more tolerant and friendly. (p. 743)


      We now commonly see oracy as parallel to literacy skills, and this
      lends a gravitas to oral skills development in language arts education.
         Oracy includes oratory but is more inclusive, as oratory is often asso-
      ciated with charismatic and persuasive leaders whereas most individu-
      als practice oracy every day. Core to both oratory and oracy is a listening
      audience, whether it be an audience of one or a group. Either formal or
         Oral Dissemination and Capacity Building in Indigenous Methodologies   249


informal, oratory and oracy mobilize knowledge. In Indigenous ­research
and scholarship, there is a consistent and pervasive call for the oral tradi-
tion. In research, the practice of oratory or oracy falls under the purview
of the oral dissemination of knowledge. Because of oral tradition, oral
dissemination is integral in Indigenous methodological design.
   In academia, oral dissemination of knowledge is a term signalling
the reporting of research through non-text processes. (In this sense,
performativity is included.) In traditional academic culture, oral
dissemination is code for conference presentations whereas written
research takes the form of peer-reviewed journal articles or books.
However, within Indigenous research, assigning the label of “con-
ference presentation” to oral tradition is limiting and diminishing
because it does not capture the philosophical, purposeful, holistic to-
tality of oral dissemination with a witnessing audience as understood
from an Indigenous perspective. Further, in Western academic tradi-
tion, written work is valorised and prevails over the oral dissemina-
tion of knowledge. This is simply not the case in Indigenous culture.
   It will be an uphill climb to bring the nuance of an oral tradition as
practised in community into an academic culture that privileges prod-
uct over process. On writing what is meant to be spoken, Indigenous
scholar Gerald Vizenor points out, “So much is lost in translation –
the communal context of performance, gesture, intonation – even the
best translations are scriptural reductions of the rich oral nuance” (as
quoted in Stevenson, 2000, p. 19). Research is a particularly lettered
activity. Still, Indigenous researchers and allies must create space
for oral tradition, Indigenous style, in contemporary academic envi-
ronments. Indigenous oral tradition keeps us connected to commu-
nity and when connected to community we are more like to have a
­capacity-building mind-set as/where needed.



CAPACITY BUILDING IN INDIGENOUS
METHODOLOGIES

Relational ethics, the miýo ethos, is about engendering trust through
respect, responsibility, and reciprocity. Métis scholar Jeannine Carri-
ere (2006) reflects that completing a doctoral degree was only part of
a larger responsibility to community, only part of a larger relational
250   Applying Indigenous Methodologies


      commitment associated with her research: “Another thing that
      I learned that is very different from western approaches is that I was
      always having a relationship with these people. It doesn’t end with
      the research report or whatever” (p. 176). Kathy Absolon (Chapter 4)
      referenced community accountability in relation to purpose and
      reciprocity: “So, the PhD has to be meaningful; it has to help – that
      there is a purpose in terms of the bigger picture of who we are as
      ­Anishinaabe.” Thus, it is important to ensure that the research returns
       to the community in a manner that is accessible and useful.
          Given the ethical links between knowledge mobilization, capacity
       building, and the oral tradition of knowledge sharing, it is no wonder
       that the Canadian Tri-Council Policy Statement on Ethical Conduct
       for Research Involving Humans (TCPS2) states that

           Final reports shall be made available to the territorial or organi-
           zational community or community of interest participating in the
           research. Researchers and communities should clarify the extent
           to which research findings will require translation, plain lan-
           guage summaries or oral presentations to community members,
           in order to make the research findings accessible to the commu-
           nity. (CIHR et al., 2018, p. 128)


      According to TCPS2, user-friendly written reports and oral presenta-
      tion are highlighted, and research projects should strengthen commu-
      nity capacity. In identifying community capacity in research, it states
      that this should include reciprocal, collaborative learning opportu-
      nities that include a transfer of skills between researcher and com-
      munity; offering education and training workshops; and employing
      Indigenous research assistants and knowledge translators (CIHR
      et al., 2018).
         Increasingly, as communities heal from the colonial interruption
      and experience a cultural renaissance, the desire for proficiency in
      mainstream domains (e.g., research and higher education) is viewed
      as a capacity-building strategy to protect culture. Capacity build-
      ing requires planning throughout the research process. Therefore, it
      is critical to communicate with community and articulate capacity
      building within a co-created community engagement plan. This will
      involve seeking community input through face-to-face events and
         Oral Dissemination and Capacity Building in Indigenous Methodologies         251


oral updates throughout the research (in the midst of the 2020 pan-
demic, face-to-face meetings have meant Zoom or other technology-
mediated processes). Advisory committees are ideally situated to
o­ffer guidance on capacity-building activities. Researchers with fund-
ing can arrange financial transfer agreements with a community to
fund community-based research assistants and engagement events.
In the “nuts and bolts” of contemporary Indigenous research, com-
munity capacity building requires its own line item in any research
project’s budget.
    Conducting research in a good way means honouring the protocol
of reciprocity. A capacity-building ethos is a way to give back. Within
a Nêhiyaw research framework, the Nêhiyaw values of respect, kind-
ness, and reciprocity are affirmed in ceremony. Protocols matter, such
as the offering of tobacco, because they signify a relational mutual-
ity. Oral tradition matters in Indigenous methodologies because sto-
ries are nested in a holism that invites relationships with land, place,
and nature and that encompasses language, dreams, humour, pur-
pose, ceremony, and spirit. The sacred does not reveal itself in isola-
tion, away from the life that swells amid, among, and around it. It is
through story, and the telling of story in the presence of our other, that
we come to know. In the sacredness of the whole, the conventional
academic efficiencies must wait. Within Indigenous methodologies,
an oral tradition and giving back will be expected.

        Elder Jim recounts a story of the Blackfoot hunting party alerting
        Chief Crowfoot of the Canadian railroad track being laid on the prai-

        towards us. It is iron with a flooring, there is fire with iron that smokes
        walking on it” (Office of Specific Claims and Research, 1974, p. 3).


  In a small group, reflect upon stories you have heard from Indigenous
  people, stories that have stayed with you. With your group, share
  what you remember from the story, including your thought on why
252   Applying Indigenous Methodologies


        it stayed in your memory. Was it the imagery, voice, movement of the
        ­storyteller? Was it the storyteller’s ability to make the story relevant to
         you at the time you heard the story? How might this skill be useful in
         Indigenous research (or any research)?
      2 In a pair-and-share session, discuss the concepts of oratory, oracy, and
         oral dissemination. How are they interrelated? How do they differ? Why
         does oral dissemination matter in Indigenous methodologies?
      3 Think about an experience or story that you wish to share with
         ­others. Reflect upon how you might animate the telling. How might

          ­witnessing audience? How would you prepare? In a group setting,

      4 Review Article 9.13 (Mutual Benefits in Research) and Article 9.14
           (Strengthening Research Capacity) of the TCPS2 (2018). These ­articles

           Inuit and Métis Peoples of Canada) of the TCPS2 (see the Panel of
           Research Ethics website, www.ethics.gc.ca). Sketch out a research
           ­capacity-building plan. Who do you need to contact and communicate

            building require? What is your budget?
