CONCLUSION

 Decolonizing the Academy through
 Asserting Indigenous Methodologies




      There is an old Native American myth about a family of women who
      have tried to touch the sky, year after year, century after century, and
      failed. Finally, one day a young woman reaches up and succeeds. Fa-
      ther Sky is surprised and asks, “How did you get to be so tall?” The
      young woman replies, “I’m standing on a lot of shoulders.”
                                                               Alvarez (2000)



 In 2005, Indigenous scholar Marlene Brant-Castellano shared words
 that resonate today. She said that the challenge of, and responsibility
 for, Indigenous research lies with all of us. In speaking specifically
 about academic research, Dr. Brant-Castellano (2005) said that In-
 digenous people must suspend distrust and non-Indigenous people
 must suspend disbelief. This means engaging the once invisible and
 excluded and evoking new theoretical discourses and research prac-
 tices. It is an exciting time for theorists, researchers, and scholars, but
 it is also testing every aspect of the “tried and true” customs of in-
 stitutional knowledge centres. This new landscape asks for a critical
 re-imagination.
     Imagining a new way means pushing back against racism at large.
 Imagining a new way means full recognition of the colonial wound. For
 one’s country, there can be no full racial reckoning, no healing, with-
 out tending to this primary colonial wounding. Part of this reckoning
256   Indigenous Methodologies Strong


      means accepting that Indigenous people have a special relationship
      with land and country. Without this acknowledgment, societies and
      their institutions will veer towards equity and diversity strategies that
      perpetuate the erasure of Indigenous peoples. Choosing to see Indige-
      nous people and colonial history is facing racism square on. To do oth-
      erwise is not. Racism will return. This is not to diminish other groups
      but to point out that the distinctive relationship between Indigenous
      people and country calls for this response.
         In post-secondary institutions, an Indigenous cultural sustainabil-
      ity response will require recruiting and retaining Indigenous peoples.
      An anti-colonial response will require non-Indigenous allies and the
      collegium. In research, Indigenous methodologies will not survive in
      the absence of this human capacity. The bottom line? There can be no
      advancement in Indigenous methodologies without Indigenous grad-
      uate students, researchers, scholars, and allies. Thus, the conclusion
      of this book directs attention to human capacity and agency in the
      anti-colonial, decolonizing project of Indigenous methodologies.



      ON THE EDGE OF THIS PLACE: INDIGENOUS PEOPLE
      IN THE ACADEMY

      Indigenous peoples do not have a long history with post-secondary
      institutions. While compulsory elementary and secondary education
      for status Indians has been clearly delineated as a federal responsi-
      bility, the relation between Indigenous peoples and post-secondary
      education has not been as clearly spelled out. In fact, until 1961, the
      clearest reference to post-secondary education in the Indian Act dealt
      with enfranchisement. Enfranchisement was the requirement for
      status Indians (as defined by the Indian Act) to relinquish all rights
      and responsibilities associated with being a status Indian for specific
      reasons (such attending university). Until the 1920s, a status Indian
      wishing to attend a university could face automatic “enfranchise-
      ment.” The term automatic enfranchisement was later revised in a 1927
      amendment to the Indian Act (Stonechild, 2006). Even though the
      ­terminology was revised in 1927, this sort of “enfranchisement” was
       part of the Indian Act: “until the 1940s, First Nations people enrolling
       in post-secondary education were required to surrender their status
                                                                Conclusion     257


as ‘Indians’” (Standing Committee on Aboriginal Affairs and North-
ern Development [SCAAND], 2006, p. 2). The revised 1951 Indian Act
allowed a board to decide whether a status Indian person would be
enfranchised upon completion of a post-secondary degree. In 1961,
compulsory enfranchisement was removed from the Indian Act.
    As with the general education policy, a consistent Canadian gov-
ernment approach to post-secondary education focuses on its role as
a socio-economic equalizer. In this context, the 1968 post-secondary
programming for status Indian students and the subsequent 1977
Post-Secondary Educational Assistance Program of the Department
of Indian and Northern Affairs (SCAANC, 2006) were largely funding
policy mechanisms to help bridge the equity gap between status In-
dians and mainstream society. The aims and philosophies underlying
these policies differ substantially from the Indigenous community’s
perspective on education. Indigenous peoples view education as a
foundational right that should simultaneously serve and sustain cul-
ture and minimize socio-economic disparity.
    In his book The New Buffalo: The Struggle for Aboriginal Post-­Secondary
Education in Canada (2006), Nêhiyaw scholar Blair Stonechild points to
the ideological distinction between the federal government’s rationale
for First Nation post-secondary educational policy and First Nations’
aspirations for education. For the federal government, First Nation
post-secondary education is viewed “as a social program only in
­order to bring First Nations participation rates to a level comparable
 to the rest of society” (Stonechild, 2006, p. 138). The socio-economic
 equity approach is laudable but limited because it does not deal with
 rights and cultural sustainability alongside socio-economic indicators
 or the role of educational institutions within that larger discussion.
    Post-1990s, the spotlight on the intersectionality between cultural
 sustainability and education re-emerged through the 1991–1996 Royal
 Commission on Aboriginal Peoples (RCAP). RCAP, an i­nquiry inves-
 tigating the relationship between Aboriginal people, the ­Canadian
 government, and Canadian society, recognized the work done in
 reclaiming education but stated there was still work to do. Post-­
 secondary education was identified as an assimilative educational
 experience. RCAP was firm – the assimilationist approach must end.
 RCAP recommended that Indigenous knowledges be integrated into
 university settings (RCAP, 1996a, 1996b). Since then, Indigenous
258   Indigenous Methodologies Strong


      community members, Elders, political leaders, post-secondary ­policy
      advocates, scholars, researchers, and writers have worked to imple-
      ment multiple strategies to ensure that higher learning (involving
      both pedagogy and research methodology) is not a mechanism of as-
      similation but a tool for cultural survival.
          Post-RCAP, the late 1990s and early 2000s saw starts and stops in
      Aboriginal-state relations. An action emerging from an RCAP recom-
      mendation was the establishment in 2008 of the Truth and Reconcilia-
      tion Commission on Indian Residential Schools – to hear the stories of
      residential school survivors. During the period of 1867 to 2000, over
      150,000 children were mandated to attend residential school. These
      children were taken from their homes and sent to church-operated
      residential schools, funded by the Canadian state, in an effort to “civ-
      ilize” Aboriginal children and erase Aboriginal culture. Aboriginal
      children were separated from their families and culture, amounting to
      the cultural genocide of Indigenous peoples (Truth and Reconciliation
      Commission of Canada, 2015a, 2015b). Many of the children in these
      schools experienced neglect and physical and sexual abuse.
          The ask of the truth and reconciliation process is that Indigenous
      people are not only seen but granted the dignity that all human be-
      ings ought to be afforded. This assertion of Indigenous presence
      and dignity, as a value-based proposition, is something that Cana-
      dian society has started to reckon with. However, as a strategy for
      concrete action, reconciliation is complicated. The Canadian state,
      and arguably the majority of Canadians, often view reconciliation
      less as a relationship and more as a thing, an accommodation thing.
      ­Indigenous scholar Glen Coulthard (2014), in his book Red Skin, White
       Masks: Rejecting the Colonial Politics of Recognition, articulates contem-
       porary Canadian efforts at reconciliation as arising from a reformist
       liberal recognition approach that seeks not to inconvenience or unset-
       tle. Coulthard reframes this as “reconciliation politics” (p. 106). As a
       restorative measure, without mutual agreement on restoring to what
       and in the absence of an equity ethos that respects Indigenous self-­
       determination, reconciliation is blemished with inconsistencies and
       contradictions. “Reconciliation politics” occurs wherever there is a
       desire to respond to individual and systematic racism against Indige-
       nous peoples through liberal reformist measures. This includes Cana-
       dian post-secondary institutions.
                                                               Conclusion   259


   Indigenous inclusion in higher education is tainted by a history
that saw people forced to disfranchise a part of self to attend universi-
ties. Where colonial racism is not acknowledged and where it prevails,
post-secondary educational environments will not be safe zones. If
we do not remove the roots of racism from our collective soil, racism
will grow again. This means – subconsciously or overtly – repeating
a troubling historical pattern that veers towards assimilation, on one
hand, and abandonment, on the other. Indigenous peoples currently
in the academy are not yet in the clear; we still find ourselves on the
edge of a colonial space.
   Amid the inconsistencies and contradictions that accompany rec-
onciliation within Canadian consciousness, the testimonies of Indige-
nous survivors through the Canadian truth and reconciliation process
makes that which was invisible, visible. Reconciliation is a call to
moral society. The 2015 final report of the Truth and Reconciliation
Commission (2015c) contained ninety-four calls to action to redress
the personal, cultural, and political harm done to Indigenous peoples
by the Canadian state. The calls to action are wide-ranging, envelop-
ing education and research. It is in this Canadian context that we find
Indigenous post-secondary education and research; and it is here that
we have an opportunity to clear new paths and breath new air.
   New conceptual tools, such as methodologies, are a powerful strat-
egy with which to respond to the Truth and Reconciliation Commis-
sion’s call to decolonizing action. For these new tools demand a new
relationship. Indigenous research frameworks provide opportunities
for Indigenous epistemologies to enter the tightly guarded academic
research community and serve Indigenous worldviews and the acad-
emy. Supporting Indigenous research frameworks means supporting
Indigenous researchers. A focus on graduate programs is critical be-
cause it is here that Indigenous research frameworks take flight.



SUPPORTING INDIGENOUS GRADUATE STUDENTS AND
PROGRAMS

Indigenous graduate research occurs at the intersection of Indigenous
and Western thought. The difficulty arises when Indigenous graduate
researchers are told to look “a certain way” and to follow the prescribed
260   Indigenous Methodologies Strong


      steps of a particular culture that is not congruent with the steps (or o
                                                                             ­ rder)
      that would occur in another culture (i.e., Indigenous culture). The feeling
      of internal conflict that many Indigenous graduate students e­ xperience
      is an indicator that they are struggling to hold onto their sense of self.
      This emotional work heightens research productivity stress.
         Indigenous graduate students are trained to be cognizant of the ac-
      ademic and Indigenous community. Jeannine Carriere (2006) describes
      this experience as a form of dual accountability. Non-Indigenous fac-
      ulty members who appreciate the dual accountabilities in Indigenous
      graduate research are a lifeline. Such allies are open to Indigenous
      methodologies, assist with researcher-community relationships, and
      acknowledge the emotional work of Indigenous research. The pres-
      ence of such support (or lack of it) for Indigenous graduate research-
      ers is felt most intensely through relationships established within
      academic structures, such as graduate supervisory committees.
         The graduate supervisor and thesis committee are critical to grad-
      uate student success, and graduate students must think carefully
      before choosing or agreeing to both. (This extends to all thesis stu-
      dents, not solely Indigenous or minority students.) The relationship
      between supervisor and student matters, and it is wise to learn about
      each other as much as possible before committing to the supervisory
      relationship. The entire supervisory committee composition ought to
      be thought through. There may be power and personality dynamics
      amongst the faculty members who populate thesis committees, which
      can impact thesis progress. Generally, graduate work is hard, and a
      supportive committee helps.
         For Indigenous and minority students, supervisory and thesis com-
      mittee selection holds extra considerations. Does the faculty member
      have a consciousness of racialization? There are faculty members not
      sensitive to the deficit theorizing many Indigenous students endure
      in their graduate experience. The worth of a critically aware supervi-
      sor should not be discounted. One reason is that writing a thesis in-
      volves assessment and judgment on student interpretations, writing,
      and scholarly products. Feedback can be hard to accept. When there
      is sensitivity and trust between student, supervisor, and committee
      members, the experience is smoother.
         Where students veer towards Indigenous methodology studies,
      Indigenous supervisors make sense because they are apt to validate
                                                                  Conclusion    261


Indigenous theory in research design and guide students in articulat-
ing an Indigenous research framework. In reaching out to c­ ommunity –
so critical in Indigenous research – Indigenous supervisors can be an
­invaluable liaison. As a support, most Indigenous faculty have been
 (and continue to be) racialized and can empathize with the isolating
 experience of being an Indigenous graduate student. That being said,
 simply matching Indigenous graduate students with Indigenous fac-
 ulty because they are both Indigenous is problematic. Indigenous
 people are not a monolith. We are individuals with different experi-
 ences, personalities, intellectual interests, and so forth. Further, not all
 ­Indigenous faculty members situate themselves as scholars of Indige-
  nous knowledges (which has implications for Indigenous methodol-
  ogy projects). The right match between student and supervisor matters.
       In reflecting upon non-Indigenous thesis supervisors, Jeannine
  Carriere (2006) says that though her supervisor was not Indigenous,
  she was very attuned and a strong ally. I, too, was fortunate in this
  way. My co-supervisors, neither of whom were Indigenous, were able
  to provide guidance and latitude at the right intervals. What qualities
  mark an effective supervisor-student relationship? Experience, in-
  stinct, sensitivity, agreement on supervisor-student expectations, and
  an attuned, embodied awareness that graduate supervision occurs in
  the context of life, not outside of it. With the increase in Indigenous
  graduate students, the need for Indigenous faculty supervisors and
  thesis committee members becomes more urgent.
       Graduate program delivery is also an important consideration.
  At the master’s level, a cohort approach is an effective way to recruit
  Indigenous graduate students – and to advance Indigenous higher
  education. Kathy Absolon (2006) reflects: “I was interested in primar-
  ily being in an Aboriginal cohort and going to a university that was
  ­developing something with Aboriginal PhD students and I did not
   want to go through another degree program in isolation” (pp. 252–253).
       Along with academic programmatic support, Indigenous graduate
   student support is critical. One such program is SAGE (­Supporting
   Aboriginal Graduate Enhancement) at the University of British
   ­Columbia (UBC) and Simon Fraser University (SFU). As an aspiration,
    one SAGE program goal is to support the increase of Indigenous peo-
    ple with doctorates to achieve a critical mass of credentialed individu-
    als who will transform education. Razack (2001) argues that numbers
262   Indigenous Methodologies Strong


      matter when growing a critical mass. Smith (2006) contends that both
      numbers and political reclamation matter. He says that it is not just
      about producing a mass of PhD-qualified Indigenous leaders but also
      of creating a critical mass of Indigenous leaders working for transfor-
      mation. Programs such as SAGE, which build Indigenous ­capacity,
      reflect institutional support and deflect assimilative responses by
      ­focusing on Indigenous cultural sustainability in scholarship.



      SUPPORTING INDIGENOUS FACULTY AND
      SCHOLARSHIP

      A study by the Canadian Association of University Teachers (CAUT,
      2018) found that “Aboriginal academics remain significantly under-
      represented in the academy, making up just 1.4% of all university pro-
      fessors and 3% of college instructors in 2016” (p. 2). This study did not
      include a disaggregation of rank within the 1.4 per cent. We do not
      know the current numbers of assistant, associate, and full professors
      that comprise the Indigenous professoriate. Anecdotal evidence sug-
      gests that the majority of Indigenous professors are assistant profs.
      The Indigenous professoriate capacity crisis is a clear systemic barrier
      influencing Indigenous graduate mentoring and the Indigenization
      project generally. Supporting Indigenous graduate researchers means
      supporting Indigenous faculty.
         The comfort level with Indigenous scholarship varies across dis-
      ciplines. Some disciplines, such as social work, education, and law,
      have historically invited an Indigenous focus into their programming.
      However, within these disciplines, as in all disciplines, Indigenous in-
      clusion is at different levels of engagement. In the past decade, there
      has been good news. Universities Canada (2018) tells us that since
      2013, there has been a 55 per cent increase in academic programs with
      an Indigenous focus or design for Indigenous students. Also, as of
      2017, 65 per cent of Canadian universities had incorporated Indige-
      nous knowledge, methods, and protocols in research and teaching
      policies, programs, and practices aimed at Indigenous engagement;
      there was increasing partnership with Indigenous communities; and
      71 per cent of Canada’s universities had Indigenous representation
      in governance. However, the 2016 CAUT statistics cannot be pushed
                                                                Conclusion    263


aside – the Indigenous faculty represents a mere 1.4 per cent of the
Canadian professoriate. As an Indigenous colleague of mine noted,
1.4 per cent is not going to Indigenize the academy. There have been
efforts at recruiting Indigenous professors; however, not only in-
creased recruitment but also increased retention of those recruited
professors will make the difference.
   Senior academic leadership will determine whether there is Indig-
enous inclusion or Indigenous exodus. The colleague who does not
identify as Indigenous has a role to play in this inclusion, through
examining unconscious racial biases; creating a culturally inclusive
collegium; mentoring Indigenous researchers on the nuances of access-
ing grants, such as developing effective research proposals for large
funding bodies; and supporting Indigenous-led grants. Co-partnered
research workshops on Indigenous community engagement and pub-
lishing are other examples of support activities. Workshop and mentor-
ship activities ought to be developed with consideration of Indigenous
scholarly responsibilities and recognition of Indigenous knowledges.
   Scholars research, teach, serve, and evaluate. Central to the aca-
demic project is evaluating and being evaluated for tenure and pro-
motion; this occurs alongside annual reviews, salary reviews, and peer
reviews. Discourse on what is and isn’t worthy scholarship is the defi-
nition of a prickly issue in the academy. Academics have strong beliefs
about what constitutes scholarship. Inviting diverse scholarship into
higher education means developing standards for the evaluation of
such scholarship. Broadening standards on scholarship is often met
with suspicion and with fears that doing so will threaten academic
rigour. Reimaging scholarly standards to invite a more inclusive defi-
nition of scholarship does not forfeit intellectual thoroughness. How-
ever, it does demand that the collegium re-examine the current status
quo of what scholarly works are viewed as worthy (and why); what
weight is given to said scholarly works (and why); and who benefits,
individually and socially, from these scholarly products.
   At present, Indigenous scholarship is evaluated against tenure and
promotion standards that are developed according to traditional Eu-
rocentric academic custom. It is akin to fitting a circle peg into a square
cast. For example, the significance of oral tradition in Indigenous
scholarship means that applicants for tenure (or promotion) working
in and through Indigenous scholarship should have a balance of oral
264   Indigenous Methodologies Strong


      and written scholarly work on their curriculum vitae. If tenure and
      promotion policy valorises written scholarship (e.g., peer-reviewed
      publications), the full scope of Indigenous scholarship will not be
      given due weight in evaluations, and Indigenous scholars are disad-
      vantaged in this process. The result is that Indigenous scholars ex-
      perience difficulties progressing through the ranks (which becomes
      a retention issue), or they burn out from doing double duty trying to
      meet dual accountabilities (which becomes a retention issue). In both
      scenarios, they tend to leave the academy.
           The tenure and promotion policy language is problematic, but that
      is only half the challenge. Academics adjudicate tenure and promo-
      tion standards. Those appointed to tenure and promotion commit-
      tees are not off the hook. Given that the Indigenous professoriate is
      at a 1.4 per cent representation (and arguably most of the 1.4 per cent
      are in a junior rank), those evaluating Indigenous academic work
      are primarily academics who are not Indigenous. Certainly, there are
      ­well-informed allies. However, there are those who are reluctant to
       evaluate Indigenous scholarship for fear they may be misinterpreted
       and considered colonial, racist, or hurtful. Finally, there are other
       ­academics who are not hesitant at all. They critique Indigenous schol-
        arship on long-held Eurocentric standards. This is often done harshly
        without recognizing or caring about cultural bias – implicit or oth-
        erwise. ­Policy and the culture of the collegium both count in creat-
        ing change. I am reminded of the oft-quoted words of Peter Drucker:
        “Culture eats strategy for breakfast.” This does not mean to say that
        culture outweighs policy reform, or strategy for that matter. It does,
        however, point to the power of the academic collegium (as a cultural
        group) in deciding what scholarship has worth. Western academic
        scholarship is typically evaluated through written and peer-reviewed
        works. In tenure and promotion decisions, it is too often the case that
        the collegium resorts to interpreting Indigenous scholarship accord-
        ing to Western academic tradition. Indigenous community and oral
        scholarship is perceived as either lacking or not having value.
           On a positive note, change is happening. There is increased open-
        ness to tenure and promotion policy reform, and the burgeoning of a
        willing collegial culture is encouraging. Certainly not all Indigenous
        scholars and researchers will use Indigenous conceptual models,
        and this choice needs to be respected. However, those who choose
                                                                Conclusion    265


to practice Indigenous scholarship deserve appropriate evaluation.
Reformation of tenure and promotion standards is a must, as is the
improved mentoring of Indigenous scholars through the processes of
departmental, faculty, and university review committees. Organized
and resourced mentoring programs to support Indigenous professors’
movement through the ranks are critical. These mentoring programs,
including decisions about who will mentor and how mentoring will be
designed, will require specific attention by university leadership, the
collegium, and Indigenous faculty. However, Indigenous faculty and
the Indigenous community must not be overburdened in this ­effort.
Evaluating Indigenous scholarship will require building peer-review
capacity in publication venues, academic case files, teaching evalua-
tions, faculty searches, and so forth. The non-Indigenous collegium
has a particular role and responsibility in this transformation.



THE NON-INDIGENOUS COLLEGIUM

In 2020, many Indigenous students are the first generation to graduate
from university. This is because Indigenous people have not had equi-
table access to higher education, as evidenced in the numbers. Nêhiyaw
scholar Blair Stonechild (2006) tells us, “In the 1940s, there were two; in
the 1950s, there were thirty; and in the 1960s, a total of 107 status Indi-
ans acquired post-secondary degrees. In the period 1934 to 1976, there
was only one status Indian Ph.D. recorded” (p. 42). Given their trou-
bling history with schooling on a personal and policy level, Indigenous
peoples have a healthy scepticism about university education. This is a
time of trust building and will involve the non-­Indigenous collegium.
   Currently, there is a growing number of non-Indigenous scholars
who are trusted by Indigenous people or communities because of
their relationships. We might call these individuals “bridge scholars,”
as they help bridge the divide between the academy and the Indige-
nous community as well as between different management systems
inside the academy. In reflecting upon the non-Indigenous supervisor
of her PhD studies, Kathy Absolon (2006) commented, “[She] is one
that is getting me through the hoops – she is the one that says you
need to do this and don’t worry about that – she is kind of the one
that is helping me to be a good hoop dancer” (p. 250). These bridge
266   Indigenous Methodologies Strong


      scholars can be called upon to assist in facilitating Indigenous knowl-
      edges and research. The caution is that they are not a substitute for
      Indigenous people. Working with these individuals does not replace
      connecting with Indigenous peoples. If one wishes to conduct Indige-
      nous research and scholarship, one must be in connection with Indig-
      enous peoples. There is no substitute for this relationship building.
          Non-Indigenous faculty members can also be allies in re-imaging
      academic research policy. For example, there needs to be increased dis-
      cussion on issues such as the responsibilities of the principal investiga-
      tor (PI) and co-investigator (CI). Generally, seasoned scholars (typically
      White scholars) who have had previous research grants tend to be PIs
      while Indigenous academics are invited to contribute as CIs or collabo-
      rators. Setting up two co-principal investigators (CPIs) is preferable in
      these sorts of research projects. However, there needs to be equity in au-
      thority and in the allocation of research funding, through sub-transfer
      or otherwise, if this arrangement is to have force. Continued discussion
      is also needed regarding how research funding bodies structure grants
      and how this perpetuates the status quo. Large grants that allocate
      funding to one individual, and then repeat this funding based upon
      that individual’s research record, exclude more than they include.
          As an Indigenous presence surfaces within Western universities, it
      brings with it all that is Indigenous: thought, custom, culture, practice,
      and bodies. This is causing the academy to pause for a number of rea-
      sons. For some, the hesitancy reflects an active resistance to change,
      while for others, it is born of a passive dismissal. Still others in the acad-
      emy are uncertain how to include, without subsuming, Indigenous
      knowledges. These are Indigenous allies. These individuals under-
      stand the risk to non-Western knowledges within the cultural terrain of
      a Western normative system of established philosophies, protocols, and
      practices. The allies know that “add Indigenous and stir” is not a valid
      response. This is new territory, and an inclusion of Indigenous knowl-
      edges requires multiple strategies for negotiating the existing system.
      When invested non-Indigenous academics listen attentively to what
      upholds Indigenous research and scholarship, possibilities ­expand.
      The new non-homogeneous academic landscape asks that scholars not
      only listen anew but listen differently to what is being said.
          As the academy transforms, the non-Indigenous collegium’s role
      in supporting Indigenous students and faculty is evolving, iterative,
                                                                     Conclusion   267


and cannot be overly prescribed. Consistently, it must uphold rather
than weaken Indigenous research and scholarship. The collegium is
not a homogenous group, and non-Indigenous academics have dif-
fering histories and relationships with Indigenous peoples. For all,
supporting Indigenous methodologies means exploring one’s own be-
liefs and values about knowledge and how these shape practices. For
White scholars, it is about examining whiteness. It is about examining
power. It is ongoing. It is only after scholars carry out this personal
and ­institutional examination that they and their disciplines can be in
a position to acknowledge Indigenous knowledge and what it means
in changing an organizational culture. A new decolonizing relation-
ship between peoples begins with decolonizing hearts and minds.
    Not all academic researchers will engage with Indigenous commu-
nities if doing so is too far from their level of comfort. The spiritual,
holistic nature of Indigenous knowledges can be problematic for the
more traditional, empiricist approach to knowledge. However, for
those seeking to support Indigenous scholarship, the responsibility
is to avoid the Edward S. Curtis lens through which Indigenous peo-
ples are viewed as a studied, exotic other. Awareness generates hope,
which powers the relationships that change the world. A note from
my doctoral research journaling:

     While I sit here, on a self-imposed writing retreat (a great strategy
     that I heard about from Graham Smith), I receive word from a
     Gitxsan friend that she has just been accepted into a PhD pro-
     gram in Toronto within the last two weeks. I have also heard that
     two more of my Indigenous colleagues have been accepted in
     ­doctoral programs in education. This is praxis. Finally, the face
     of an ­Anishinaabe sister comes to mind, a master’s level graduate
     student who attended one of my workshops in February of 2006
     where I presented on this research. She has since been accepted
     into PhD studies in law. She said, “Hurry up Maggie and get
     your research finished, I want to read it.” This is what this whole
     ­journey has been about. Through our minds, hearts, bodies, and
     spirits, we are pushing the edges here in these Western schools.
     We are taking a little bit of friggin’ space …


  And we hold our hands up to those who helped create it.
268   Indigenous Methodologies Strong


      INDIGENOUS METHODOLOGIES AND DECOLONIZING
      THE ACADEMY

      In this conclusion, I return to the question that starts this book: Why a book
      on Indigenous methodologies? Work such as this serves knowledge. It
      evokes an invigorating conversation about Indigenous-­informed research.
      Indigenous inquiry is a relational methodology: its methods are depend-
      ent upon deep respect for those (or that) which it will involve, and those
      (or that) which will feel its consequence. In re-examining relationships that
      serve knowledge, Indigenous inquiry calls forth inherent stewardship re-
      sponsibilities. In both its procedure and consequences, Indigenous meth-
      odologies ask researchers to demonstrate how research gives back to the
      individual and collective good. Research in service of social and ecolog-
      ical justice is inseparable from this value. Global warming, oil wars, reli-
      gious dogma, poverty, pandemics, isolation, unhealthy coping – there is
      evidence everywhere of a world that is struggling, angry, and fragmented.
      Researchers are becoming conscious of their complicity in this fragmenta-
      tion. A desire to take responsibility is growing incrementally, albeit slowly,
      and in its wake is an appetite to learn of knowledge-seeking systems that
      are premised on the stewardship of the earth and care for the other.
          On the qualitative research horizon, there are whisperings of a rich,
      inclusive relationship building. Given a mutual desire, the relationship
      between Indigenous methodologies and qualitative research can deepen,
      build trust, and open once-closed doors. The partners in this new rela-
      tionship must recognize both a distinctive and a common destiny, not
      as a polarized or conversely assimilative conjecture, but as something
      different, something more. This relationship holds promise for ethical
      space, invigorating theory development, and new ways of relating. Cer-
      tainly, coyote will appear – his teachings will cause a stir. But then growth
      mostly depends on the ability to hang in through the stir. Qualitative re-
      search has great potential as a support of ­Indigenous methodologies, and
      supporting Indigenous methodologies will enrich research overall.
          A purpose of this book is to assert Indigenous methodologies as
      grounded in Indigenous knowledge systems. Indigenous knowledge
      systems are holistic, affective, and spiritual, and they move beyond
      the cognitive to the kinetic. They are fluid. Indigenous knowledge sys-
      tems are born of self in relation, and within that social nesting, silent
      self-knowledge is valued. Story is an Indigenous method for sharing
      experience, and interpretative, subjective understanding is accepted.
                                                               Conclusion   269


That which contextualizes life – land, place, kinship, ceremony, lan-
guage, purpose – matters greatly in how we come to know. Indigenous
methodologies are about relationships. In Indigenous country, it will
always come down to good relations. Continued effort to ensure the
ethical conduct of research within Indigenous communities must pro-
ceed. We need to know our communities: tribal nations, urban, Métis,
Inuit. Indigenous inquiry methodologies must be situated in place –
where Indigenous methodologies can breathe. Indigenous methodolo-
gies were not born in the ivory tower; they cannot be held captive there.
    This book stakes a claim for a research design founded upon Indig-
enous epistemology and ethics, Indigenous community engagement,
and the investment of personal experience. Upon this foundation, In-
digenous theory, method, analysis, and findings dissemination flourish.
Emerging from this intellectual, practical, and relational commitment,
Indigenous methodologies rise from the spirit of the ancestors. Though
founded on Indigenous philosophy, Indigenous methodologies are not
silent on colonialism, racism, and the deficit theorizing of Indigenous
people, which too often result in the diminishment of Indigenous cul-
ture. Indigenous theory helps us to push back racism.
    The purpose of an Indigenous methodology book, such as this, is to
guide, not prescribe. Within Indigenous methodological design, there
is no prescribed “look.” How Indigenous knowledges are presented;
the way in which the researcher bundles Indigenous epistemology
and theory with methods, as well as the metaphoric framework that
is utilized to show this relationship; and the manner in which the
researcher gives back – all of these are researcher dependent. Indig-
enous methodologies allow flexibility: there can be no “check-box”
approach.
    Not all Indigenous researchers will choose Indigenous meth-
odologies, nor should this be expected. The tendency to categorize
­Indigenous people as homogeneous, wiping out personal identity and
 choice, haunts Indigenous peoples everywhere, all the time. Simply
 because a researcher is Indigenous does not mean that she ought to, or
 will, conduct research via an Indigenous form of inquiry. Researchers
 not of Indigenous identity, as graduate supervisors, must not assume
 that all Indigenous researchers will go an Indigenous methodologi-
 cal route, though they should not discourage this path either. Indige-
 nous researchers have a responsibility to clarify their methodology or
 methodologies and the rationale for their choices.
270   Indigenous Methodologies Strong


          We must name and claim Indigenous methodologies. Naming mat-
      ters. Naming gives purpose and offers grounding. Reclaiming is nam-
      ing. At this time, Indigenous methodologies grow stronger, but choosing
      this research path remains a political act. As Indigenous researchers,
      we must “take back” research and work towards eradicating egregious
      research practices. That being said, this is not just the responsibility of
      Indigenous researchers; numerous communities have a role: the settler
      community, ally community, Indigenous community, academic com-
      munity, and research community. Specific r­ esponsibilities will depend
      upon the particular community and how it guides, directs, or evaluates
      research. These roles may include providing conversation, support, and
      collegiality. It may mean advocacy. It is a different world when some-
      one has your back. Responsibility implies serving others with knowl-
      edge and action arising from an ethos of respect and reciprocity.
          Why a book on Indigenous methodologies? It is a way to give back
      to community, sustain Indigenous culture, and decolonize the academy.
      Writing these words, I have a memory flash of the young ­Aboriginal
      student wondering about Indigenous methodologies mentioned at the
      start of this book. I think of all those who uphold culture; who find ways
      home, literally or ­figuratively; and who persist through long days of
      one step forward, two back. I hold my hands up to you. I know that not
      all are researchers, but for those who are, I hope this book is a friend.
          This book holds what I have learned about Indigenous methodolo-
      gies. I have sought to communicate these ideas with clarity and com-
      passion. As mentioned in the prologue of this book, in 2020 I made the
      decision to leave the University of Saskatchewan and join the University
      of British Columbia. I remain committed to Indigenous communities in
      Saskatchewan, and it is to Treaty Four I will always return. This was a dif-
      ficult choice. Prior to making this decision, during a time of deep contem-
      plation, a close friend shared these words by Mandy Hale. They would
      not leave my head (or heart) as I offered my thoughts at the closing of
      this second edition of Indigenous Methodologies: “Trust the wait. Embrace
      the uncertainty. When nothing is certain, anything is possible.” To those
      of you who are conducting Indigenous methodologies in a way that is re-
      spectful, responsible, relevant, and compelled by an ethos of reciprocity,
      I wish for you a circle of community with open arms as you draw near.
      I wish for you the joy of knowing a friend and being a friend. I wish for
      you a cuppa hot tea and warm bannock as the storyteller begins. And as
      you walk this earth, I wish you all the love in the world. Ekosi.
Closing Circle Words and an Epilogue




It has been over ten years since Indigenous Methodologies: Characteris-
tics, Conversations, and Contexts first appeared on bookshelves. Since
then, much has happened. In the winter of 2015, the Government of
Canada accepted the final report of the Truth and Reconciliation Com-
mission with its ninety-four calls to action. Shortly thereafter, in 2016,
responding to the calls to action (numbers 43 and 44), the Government
of Canada announced full support of the United Nations Declaration
on the Rights of Indigenous Peoples – without qualification and with
full commitment towards its implementation. In 2019, the final report
of the inquiry into murdered and missing Aboriginal women, Reclaim-
ing Power and Place: The Final Report of the National Inquiry into Missing
and Murdered Indigenous Women and Girls, was published (National
­Inquiry into Missing and Murdered Indigenous Women and Girls,
 2019).
    This movement aligns with a growth in Indigenous higher educa-
 tion. A 2017 survey conducted by Universities Canada (2018) found
 that almost 70 per cent of Canadian universities reference advancement
 of Indigenous reconciliation in their strategic plans. The topography
 of the current Canadian academic landscape is marked by a burgeon-
 ing Indigenous student population, Indigenous-focused academic
 programming, Indigenous student supports, the recruitment of Indig-
 enous faculty, and Indigenous post-secondary leadership. With Indige-
 nization initiatives expanding across the academy, Indigenous research
 and scholarship are seeing traction, as evidenced by an increase in
272   Indigenous Methodologies Strong


      Indigenous-specific research grants and a surge of Indigenous schol-
      arly publications. Yet amid all this progress, there are troubling signs.
         Over the past decade, Canadian universities have experienced se-
      vere budget cutbacks forcing an institutional identity shift. This new
      identity is indicative of a corporatization approach (Woodhouse, 2009);
      universities have an eye out for financial efficiencies and are searching
      for dollars. As Woodhouse reflects (2019) in his article “Knowledge as
      Public Good,” “Governments and the corporate sector share a com-
      mon view that universities should contribute to the economy and max-
      imize private wealth resulting in a pincer movement that has caught
      universities in its grip” (p. 166). The monetization of scholarship is on
      the rise. In the new chill of monetization (not much different from the
      persistent old chill of Eurocentrism), there is, again, a lopsided valu-
      ing of what is academic worth within which Indigenization finds itself
      caught. Certainly, Indigenous research and Indigenization have been
      positive, resulting in increased Indigenous inclusion. I hold my hands
      up to this effort, but I wonder about the sustainability of commodify-
      ing Indigenization, commodifying research, commodifying anything.
         As I write, an often-quoted Cree prophecy comes to mind. I have
      seen this quotation sporadically on posters with chewed edges, pa-
      pering academic office doors, posters that are half falling away, but
      resistant, as though a sign from the ancestors beseeching of us a con-
      sciousness: “Only after the last tree has been cut down, only after the
      last river has been poisoned, only after the last fish has been caught,
      only then will you find that money cannot be eaten.” And I wonder
      about the paradox(es) inherent in Indigenizing the academy. The
      protective buffer is that though Indigenization may be subject to the
      machinations and manipulations of the academy, Indigeneity, itself,
      will never be. Indigeneity is bound to Indigenous bodies. And when
      all the dust settles, it doesn’t much matter where Indigenous peoples
      are – our bodies know home. Home is the earth, the sky, the stars, the
      ceremonies. Home has been here before the academy ever was. Home
      waits. This is our power. This has always been Indigenous power. The
      tension now for Indigenous researchers is negotiating the manifesta-
      tion of neo-individualism, neo-liberalism, neo-colonialism … Money
      makes the world go ’round – I get that. But the problem is that money
      can push aside compassion if we let it. It’s worth remembering that
      when that last fish dies and all the rivers are poisoned, money might
                                        Closing Circle Words and an Epilogue   273


not matter all that much. No doubt this both angers and saddens, but
as all people begin to see that a consumptive world might sink us all,
perhaps there is hope yet.
   As I conclude this epilogue in 2020, I cannot help but comment
on this particular time in history. I find myself finishing this manu-
script as the world is amidst a global pandemic. Time melts, and the
morning birdsongs bring us into another day. Each day we learn of
more deaths, individuals separated from each other as loved ones
make their transition to another world without us there. The econ-
omy seems to be dissolving around us. This novel coronavirus preys
on those disenfranchised from societal equity. The Black, the Brown,
the poor are in jeopardy again. COVID-19 reveals the death divide
between the vulnerable and the privileged. Hearts break.
   Still, amidst this global pause, kindness speckles our world. Song
comes through laptop speakers and escapes from balconies to the
street below. Quarantining sparks imagination, as we performatively
recreate artworks, such as a Cézanne or a Koons, using items – kitchen
socks, toilet paper, apples – that we find in our homes, click a pic,
and share on Instagram to make others smile. Reaching out, we post
online. Cyber toxicity momentarily subsides, and we use technology
to comfort one another. For academics, it is the “Year of the Zoom” as
researchers, teachers, and learners adapt to knowledge transfer on the
threshold of connection. Remote but not alone. Western science works
overtime to benefit us all in the race to find therapeutics and a vaccine.
As stillness descends, together, the whole wide world is paradoxing
to the sound of a global pause. A hush. An echo. A wonder. Pandem-
ics change things. It is a global community in need of reconciliation
and healing. In this space, we remember our ache for the other. As
the Ubuntu say, “I am because you are.” We are societies in search
of justice – research is a light. How might Indigenous methodologies
help illuminate a path? What if the power zone of academia could be
a place of decolonization, reconciliation, and healing? What if …

Having completed the ”closing circle” words of the second edition,
I was ready to release the manuscript to my publisher. Yet I could not
hit send. Sitting in this odd space, it struck me that the first edition
epilogue did not wish to be put away. It quietly and forcefully re-
minded me that our past, if we choose, can inform our future. With a
274   Indigenous Methodologies Strong


      persistent spirit, the epilogue of the first edition of Indigenous Method-
      ologies sought inclusion, and so it closes this work for today.



      EPILOGUE FROM THE FIRST EDITION OF INDIGENOUS
      METHODOLOGIES

      Miskâsowin is a Nêhiyaw term that means going to the centre of your-
      self to find your own belonging (Cardinal & Hildebrandt, 2000, p. 79).
      It is personal knowing; it is Nêhiyaw Kiskêýihtamowin. This word found
      me when I was trying to connect inward knowing, preparation, and
      purpose to my research. For me, it asks, “Why are you doing this re-
      search, and why are you doing it this way?” Answers to those ques-
      tions could only be found in my personal story. Eber Hampton (1995)
      argues that knowing the personal motives behind research matters.
      Conceptually, I first understood this as good research practice; I came
      to realize that it asks that we move beyond intellectual rationales to
      spirit and heart.
          I began doctoral studies thinking that this would be a cognitively
      taxing, intellectual commitment, but that would be the extent. The
      first year went as planned. Entering the second year, my perfect plan
      started to dissipate. I am not sure why. By this time, I had chosen my
      topic, Indigenous methodologies, but it was not gelling as I expected.
      I was taking a pan-Indigenous approach, and it just did not feel in-
      tuitively or conceptually right. For one thing, I was trying to squeeze
      Indigenous holism into a Western methodological mould. The rea-
      sons for this were numerous: personal, unexamined Western bias; ac-
      quiescence to the Western thought that oiled the academic machine;
      a ­reluctance to commit to a methodology; and, the major reason –
      I could not conceptually envision an Indigenous research framework.
      There were few resources to help me. I see the predicament clearly
      now, but at the time, I was lost. In a state of paralyzing ambivalence
      about the possibility of holistic knowledge in the academy, I was si-
      multaneously manifesting experiences that aggravated that which
      had worked well for me in the past. People I met and works I read
      underlined the n  ­ ecessity of experiential knowing as a rich source of
      knowledge. And this was unnerving to me. Although unnerving,
      there was a need to persevere.
                                        Closing Circle Words and an Epilogue   275


    At that point, I cannot say that I really knew what I was looking
for, but I just kept going. I was feeling uncertain, grappling with in-
fluences that challenged the detached linearity that I coveted. In short,
my world was feeling gelatinous. I was reading works by Indigenous
scholars who wrote of sacred knowledges, and the more that I read,
the more I became acutely aware that knowledge is culturally defined
and that Western notions of “truth” or “fact” are bound in their own
paradigms or constructs of belief. So many Indigenous writers were
saying that Indigenous knowledge dwells beyond what the Western
knowledge paradigm can or will accommodate. This was not new to
me from an analytical perspective. It was just that I was starting to in-
tegrate this possibility into my academic life on an intuitive, emotional,
and experiential level. It was a visceral, raw, and bewildering time.
    I opened myself to sacred offerings of knowledge coming from un-
expected places. I paid attention to my dream life. This was not the
first time I embraced holistic knowledges, but it was the first time in
my academic world. I do not know how or why we are shown paths,
how the Great Manitou, the universe, a sacred force, guides us along at
the most confusing times. I still cannot explain exactly what happened
except that I became confused, uncomfortable, alone, sad, and angry.
I think I was confused and mad because I could not understand what
was happening or why I was feeling so alone in this search. ­Because
I was feeling so alone, I had a deep ache to go home, but it was more
than that. I was also experiencing a strong gut feeling that I could not
ignore and that would not go away. I could not explain this to others
who may have helped. It was silent work.
    I spiralled into a visceral journey back through memories of my
Plains childhood. Sharon Butala (2004), a Saskatchewan writer, writes
about her sacred connection to the landscape of the Cyprus Hills in
southwestern Saskatchewan. She recounts how her spiritual relation-
ship with the land emerged during a deep crisis in her life. As I read
her experience, I realized that I could relate. I began to keep a journal
of what I was remembering and the feelings that evoked. I wrote about
my first memory as a young child at a Cree powwow, and how I stood
there shaking. It was my first conscious awareness of a belonging that
would take me years to understand. I had flashbacks of myself search-
ing for arrowheads on the prairie. These memories came back to me as
if no time had passed. I had not lived in Saskatchewan since 1987 and
276   Indigenous Methodologies Strong


      only returned for yearly holidays, and though I had missed family,
      I was comfortable with this arrangement. Yet, as I engaged with my
      research, I was drawn into memories associated with this place on
      both a conscious and subconscious level. I was having vibrant dreams
      with unfamiliar symbols that were speaking to me of sacred knowing
      and identity, and exploring the deep abyss of my personal story that
      was intricately bound with place. I was having a journey into my own
      private miskâsowin.
          During that silent time, I had two significant experiences. These
      experiences led me to consider cultural knowledges coming from a
      non-rational, non-time sort of place about which Little Bear, Deloria
      Jr., and others were writing. The first experience came in the form of
      a dream. I dreamed of the Pueblo poet Leslie Marmon Silko (1977).
      Her message to me was that home was important for Indigenous peo-
      ple and that I needed to think about that. At the time of the dream,
      I had not been consciously planning to go back to Saskatchewan for
      my ­research. I felt assured that I could finish this work from the place
      where I was living, Sidney, British Columbia. I sensed that this dream
      was alerting me, but I was not quite sure what to do about it, and let
      things lie. In the back of my mind, though, I knew enough about my
      culture to pay attention. Later in the fall, the occurrence of a second
      extraordinary happening resolved my ambiguity.
          The second happening revolved around my own Indigenous iden-
      tity. I was contemplating my upbringing and how this would impact
      my research, and I questioned whether, given my experience, I could
      authentically approach Indigenous knowledges. I knew that I had to
      square doing so with being raised outside the culture, particularly if
      my research touched on cultural matters. One week in October, I was
      obsessing about this. I was reflecting on European culture, and the
      metaphor of white pearls kept reappearing in my mind. There were a
      number of reasons for this association, but mostly it occurred because
      pearls for me have strong associations with my childhood in rural Sas-
      katchewan. The Friday before that weekend, I wrote these thoughts
      in my journal. On the following Sunday, I was feeling overwhelmed
      by all the introspection and wanted to go for a drive. I headed out the
      door, and when I got to the car there was a strange gift waiting for
      me – hanging from the handle of the car door was a strand of pearls,
      costume, but unmarred. I was dumbfounded. It was the strangest
                                       Closing Circle Words and an Epilogue   277


synchronicity that I had ever experienced, and I did not know what
to make of it. Yet it sat with me, and I knew instinctively that it was
about facing things, about miskâsowin.
    I will not say that these two events were definitely causally con-
nected, but by the winter of that year, I made the exhausting, emo-
tional decision to go home in the spring to conduct my research after
completing my comprehensive exams and proposal. After reaching
my decision to go home, life had, to a certain extent, gotten back
on track. I was busy teaching and preparing for my comprehensive
­exams. I put the strand of pearls out of my mind and packed it away
 out of sight. I had one vivid dream about it, and would fleetingly
 think about the necklace whenever I saw pearls, but otherwise I put it
 out of my mind. I wrote a proposal for my research outlining a project
 that involved going home to interview Cree researchers and scholars
 and writing the dissertation while in my ancestral territory. In May,
 my partner and I left Vancouver Island for Regina.
    In the spring, I arrived home in Saskatchewan and had the pleasure
 of meeting the five-year-old daughter of my cousin for the first time, a
 beautiful little girl of mixed blood heritage (Cree and Hungarian). She
 was with my Auntie, who was visiting my mother, and she was play-
 ing dress-up with my young niece when I stopped by. As I walked
 into the house, she ran into the kitchen to see who had come in, and
 my Auntie (her kôkom) introduced her to me. She had been into the
 make-up and costume jewellery and was having a fine time. When I
 saw her, I stopped short: she was wearing a child’s toy necklace made
 of pearls. While this was not synchronicity or an extraordinary hap-
 pening of any kind – it was simply a little girl wearing a child’s toy
 necklace – it had meaning for me, bound in the memories of my own
 history. I chose to take this experience to be the universe giving me a
 nod that coming home was important. I still do not understand these
 experiences fully. I have tried to analyse, theorize, and rationalize,
 but there are some things that you cannot deconstruct. As an Elder
 said, some knowledges we cannot know. What I am left with is an
 acceptance that these knowings matter to me inwardly, and because I
 allowed them, they impacted my research path in a good way.
    By being in Saskatchewan, I was able to consider not just Indige-
 nous ways of knowing but my own Nêhiyaw and Saulteaux heritage,
 and I spent time with each of my two families. I began learning Plains
278   Indigenous Methodologies Strong


      Cree. I crossed over from being a younger Auntie to a kôkom (because,
      as one of my sisters says, that is what I am now). I made bannock and
      pickles. I attended traditional ceremonies and community fundraisers
      for school trips. I made road trips from Regina to Winnipeg, Vancou-
      ver, and Victoria, where I had conversations with Indigenous scholars
      and researchers, simultaneously rekindling old friendships and mak-
      ing new ones. I developed a research framework based on a tribal
      epistemology, or Nêhiyaw Kiskêýihtamowin. The list goes on. When
      I left Victoria, I was focusing so intently on how the sacred comes into
      research that I almost missed how the sacred is our research.
           In the winter, just before heading back to Victoria, the dream, the
      pearls, and the conversations that they elicited became clearer. In
      December, just before Christmas, I arranged, at their request, for my
      two mothers to meet. Although it had its awkward moments, it was
      a mostly nice time. During the visit, I had an opportunity to hear
      the story of my birth father. It took me forty years, but the story was
      waiting for me. After the visit, I had some difficulty returning to my
      research because I felt a sense of completion. The project seemed fin-
      ished. The hard work was done, and through it, something changed.
      As my partner Monty said, “You’re different from when you started
      this research.” Of course, I wanted to know how, but he could not
      say – just that it was a “good different.” About a week or so later, I was
      back on track with my research, began working with my data, flew
      back to Victoria, and started teaching again. I graduated and shortly
      afterward accepted a faculty position at the University of Saskatche-
      wan, making the decision to return home permanently. The research
      in which I engaged and the approach that I took was deeply moti-
      vated by miskâsowin.
           Everyone’s experience is different, but the gift of Indigenous ­research
      frameworks is that it allows our story to be a part of our ­research.
      ­Research stories teach us much; they give us much. They tell us who
       we are as researchers, as people. As I write this, I look over to the small
       bookshelf that is next to my desk. On top of the shelf sit several objects
       that have been companions to me. At the base is a miniature easel,
       upon which rests a small four-by-six painting by one of my aunties.
       It is a painting of a wooded space with wild flowers growing among
       the trees. She painted this in the early 1970s, when I was around seven
       or eight years old. To me, this is a place of both magic and possibility.
                                        Closing Circle Words and an Epilogue   279


Sitting on the easel against the picture is a handcrafted dream catcher
that my brother’s son made in school. When I look at it, I feel hope.
Hanging over the easel, on top of the picture, is the strand of pearls. It
propelled me home. At my defence and convocation, I wore it with a
beaded choker. Finally, in a protective circle around all these items lies
a piece of sweet grass, signifying the sacredness of this work. I know
that these symbols represent stories of my life. What I also finally get
from this whole experience is that stories will wait for us until we are
ready. Then they will reveal themselves in purposeful, powerful ways,
and when this happens, we are in the midst of the sacred.
